# 0.1 Foundations: Sets, Relations, and Equivalence Classes

Most textbooks in real analysis begin on page one by handing you the set of real numbers $\mathbb{R}$ as a finished product, listing its algebraic, order, and completeness properties as axioms as if they were carved into stone tablets. While that axiomatic shortcut lets you jump quickly into limits and derivatives, it leaves a nagging foundational question unanswered: *why are we allowed to assume such a number system even exists, and how is it actually built from the ground up?*

In your Calculus I and II courses, you relied on geometric intuition about the number line—treating real numbers as unending decimal expansions and trusting that intuitive properties like the Intermediate Value Theorem simply worked. In Linear Algebra and Probability, you saw how mathematical structures (like vector spaces $\mathbb{R}^n$ or probability spaces $(\Omega, \mathcal{F}, \mathbb{P})$) are defined by precise rules. Real Analysis brings that same rigor to calculus itself. To prove *why* calculus works without circular reasoning or hand-waving pictures, we must first understand what a real number actually is.

In this preliminary chapter, we are going to construct the real number system completely from scratch, marching step-by-step along the classical foundational pipeline:
$$
\emptyset \longrightarrow \mathbb{N} \longrightarrow \mathbb{Z} \longrightarrow \mathbb{Q} \longrightarrow \mathbb{R}
$$
Starting from nothing at all—the empty set $\emptyset$—we will build the natural numbers $\mathbb{N}$, extend them to the integers $\mathbb{Z}$, form the rational numbers $\mathbb{Q}$, and finally complete the rationals to obtain the real numbers $\mathbb{R}$. Before we can take the first step, however, we need a precise toolkit of set-theoretic language. Specifically, we need **ordered pairs**, **arbitrary indexed families of sets**, **direct and inverse images**, **equivalence relations**, and **quotient sets**, because every single jump from one number system to the next ($\mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{Z}$, $\mathbb{Z} \to \mathbb{Q}$, and $\mathbb{Q} \to \mathbb{R}$) relies on bundling simpler objects together into sets or equivalence classes.

## Naive Set Theory Essentials

At the bedrock of modern mathematics lies the concept of a **set**. In everyday mathematical work—often called *naive set theory*—we think of a set intuitively as any well-defined collection of distinct mathematical objects, called its **elements** or **members**. If an object $x$ belongs to a set $A$, we write $x \in A$; if $x$ does not belong to $A$, we write $x \notin A$.

What makes two sets the same? Unlike a list or a sequence in calculus, a set does not care about the order in which its elements are written down, nor does it keep track of duplicates. A set is determined *entirely* by which elements belong to it. In formal set theory, this foundational rule is called the **Axiom of Extensionality**.

> [!abstract] Definition: Subset, Set Equality, and Proper Subset
> Let $A$ and $B$ be sets. We say that $A$ is a **subset** of $B$, written $A \subseteq B$, if every element of $A$ is also an element of $B$:
> $$
> A \subseteq B \iff (\forall x, \; x \in A \implies x \in B)
> $$
> By the **Axiom of Extensionality**, two sets $A$ and $B$ are **equal**, written $A = B$, if and only if they have the exact same elements—which is logically equivalent to saying that each is a subset of the other:
> $$
> A = B \iff (A \subseteq B \text{ and } B \subseteq A)
> $$
> If $A \subseteq B$ and $A \neq B$ (meaning every element of $A$ lies in $B$, and $B$ contains at least one element not in $A$), we say that $A$ is a **proper subset** of $B$ and write $A \subsetneq B$.

In other words, the definition of set equality gives us our workhorse proof strategy throughout Real Analysis: whenever you are asked to prove that two sets $A$ and $B$ are equal ($A = B$), you almost always break the proof into two halves called a **mutual inclusion** (or **double inclusion**) argument:
1. **Show $A \subseteq B$:** Pick an arbitrary element $x \in A$ and deduce through definitions and logic that $x \in B$.
2. **Show $B \subseteq A$:** Pick an arbitrary element $x \in B$ and deduce that $x \in A$.

We also postulate the existence of a set that contains no elements whatsoever, called the **empty set** and denoted by $\emptyset$. Notice that by the Axiom of Extensionality, there can be *only one* empty set: if $\emptyset_1$ and $\emptyset_2$ both have no elements, then the statement "$x \in \emptyset_1 \implies x \in \emptyset_2$" is **vacuously true** (because the hypothesis $x \in \emptyset_1$ is always false!), so $\emptyset_1 \subseteq \emptyset_2$ and $\emptyset_2 \subseteq \emptyset_1$, forcing $\emptyset_1 = \emptyset_2$. By the exact same vacuous truth logic, $\emptyset \subseteq A$ for every set $A$.

### Why "Naive" Set Theory Needs Guardrails: Russell's Paradox and ZFC

You might wonder why we call our intuitive view of sets *naive* set theory. In the late nineteenth century, pioneers of set theory assumed a rule called *Unrestricted Comprehension*: for any logical property $P(x)$ you can write down, you can form the set $\{x : P(x)\}$ of all objects satisfying $P(x)$.

In 1901, the philosopher and mathematician Bertrand Russell discovered that this seemingly innocent rule blows up the foundations of mathematics. Suppose we let $P(x)$ be the property "$x$ is a set that does not contain itself as an element," i.e., $x \notin x$, and we try to form the set
$$
R := \{x : x \notin x\}
$$
Now ask the fatal question: **does $R$ belong to $R$?**
- If $R \in R$, then by the defining membership rule of $R$, $R$ must satisfy the property $R \notin R$—a contradiction!
- If $R \notin R$, then $R$ satisfies the exact requirement for membership in $R$, which forces $R \in R$—again a contradiction!

This contradiction ($R \in R \iff R \notin R$) is **Russell's Paradox**. To rescue mathematics without giving up set theory, logicians developed **Zermelo–Fraenkel set theory with the Axiom of Choice (ZFC)**. You do not need to memorize the axioms of ZFC for Real Analysis, but you should understand its core safety rule, known as the **Axiom Schema of Specification** (or *Restricted Comprehension*): you are *not* allowed to form $\{x : P(x)\}$ out of thin air across the entire universe; instead, you must always start with an **already existing set $A$** and carve out the subset of elements in $A$ that satisfy $P(x)$:
$$
\{x \in A : P(x)\}
$$
If you try to run Russell's Paradox inside an existing set $A$ by defining $R_A := \{x \in A : x \notin x\}$, the contradiction vanishes! Instead of a paradox, checking whether $R_A \in R_A$ simply proves the harmless theorem that $R_A \notin A$—which in particular proves there is no "universal set of all sets."

Alongside carving out subsets, ZFC guarantees that for any set $X$, we can collect *all subsets of $X$* into a single valid set.

> [!abstract] Definition: Power Set
> Let $X$ be a set. The **power set** of $X$, denoted $\mathcal{P}(X)$ (or $2^X$), is the set whose elements are all the subsets of $X$:
> $$
> \mathcal{P}(X) := \{A : A \subseteq X\}
> $$

If you have taken Probability, you have already met power sets under another name: when $\Omega$ is a discrete sample space (like the outcomes $\Omega = \{1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6\}$ of rolling a die), an *event* is a subset $E \subseteq \Omega$, and the collection of all possible events is precisely the power set $\mathcal{P}(\Omega)$. Always keep track of the "type level" when working with power sets: elements of $X$ are points $x \in X$, whereas elements of $\mathcal{P}(X)$ are *subsets* $A \subseteq X$ (so $A \in \mathcal{P}(X) \iff A \subseteq X$). Notice that $\emptyset \in \mathcal{P}(X)$ and $X \in \mathcal{P}(X)$ for every set $X$; in fact, even for the empty set $X = \emptyset$, its power set is non-empty: $\mathcal{P}(\emptyset) = \{\emptyset\}$, a set containing one element!

### Encoding Order Using Unordered Sets: Kuratowski's Ordered Pair

In set theory, a brace-enclosed collection $\{a, b\}$ is inherently *unordered*: by the Axiom of Extensionality, $\{a, b\} = \{b, a\}$ because both sets have the same members, and $\{a, a\} = \{a\}$ because an element is either in a set or not.

However, almost everything we do in mathematics requires **order**! In Linear Algebra, the vector $(2, 5) \in \mathbb{R}^2$ is completely different from $(5, 2)$. When we construct the integers in Section 0.3 as formal differences $a - b$ of natural numbers, or the rationals in Section 0.4 as formal fractions $a / b$, order is essential because $2 - 5 \neq 5 - 2$ and $2/5 \neq 5/2$. If everything in mathematics is built purely out of unordered sets, how can we possibly define an **ordered pair** $(a, b)$ using only curly braces $\{\,\cdot\,\}$ so that $(a, b)$ remembers which element came first and which came second?

In 1921, the Polish mathematician Kazimierz Kuratowski gave a remarkably clever solution.

> [!abstract] Definition: Ordered Pair and Cartesian Product
> Given any two objects $a$ and $b$, the **ordered pair** $(a, b)$ is defined set-theoretically as
> $$
> (a, b) := \{\{a\}, \{a, b\}\}
> $$
> Given two sets $A$ and $B$, their **Cartesian product** $A \times B$ is the set of all ordered pairs whose first coordinate lies in $A$ and whose second coordinate lies in $B$:
> $$
> A \times B := \{(a, b) : a \in A \text{ and } b \in B\}
> $$

Why does Kuratowski's definition $\{\{a\}, \{a, b\}\}$ successfully capture order? Look closely at the two member sets inside $\{\{a\}, \{a, b\}\}$:
- The first coordinate $a$ is singled out as the **only element that belongs to both inner sets** $\{a\}$ and $\{a, b\}$.
- Once $a$ is identified, the second coordinate $b$ is recovered as the other element in $\{a, b\}$ (or, if $\{a, b\}$ only has one element because $a = b$, then $b$ is simply $a$ as well).

This asymmetry between the roles of $a$ and $b$ guarantees the **fundamental property of ordered pairs** (which you will prove rigorously in **Problem 0.1.1**):
$$
(a, b) = (c, d) \iff a = c \text{ and } b = d
$$
Once we have ordered pairs and Cartesian products, we can demystify two concepts that often feel vague in introductory courses: **relations** and **functions**. Instead of thinking of a relation or a function as a mysterious "process" or "rule" floating outside set theory, we define both as ordinary subsets of Cartesian products!

> [!abstract] Definition: Binary Relations and Functions as Sets
> 1. Let $A$ and $B$ be sets. A **binary relation** between $A$ and $B$ is any subset $R \subseteq A \times B$. When $A = B = X$, we call $R \subseteq X \times X$ a **binary relation on $X$**, and we write $x R y$ (or $x \sim y$) as shorthand for $(x, y) \in R$.
> 2. A **function** (or **map**) from $A$ to $B$, written $f: A \to B$, is a binary relation $f \subseteq A \times B$ satisfying the **vertical line property**: for every input $a \in A$, there exists a *unique* output $b \in B$ such that $(a, b) \in f$:
>    $$
>    \forall a \in A, \; \exists! b \in B \text{ such that } (a, b) \in f
>    $$
>    When $(a, b) \in f$, we write $f(a) = b$.

Notice how natural this is if you connect it to Calculus I: in calculus, you drew the *graph* of a function $f: \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$ as the set of points $\{(x, f(x)) : x \in \mathbb{R}\}$ in the Cartesian plane $\mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{R}$, and you checked whether a curve was a valid function using the *vertical line test* (every vertical line through $x \in A$ hits the graph in exactly one point $y \in B$). In set theory, we simply cut out the middleman and identify the function $f$ directly with its graph! Similarly, familiar comparisons like $<$ or $\le$ on $\mathbb{R}$ are not magical symbols—the relation $\le$ is literally the subset $\{(x, y) \in \mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{R} : x \le y\}$ lying above the diagonal line $y = x$ in the plane.

## Set Operations, Indexed Families, and De Morgan's Laws

In Probability and Statistics, you worked extensively with events $A, B \subseteq \Omega$ inside a sample space $\Omega$: the event "$A$ or $B$ occurs" is the union $A \cup B$, the event "both $A$ and $B$ occur" is the intersection $A \cap B$, and the event "$A$ does not occur" is the complement $A^c = \Omega \setminus A$.

In Real Analysis, however, we rarely combine just two sets or even a finite list of sets $A_1, A_2, \dots, A_n$. When we study limits, open sets, compact sets, or Dedekind cuts, we routinely take unions and intersections of **infinitely many sets**—and often *uncountably* many sets! To handle this cleanly, we introduce the notion of an **indexed family of sets**.

Suppose we have a non-empty set $I$, which we call an **index set**, and for every index $i \in I$ we are given a subset $A_i \subseteq X$. We denote this collection by $\{A_i\}_{i \in I}$.
- If $I = \{1, 2, \dots, n\}$, then $\{A_i\}_{i \in I}$ is just a finite list of sets $A_1, A_2, \dots, A_n$.
- If $I = \mathbb{N} = \{1, 2, 3, \dots\}$, then $\{A_n\}_{n \in \mathbb{N}}$ is a sequence of sets $A_1, A_2, A_3, \dots$ (just like a sequence of events in probability).
- Crucially, $I$ does **not** have to be $\mathbb{N}$! The index set $I$ can be any set whatsoever, such as the real interval $I = (0, 1)$ or all of $\mathbb{R}$. For instance, for each real number $r \in (0, 1)$, we might define the open interval $A_r := (-r, r) \subseteq \mathbb{R}$; then $\{A_r\}_{r \in (0, 1)}$ is an uncountable family of intervals indexed by $r \in (0, 1)$.

Let's state the formal definitions of binary and arbitrary indexed set operations.

> [!abstract] Definition: Set Operations and Arbitrary Indexed Unions/Intersections
> Let $A, B$ be subsets of a universal set $X$, and let $\{A_i\}_{i \in I}$ be a family of subsets of $X$ indexed by a non-empty set $I$.
> 1. **Binary Union, Intersection, and Set Difference:**
>    $$
>    \begin{aligned}
>    A \cup B &:= \{x \in X : x \in A \text{ or } x \in B\} \\
>    A \cap B &:= \{x \in X : x \in A \text{ and } x \in B\} \\
>    A \setminus B &:= \{x \in X : x \in A \text{ and } x \notin B\}
>    \end{aligned}
>    $$
>    The **complement** of $A$ (relative to the ambient space $X$) is $A^c := X \setminus A = \{x \in X : x \notin A\}$. Two sets $A$ and $B$ are called **disjoint** if they have no elements in common, i.e., $A \cap B = \emptyset$.
> 2. **Arbitrary Indexed Union and Intersection:**
>    $$
>    \begin{aligned}
>    \bigcup_{i \in I} A_i &:= \{x \in X : \exists i \in I \text{ such that } x \in A_i\} \\
>    \bigcap_{i \in I} A_i &:= \{x \in X : \forall i \in I, \; x \in A_i\}
>    \end{aligned}
>    $$

Pay close attention to the exact translation between set operations and logical quantifiers—this translation is the secret to writing clean proofs in Real Analysis:
- **Union ($\bigcup_{i \in I} A_i$) translates to $\exists i \in I$:** to prove $x \in \bigcup_{i \in I} A_i$, you only need to find *at least one* index $i_0 \in I$ such that $x \in A_{i_0}$.
- **Intersection ($\bigcap_{i \in I} A_i$) translates to $\forall i \in I$:** to prove $x \in \bigcap_{i \in I} A_i$, you must show that $x \in A_i$ holds for *every single* index $i \in I$.

Let's work through a concrete example on the real line to see how infinite unions and intersections behave—and why they can produce surprises that finite operations never do!

> [!example] Example: Infinite Unions and Intersections of Shrinking Intervals
> For each positive integer $n \in \mathbb{N} = \{1, 2, 3, \dots\}$, consider the open interval $A_n := \left(-\frac{1}{n}, \frac{1}{n}\right) \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ and the half-open interval $B_n := \left(0, \frac{1}{n}\right] \subseteq \mathbb{R}$. Determine the infinite union and infinite intersection of both families:
> $$
> \bigcup_{n \in \mathbb{N}} A_n, \qquad \bigcap_{n \in \mathbb{N}} A_n, \qquad \text{and} \qquad \bigcap_{n \in \mathbb{N}} B_n
> $$

**Solution:**
First, notice that as $n$ increases, $\frac{1}{n}$ shrinks toward $0$, so the sets are nested inside one another: $A_1 \supseteq A_2 \supseteq A_3 \supseteq \cdots$, where $A_1 = (-1, 1)$.
1. **Finding $\bigcup_{n \in \mathbb{N}} A_n$:** Since $A_n = \left(-\frac{1}{n}, \frac{1}{n}\right) \subseteq (-1, 1) = A_1$ for every $n \in \mathbb{N}$, any element $x$ belonging to at least one $A_n$ automatically lies in $A_1$. Conversely, every element of $A_1$ belongs to $\bigcup_{n \in \mathbb{N}} A_n$ (taking the index $n = 1$). By mutual inclusion, $\bigcup_{n \in \mathbb{N}} A_n = (-1, 1)$.
2. **Finding $\bigcap_{n \in \mathbb{N}} A_n$:** We claim that $\bigcap_{n \in \mathbb{N}} \left(-\frac{1}{n}, \frac{1}{n}\right) = \{0\}$.
   - *Inclusion $\{0\} \subseteq \bigcap_{n \in \mathbb{N}} A_n$:* For every $n \in \mathbb{N}$, we have $-\frac{1}{n} < 0 < \frac{1}{n}$, so $0 \in A_n$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$. Thus $0 \in \bigcap_{n \in \mathbb{N}} A_n$.
   - *Inclusion $\bigcap_{n \in \mathbb{N}} A_n \subseteq \{0\}$:* Suppose $x \in \mathbb{R}$ is non-zero ($x \neq 0$). Then $|x| > 0$. By choosing a positive integer $n_0 > \frac{1}{|x|}$ (so that $\frac{1}{n_0} < |x|$), we see that $x \notin \left(-\frac{1}{n_0}, \frac{1}{n_0}\right) = A_{n_0}$. Since $x$ fails to belong to $A_{n_0}$, $x$ cannot belong to the intersection $\bigcap_{n \in \mathbb{N}} A_n$. Therefore, the only element left in the intersection is $0$, proving $\bigcap_{n \in \mathbb{N}} A_n = \{0\}$. Notice the moral: even though every $A_n$ is an *open interval* of positive length, their infinite intersection collapses to a *single point*!
3. **Finding $\bigcap_{n \in \mathbb{N}} B_n$:** Here $B_n = \left(0, \frac{1}{n}\right]$ excludes $0$. If $x \le 0$, then $x \notin B_1$. If $x > 0$, then choosing an integer $n_0 > \frac{1}{x}$ gives $\frac{1}{n_0} < x$, so $x \notin B_{n_0}$. Thus *no* real number $x$ belongs to every $B_n$, which means $\bigcap_{n \in \mathbb{N}} \left(0, \frac{1}{n}\right] = \emptyset$. Even though every $B_n$ is non-empty, their infinite intersection is completely empty! $\blacksquare$

Because union corresponds to $\exists$ and intersection corresponds to $\forall$, what happens when we take the complement of an arbitrary union or intersection? In logic, negating an existential statement turns it into a universal statement: saying "it is *false* that there exists an index $i$ with $x \in A_i$" is the exact same thing as saying "for *every* index $i$, $x \notin A_i$." In symbols:
$$
\neg\left(\exists i \in I, \; x \in A_i\right) \iff \forall i \in I, \; x \notin A_i
$$
Similarly, negating a universal statement turns it into an existential statement ($\neg(\forall i \in I, \; x \in A_i) \iff \exists i \in I, \; x \notin A_i$). This duality between $\exists$ and $\forall$ under negation immediately gives us **De Morgan's Laws** for arbitrary indexed families—generalizing the familiar two-event De Morgan rules $(A \cup B)^c = A^c \cap B^c$ and $(A \cap B)^c = A^c \cup B^c$ from Probability to any collection of sets, finite or infinite.

> [!theorem] De Morgan's Laws for Arbitrary Indexed Families
> Let $\{A_i\}_{i \in I}$ be a family of subsets of $X$ indexed by a non-empty set $I$. Then:
> $$
> \left(\bigcup_{i \in I} A_i\right)^c = \bigcap_{i \in I} A_i^c \qquad \text{and} \qquad \left(\bigcap_{i \in I} A_i\right)^c = \bigcup_{i \in I} A_i^c
> $$

**Proof:**
To prove the first identity, we track an arbitrary element $x \in X$ step-by-step through the quantifier definitions of complement, union, and intersection:
$$
\begin{aligned}
x \in \left(\bigcup_{i \in I} A_i\right)^c &\iff x \notin \bigcup_{i \in I} A_i \\
                                           &\iff \neg \left(\exists i \in I \text{ such that } x \in A_i\right) \\
                                           &\iff \forall i \in I, \; x \notin A_i \\
                                           &\iff \forall i \in I, \; x \in A_i^c \\
                                           &\iff x \in \bigcap_{i \in I} A_i^c
\end{aligned}
$$
Since every step is an "if and only if" ($\iff$), the two sets contain the exact same elements and are therefore equal by the Axiom of Extensionality.

For the second identity, we track $x \in X$ in the exact same way, flipping $\forall$ into $\exists$:
$$
\begin{aligned}
x \in \left(\bigcap_{i \in I} A_i\right)^c &\iff x \notin \bigcap_{i \in I} A_i \\
                                           &\iff \neg \left(\forall i \in I, \; x \in A_i\right) \\
                                           &\iff \exists i \in I \text{ such that } x \notin A_i \\
                                           &\iff \exists i \in I \text{ such that } x \in A_i^c \\
                                           &\iff x \in \bigcup_{i \in I} A_i^c
\end{aligned}
$$
(Alternatively, one can deduce the second law directly from the first by applying the first law to the family of complements $B_i := A_i^c$ and taking the complement of both sides, using $(E^c)^c = E$.) $\blacksquare$

## Functions, Bijections, and Direct/Inverse Images

Given a function $f: A \to B$, the set $A$ of allowable inputs is called the **domain** of $f$, and the target set $B$ where outputs are declared to live is called the **codomain** of $f$. Be careful not to confuse the codomain $B$ with the **range** (or **image**) of $f$, which is the subset $f(A) := \{f(a) : a \in A\} \subseteq B$ consisting of those elements in $B$ that actually get hit by at least one input from $A$. For example, if we define $f: \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$ by $f(x) = x^2$, the codomain is all of $\mathbb{R}$, whereas the range is only $[0, \infty)$.

In Linear Algebra, you classified linear transformations $T: V \to W$ by asking two fundamental questions about solving the equation $T(x) = b$:
1. **Uniqueness of solutions:** Can two different inputs $x_1 \neq x_2$ collide at the same output $T(x_1) = T(x_2)$? (For linear maps, this is controlled by whether the nullspace $\ker(T)$ is $\{0\}$.)
2. **Existence of solutions:** Does *every* target vector $b \in W$ get hit by at least one input $x \in V$? (For linear maps, this is whether the column space / range spans all of $W$.)

These exact same two questions apply to *any* function $f: A \to B$ between arbitrary sets, giving us the concepts of **injectivity**, **surjectivity**, and **bijectivity**.

> [!abstract] Definition: Injective, Surjective, and Bijective Functions
> Let $f: A \to B$ be a function.
> 1. **Injective (One-to-One):** We say $f$ is **injective** if distinct inputs in $A$ always produce distinct outputs in $B$. In contrapositive form (which is the form you almost always use in proofs):
>    $$
>    \forall a_1, a_2 \in A, \quad f(a_1) = f(a_2) \implies a_1 = a_2
>    $$
>    (Equivalently, $a_1 \neq a_2 \implies f(a_1) \neq f(a_2)$.)
> 2. **Surjective (Onto):** We say $f$ is **surjective** if every element of the codomain $B$ is hit by at least one element of $A$:
>    $$
>    \forall b \in B, \; \exists a \in A \text{ such that } f(a) = b
>    $$
>    (Equivalently, the range equals the entire codomain: $f(A) = B$.)
> 3. **Bijective (One-to-One Correspondence):** We say $f$ is **bijective** (or a **bijection**) if it is both injective and surjective. That is, for every $b \in B$, there exists a *unique* $a \in A$ such that $f(a) = b$.

Why are bijections the gold standard of functions? Suppose you have a function $f: A \to B$ and you want to run the arrows backward to create a two-sided **inverse function** $f^{-1}: B \to A$ (satisfying $f^{-1}(f(a)) = a$ for all $a \in A$ and $f(f^{-1}(b)) = b$ for all $b \in B$). What could go wrong if you try to define $f^{-1}(b)$ as "the input $a \in A$ that maps to $b$"?
- If $f$ is **not surjective**, there is some $b \in B$ that is never hit by $f$, so $f^{-1}(b)$ has *no output* in $A$!
- If $f$ is **not injective**, there are two distinct inputs $a_1 \neq a_2$ in $A$ that map to the same $b \in B$, so $f^{-1}(b)$ would try to point to *two different outputs* at once, violating the definition of a function!

Therefore, a function $f: A \to B$ possesses a two-sided inverse function $f^{-1}: B \to A$ **if and only if $f$ is bijective**! When $f$ is bijective, it pairs up the elements of $A$ and $B$ in an exact one-to-one partner dance; in Section 0.7, we will use bijections as our mathematical yardstick for comparing the sizes (cardinalities) of infinite sets.

### Pushing Sets Forward and Pulling Sets Back: Direct and Inverse Images

What if a function $f: A \to B$ is **not** bijective—say $f(x) = x^2$ from $\mathbb{R}$ to $\mathbb{R}$, or a non-invertible matrix transformation $T: \mathbb{R}^3 \to \mathbb{R}^2$? Even though there is no point-to-point inverse function $f^{-1}: B \to A$, we can still use $f$ to move **subsets** back and forth between $A$ and $B$!
- Given a subset $E \subseteq A$ of inputs, we can collect all outputs generated by elements of $E$; this pushes $E$ forward into a subset of $B$.
- Given a target subset $H \subseteq B$ of outputs, we can collect all inputs in $A$ whose outputs land inside $H$; this pulls $H$ backward into a subset of $A$.

> [!abstract] Definition: Direct Image and Inverse Image (Preimage)
> Let $f: A \to B$ be any function (not necessarily injective or surjective).
> 1. For any subset $E \subseteq A$, the **direct image** (or simply **image**) of $E$ under $f$ is the subset of $B$ defined by:
>    $$
>    f(E) := \{f(x) : x \in E\} = \{y \in B : \exists x \in E \text{ such that } f(x) = y\}
>    $$
> 2. For any subset $H \subseteq B$, the **inverse image** (or **preimage**) of $H$ under $f$ is the subset of $A$ defined by:
>    $$
>    f^{-1}(H) := \{x \in A : f(x) \in H\}
>    $$

> [!warning] Caution: Preimages Do NOT Require $f$ to Be Invertible, and They Behave Much Better Than Direct Images!
> Look closely at the notation $f^{-1}(H)$: beginners often panic when they see $f^{-1}(H)$ for a non-bijective function like $f(x) = x^2$ or $f(x) = \sin x$, thinking "Wait, $f$ isn't invertible!" Here is the crucial distinction:
> - As a **point-to-point function** $B \to A$, $f^{-1}(b)$ only exists when $f$ is a bijection.
> - As a **set-to-set operation** $\mathcal{P}(B) \to \mathcal{P}(A)$, the preimage $f^{-1}(H) = \{x \in A : f(x) \in H\}$ is **always well-defined for every function $f: A \to B$ and every subset $H \subseteq B$**! If no inputs land in $H$, then $f^{-1}(H)$ is simply the empty set $\emptyset$; if multiple inputs land in $H$, $f^{-1}(H)$ simply collects all of them into a subset of $A$.
>
> Even more importantly for Real Analysis and Topology: **inverse images $f^{-1}(\cdot)$ preserve ALL set operations** (unions, intersections, set differences, and complements), whereas **direct images $f(\cdot)$ preserve unions but generally FAIL to preserve intersections and complements**!

Why is there such a dramatic difference in behavior between $f^{-1}(\cdot)$ and $f(\cdot)$? Look at their logical definitions:
- Checking whether $x \in f^{-1}(H)$ is a **quantifier-free test on a single element**: you just plug $x$ into $f$ and ask whether $f(x) \in H$.
- Checking whether $y \in f(E)$ hides an **existential quantifier** ($\exists x \in E \text{ such that } f(x) = y$). While existential quantifiers commute with "or" ($\exists$ commutes with $\bigcup$), they do *not* commute with "and" ($\exists$ does not commute with $\forall$ / $\bigcap$)!

Let's see this collision phenomenon in action with a familiar function from Calculus I before proving the general theorem.

> [!example] Example: Direct and Inverse Images Under $f(x) = x^2$
> Let $f: \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$ be defined by $f(x) = x^2$.
> 1. Compute the preimages $f^{-1}([1, 4])$, $f^{-1}([-9, -1])$, and $f^{-1}([-4, 9])$.
> 2. Let $E_1 = [-2, 0]$ and $E_2 = [0, 2]$. Compare $f(E_1 \cap E_2)$ with $f(E_1) \cap f(E_2)$, and explain geometrically why they differ.

**Solution:**
1. By definition, $f^{-1}(H) = \{x \in \mathbb{R} : x^2 \in H\}$:
   - For $H = [1, 4]$, we ask which real numbers satisfy $1 \le x^2 \le 4$. Taking square roots gives $1 \le |x| \le 2$, which includes both positive and negative inputs:
     $$
     f^{-1}([1, 4]) = [-2, -1] \cup [1, 2]
     $$
   - For $H = [-9, -1]$, no real number satisfies $-9 \le x^2 \le -1$ because $x^2 \ge 0$ for all $x \in \mathbb{R}$. Thus:
     $$
     f^{-1}([-9, -1]) = \emptyset
     $$
   - For $H = [-4, 9]$, the condition $-4 \le x^2 \le 9$ simplifies to $0 \le x^2 \le 9$ (since $x^2 \ge 0$ is automatic), which gives $|x| \le 3$, so:
     $$
     f^{-1}([-4, 9]) = [-3, 3]
     $$
2. Now let $E_1 = [-2, 0]$ and $E_2 = [0, 2]$.
   - First, intersect the inputs in the domain and then push forward: $E_1 \cap E_2 = [-2, 0] \cap [0, 2] = \{0\}$, so
     $$
     f(E_1 \cap E_2) = f(\{0\}) = \{0^2\} = \{0\}
     $$
   - Next, push each set forward first and then intersect the outputs in the codomain: as $x$ ranges over $E_1 = [-2, 0]$, $x^2$ ranges over $[0, 4]$; similarly, as $x$ ranges over $E_2 = [0, 2]$, $x^2$ also ranges over $[0, 4]$. Therefore:
     $$
     f(E_1) \cap f(E_2) = [0, 4] \cap [0, 4] = [0, 4]
     $$
   Clearly $\{0\} \subsetneq [0, 4]$, so $f(E_1 \cap E_2) \neq f(E_1) \cap f(E_2)$! Geometrically, why did $y = 4$ end up in $f(E_1) \cap f(E_2)$ even though it is not in $f(E_1 \cap E_2)$? Because $f$ folded the negative half-line onto the positive half-line: $E_1$ produced $y = 4$ using the negative input $x_1 = -2 \in E_1$, while $E_2$ produced the *same* output $y = 4$ using a *different* input $x_2 = +2 \in E_2$. Even though $E_1$ and $E_2$ didn't share the input $\pm 2$, their distinct inputs collided at the output $4$! $\blacksquare$

Now let's state and prove the complete theorem governing how direct and inverse images interact with set operations.

> [!theorem] Behavior of Direct and Inverse Images under Set Operations
> Let $f: A \to B$ be a function, let $\{E_i\}_{i \in I}$ be a family of subsets of $A$ indexed by a non-empty set $I$, and let $\{H_i\}_{i \in I}$ and $H$ be subsets of $B$.
> 1. **Inverse Images Preserve Unions, Intersections, and Complements:**
>    $$
>    f^{-1}\left(\bigcup_{i \in I} H_i\right) = \bigcup_{i \in I} f^{-1}(H_i), \qquad f^{-1}\left(\bigcap_{i \in I} H_i\right) = \bigcap_{i \in I} f^{-1}(H_i), \qquad f^{-1}(B \setminus H) = A \setminus f^{-1}(H)
>    $$
> 2. **Direct Images Preserve Unions, but Only Contained in Intersections:**
>    $$
>    f\left(\bigcup_{i \in I} E_i\right) = \bigcup_{i \in I} f(E_i), \qquad f\left(\bigcap_{i \in I} E_i\right) \subseteq \bigcap_{i \in I} f(E_i)
>    $$
>    Moreover, equality $f\left(\bigcap_{i \in I} E_i\right) = \bigcap_{i \in I} f(E_i)$ holds for *all* families of subsets $\{E_i\}_{i \in I}$ of $A$ if and only if $f$ is **injective**.

**Proof:**
1. Every inverse image identity is a direct chain of logical equivalences using the defining rule $x \in f^{-1}(S) \iff f(x) \in S$:
   - **Preimage of a union:** For any $x \in A$,
     $$
     \begin{aligned}
     x \in f^{-1}\left(\bigcup_{i \in I} H_i\right) &\iff f(x) \in \bigcup_{i \in I} H_i \\
                                                    &\iff \exists i \in I \text{ such that } f(x) \in H_i \\
                                                    &\iff \exists i \in I \text{ such that } x \in f^{-1}(H_i) \\
                                                    &\iff x \in \bigcup_{i \in I} f^{-1}(H_i)
     \end{aligned}
     $$
   - **Preimage of an intersection:** Replacing $\exists i \in I$ with $\forall i \in I$ in the chain above gives:
     $$
     \begin{aligned}
     x \in f^{-1}\left(\bigcap_{i \in I} H_i\right) &\iff f(x) \in \bigcap_{i \in I} H_i \\
                                                    &\iff \forall i \in I, \; f(x) \in H_i \\
                                                    &\iff \forall i \in I, \; x \in f^{-1}(H_i) \\
                                                    &\iff x \in \bigcap_{i \in I} f^{-1}(H_i)
     \end{aligned}
     $$
   - **Preimage of a complement:** For any $x \in A$,
     $$
     x \in f^{-1}(B \setminus H) \iff f(x) \in B \setminus H \iff f(x) \notin H \iff x \notin f^{-1}(H) \iff x \in A \setminus f^{-1}(H)
     $$
2. Now we analyze direct images:
   - **Direct image of a union:** For any $y \in B$, two existential quantifiers commute freely:
     $$
     \begin{aligned}
     y \in f\left(\bigcup_{i \in I} E_i\right) &\iff \exists x \in \bigcup_{i \in I} E_i \text{ such that } f(x) = y \\
                                               &\iff \exists i \in I \text{ and } \exists x \in E_i \text{ such that } f(x) = y \\
                                               &\iff \exists i \in I \text{ such that } y \in f(E_i) \\
                                               &\iff y \in \bigcup_{i \in I} f(E_i)
     \end{aligned}
     $$
   - **Direct image of an intersection:** Suppose $y \in f\left(\bigcap_{i \in I} E_i\right)$. By definition, there exists a *single* element $x \in \bigcap_{i \in I} E_i$ such that $f(x) = y$. Because $x$ belongs to the intersection, $x \in E_i$ for every $i \in I$. Consequently, $y = f(x) \in f(E_i)$ for every $i \in I$, which means $y \in \bigcap_{i \in I} f(E_i)$. This proves the inclusion $f\left(\bigcap_{i \in I} E_i\right) \subseteq \bigcap_{i \in I} f(E_i)$.
   - **When does equality hold for all families?** Suppose first that $f$ is injective, and take any $y \in \bigcap_{i \in I} f(E_i)$. Since $I$ is non-empty, pick some index $i_0 \in I$; because $y \in f(E_{i_0})$, there is some $x \in E_{i_0}$ with $f(x) = y$. Now for any other index $i \in I$, we also have $y \in f(E_i)$, so there exists some $x_i \in E_i$ with $f(x_i) = y$. Since $f(x_i) = y = f(x)$ and $f$ is injective, we must have $x_i = x$! Therefore, this single element $x$ belongs to $E_i$ for *every* $i \in I$, meaning $x \in \bigcap_{i \in I} E_i$ and thus $y = f(x) \in f\left(\bigcap_{i \in I} E_i\right)$, establishing equality.
     Conversely, suppose $f(E_1 \cap E_2) = f(E_1) \cap f(E_2)$ holds for all pairs of subsets $E_1, E_2 \subseteq A$. To show $f$ is injective, take any $a_1, a_2 \in A$ with $f(a_1) = f(a_2) = y$, and test the singleton sets $E_1 = \{a_1\}$ and $E_2 = \{a_2\}$. Then $f(E_1) \cap f(E_2) = \{y\} \cap \{y\} = \{y\} \neq \emptyset$. By our hypothesis, $f(\{a_1\} \cap \{a_2\}) = \{y\} \neq \emptyset$, which forces $\{a_1\} \cap \{a_2\} \neq \emptyset$ and hence $a_1 = a_2$. Thus $f$ is injective. $\blacksquare$

## Equivalence Relations and Quotients

Why do we care so much about binary relations when building the number systems of Real Analysis? Let's pause and think about how you already work with fractions in everyday arithmetic.

Consider the expressions $\frac{1}{2}$, $\frac{2}{4}$, $\frac{-3}{-6}$, and $\frac{50}{100}$. If we look at the raw ingredients used to write them down, they are four *different* ordered pairs of integers: $(1, 2)$, $(2, 4)$, $(-3, -6)$, and $(50, 100)$. Yet in mathematics, we treat $\frac{1}{2}$ and $\frac{2}{4}$ as the **exact same rational number**! How do we turn "different pairs of integers that represent the same ratio" into a *single* mathematical object?

Set theory gives us an extraordinarily powerful way to do this: instead of picking one "favorite" pair to represent the fraction, we **bundle all pairs that have the same ratio together into a single set**—namely, the set $\{(1, 2), (2, 4), (-1, -2), (3, 6), \dots\}$—and we *define* the rational number $\frac{1}{2}$ to be that entire bundle!

You have already seen this exact bundling idea in two of your prerequisite courses:
- **In Linear Algebra:** Given an $m \times n$ matrix $A$ (or a linear transformation $T: V \to W$), when does the inhomogeneous linear system $T(x) = b$ have the same output for two vectors $x_1, x_2 \in V$? Precisely when $T(x_1) = T(x_2)$, which is equivalent to saying $x_1 - x_2 \in \ker(T)$. All vectors $x$ that map to the same right-hand side $b$ form a single parallel translate (or *coset* / *fiber*) $x_0 + \ker(T)$ of the nullspace!
- **In Probability:** When applying the Law of Total Probability $\mathbb{P}(B) = \sum_{k} \mathbb{P}(B \cap E_k)$, you slice the sample space $\Omega$ into a **partition** of mutually exclusive, collectively exhaustive events $E_1, E_2, \dots$ so that every outcome $\omega \in \Omega$ belongs to one and only one bucket $E_k$.

The mathematical machinery for "gluing together different representations of the same underlying concept into disjoint buckets" is called an **equivalence relation**.

> [!abstract] Definition: Equivalence Relation, Equivalence Class, and Quotient Set
> Let $X$ be a set. A binary relation $\sim$ on $X$ is called an **equivalence relation** if it satisfies the following three axioms for all $x, y, z \in X$:
> 1. **Reflexivity:** $x \sim x$ (every element is equivalent to itself).
> 2. **Symmetry:** If $x \sim y$, then $y \sim x$ (equivalence is a two-way street).
> 3. **Transitivity:** If $x \sim y$ and $y \sim z$, then $x \sim z$ (equivalence chains together).
>
> Given an equivalence relation $\sim$ on $X$ and an element $x \in X$, the **equivalence class** of $x$, denoted $[x]$ (or $[x]_\sim$), is the subset of $X$ consisting of all elements equivalent to $x$:
> $$
> [x] := \{y \in X : y \sim x\}
> $$
> Any element $y \in [x]$ is called a **representative** of the equivalence class $[x]$.
>
> Finally, the collection of all equivalence classes of $X$ under $\sim$ is called the **quotient set** of $X$ by $\sim$, denoted $X / {\sim}$ (read "$X$ modulo $\sim$" or "$X$ mod $\sim$"):
> $$
> X / {\sim} := \{[x] : x \in X\} \subseteq \mathcal{P}(X)
> $$
> The natural map $\pi: X \to X / {\sim}$ defined by $\pi(x) := [x]$, which sends each element of $X$ to the equivalence class containing it, is called the **canonical projection** (or **quotient map**).

Let's pause to appreciate the hierarchy of objects here, because keeping the "type levels" straight is essential:
- $X$ is the original set of raw objects, and $x \in X$ is a single element of $X$.
- $[x]$ is a **subset of $X$** ($[x] \subseteq X$, so $[x] \in \mathcal{P}(X)$) formed by gathering everyone in $X$ equivalent to $x$.
- $X / {\sim}$ is a **set of subsets of $X$** ($X / {\sim} \subseteq \mathcal{P}(X)$): each *single element* of the quotient set $X / {\sim}$ is an entire equivalence class $[x]$!

Why are the three axioms—reflexivity, symmetry, and transitivity—chosen the way they are? Because those three rules are the *exact* logical properties shared by any notion of "having the same feature" (i.e., $x \sim y \iff f(x) = f(y)$ for some feature map $f$). Geometrically, those three axioms force the equivalence classes $[x]$ to carve the set $X$ into non-overlapping buckets that cover all of $X$.

> [!theorem] Fundamental Theorem of Equivalence Relations and Partitions
> Let $\sim$ be an equivalence relation on a set $X$. Then for any $x, y \in X$:
> 1. **Non-Emptiness & Coverage:** $x \in [x]$. Consequently, every equivalence class is non-empty, and the union of all equivalence classes is the entire set $X$:
>    $$
>    \bigcup_{[x] \in X / {\sim}} [x] = X
>    $$
> 2. **Same Class Criterion:** $[x] = [y]$ if and only if $x \sim y$. (In other words, any element $y \in [x]$ can serve as the representative name for the entire class $[x]$!)
> 3. **Dichotomy (Identical or Disjoint):** Two equivalence classes are either completely identical or completely disjoint:
>    $$
>    \text{Either } [x] = [y] \quad \text{or} \quad [x] \cap [y] = \emptyset
>    $$

**Proof:**
1. By **reflexivity** of $\sim$, we have $x \sim x$ for every $x \in X$. By the definition of $[x] = \{z \in X : z \sim x\}$, this means $x \in [x]$. Since every $[x]$ is a subset of $X$ and every $x \in X$ belongs to its own class $[x]$, the union of all equivalence classes equals $X$.
2. We prove both directions of $[x] = [y] \iff x \sim y$:
   - $(\implies)$ Suppose $[x] = [y]$. By part (1), we know $x \in [x]$. Since $[x] = [y]$, it follows that $x \in [y]$. By the definition of $[y]$, $x \in [y]$ means $x \sim y$.
   - $(\Longleftarrow)$ Conversely, suppose $x \sim y$. We show $[x] = [y]$ by mutual inclusion. First, take any $z \in [x]$; then $z \sim x$. Since $z \sim x$ and $x \sim y$, **transitivity** implies $z \sim y$, which means $z \in [y]$. Thus $[x] \subseteq [y]$. Next, since $x \sim y$, **symmetry** gives $y \sim x$; repeating the exact same transitivity argument with the roles of $x$ and $y$ swapped shows $[y] \subseteq [x]$. By mutual inclusion, $[x] = [y]$.
3. To prove that $[x]$ and $[y]$ are either identical or disjoint, suppose that they are *not* disjoint, i.e., $[x] \cap [y] \neq \emptyset$. Then we can pick at least one common element $z \in [x] \cap [y]$ lying in the overlap. Because $z \in [x]$ and $z \in [y]$, we have:
   $$
   z \sim x \qquad \text{and} \qquad z \sim y
   $$
   Applying **symmetry** to $z \sim x$ gives $x \sim z$. Chaining $x \sim z$ and $z \sim y$ together via **transitivity** yields $x \sim y$. Finally, by part (2), $x \sim y$ immediately forces $[x] = [y]$! Thus, if two equivalence classes overlap in even a *single* element, they are forced to be the exact same set. $\blacksquare$

> [!note] Theoretical Remark: The Exact Duality Between Equivalence Relations and Partitions
> A collection $\mathcal{P} = \{P_i\}_{i \in I}$ of subsets of $X$ is called a **partition** of $X$ if:
> 1. Every block is non-empty: $P_i \neq \emptyset$ for all $i \in I$.
> 2. The blocks cover $X$: $\bigcup_{i \in I} P_i = X$.
> 3. Distinct blocks are disjoint: if $P_i \neq P_j$, then $P_i \cap P_j = \emptyset$.
>
> The Fundamental Theorem above shows that for any equivalence relation $\sim$ on $X$, the quotient set $X / {\sim}$ is a partition of $X$! Conversely, if you start with *any* partition $\mathcal{P}$ of $X$ (such as a partition of a sample space $\Omega$ in Probability), you can define a relation $\sim_{\mathcal{P}}$ on $X$ by declaring "$x \sim_{\mathcal{P}} y$ if and only if $x$ and $y$ belong to the same block $P_i \in \mathcal{P}$." It is immediate that $\sim_{\mathcal{P}}$ is reflexive (every $x$ is in the same block as itself), symmetric, and transitive (since blocks are disjoint, if $x, y \in P_i$ and $y, z \in P_j$, then $y \in P_i \cap P_j$ forces $P_i = P_j$, so $x, z \in P_i$), and its quotient set $X / {\sim_{\mathcal{P}}}$ recovers the exact partition $\mathcal{P}$! Thus, **equivalence relations on $X$ and partitions of $X$ are two ways of looking at the exact same mathematical structure.**

Let's make equivalence classes and quotient sets completely concrete with an example that bridges modular arithmetic and Linear Algebra.

> [!example] Example: Equivalence Classes in Modular Arithmetic and Linear Algebra Fibers
> 1. On the set of integers $X = \mathbb{Z}$, define the relation $x \sim y \iff x - y$ is divisible by $3$ (i.e., $x - y = 3k$ for some $k \in \mathbb{Z}$, often written $x \equiv y \pmod 3$). Verify that $\sim$ is an equivalence relation and explicitly describe all elements of the quotient set $\mathbb{Z} / {\sim}$.
> 2. Let $T: \mathbb{R}^3 \to \mathbb{R}^2$ be a linear transformation. Define a relation on $\mathbb{R}^3$ by $u \sim v \iff T(u) = T(v)$. Verify that $\sim$ is an equivalence relation on $\mathbb{R}^3$, and describe the equivalence class $[u]$ geometrically in terms of the nullspace $\ker(T) = \{w \in \mathbb{R}^3 : T(w) = 0\}$.

**Solution:**
1. **Modular arithmetic ($\mathbb{Z}$ modulo $3$):**
   - *Reflexivity:* For any $x \in \mathbb{Z}$, $x - x = 0 = 3(0)$, so $x \sim x$.
   - *Symmetry:* If $x \sim y$, then $x - y = 3k$ for some $k \in \mathbb{Z}$, so $y - x = 3(-k)$ with $-k \in \mathbb{Z}$, giving $y \sim x$.
   - *Transitivity:* If $x \sim y$ and $y \sim z$, then $x - y = 3k$ and $y - z = 3m$ for some $k, m \in \mathbb{Z}$. Adding the two equations gives $x - z = (x - y) + (y - z) = 3(k + m)$, so $x \sim z$.
   What are the equivalence classes? Every integer leaves a remainder of $0$, $1$, or $2$ when divided by $3$, so $\mathbb{Z}$ is partitioned into exactly three disjoint equivalence classes based on that remainder:
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   [0] &= \{\dots, -6, -3, 0, 3, 6, \dots\} = [-3] = [3] = [6] \\
   [1] &= \{\dots, -5, -2, 1, 4, 7, \dots\} = [-2] = [4] = [7] \\
   [2] &= \{\dots, -4, -1, 2, 5, 8, \dots\} = [-1] = [5] = [8]
   \end{aligned}
   $$
   Notice Part 2 of the Fundamental Theorem in action: $[1]$, $[4]$, and $[-2]$ are not three different sets—they are three different *nametags* (using different representatives $1, 4, -2$) for the **exact same infinite set**! Thus the quotient set $\mathbb{Z} / {\sim}$ is a finite set containing just **three elements**:
   $$
   \mathbb{Z} / {\sim} = \{[0], [1], [2]\}
   $$
2. **Linear Algebra fibers:** Since equality $=$ on $\mathbb{R}^2$ is reflexive ($T(u) = T(u)$), symmetric ($T(u) = T(v) \implies T(v) = T(u)$), and transitive ($T(u) = T(v)$ and $T(v) = T(w) \implies T(u) = T(w)$), $\sim$ is automatically an equivalence relation on $\mathbb{R}^3$. By linearity of $T$,
   $$
   v \in [u] \iff T(v) = T(u) \iff T(v - u) = 0 \iff v - u \in \ker(T) \iff v \in u + \ker(T)
   $$
   Thus the equivalence class $[u] = u + \ker(T) = \{u + w : w \in \ker(T)\}$ is the **solution fiber** (or affine translate of the nullspace $\ker(T)$ passing through $u$) of the linear system $T(x) = T(u)$! If $\ker(T)$ is a line through the origin in $\mathbb{R}^3$, then the quotient set $\mathbb{R}^3 / {\sim}$ is the set of all lines in $\mathbb{R}^3$ parallel to $\ker(T)$. $\blacksquare$

### The "Well-Definedness" Requirement on Quotient Sets

There is one last—and critically important—concept we must master before leaving Section 0.1.

Once we form a quotient set $X / {\sim}$, we almost always want to define functions or algebraic operations (like addition $\oplus$, multiplication $\odot$, or order $<$) on the equivalence classes $[x] \in X / {\sim}$. How do you compute with an equivalence class $[x]$ when $[x]$ is an entire infinite bucket of elements? Naturally, you reach into the bucket $[x]$, pull out a single **representative** $x$, perform a calculation using $x$, and use the result!

For instance, in $\mathbb{Z} / {\sim} = \{[0], [1], [2]\}$ from the example above, we might want to define addition of two equivalence classes by adding their representatives:
$$
[x] \oplus [y] := [x + y]
$$
Wait—stop and think about what could go catastrophically wrong here! Remember that a single equivalence class has *many different representatives*: in modulo $3$ arithmetic, $[1]$ is the exact same object as $[4]$, and $[2]$ is the exact same object as $[-1]$.
- If Alice computes $[1] \oplus [2]$ using the representatives $1$ and $2$, she gets $[1 + 2] = [3] = [0]$.
- What if Bob computes the *exact same sum* $[1] \oplus [2]$, except he writes $[1]$ as $[4]$ and $[2]$ as $[-1]$? Using our formula on his representatives $4$ and $-1$, Bob gets $[4 + (-1)] = [3] = [0]$. Here Alice and Bob got the same answer.
- Now suppose instead someone tried to define a function $g: \mathbb{Z} / {\sim} \to \mathbb{Z}$ by the formula $g([x]) := x^2$. If Alice plugs in $[1]$, she gets $g([1]) = 1^2 = 1$. If Bob plugs in the *exact same input set* $[1] = [4]$, the formula tells him $g([4]) = 4^2 = 16 \neq 1$! That means $g([x]) = x^2$ is **not a valid function on $\mathbb{Z} / {\sim}$ at all**, because a single input set $[1] = [4]$ tries to produce multiple different outputs depending on which nametag you used to write it down!

> [!warning] Caution: The "Well-Definedness" Requirement on Quotient Sets
> Whenever we propose a function $F: X / {\sim} \to Y$ via a formula $F([x]) := f(x)$ that uses a representative $x \in [x]$, or whenever we propose a binary operation $\oplus$ on $X / {\sim}$ via a formula $[x] \oplus [y] := [f(x, y)]$ that uses representatives $x$ and $y$, we must **prove that the output does not depend on the choice of representatives**!
>
> When the output is independent of the choice of representatives, we say that the function or operation is **well-defined** (or *unambiguous*). Specifically:
> 1. For a function $F([x]) := f(x)$ into a set $Y$ to be **well-defined**, we must prove:
>    $$
>    \forall x, x' \in X, \quad [x] = [x'] \text{ (i.e., } x \sim x'\text{)} \implies f(x) = f(x')
>    $$
> 2. For a binary operation $[x] \oplus [y] := [f(x, y)]$ taking values in $X / {\sim}$ to be **well-defined**, we must prove:
>    $$
>    \forall x, x', y, y' \in X, \quad x \sim x' \text{ and } y \sim y' \implies f(x, y) \sim f(x', y')
>    $$
>    (which guarantees $[f(x, y)] = [f(x', y')]$ by the Fundamental Theorem of Equivalence Relations).
>
> Keep your eyes locked on this requirement: verifying **well-definedness** will be the single most important proof step we perform when constructing integer arithmetic in Section 0.3 and rational arithmetic in Section 0.4!

## 0.1 Exercises

### Problem 0.1.1: Kuratowski's Ordered Pair Uniqueness Theorem

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Using only the Axiom of Extensionality and the definition of the Kuratowski ordered pair $(a, b) := \{\{a\}, \{a, b\}\}$, prove that for any objects $a, b, c, d$,
> $$
> \{\{a\}, \{a, b\}\} = \{\{c\}, \{c, d\}\} \iff a = c \text{ and } b = d
> $$
> Be sure to explicitly account for the degenerate case in which $a = b$ (so that $\{\{a\}, \{a, b\}\}$ collapses to the singleton $\{\{a\}\}$).

**Solution:**
We must prove both directions of the equivalence $\{\{a\}, \{a, b\}\} = \{\{c\}, \{c, d\}\} \iff (a = c \text{ and } b = d)$.

**Direction 1 ($\Longleftarrow$):** Suppose $a = c$ and $b = d$. Then by direct substitution of equal objects, $\{a\} = \{c\}$ and $\{a, b\} = \{c, d\}$, so $\{\{a\}, \{a, b\}\} = \{\{c\}, \{c, d\}\}$ immediately.

**Direction 2 ($\implies$):** Suppose that $\{\{a\}, \{a, b\}\} = \{\{c\}, \{c, d\}\}$. Before splitting into cases, let's establish a quick preliminary fact about singletons and two-element sets using the Axiom of Extensionality:
- For any $x, y$, if $\{x\} = \{y\}$, then since $x \in \{x\}$, extensionality forces $x \in \{y\}$, so $x = y$.
- More generally, if $\{x, y\} = \{z\}$, then both $x \in \{z\}$ and $y \in \{z\}$, which forces $x = z$ and $y = z$ (so $x = y = z$).

Now we split our analysis into two exhaustive cases based on whether $a = b$ or $a \neq b$:

1. **Case 1: The degenerate case $a = b$.**
   If $a = b$, then by extensionality $\{a, b\} = \{a, a\} = \{a\}$. Consequently, the left-hand side collapses to a singleton set:
   $$
   \{\{a\}, \{a, b\}\} = \{\{a\}, \{a\}\} = \{\{a\}\}
   $$
   Our hypothesis $\{\{a\}, \{a, b\}\} = \{\{c\}, \{c, d\}\}$ now reads:
   $$
   \{\{a\}\} = \{\{c\}, \{c, d\}\}
   $$
   By the Axiom of Extensionality applied to this equality of sets, both elements $\{c\}$ and $\{c, d\}$ of the right-hand side must belong to the singleton $\{\{a\}\}$, which forces:
   $$
   \{c\} = \{a\} \qquad \text{and} \qquad \{c, d\} = \{a\}
   $$
   From $\{c\} = \{a\}$ we get $c = a$. From $\{c, d\} = \{a\}$, every element of $\{c, d\}$ must equal $a$, so $d = a$ as well. Combining $a = b$, $c = a$, and $d = a$ yields $a = c$ and $b = d$.

2. **Case 2: The non-degenerate case $a \neq b$.**
   Suppose $a \neq b$. First, note that $c \neq d$ must also hold: for if $c = d$, then by the exact same reasoning as in Case 1 (with the roles of $(a, b)$ and $(c, d)$ swapped), $\{\{c\}, \{c, d\}\} = \{\{c\}\}$ would force $a = c = b$, contradicting $a \neq b$.
   Now, since $a \neq b$, the set $\{a, b\}$ contains two distinct elements, so $\{a, b\} \neq \{c\}$ (because if $\{a, b\} = \{c\}$, then both $a = c$ and $b = c$, contradicting $a \neq b$).
   Look at the element $\{a\} \in \{\{a\}, \{a, b\}\} = \{\{c\}, \{c, d\}\}$. By extensionality, $\{a\}$ must equal one of the elements of $\{\{c\}, \{c, d\}\}$: either $\{a\} = \{c\}$ or $\{a\} = \{c, d\}$.
   - Could $\{a\} = \{c, d\}$? No, because $\{a\} = \{c, d\}$ would force $c = a = d$, contradicting $c \neq d$.
   - Therefore, we must have $\{a\} = \{c\}$, which gives:
     $$
     a = c
     $$
   Next, look at the element $\{a, b\} \in \{\{a\}, \{a, b\}\} = \{\{c\}, \{c, d\}\}$. By extensionality, either $\{a, b\} = \{c\}$ or $\{a, b\} = \{c, d\}$. We already observed that $\{a, b\} \neq \{c\}$ since $a \neq b$. Thus we must have:
   $$
   \{a, b\} = \{c, d\}
   $$
   Finally, since $b \in \{a, b\}$ and $\{a, b\} = \{c, d\}$, extensionality tells us that $b \in \{c, d\}$, meaning either $b = c$ or $b = d$. However, we already proved $a = c$ and we are in Case 2 where $b \neq a$, so $b$ cannot equal $c$ ($b \neq a = c$). This leaves only one possibility:
   $$
   b = d
   $$
In both cases, we have proved that $a = c$ and $b = d$. $\blacksquare$

### Problem 0.1.2: Characterizing Injectivity and Surjectivity via Image–Preimage Compositions

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $f: A \to B$ be any function.
> 1. Prove that for every subset $E \subseteq A$, $E \subseteq f^{-1}(f(E))$. Then prove that $f$ is **injective** if and only if $E = f^{-1}(f(E))$ holds for all subsets $E \subseteq A$.
> 2. Prove that for every subset $H \subseteq B$, $f(f^{-1}(H)) \subseteq H$. Then prove that $f$ is **surjective** if and only if $f(f^{-1}(H)) = H$ holds for all subsets $H \subseteq B$.

**Solution:**
Before diving into the algebra, let's understand the intuition behind both parts:
- In Part 1, $f^{-1}(f(E))$ takes a subset $E \subseteq A$, pushes it forward to its outputs $f(E) \subseteq B$, and then pulls back *all* inputs in $A$ that land in $f(E)$. Clearly every original element $x \in E$ lands in $f(E)$, so $E \subseteq f^{-1}(f(E))$ is automatic. When can extra "impostor" elements $x' \notin E$ sneak into $f^{-1}(f(E))$? Precisely when some $x' \notin E$ collides with an element $x \in E$ at the same output ($f(x') = f(x)$)—which happens if and only if $f$ fails to be injective!
- In Part 2, $f(f^{-1}(H))$ takes a target set $H \subseteq B$, pulls back all inputs in $A$ that land in $H$, and pushes those inputs forward into $B$. Every output produced this way obviously lies in $H$, so $f(f^{-1}(H)) \subseteq H$ is automatic. Which elements of $H$ get left out of $f(f^{-1}(H))$? Precisely those elements of $H$ that were never hit by any input in $A$—which happens if and only if $f$ fails to be surjective!

Now let's write out the rigorous proofs.

**Part 1 (Injectivity and $f^{-1}(f(E)) = E$):**
- **Universal inclusion $E \subseteq f^{-1}(f(E))$:** Let $E \subseteq A$ be any subset, and pick an arbitrary $x \in E$. By definition of the direct image $f(E) = \{f(e) : e \in E\}$, we have $f(x) \in f(E)$. By definition of the preimage of $f(E)$, the fact that $f(x) \in f(E)$ means $x \in f^{-1}(f(E))$. Thus $E \subseteq f^{-1}(f(E))$.
- **($\implies$) Injectivity implies $E = f^{-1}(f(E))$ for all $E \subseteq A$:** Suppose $f$ is injective, and let $E \subseteq A$. Since we already proved $E \subseteq f^{-1}(f(E))$, it remains only to prove the reverse inclusion $f^{-1}(f(E)) \subseteq E$. Take any $x \in f^{-1}(f(E))$. By definition of preimage, $f(x) \in f(E)$. By definition of direct image, there exists some element $e \in E$ such that $f(x) = f(e)$. Because $f$ is injective, $f(x) = f(e)$ implies $x = e$. Since $e \in E$, we conclude $x \in E$. Thus $f^{-1}(f(E)) \subseteq E$, and mutual inclusion gives $E = f^{-1}(f(E))$.
- **($\Longleftarrow$) $E = f^{-1}(f(E))$ for all $E \subseteq A$ implies injectivity:** Suppose that $E = f^{-1}(f(E))$ holds for every subset $E \subseteq A$. To prove $f$ is injective, let $a_1, a_2 \in A$ satisfy $f(a_1) = f(a_2)$. Apply our hypothesis to the singleton subset $E := \{a_1\} \subseteq A$. Since $f(a_2) = f(a_1) \in \{f(a_1)\} = f(\{a_1\})$, the definition of preimage tells us that $a_2 \in f^{-1}(f(\{a_1\}))$. By our hypothesis, $f^{-1}(f(\{a_1\})) = \{a_1\}$, so $a_2 \in \{a_1\}$, which forces $a_2 = a_1$. Thus $f$ is injective.

**Part 2 (Surjectivity and $f(f^{-1}(H)) = H$):**
- **Universal inclusion $f(f^{-1}(H)) \subseteq H$:** Let $H \subseteq B$ be any subset, and take an arbitrary $y \in f(f^{-1}(H))$. By definition of direct image, there exists some $x \in f^{-1}(H)$ such that $y = f(x)$. By definition of preimage, $x \in f^{-1}(H)$ means $f(x) \in H$. Since $y = f(x)$, we have $y \in H$. Thus $f(f^{-1}(H)) \subseteq H$ (and in fact, more precisely, $f(f^{-1}(H)) = H \cap f(A)$).
- **($\implies$) Surjectivity implies $f(f^{-1}(H)) = H$ for all $H \subseteq B$:** Suppose $f$ is surjective, and let $H \subseteq B$. Since $f(f^{-1}(H)) \subseteq H$ always holds, we only need to show $H \subseteq f(f^{-1}(H))$. Pick any $y \in H \subseteq B$. Because $f$ is surjective, there exists at least one $x \in A$ such that $f(x) = y$. Since $f(x) = y \in H$, the definition of preimage gives $x \in f^{-1}(H)$. Pushing $x \in f^{-1}(H)$ forward under $f$ gives $y = f(x) \in f(f^{-1}(H))$. Thus $H \subseteq f(f^{-1}(H))$, so $f(f^{-1}(H)) = H$.
- **($\Longleftarrow$) $f(f^{-1}(H)) = H$ for all $H \subseteq B$ implies surjectivity:** Suppose $f(f^{-1}(H)) = H$ holds for all subsets $H \subseteq B$. Choose the specific subset $H := B$. Since $f^{-1}(B) = \{x \in A : f(x) \in B\} = A$, our hypothesis gives:
  $$
  f(A) = f(f^{-1}(B)) = B
  $$
  Because the range $f(A)$ equals the entire codomain $B$, $f$ is surjective. $\blacksquare$

### Problem 0.1.3: Universal Factorization of Functions Through Quotient Sets

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $\sim$ be an equivalence relation on a set $X$, and let $\pi: X \to X / {\sim}$ be the canonical projection map defined by $\pi(x) := [x]$. Suppose $f: X \to Y$ is a function into another set $Y$.
> 1. Prove that there exists a well-defined function $\tilde{f}: X / {\sim} \to Y$ satisfying $\tilde{f} \circ \pi = f$ (meaning $\tilde{f}([x]) = f(x)$ for all $x \in X$) if and only if $f$ is constant on each equivalence class of $\sim$ (i.e., $x \sim x' \implies f(x) = f(x')$), and show that $\tilde{f}$ is unique when it exists.
> 2. Assuming $\tilde{f}$ exists, state and prove the necessary and sufficient conditions on $f$ and $\sim$ for the induced quotient map $\tilde{f}: X / {\sim} \to Y$ to be injective, and to be surjective.

**Solution:**
This theorem—often called the **Universal Property of Quotients**—is the blueprint for how functions descend from a set $X$ to its quotient set $X / {\sim}$ (and is the set-theoretic foundation of the First Isomorphism Theorem in Linear Algebra and Abstract Algebra).

**Part 1 (Existence and Uniqueness of $\tilde{f}$):**
- **($\implies$) Necessity:** Suppose there exists a function $\tilde{f}: X / {\sim} \to Y$ satisfying $\tilde{f} \circ \pi = f$, i.e., $\tilde{f}([x]) = f(x)$ for all $x \in X$. Take any $x, x' \in X$ such that $x \sim x'$. By the Fundamental Theorem of Equivalence Relations, $x \sim x'$ implies that $[x] = [x']$ are the exact same element of $X / {\sim}$. Since $\tilde{f}$ is a function on $X / {\sim}$, applying $\tilde{f}$ to this single element yields:
  $$
  f(x) = \tilde{f}([x]) = \tilde{f}([x']) = f(x')
  $$
  Thus $f$ is constant on each equivalence class of $\sim$.
- **($\Longleftarrow$) Sufficiency and Uniqueness:** Conversely, suppose $f$ satisfies $x \sim x' \implies f(x) = f(x')$ for all $x, x' \in X$. For any equivalence class $C \in X / {\sim}$, we know $C = [x]$ for some representative $x \in X$. The condition $\tilde{f}(\pi(x)) = f(x)$ leaves us **no choice** for how to define $\tilde{f}$: we *must* set
  $$
  \tilde{f}([x]) := f(x)
  $$
  This immediately proves **uniqueness**: any map $\tilde{f}$ satisfying $\tilde{f} \circ \pi = f$ must be given by $\tilde{f}([x]) = f(x)$ on every $[x] \in X / {\sim}$.
  To prove **existence**, we just need to verify that this formula defines a **well-defined** function $\tilde{f}: X / {\sim} \to Y$ (independent of the choice of representative $x$). Suppose $[x] = [x']$ in $X / {\sim}$. By the Fundamental Theorem of Equivalence Relations, $[x] = [x']$ implies $x \sim x'$. By our hypothesis on $f$, $x \sim x'$ implies $f(x) = f(x')$. Therefore, $\tilde{f}([x])$ assigns a single, unambiguous output in $Y$ to every equivalence class in $X / {\sim}$, and by construction $(\tilde{f} \circ \pi)(x) = \tilde{f}([x]) = f(x)$ for all $x \in X$.

**Part 2 (Injectivity and Surjectivity of $\tilde{f}$):**
Assume now that $x \sim x' \implies f(x) = f(x')$, so the induced map $\tilde{f}: X / {\sim} \to Y$ given by $\tilde{f}([x]) = f(x)$ exists.
1. **Criterion for Injectivity of $\tilde{f}$:** We claim that $\tilde{f}: X / {\sim} \to Y$ is **injective if and only if** for all $x, x' \in X$,
   $$
   f(x) = f(x') \implies x \sim x'
   $$
   (Combine this with our standing assumption $x \sim x' \implies f(x) = f(x')$: $\tilde{f}$ is injective if and only if $x \sim x' \iff f(x) = f(x')$, meaning the equivalence classes of $\sim$ are *precisely* the level sets / fibers $f^{-1}(\{y\})$ of $f$!)
   - *Proof:* By definition of injectivity on $X / {\sim}$, $\tilde{f}$ is injective if and only if for all $[x], [x'] \in X / {\sim}$,
     $$
     \tilde{f}([x]) = \tilde{f}([x']) \implies [x] = [x']
     $$
     By definition of $\tilde{f}$, the left-hand side $\tilde{f}([x]) = \tilde{f}([x'])$ is equivalent to $f(x) = f(x')$. By the Fundamental Theorem of Equivalence Relations, the right-hand side $[x] = [x']$ is equivalent to $x \sim x'$. Thus $\tilde{f}$ is injective if and only if $f(x) = f(x') \implies x \sim x'$ for all $x, x' \in X$.
2. **Criterion for Surjectivity of $\tilde{f}$:** We claim that $\tilde{f}: X / {\sim} \to Y$ is **surjective if and only if the original function $f: X \to Y$ is surjective**.
   - *Proof:* Notice that the range of $\tilde{f}$ and the range of $f$ are identical:
     $$
     \tilde{f}(X / {\sim}) = \{\tilde{f}([x]) : [x] \in X / {\sim}\} = \{f(x) : x \in X\} = f(X)
     $$
     Therefore, $\tilde{f}(X / {\sim}) = Y$ holds if and only if $f(X) = Y$, i.e., $\tilde{f}$ is surjective if and only if $f$ is surjective. $\blacksquare$

### Problem 0.1.4: Saturation of Subsets and Exactness of Direct Images on Intersections

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $f: A \to B$ be a function. In **Problem 0.1.2**, we saw that for any subset $E \subseteq A$, we always have $E \subseteq f^{-1}(f(E))$, and that equality holds for *all* $E \subseteq A$ if and only if $f$ is injective. Even when $f$ is not injective, however, some specific subsets $E \subseteq A$ still satisfy $E = f^{-1}(f(E))$.
>
> We define the **kernel equivalence relation** $\sim_f$ on $A$ by declaring $x \sim_f x' \iff f(x) = f(x')$. A subset $E \subseteq A$ is called **$f$-saturated** (or **saturated with respect to $\sim_f$**) if whenever $x \in E$ and $x' \in A$ satisfies $x' \sim_f x$, we also have $x' \in E$ (in other words, $E$ is a union of complete equivalence classes of $\sim_f$).
> 1. Prove that for any subset $E \subseteq A$, the set $f^{-1}(f(E))$ is equal to the **saturation** of $E$ under $\sim_f$:
>    $$
>    f^{-1}(f(E)) = \{x \in A : \exists e \in E \text{ such that } x \sim_f e\} = \bigcup_{e \in E} [e]_{\sim_f}
>    $$
> 2. Using Part 1, prove that the following three conditions on a subset $E \subseteq A$ are logically equivalent:
>    - (a) $E$ is $f$-saturated.
>    - (b) $E = f^{-1}(f(E))$.
>    - (c) $E = f^{-1}(H)$ for some subset $H \subseteq B$.
> 3. Suppose $E_1, E_2 \subseteq A$ are two subsets of $A$, and suppose that **at least one** of $E_1$ or $E_2$ is $f$-saturated. Prove that direct image preserves their intersection without requiring $f$ to be injective:
>    $$
>    f(E_1 \cap E_2) = f(E_1) \cap f(E_2)
>    $$
>    Conclude in particular that for any $E \subseteq A$ and any $H \subseteq B$, the **projection formula** $f(E \cap f^{-1}(H)) = f(E) \cap H$ holds.

**Solution:**
Let's first appreciate the geometric picture: the relation $x \sim_f x' \iff f(x) = f(x')$ partitions the domain $A$ into equivalence classes $[x]_{\sim_f} = f^{-1}(\{f(x)\})$ (the level sets or fibers of $f$). A subset $E \subseteq A$ is $f$-saturated if it never cuts an equivalence class in half: for each fiber of $f$, $E$ either contains the *entire* fiber or misses it completely!

**Part 1 (Formula for $f^{-1}(f(E))$):**
Let $x \in A$ be arbitrary. Unpacking the definitions of preimage, direct image, and $\sim_f$ step by step gives:
$$
\begin{aligned}
x \in f^{-1}(f(E)) &\iff f(x) \in f(E) \\
                   &\iff \exists e \in E \text{ such that } f(x) = f(e) \\
                   &\iff \exists e \in E \text{ such that } x \sim_f e \\
                   &\iff \exists e \in E \text{ such that } x \in [e]_{\sim_f} \\
                   &\iff x \in \bigcup_{e \in E} [e]_{\sim_f}
\end{aligned}
$$
Thus $f^{-1}(f(E)) = \{x \in A : \exists e \in E \text{ such that } x \sim_f e\} = \bigcup_{e \in E} [e]_{\sim_f}$.

**Part 2 (Equivalence of (a), (b), and (c)):**
We prove the cycle of implications $\text{(a)} \implies \text{(b)} \implies \text{(c)} \implies \text{(a)}$:
- **$\text{(a)} \implies \text{(b)}$:** Suppose $E$ is $f$-saturated. By **Problem 0.1.2**, we always have $E \subseteq f^{-1}(f(E))$. For the reverse inclusion, take any $x \in f^{-1}(f(E))$. By Part 1, there exists some $e \in E$ such that $x \sim_f e$. Because $E$ is $f$-saturated and $e \in E$ with $x \sim_f e$, the definition of saturation forces $x \in E$. Thus $f^{-1}(f(E)) \subseteq E$, giving $E = f^{-1}(f(E))$.
- **$\text{(b)} \implies \text{(c)}$:** Suppose $E = f^{-1}(f(E))$. Setting $H := f(E) \subseteq B$, we immediately have $E = f^{-1}(H)$.
- **$\text{(c)} \implies \text{(a)}$:** Suppose $E = f^{-1}(H)$ for some subset $H \subseteq B$. To prove $E$ is $f$-saturated, suppose $e \in E$ and $x \in A$ satisfies $x \sim_f e$. Because $e \in E = f^{-1}(H)$, we have $f(e) \in H$. Because $x \sim_f e$, we have $f(x) = f(e)$. Combining these gives $f(x) = f(e) \in H$, which by definition of preimage means $x \in f^{-1}(H) = E$. Thus $E$ is $f$-saturated.

**Part 3 (Intersection with an $f$-Saturated Set and the Projection Formula):**
Suppose without loss of generality that $E_2$ is $f$-saturated (if $E_1$ is $f$-saturated, simply swap the labels of $E_1$ and $E_2$).
- We already proved in the main text that $f(E_1 \cap E_2) \subseteq f(E_1) \cap f(E_2)$ holds for all subsets $E_1, E_2 \subseteq A$.
- To prove the reverse inclusion $f(E_1) \cap f(E_2) \subseteq f(E_1 \cap E_2)$, let $y \in f(E_1) \cap f(E_2)$.
  Because $y \in f(E_1)$ and $y \in f(E_2)$, there exist elements $x_1 \in E_1$ and $x_2 \in E_2$ such that:
  $$
  f(x_1) = y \qquad \text{and} \qquad f(x_2) = y
  $$
  Notice that $f(x_1) = y = f(x_2)$, which means $x_1 \sim_f x_2$! Now use the fact that $E_2$ is $f$-saturated: since $x_2 \in E_2$ and $x_1 \sim_f x_2$, saturation of $E_2$ forces $x_1$ to belong to $E_2$ as well ($x_1 \in E_2$)!
  Consequently, the single element $x_1$ lies in *both* $E_1$ and $E_2$, so $x_1 \in E_1 \cap E_2$. Pushing $x_1$ forward under $f$ gives $y = f(x_1) \in f(E_1 \cap E_2)$. By mutual inclusion, $f(E_1 \cap E_2) = f(E_1) \cap f(E_2)$.

Finally, let $E \subseteq A$ and $H \subseteq B$ be arbitrary. By Part 2(c), the preimage $E_2 := f^{-1}(H)$ is automatically $f$-saturated! Applying our result with $E_1 = E$ and $E_2 = f^{-1}(H)$ gives $f(E \cap f^{-1}(H)) = f(E) \cap f(f^{-1}(H))$. Moreover, for any $y \in f(E) \cap H$, we have $y = f(x)$ for some $x \in E$; since $f(x) = y \in H$, we also have $x \in f^{-1}(H)$, so $y = f(x) \in f(f^{-1}(H))$. Thus $f(E) \cap f(f^{-1}(H)) = f(E) \cap H$, yielding the projection formula:
$$
f(E \cap f^{-1}(H)) = f(E) \cap H
$$
$\blacksquare$

### Problem 0.1.5: Monotone Maps on Power Sets and Tarski's Fixed-Point Lemma

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $X$ be a set, and let $\Phi: \mathcal{P}(X) \to \mathcal{P}(X)$ be a function from the power set of $X$ to itself. We say that $\Phi$ is **inclusion-preserving** (or **monotone**) if for all subsets $A, B \subseteq X$,
> $$
> A \subseteq B \implies \Phi(A) \subseteq \Phi(B)
> $$
> Define the family $\mathcal{F} \subseteq \mathcal{P}(X)$ of "pre-fixed" subsets of $X$ by
> $$
> \mathcal{F} := \{A \subseteq X : \Phi(A) \subseteq A\}
> $$
> and define $E^* \subseteq X$ to be the intersection of all sets in $\mathcal{F}$:
> $$
> E^* := \bigcap_{A \in \mathcal{F}} A
> $$
> 1. Explain why the index family $\mathcal{F}$ is guaranteed to be non-empty, and prove that for every $A \in \mathcal{F}$, we have $\Phi(E^*) \subseteq A$.
> 2. Deduce from Part 1 that $\Phi(E^*) \subseteq E^*$, and then apply $\Phi$ to both sides of this inclusion to prove that $\Phi(E^*) = E^*$ (so $E^*$ is a **fixed point** of $\Phi$, and in fact the smallest fixed point of $\Phi$ under set inclusion).
> 3. **Application (Preview of Cantor–Schröder–Bernstein):** Suppose $f: X \to Y$ and $g: Y \to X$ are two functions. Verify that the map $\Phi: \mathcal{P}(X) \to \mathcal{P}(X)$ defined by
>    $$
>    \Phi(A) := X \setminus g(Y \setminus f(A))
>    $$
>    is inclusion-preserving, and conclude by Parts 1–2 that there exists a subset $E^* \subseteq X$ satisfying $X \setminus E^* = g(Y \setminus f(E^*))$.

**Solution:**
This gorgeous result—a set-theoretic case of the **Knaster–Tarski Fixed-Point Theorem**—shows how arbitrary indexed intersections on $\mathcal{P}(X)$ can construct a self-referential "fixed point" set $\Phi(E^*) = E^*$ in just a few lines without any iterative limits! In Section 0.7, this exact lemma will give us our cleanest proof of the celebrated Cantor–Schröder–Bernstein Theorem on infinite cardinalities.

**Part 1 (Non-emptiness of $\mathcal{F}$ and $\Phi(E^*) \subseteq A$):**
- **Why $\mathcal{F} \neq \emptyset$:** Consider the entire set $X \in \mathcal{P}(X)$. Since $\Phi(X)$ is a subset of $X$, we automatically have $\Phi(X) \subseteq X$, which means $X \in \mathcal{F}$. Thus $\mathcal{F}$ is non-empty, so the intersection $E^* = \bigcap_{A \in \mathcal{F}} A$ is a well-defined subset of $X$.
- **Proof that $\Phi(E^*) \subseteq A$ for all $A \in \mathcal{F}$:** Let $A \in \mathcal{F}$ be any member of the family $\mathcal{F}$. Because $E^* = \bigcap_{B \in \mathcal{F}} B$ is the intersection of *all* sets in $\mathcal{F}$, and $A$ is one of the sets being intersected, every element of $E^*$ belongs to $A$:
  $$
  E^* \subseteq A
  $$
  Now apply the hypothesis that $\Phi$ is inclusion-preserving to the inclusion $E^* \subseteq A$:
  $$
  \Phi(E^*) \subseteq \Phi(A)
  $$
  Finally, because $A \in \mathcal{F}$, the defining property of $\mathcal{F}$ tells us that $\Phi(A) \subseteq A$. Chaining these two inclusions together gives:
  $$
  \Phi(E^*) \subseteq \Phi(A) \subseteq A
  $$
  Thus $\Phi(E^*) \subseteq A$ holds for every $A \in \mathcal{F}$.

**Part 2 (Proof that $\Phi(E^*) = E^*$ is the least fixed point):**
We prove $\Phi(E^*) = E^*$ by mutual inclusion:
- **Inclusion $\Phi(E^*) \subseteq E^*$:** In Part 1, we showed that for every $A \in \mathcal{F}$, every element $x \in \Phi(E^*)$ belongs to $A$. By the quantifier definition of arbitrary intersection ($\forall A \in \mathcal{F}, \; x \in A \iff x \in \bigcap_{A \in \mathcal{F}} A$), this means every element of $\Phi(E^*)$ belongs to $\bigcap_{A \in \mathcal{F}} A = E^*$. Therefore:
  $$
  \Phi(E^*) \subseteq E^*
  $$
- **Reverse inclusion $E^* \subseteq \Phi(E^*)$:** Here is Tarski's brilliant trick! Because we just proved $\Phi(E^*) \subseteq E^*$, apply the inclusion-preserving map $\Phi$ to both sides of $\Phi(E^*) \subseteq E^*$:
  $$
  \Phi(\Phi(E^*)) \subseteq \Phi(E^*)
  $$
  Look closely at what $\Phi(\Phi(E^*)) \subseteq \Phi(E^*)$ says: it says that the set $C := \Phi(E^*)$ satisfies $\Phi(C) \subseteq C$, which means **$C = \Phi(E^*)$ is itself a member of the family $\mathcal{F}$**!
  Since $E^* = \bigcap_{A \in \mathcal{F}} A$ is contained in every member of $\mathcal{F}$, and $\Phi(E^*) \in \mathcal{F}$, it immediately follows that:
  $$
  E^* \subseteq \Phi(E^*)
  $$
By mutual inclusion, $\Phi(E^*) = E^*$. Moreover, if $F \subseteq X$ is any other fixed point ($\Phi(F) = F$), then in particular $\Phi(F) \subseteq F$, so $F \in \mathcal{F}$ and therefore $E^* \subseteq F$, proving $E^*$ is the smallest fixed point of $\Phi$.

**Part 3 (Monotonicity of $\Phi(A) = X \setminus g(Y \setminus f(A))$):**
Let $A, B \subseteq X$ satisfy $A \subseteq B$. We trace how each of the four steps in $\Phi$ affects the inclusion direction:
1. **Direct image under $f$ preserves inclusion:** Since $A \subseteq B$, any $y \in f(A)$ has the form $y = f(a)$ for some $a \in A \subseteq B$, so $y \in f(B)$. Thus:
   $$
   f(A) \subseteq f(B)
   $$
2. **Complement in $Y$ reverses inclusion:** Taking complements relative to $Y$ flips the inclusion direction (if $y \notin f(B)$, then since $f(A) \subseteq f(B)$, certainly $y \notin f(A)$):
   $$
   Y \setminus f(B) \subseteq Y \setminus f(A)
   $$
3. **Direct image under $g$ preserves inclusion:** Applying the direct image map $g(\cdot)$ to the inclusion above gives:
   $$
   g(Y \setminus f(B)) \subseteq g(Y \setminus f(A))
   $$
4. **Complement in $X$ reverses inclusion back:** Taking complements relative to $X$ flips the inclusion direction a second time:
   $$
   X \setminus g(Y \setminus f(A)) \subseteq X \setminus g(Y \setminus f(B))
   $$
That is, $\Phi(A) \subseteq \Phi(B)$! Because two complement reversals cancel out, $\Phi$ is inclusion-preserving. By Parts 1 and 2, $\Phi$ has a fixed point $E^* \subseteq X$ satisfying $\Phi(E^*) = E^*$, which means:
$$
X \setminus g(Y \setminus f(E^*)) = E^*
$$
Taking the complement in $X$ of both sides yields $X \setminus E^* = g(Y \setminus f(E^*))$. (Notice why this is so powerful: when $f$ and $g$ are injective, $f$ maps $E^*$ bijectively onto $f(E^*) \subseteq Y$, while $g$ maps the remaining part $Y \setminus f(E^*)$ bijectively onto the remaining part $X \setminus E^*$, allowing us to stitch $f$ and $g^{-1}$ together into a bijection between $X$ and $Y$!) $\blacksquare$

---

# 0.2 The Natural Numbers

In Section 0.1, we assembled the foundational grammar of mathematics: sets, subsets, Cartesian products, functions, and equivalence relations. Now we are ready to build our very first number system from scratch: the **natural numbers** $\mathbb{N} = \{0, 1, 2, 3, \dots\}$.

Why do we bother "constructing" or "axiomatizing" the natural numbers when you have been counting, adding, and multiplying since elementary school, and taking derivatives and integrals throughout Calculus I and II? In Calculus, we took the real number line $\mathbb{R}$—along with all its algebraic and order properties—as a given intuition. In Real Analysis, our goal is to prove *why* calculus works without relying on unexamined geometric pictures. As the 19th-century mathematician Leopold Kronecker famously remarked, *"God made the natural numbers; all else is the work of man."* Once we secure a rock-solid foundation for $\mathbb{N}$, we will construct the integers $\mathbb{Z}$ (Section 0.3), the rationals $\mathbb{Q}$ (Section 0.4), and finally the real numbers $\mathbb{R}$ (Section 0.5) step by step out of $\mathbb{N}$ using the set-theoretic tools of Section 0.1.

> [!note] Convention on Zero
> In foundational set theory, computer science, and modern analysis texts (such as Terence Tao's *Analysis I*), $0$ is included in the natural numbers:
> $$
> \mathbb{N} = \{0, 1, 2, 3, \dots\}
> $$
> When we want to refer specifically to the strictly positive integers $\{1, 2, 3, \dots\}$, we write $\mathbb{N}^+$ or $\mathbb{Z}^+$. Including $0$ from the very beginning makes recursive definitions cleaner, corresponds naturally to the empty set $\emptyset$ (which has $0$ elements), and provides an immediate additive identity ($n + 0 = n$).

How should we bring $\mathbb{N}$ into existence mathematically? Historically, mathematicians developed two complementary perspectives:
1. **The Set-Theoretic Construction (Von Neumann Ordinals):** Build $0, 1, 2, \dots$ explicitly out of the empty set $\emptyset$ so we know with certainty that a set behaving like the natural numbers actually exists within set theory.
2. **The Axiomatic Interface (Peano Axioms):** Specify the five minimal structural rules that govern counting and mathematical induction, and derive all of arithmetic and order from those five rules alone—much like how in Linear Algebra, we define an abstract vector space by its axioms and prove theorems that hold for *any* vector space regardless of how its vectors are represented internally.

The cleanest approach is a hybrid of both: we first show briefly how John von Neumann constructed the natural numbers out of pure sets (so $\mathbb{N}$ is not an ungrounded assumption), and then we state the **Peano Axioms** as our clean working interface for proving all arithmetic and order properties of $\mathbb{N}$.

## Set-Theoretic Realization: Von Neumann Ordinals

Suppose all we have in our mathematical universe is the empty set $\emptyset$ and the ability to form new sets using unions and curly braces $\{\cdot\}$. There are no "digits" or "numbers" written in the sky yet. How can we represent the act of counting using pure sets?

In the 1920s, John von Neumann proposed a wonderfully concrete idea: define each natural number $n$ to be the **set of all previous natural numbers**:
$$
n = \{0, 1, 2, \dots, n - 1\}
$$
Think about why this is so clever:
- For $n = 0$, there are *no* previous natural numbers, so $0$ is simply the empty set $\emptyset$, which literally contains $0$ elements.
- For $n = 1$, the only previous natural number is $0$, so $1$ is the set $\{0\} = \{\emptyset\}$, which literally contains $1$ element.
- For $n = 2$, the previous natural numbers are $0$ and $1$, so $2$ is the set $\{0, 1\} = \{\emptyset, \{\emptyset\}\}$, which literally contains $2$ elements.
- In general, to step from any number $n$ to the **next number**—called the **successor** of $n$ and denoted $S(n)$—we take all the elements already inside the set $n$ (which are $0, 1, \dots, n - 1$) and toss in $n$ itself as one new element: $S(n) = n \cup \{n\}$.

> [!abstract] Definition: Von Neumann Construction of the Naturals
> In set theory, the **Von Neumann ordinals** representing the natural numbers are constructed by defining $0$ to be the empty set, and for any set $n$, defining its **successor** $S(n)$ by adjoining $n$ as a new element to the set $n$ itself:
> $$
> \begin{aligned}
> 0 &:= \emptyset \\
> S(n) &:= n \cup \{n\}
> \end{aligned}
> $$
> Applying the successor operation repeatedly generates the individual natural numbers:
> $$
> \begin{aligned}
> 0 &:= \emptyset \\
> 1 &:= S(0) = 0 \cup \{0\} = \emptyset \cup \{\emptyset\} = \{\emptyset\} = \{0\} \\
> 2 &:= S(1) = 1 \cup \{1\} = \{0\} \cup \{1\} = \{0, 1\} = \{\emptyset, \{\emptyset\}\} \\
> 3 &:= S(2) = 2 \cup \{2\} = \{0, 1\} \cup \{2\} = \{0, 1, 2\} = \{\emptyset, \{\emptyset\}, \{\emptyset, \{\emptyset\}\}\} \\
> &\;\;\vdots \\
> n &:= \{0, 1, 2, \dots, n - 1\}
> \end{aligned}
> $$

Be careful with the difference between $n$ and $\{n\}$ here! The symbol $n$ is a set (whose elements are $0, 1, \dots, n - 1$), whereas $\{n\}$ is a *singleton set* whose single element is the entire set $n$. Thus $n \cup \{n\}$ takes all the old elements of $n$ and adds $n$ itself as one additional element.

> [!example] Example: Exploring Set Operations on Von Neumann Ordinals
> Using the Von Neumann definition of $0, 1, 2, 3$, verify the following statements directly from set membership ($\in$) and subset inclusion ($\subseteq$):
> 1. Show that for every set $n$, $S(n) \neq 0$.
> 2. Verify that for the numbers $0, 1, 2, 3$, both $m \in n$ and $m \subsetneq n$ hold whenever $m$ comes before $n$ in the counting sequence.

**Solution:**
1. By definition, $0 = \emptyset$ has no elements. On the other hand, for any set $n$, the set $S(n) = n \cup \{n\}$ contains $n$ as an element (since $n \in \{n\} \subseteq n \cup \{n\}$). Because $S(n)$ has at least the element $n$, it is non-empty, so $S(n) \neq \emptyset = 0$.
2. Inspecting the explicit sets $0 = \emptyset$, $1 = \{0\}$, $2 = \{0, 1\}$, and $3 = \{0, 1, 2\}$:
