   - Whenever $m$ precedes $n$ (for example, $m = 1$ and $n = 3$), $m$ is listed directly among the elements of $n = \{0, 1, 2\}$, so $1 \in 3$.
   - At the same time, the elements of $m = 1 = \{0\}$ are also elements of $n = 3 = \{0, 1, 2\}$, so $1 \subsetneq 3$.

   In fact, this dual property—where "less than" ($m < n$) coincides simultaneously with set membership ($m \in n$) and strict subset inclusion ($m \subsetneq n$)—is the hallmark of Von Neumann ordinals in set theory! $\blacksquare$

How do we know that all of these individual sets $0, 1, 2, \dots$ can be gathered together into a single completed infinite set $\mathbb{N}$? In Zermelo–Fraenkel set theory (ZFC), the **Axiom of Infinity** guarantees the existence of at least one **inductive set**—a set $I$ that contains $\emptyset$ and satisfies $(n \in I \implies n \cup \{n\} \in I)$. If we take the intersection of *all* such inductive sets, we obtain the smallest inductive set, which we call $\mathbb{N}$.

Having seen that $\mathbb{N}$, $0$, and the successor map $S: \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N}$ can be realized concretely from the empty set, we never again need to peer inside the nested curly braces $\{\emptyset, \{\emptyset\}\}$. Just as a computer programmer uses an abstract data type through its public interface without worrying about its raw bits in memory, we now extract the five essential structural properties of $(\mathbb{N}, 0, S)$ as the **Peano Axioms** and perform all of our mathematics through them.

## The Peano Axioms

In 1889, the Italian mathematician Giuseppe Peano asked: *What are the bare-minimum rules of counting from which all of arithmetic follows?* He realized that we need only three primitive ingredients:
- A set $\mathbb{N}$ (whose elements we call **natural numbers**),
- A distinguished starting element $0 \in \mathbb{N}$ (called **zero**), and
- A function $S: \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N}$ (called the **successor function**, which you should picture intuitively as stepping one unit to the right on the number line: $S(n)$ is "the next number after $n$").

These three ingredients are governed by five foundational rules.

> [!abstract] Definition: The Peano Axioms
> The **natural numbers** consist of a set $\mathbb{N}$, an element $0 \in \mathbb{N}$, and a successor function $S: \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N}$ satisfying the following five axioms:
> 1. **Zero is a Natural Number:** $0 \in \mathbb{N}$.
> 2. **Closure under Successor:** If $n \in \mathbb{N}$, then $S(n) \in \mathbb{N}$.
> 3. **Zero is Not a Successor:** For every $n \in \mathbb{N}$, $S(n) \neq 0$.
> 4. **Injectivity of Successor:** For all $n, m \in \mathbb{N}$, if $S(n) = S(m)$, then $n = m$.
> 5. **Principle of Mathematical Induction:** If $P \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ is a subset of $\mathbb{N}$ (or equivalently, if $P(n)$ is a logical property of natural numbers) such that:
>    - **Base Case:** $0 \in P$ (i.e., $P(0)$ is true), and
>    - **Inductive Step:** For every $n \in \mathbb{N}$, if $n \in P$ then $S(n) \in P$ (i.e., $P(n) \implies P(S(n))$),
>
>    then $P = \mathbb{N}$ (i.e., $P(n)$ is true for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$).

Let's unpack what each axiom is doing in plain English, and why dropping even one of them would allow bizarre, broken "number systems" to sneak in:
- **Axioms 1 and 2** get the counting process started and keep it going forever: $0$ is in $\mathbb{N}$, which forces $1 := S(0)$ to be in $\mathbb{N}$, which forces $2 := S(1) = S(S(0))$ to be in $\mathbb{N}$, and so on without end.
- **Axiom 3 ($S(n) \neq 0$)** says that $0$ is the true starting point of the number line—no number has $0$ as its next number. Without Axiom 3, the number line could loop back onto $0$ like a twelve-hour clock (or arithmetic modulo $4$: $0 \to 1 \to 2 \to 3 \to 0$, where $S(3) = 0$).
- **Axiom 4 ($S(n) = S(m) \implies n = m$)** says that the successor map $S$ is **one-to-one (injective)**: two *different* numbers ($n \neq m$) cannot have the *same* next number ($S(n) \neq S(m)$). Without Axiom 4, the number line could start at $0$ and then get trapped in a loop later on, like a $\rho$-shaped graph: $0 \to 1 \to 2 \to 1 \to 2 \to \dots$, where $S(0) = 1$ and $S(2) = 1$ even though $0 \neq 2$.Together, Axioms 3 and 4 guarantee that the chain $0, S(0), S(S(0)), \dots$ never loops back to $0$ and never collides with an earlier number, forcing all of these elements to be distinct!
- **Axiom 5 (Mathematical Induction)** is the famous **domino principle**: if you knock over the first domino ($0 \in P$), and if every falling domino $n$ knocks over the next domino $S(n)$ ($n \in P \implies S(n) \in P$), then *every* domino in $\mathbb{N}$ falls ($P = \mathbb{N}$). Structurally, why do we need Axiom 5? Even if Axioms 1–4 hold, the set $\mathbb{N}$ could contain extra "rogue" elements (like $0.5$, or an isolated transfinite chain $\omega \to \omega + 1 \to \omega + 2 \to \dots$) floating completely disconnected from the main chain $0, 1, 2, \dots$. Knocking over domino $0$ would knock over $0, 1, 2, \dots$, but would never reach those rogue elements! By declaring that any set $P$ containing $0$ and closed under $S$ *must equal all of $\mathbb{N}$*, Axiom 5 guarantees that $\mathbb{N}$ contains **only** the elements reachable from $0$ by applying $S$ finitely many times. (You will construct explicit examples of all three pathological models in Problem 0.2.1!)

Let's immediately put Mathematical Induction to work by proving a simple but vital structural theorem: every natural number except $0$ has a unique **predecessor** (the number immediately before it).

> [!theorem] Existence and Uniqueness of Predecessors
> For every $n \in \mathbb{N}$ with $n \neq 0$, there exists a unique $m \in \mathbb{N}$ such that $S(m) = n$.

**Proof:**
Why can't we just say "subtract $1$ from $n$"? Because we haven't defined subtraction—or even addition—yet! All we have are the five Peano Axioms.

Let $P(n)$ be the logical statement:
$$
\text{``Either } n = 0, \text{ or there exists } m \in \mathbb{N} \text{ such that } S(m) = n.\text{''}
$$
We will prove that $P(n)$ holds for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$ using the Principle of Mathematical Induction (Peano Axiom 5):
- **Base Case ($n = 0$):** When $n = 0$, the first clause of $P(0)$ ("$0 = 0$") is immediately true, so $P(0)$ holds.
- **Inductive Step:** Suppose $P(n)$ holds for some $n \in \mathbb{N}$. We need to show that $P(S(n))$ holds, meaning either $S(n) = 0$ or there exists $m \in \mathbb{N}$ such that $S(m) = S(n)$. Looking at that second clause, we don't even need to work hard: simply choose $m = n \in \mathbb{N}$! Then $S(m) = S(n)$ is trivially true, so $P(S(n))$ holds. (Notice how amusing this induction is: we didn't even need to use the inductive hypothesis $P(n)$ inside the inductive step, because the mere fact that an element is of the form $S(n)$ hands us its predecessor $n$ on a silver platter!)

By Peano Axiom 5, $P(n)$ is true for every $n \in \mathbb{N}$. Therefore, if $n \neq 0$, the first clause "$n = 0$" is false, so the second clause must hold: there exists at least one $m \in \mathbb{N}$ such that $S(m) = n$.

Finally, to prove **uniqueness**, suppose $m, m' \in \mathbb{N}$ both satisfy $S(m) = n$ and $S(m') = n$. Then $S(m) = S(m')$, and Peano Axiom 4 (injectivity of $S$) immediately forces $m = m'$. $\blacksquare$

## Recursive Definition of Addition and Its Algebraic Laws

Look closely at the Peano Axioms: they give us $0$ and the single-step successor operation $S(n)$, which moves us forward by one step at a time. There is no $+$ symbol or $\cdot$ symbol anywhere in the axioms! How do we create addition $n + m$ out of $S$?

Intuitively, adding $m$ to $n$ means starting at $n$ and applying the successor operation $S$ a total of $m$ times:
$$
\begin{aligned}
n + 0 &= n && \text{(apply } S \text{ zero times)} \\
n + 1 &= S(n) = S(n + 0) && \text{(apply } S \text{ once)} \\
n + 2 &= S(S(n)) = S(n + 1) && \text{(apply } S \text{ twice)} \\
n + 3 &= S(S(S(n))) = S(n + 2) && \text{(apply } S \text{ three times)}
\end{aligned}
$$
Look at the pattern on the right-hand side: to compute $n + S(m)$, you first compute $n + m$, and then take one more successor step $S(n + m)$.

In Calculus II, you encountered this exact idea when studying **recursively defined sequences**, such as defining the factorial sequence by $a_0 = 1$ and $a_{m+1} = (m + 1) a_m$, or Newton's method $x_{m+1} = x_m - \frac{f(x_m)}{f'(x_m)}$. Instead of giving a single closed-form formula for $f(m)$ all at once, a **recursive definition** specifies:
1. A **base step** declaring the starting output $f(0) = a$, and
2. A **recursive (stepping) rule** explaining how to compute the next output $f(S(m))$ from the current index $m$ and current output $f(m)$.

Before we use recursion to define addition and multiplication, let's ask an important foundational question: *Why do we need a theorem to guarantee that a step-by-step recursive recipe actually defines a valid, conflict-free function $f: \mathbb{N} \to X$?*

To see what could go wrong on a flawed structure, imagine trying to define a function $f$ on a "clock" set $C = \{0, 1, 2\}$ where $S(0) = 1$, $S(1) = 2$, and $S(2) = 0$ (which violates Peano Axiom 3), using the recursive recipe:
$$
f(0) = 0 \quad \text{and} \quad f(S(m)) = f(m) + 1
$$
Starting at $m = 0$, the recipe forces $f(0) = 0$, then $f(1) = f(S(0)) = 0 + 1 = 1$, then $f(2) = f(S(1)) = 1 + 1 = 2$. Put $m = 2$ into the stepping rule one more time: since $S(2) = 0$ on our clock, the rule demands $f(0) = f(S(2)) = f(2) + 1 = 3$! Now $f(0)$ is forced to equal both $0$ and $3$, which contradicts the definition of a function (every input must have a *unique* output).

Why does this conflict never happen on the true natural numbers $\mathbb{N}$? Because Peano Axiom 3 prevents $S(m)$ from ever looping back to $0$, Peano Axiom 4 prevents $S(m)$ from colliding with the successor of any earlier number $S(k)$, and Peano Axiom 5 guarantees that the stepping process reaches every element of $\mathbb{N}$. Richard Dedekind's **Recursion Theorem** (published in 1888) turns this intuition into a rigorous theorem.

> [!theorem] The Recursion Theorem on $\mathbb{N}$
> Let $X$ be any set, let $a \in X$ be a fixed starting element, and let $G: \mathbb{N} \times X \to X$ be any function (the stepping rule). Then there exists a **unique** function $f: \mathbb{N} \to X$ such that:
> 1. **Base Step:** $f(0) = a$.
> 2. **Recursive Step:** For all $m \in \mathbb{N}$, $f(S(m)) = G(m, f(m))$.
>
> *(Note: In the very common special case where the stepping rule depends only on the previous output $f(m)$ via a function $g: X \to X$, the recursive step simply reads $f(S(m)) = g(f(m))$.)*

**Proof:**
This proof is a classic masterpiece of set-theoretic reasoning. Let's first prove **uniqueness** (which is a quick, transparent induction), and then construct the function $f$ explicitly as a set of ordered pairs to prove **existence**, watching carefully where Peano Axioms 3, 4, and 5 each save the day.

**Part 1: Uniqueness.**
Suppose $f_1: \mathbb{N} \to X$ and $f_2: \mathbb{N} \to X$ are two functions that both satisfy the Base Step and the Recursive Step. We want to show $f_1 = f_2$, meaning $f_1(m) = f_2(m)$ for all $m \in \mathbb{N}$. Let $P(m)$ be the statement "$f_1(m) = f_2(m)$":
- **Base Case ($m = 0$):** By the Base Step for both functions, $f_1(0) = a$ and $f_2(0) = a$, so $f_1(0) = f_2(0)$ and $P(0)$ holds.
- **Inductive Step:** Suppose $P(m)$ holds for some $m \in \mathbb{N}$, so $f_1(m) = f_2(m)$. Applying the Recursive Step for both functions gives
  $$
  f_1(S(m)) = G(m, f_1(m)) = G(m, f_2(m)) = f_2(S(m))
  $$
  Thus $P(S(m))$ holds. By **Peano Axiom 5** (Induction), $f_1(m) = f_2(m)$ for all $m \in \mathbb{N}$, so $f_1 = f_2$.

**Part 2: Existence.**
How do we build the function $f: \mathbb{N} \to X$ from scratch? Recall from Section 0.1 that a function $f: \mathbb{N} \to X$ is formally a subset of the Cartesian product, $f \subseteq \mathbb{N} \times X$ (a collection of input-output pairs $(m, x)$), such that for every input $m \in \mathbb{N}$, there exists a **unique** output $x \in X$ with $(m, x) \in f$.

What properties must a subset $R \subseteq \mathbb{N} \times X$ have if it contains the graph of our desired recursive function? It must contain the starting pair $(0, a)$, and whenever it contains a pair $(m, x)$, it must also contain the next pair $(S(m), G(m, x))$. Let's call any subset $R \subseteq \mathbb{N} \times X$ **admissible** if it satisfies these two closure conditions:
1. $(0, a) \in R$.
2. For all $(m, x) \in \mathbb{N} \times X$, if $(m, x) \in R$, then $(S(m), G(m, x)) \in R$.

Let $\mathcal{F}$ denote the collection of all admissible subsets of $\mathbb{N} \times X$. Notice that $\mathcal{F}$ is non-empty because the entire Cartesian product $\mathbb{N} \times X$ contains every possible pair and is therefore trivially admissible! However, $\mathbb{N} \times X$ is way too big to be a function—it pairs each $m$ with *every* $x \in X$. To pare away all the junk pairs and keep only the pairs generated from $(0, a)$ by the stepping rule $G$, we define $f$ to be the **intersection of all admissible subsets**:
$$
f := \bigcap_{R \in \mathcal{F}} R \subseteq \mathbb{N} \times X
$$
Let's check that $f$ itself is admissible:
- Since $(0, a) \in R$ for every $R \in \mathcal{F}$, we have $(0, a) \in f$.
- If $(m, x) \in f$, then $(m, x) \in R$ for every $R \in \mathcal{F}$. Because each $R \in \mathcal{F}$ is admissible, $(S(m), G(m, x)) \in R$ for every $R \in \mathcal{F}$, which means $(S(m), G(m, x)) \in f$.

Thus $f \in \mathcal{F}$ is the **smallest admissible subset** of $\mathbb{N} \times X$: if $R$ is any admissible subset, then $f \subseteq R$.

It remains to verify that $f$ is a genuine function from $\mathbb{N}$ to $X$. Define $P \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ to be the set of all inputs $m \in \mathbb{N}$ that have a **unique** output paired with them in $f$:
$$
P := \{m \in \mathbb{N} \mid \text{there exists a unique } x \in X \text{ such that } (m, x) \in f\}
$$
We prove $P = \mathbb{N}$ by induction:
- **Base Case ($0 \in P$):** Since $f$ is admissible, $(0, a) \in f$, so at least one output $a$ is paired with $0$. Could there be a second, conflicting output $b \neq a$ with $(0, b) \in f$? Suppose for contradiction that $(0, b) \in f$ with $b \neq a$, and consider the slightly smaller set obtained by deleting that single offending pair:
  $$
  R' := f \setminus \{(0, b)\}
  $$
  We claim $R'$ is still admissible! First, since $b \neq a$ and $(0, a) \in f$, the pair $(0, a)$ was not deleted, so $(0, a) \in R'$. Second, take any $(m, x) \in R' \subseteq f$. Since $f$ is admissible, $(S(m), G(m, x)) \in f$. Could $(S(m), G(m, x))$ equal the deleted pair $(0, b)$? **No!** By **Peano Axiom 3**, $S(m) \neq 0$, so the first coordinates cannot match! Thus $(S(m), G(m, x)) \in R'$. This shows $R'$ is admissible, so by minimality of $f$, we must have $f \subseteq R'$. But that contradicts $(0, b) \in f$ and $(0, b) \notin R'$! Hence $a$ is the *unique* output paired with $0$, so $0 \in P$.
- **Inductive Step ($m \in P \implies S(m) \in P$):** Suppose $m \in P$, meaning there is a unique $x \in X$ such that $(m, x) \in f$. Because $f$ is admissible, $(S(m), G(m, x)) \in f$, so $S(m)$ has at least the output $G(m, x)$ paired with it. Could $S(m)$ also be paired with a conflicting output $y \neq G(m, x)$ in $f$? Suppose for contradiction that $(S(m), y) \in f$ with $y \neq G(m, x)$, and again delete that single offending pair:
  $$
  R'' := f \setminus \{(S(m), y)\}
  $$
  Let's check that $R''$ is still admissible:
  1. Since $S(m) \neq 0$ by **Peano Axiom 3**, the pair $(0, a)$ is not equal to $(S(m), y)$, so $(0, a) \in R''$.
  2. Take any pair $(k, z) \in R'' \subseteq f$. Since $f$ is admissible, $(S(k), G(k, z)) \in f$. Could $(S(k), G(k, z))$ equal the deleted pair $(S(m), y)$? Look at what happens if their first coordinates match, $S(k) = S(m)$: by **Peano Axiom 4** (injectivity of $S$), $S(k) = S(m)$ forces $k = m$! Since $(k, z) = (m, z) \in f$ and $m \in P$ has the *unique* output $x$, this forces $z = x$. Consequently, the second coordinate is $G(k, z) = G(m, x) \neq y$. Thus $(S(k), G(k, z))$ can never equal $(S(m), y)$, which means $(S(k), G(k, z)) \in R''$!

  Therefore $R''$ is admissible, so $f \subseteq R''$ by minimality of $f$, contradicting $(S(m), y) \in f$. Hence $G(m, x)$ is the unique output paired with $S(m)$, so $S(m) \in P$.

By **Peano Axiom 5** (Induction), $P = \mathbb{N}$. Thus $f: \mathbb{N} \to X$ is a well-defined function satisfying $f(0) = a$ and $f(S(m)) = G(m, f(m))$ for all $m \in \mathbb{N}$. $\blacksquare$

### Defining Addition and Proving Its Laws

With the Recursion Theorem secured, we can rigorously define addition $n + m$ on $\mathbb{N}$. For each fixed natural number $n \in \mathbb{N}$, we apply the Recursion Theorem with starting value $a = n$ and stepping function $g: \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N}$ given by $g(x) = S(x)$ to define the function $m \mapsto n + m$:

> [!abstract] Definition: Addition of Natural Numbers
> Given any fixed $n \in \mathbb{N}$, we define the sum $n + m$ for all $m \in \mathbb{N}$ recursively on the second variable $m$:
> 1. **Base Step:** $n + 0 := n$.
> 2. **Recursive Step:** $n + S(m) := S(n + m)$.

Let's see how this tiny two-line definition actually computes sums of familiar numbers!

> [!example] Example: Proving $1 + 1 = 2$ and $2 + 2 = 4$ from Peano Arithmetic
> Using the standard abbreviations $1 := S(0)$, $2 := S(1)$, $3 := S(2)$, and $4 := S(3)$, prove from the recursive definition of addition that $n + 1 = S(n)$ for every $n \in \mathbb{N}$, and deduce that $1 + 1 = 2$ and $2 + 2 = 4$.

**Solution:**
First, for any $n \in \mathbb{N}$, since $1 = S(0)$, applying the Recursive Step of addition followed by the Base Step gives
$$
\begin{aligned}
n + 1 &= n + S(0) \\
      &= S(n + 0) && \text{(by the Recursive Step } n + S(m) = S(n + m) \text{ with } m = 0\text{)} \\
      &= S(n) && \text{(by the Base Step } n + 0 = n\text{)}
\end{aligned}
$$
In words, adding $1$ to $n$ is literally the successor $S(n)$! Setting $n = 1$ immediately gives
$$
1 + 1 = S(1) = 2
$$
Now let's compute $2 + 2$. Since $2 = S(1)$, we unfold the second variable one step at a time using the Recursive Step:
$$
\begin{aligned}
2 + 2 &= 2 + S(1) \\
      &= S(2 + 1) && \text{(by the Recursive Step with } m = 1\text{)} \\
      &= S(S(2)) && \text{(since } 2 + 1 = S(2)\text{)} \\
      &= S(3) && \text{(since } S(2) = 3\text{)} \\
      &= 4 && \text{(since } S(3) = 4\text{)}
\end{aligned}
$$
Every arithmetic fact you learned in elementary school is a theorem of these two recursive rules! $\blacksquare$

Now pause and notice a crucial **asymmetry** in our definition of addition. Because we defined $n + m$ by recursion on the *second* variable $m$:
- We know immediately by definition that $n + 0 = n$ (zero on the *right* does nothing), and $n + S(m) = S(n + m)$ (a successor on the *right* pulls outside).
- However, what about $0 + n = n$ (zero on the *left*), or $S(n) + m = S(n + m)$ (a successor on the *left*)?

If a student is asked why $0 + n = n$, their first instinct is usually to say: *"Well, $0 + n = n + 0 = n$ because addition is commutative!"* **Wait!** How do you know $a + b = b + a$ for all natural numbers? Commutativity is not one of the Peano Axioms—it is a theorem we have to *prove* by induction! And if you try to prove $a + b = b + a$ by induction on $b$, look at what happens:
- In the base case ($b = 0$), you need to show $a + 0 = 0 + a$. The left side is $a$ by definition, so you need $0 + a = a$!
- In the inductive step, you assume $a + b = b + a$ and want to show $a + S(b) = S(b) + a$. The left side is $S(a + b) = S(b + a)$, so to match the right side you need $S(b) + a = S(b + a)$!

This is why we must first prove the two left-slot rules ($0 + n = n$ and $S(n) + m = S(n + m)$) as a standalone helper lemma by induction before we can prove commutativity.

> [!theorem] Lemma: Left-Addition of Zero and Successors
> For all $n, m \in \mathbb{N}$:
> 1. **Left Additive Identity:** $0 + n = n$.
> 2. **Left Successor Rule:** $S(n) + m = S(n + m)$.

**Proof:**
**1. Proof that $0 + n = n$:** We induct on $n \in \mathbb{N}$.
- **Base Case ($n = 0$):** By the Base Step of the definition of addition ($n + 0 = n$ with $n = 0$), we have $0 + 0 = 0$.
- **Inductive Step:** Suppose $0 + n = n$ holds for some $n \in \mathbb{N}$. We must show $0 + S(n) = S(n)$. Using the Recursive Step of the definition of addition (which acts on the right-hand slot!) and then substituting the inductive hypothesis $0 + n = n$, we obtain
  $$
  \begin{aligned}
  0 + S(n) &= S(0 + n) && \text{(by the Recursive Step of addition)} \\
           &= S(n) && \text{(by the inductive hypothesis } 0 + n = n\text{)}
  \end{aligned}
  $$
  By Induction, $0 + n = n$ holds for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$.

**2. Proof that $S(n) + m = S(n + m)$:** Fix an arbitrary $n \in \mathbb{N}$, and induct on the right-hand variable $m \in \mathbb{N}$ (because our definition of $+$ knows how to simplify expressions when $0$ or $S$ appears in the right-hand slot!).
- **Base Case ($m = 0$):** Using the Base Step of addition ($x + 0 = x$) twice,
  $$
  S(n) + 0 = S(n) = S(n + 0)
  $$
- **Inductive Step:** Suppose $S(n) + m = S(n + m)$ holds for some $m \in \mathbb{N}$. Then for $S(m)$, we have
  $$
  \begin{aligned}
  S(n) + S(m) &= S(S(n) + m) && \text{(by the Recursive Step of addition)} \\
              &= S(S(n + m)) && \text{(by the inductive hypothesis } S(n) + m = S(n + m)\text{)} \\
              &= S(n + S(m)) && \text{(by the Recursive Step of addition in reverse)}
  \end{aligned}
  $$
By Induction, $S(n) + m = S(n + m)$ holds for all $m \in \mathbb{N}$ (and hence for all $n, m \in \mathbb{N}$). $\blacksquare$

With this helper lemma in our toolkit, the three fundamental algebraic laws of addition—associativity, commutativity, and the cancellation law—fall into place effortlessly.

> [!theorem] Algebraic Laws of Addition on $\mathbb{N}$
> For all $a, b, c \in \mathbb{N}$:
> 1. **Associativity:** $(a + b) + c = a + (b + c)$.
> 2. **Commutativity:** $a + b = b + a$.
> 3. **Additive Cancellation Law:** If $a + c = b + c$, then $a = b$ (and by commutativity, if $c + a = c + b$, then $a = b$).

**Proof:**
**1. Associativity:** Fix arbitrary $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$ and induct on $c \in \mathbb{N}$ (the rightmost variable, where the recursive definition of $+$ operates directly).
- **Base Case ($c = 0$):** Using the Base Step of addition twice,
  $$
  (a + b) + 0 = a + b = a + (b + 0)
  $$
- **Inductive Step:** Suppose $(a + b) + c = a + (b + c)$ holds for some $c \in \mathbb{N}$. For $S(c)$, we pull the successor $S$ out of the right-hand slots step by step:
  $$
  \begin{aligned}
  (a + b) + S(c) &= S((a + b) + c) && \text{(by the Recursive Step of addition)} \\
                 &= S(a + (b + c)) && \text{(by the inductive hypothesis)} \\
                 &= a + S(b + c) && \text{(by the Recursive Step of addition)} \\
                 &= a + (b + S(c)) && \text{(by the Recursive Step of addition)}
  \end{aligned}
  $$
  By Induction, $(a + b) + c = a + (b + c)$ for all $a, b, c \in \mathbb{N}$.

**2. Commutativity:** Fix $a \in \mathbb{N}$ and induct on $b \in \mathbb{N}$.
- **Base Case ($b = 0$):** By the Base Step of addition and Part 1 of our helper lemma,
  $$
  a + 0 = a = 0 + a
  $$
- **Inductive Step:** Suppose $a + b = b + a$ holds for some $b \in \mathbb{N}$. Using the Recursive Step of addition on the left side and Part 2 of our helper lemma on the right side,
  $$
  \begin{aligned}
  a + S(b) &= S(a + b) && \text{(by the Recursive Step of addition)} \\
           &= S(b + a) && \text{(by the inductive hypothesis)} \\
           &= S(b) + a && \text{(by the Left Successor Rule, Lemma Part 2)}
  \end{aligned}
  $$
  By Induction, $a + b = b + a$ for all $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$.

**3. Additive Cancellation Law:** Fix $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$ and induct on $c \in \mathbb{N}$, letting $P(c)$ be the implication "$a + c = b + c \implies a = b$."
- **Base Case ($c = 0$):** If $a + 0 = b + 0$, then since $a + 0 = a$ and $b + 0 = b$, we immediately have $a = b$.
- **Inductive Step:** Suppose $P(c)$ holds (i.e., $a + c = b + c \implies a = b$). Now assume that $a + S(c) = b + S(c)$. By the Recursive Step of addition, this equation can be rewritten as
  $$
  S(a + c) = S(b + c)
  $$
  By **Peano Axiom 4** (injectivity of the successor map $S$), we can peel off $S$ from both sides to conclude
  $$
  a + c = b + c
  $$
  Applying our inductive hypothesis $P(c)$ immediately yields $a = b$. Thus $P(S(c))$ holds, completing the induction. $\blacksquare$

> [!note] Why Additive Cancellation Is Our Lifeline Before Subtraction Exists
> In high school algebra or Calculus, if you see the equation $a + c = b + c$, you automatically add $-c$ (or subtract $c$) on both sides. However, inside $\mathbb{N} = \{0, 1, 2, \dots\}$, negative numbers and general subtraction do not exist yet! Instead, **Peano Axiom 4** ($S(x) = S(y) \implies x = y$) acts as our microscopic "cancel $+1$ from both sides" tool, and Mathematical Induction upgrades that single-step cancellation into the ability to cancel $+c$ for any natural number $c \in \mathbb{N}$. When we construct the integers $\mathbb{Z}$ in Section 0.3, this cancellation law will be the decisive algebraic ingredient that makes integer equivalence classes work!

Before moving to multiplication, let's also record one tiny, indispensable fact about when a sum of two natural numbers can equal $0$. Because there are no negative numbers in $\mathbb{N}$, you can never add a non-zero natural number to another natural number and get $0$:

> [!theorem] Lemma: Sum Equal to Zero in $\mathbb{N}$
> For any $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$, if $a + b = 0$, then $a = 0$ and $b = 0$.

**Proof:**
Suppose $a + b = 0$. Could $b \neq 0$? If $b \neq 0$, then by the Existence of Predecessors theorem, $b = S(m)$ for some $m \in \mathbb{N}$. Then
$$
a + b = a + S(m) = S(a + m)
$$
By **Peano Axiom 3**, no successor can equal $0$, so $S(a + m) \neq 0$, contradicting $a + b = 0$! Thus we must have $b = 0$. Substituting $b = 0$ into $a + b = 0$ gives $a + 0 = 0$, so $a = 0$ as well. $\blacksquare$

## Recursive Definition of Multiplication and Its Algebraic Laws

Just as addition $n + m$ was constructed by starting at $0$ (or $n$) and applying the successor operation repeatedly, multiplication $n \cdot m$ is constructed by starting at $0$ and **adding $n$ repeatedly** $m$ times:
$$
\begin{aligned}
n \cdot 0 &= 0 && \text{(add } n \text{ zero times)} \\
n \cdot 1 &= 0 + n = n && \text{(add } n \text{ once)} \\
n \cdot 2 &= n + n && \text{(add } n \text{ twice)} \\
n \cdot 3 &= (n + n) + n && \text{(add } n \text{ three times)}
\end{aligned}
$$
To step from $n \cdot m$ (the sum of $m$ copies of $n$) to $n \cdot S(m)$ (the sum of $m + 1$ copies of $n$), we simply add one more copy of $n$! Formally, for each fixed $n \in \mathbb{N}$, we apply the Recursion Theorem with starting value $a = 0$ and stepping function $g(x) = x + n$:

> [!abstract] Definition: Multiplication of Natural Numbers
> Given any fixed $n \in \mathbb{N}$, we define the product $n \cdot m$ (often written simply as $nm$) for all $m \in \mathbb{N}$ recursively on the second variable $m$:
> 1. **Base Step:** $n \cdot 0 := 0$.
> 2. **Recursive Step:** $n \cdot S(m) := (n \cdot m) + n$.

Notice that just as with addition, our recursive definition of multiplication acts on the *second* (right-hand) factor $m$. Therefore, before we can prove that multiplication is commutative ($a \cdot b = b \cdot a$), we first prove the two corresponding left-factor helper rules by induction.

> [!theorem] Lemma: Left-Multiplication by Zero and Successors
> For all $n, m \in \mathbb{N}$:
> 1. **Left Multiplication by Zero:** $0 \cdot m = 0$.
> 2. **Left Successor Multiplication Rule:** $S(n) \cdot m = (n \cdot m) + m$.

**Proof:**
**1. Proof that $0 \cdot m = 0$:** We induct on $m \in \mathbb{N}$.
- **Base Case ($m = 0$):** By the Base Step of multiplication, $0 \cdot 0 = 0$.
- **Inductive Step:** Suppose $0 \cdot m = 0$ holds for some $m \in \mathbb{N}$. Then by the Recursive Step of multiplication and the inductive hypothesis,
  $$
  0 \cdot S(m) = (0 \cdot m) + 0 = 0 + 0 = 0
  $$
  By Induction, $0 \cdot m = 0$ for all $m \in \mathbb{N}$.

**2. Proof that $S(n) \cdot m = (n \cdot m) + m$:** Fix $n \in \mathbb{N}$ and induct on $m \in \mathbb{N}$.
- **Base Case ($m = 0$):** Using the Base Step of multiplication ($x \cdot 0 = 0$) and the Base Step of addition ($0 + 0 = 0$),
  $$
  S(n) \cdot 0 = 0 = 0 + 0 = (n \cdot 0) + 0
  $$
- **Inductive Step:** Suppose $S(n) \cdot m = (n \cdot m) + m$ holds for some $m \in \mathbb{N}$. Then for $S(m)$, we use the Recursive Step of multiplication together with the associativity and commutativity of addition that we already proved:
  $$
  \begin{aligned}
  S(n) \cdot S(m) &= (S(n) \cdot m) + S(n) && \text{(by the Recursive Step of multiplication)} \\
                  &= ((n \cdot m) + m) + S(n) && \text{(by the inductive hypothesis)} \\
                  &= (n \cdot m) + (m + S(n)) && \text{(by associativity of addition)} \\
                  &= (n \cdot m) + S(m + n) && \text{(by the Recursive Step of addition)} \\
                  &= (n \cdot m) + S(n + m) && \text{(by commutativity of addition, } m + n = n + m\text{)} \\
                  &= (n \cdot m) + (n + S(m)) && \text{(by the Recursive Step of addition)} \\
                  &= ((n \cdot m) + n) + S(m) && \text{(by associativity of addition)} \\
                  &= (n \cdot S(m)) + S(m) && \text{(by the Recursive Step of multiplication)}
  \end{aligned}
  $$
  By Induction, $S(n) \cdot m = (n \cdot m) + m$ holds for all $n, m \in \mathbb{N}$. $\blacksquare$

Now we can establish the entire suite of algebraic laws for multiplication on $\mathbb{N}$. Before reading item 5 (**No Zero Divisors**) and item 6 (**Multiplicative Cancellation**), connect them to your Linear Algebra intuition: in the world of $2 \times 2$ matrices, it is completely possible to multiply two non-zero matrices $A \neq 0$ and $B \neq 0$ and get the zero matrix $AB = 0$ (such matrices are called **zero divisors**), and consequently $AC = BC$ does *not* imply $A = B$ for matrices! Item 5 below guarantees that the natural numbers have **no zero divisors**: a product of two natural numbers is $0$ if and only if at least one of the numbers is $0$.

> [!theorem] Algebraic Laws of Multiplication on $\mathbb{N}$
> For all $a, b, c \in \mathbb{N}$:
> 1. **Multiplicative Identity ($1 := S(0)$):** $a \cdot 1 = 1 \cdot a = a$.
> 2. **Distributivity over Addition:** $a \cdot (b + c) = (a \cdot b) + (a \cdot c)$ and $(b + c) \cdot a = (b \cdot a) + (c \cdot a)$.
> 3. **Commutativity:** $a \cdot b = b \cdot a$.
> 4. **Associativity:** $(a \cdot b) \cdot c = a \cdot (b \cdot c)$.
> 5. **No Zero Divisors:** $a \cdot b = 0$ if and only if $a = 0$ or $b = 0$.
> 6. **Multiplicative Cancellation Law:** If $a \cdot c = b \cdot c$ and $c \neq 0$, then $a = b$.

**Proof:**
**1. Multiplicative Identity:** Since $1 = S(0)$, the Recursive Step of multiplication and Left-Addition of Zero give
$$
a \cdot 1 = a \cdot S(0) = (a \cdot 0) + a = 0 + a = a
$$
Similarly, using our Left-Multiplication Lemma with $n = 0$ and $m = a$,
$$
1 \cdot a = S(0) \cdot a = (0 \cdot a) + a = 0 + a = a
$$

**2. Distributivity:** Let's prove left distributivity $a \cdot (b + c) = (a \cdot b) + (a \cdot c)$ by fixing $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$ and inducting on $c \in \mathbb{N}$.
- **Base Case ($c = 0$):** Since $b + 0 = b$ and $a \cdot 0 = 0$, we have
  $$
  a \cdot (b + 0) = a \cdot b = (a \cdot b) + 0 = (a \cdot b) + (a \cdot 0)
  $$
- **Inductive Step:** Suppose $a \cdot (b + c) = (a \cdot b) + (a \cdot c)$ holds for some $c \in \mathbb{N}$. Then for $S(c)$, using the recursive definitions of addition and multiplication along with associativity of addition:
  $$
  \begin{aligned}
  a \cdot (b + S(c)) &= a \cdot S(b + c) && \text{(by the Recursive Step of addition)} \\
                     &= (a \cdot (b + c)) + a && \text{(by the Recursive Step of multiplication)} \\
                     &= ((a \cdot b) + (a \cdot c)) + a && \text{(by the inductive hypothesis)} \\
                     &= (a \cdot b) + ((a \cdot c) + a) && \text{(by associativity of addition)} \\
                     &= (a \cdot b) + (a \cdot S(c)) && \text{(by the Recursive Step of multiplication)}
  \end{aligned}
  $$
  This proves left distributivity by induction. Right distributivity $(b + c) \cdot a = (b \cdot a) + (c \cdot a)$ follows immediately once we prove commutativity of multiplication in Step 3 (Notice that our proof of Step 3 below does not use distributivity, so there is no circularity!).

**3. Commutativity:** Fix $a \in \mathbb{N}$ and induct on $b \in \mathbb{N}$.
- **Base Case ($b = 0$):** By the Base Step of multiplication and Part 1 of our Left-Multiplication Lemma, $a \cdot 0 = 0 = 0 \cdot a$.
- **Inductive Step:** Suppose $a \cdot b = b \cdot a$ holds for some $b \in \mathbb{N}$. Using the Recursive Step of multiplication and Part 2 of our Left-Multiplication Lemma,
  $$
  \begin{aligned}
  a \cdot S(b) &= (a \cdot b) + a && \text{(by the Recursive Step of multiplication)} \\
               &= (b \cdot a) + a && \text{(by the inductive hypothesis)} \\
               &= S(b) \cdot a && \text{(by the Left Successor Multiplication Rule)}
  \end{aligned}
  $$
  By Induction, $a \cdot b = b \cdot a$ for all $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$.

**4. Associativity:** Fix $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$ and induct on $c \in \mathbb{N}$.
- **Base Case ($c = 0$):** $(a \cdot b) \cdot 0 = 0 = a \cdot 0 = a \cdot (b \cdot 0)$.
- **Inductive Step:** Suppose $(a \cdot b) \cdot c = a \cdot (b \cdot c)$ holds for some $c \in \mathbb{N}$. Using the Recursive Step of multiplication and left distributivity (from Step 2),
  $$
  \begin{aligned}
  (a \cdot b) \cdot S(c) &= ((a \cdot b) \cdot c) + (a \cdot b) && \text{(by the Recursive Step of multiplication)} \\
                         &= (a \cdot (b \cdot c)) + (a \cdot b) && \text{(by the inductive hypothesis)} \\
                         &= a \cdot ((b \cdot c) + b) && \text{(by left distributivity in reverse)} \\
                         &= a \cdot (b \cdot S(c)) && \text{(by the Recursive Step of multiplication)}
  \end{aligned}
  $$
  By Induction, $(a \cdot b) \cdot c = a \cdot (b \cdot c)$ for all $a, b, c \in \mathbb{N}$.

**5. No Zero Divisors:** If $a = 0$ or $b = 0$, we already know $a \cdot b = 0$. For the converse, let's prove the contrapositive: suppose $a \neq 0$ and $b \neq 0$, and let's show $a \cdot b \neq 0$. By the Existence of Predecessors theorem, since $a \neq 0$ and $b \neq 0$, we can write $a = S(n)$ and $b = S(m)$ for some $n, m \in \mathbb{N}$. Then by the Recursive Step of multiplication and the Recursive Step of addition,
$$
\begin{aligned}
a \cdot b &= S(n) \cdot S(m) \\
          &= (S(n) \cdot m) + S(n) \\
          &= S((S(n) \cdot m) + n)
\end{aligned}
$$
Because $a \cdot b$ is the successor of the natural number $(S(n) \cdot m) + n$, **Peano Axiom 3** ($S(x) \neq 0$) guarantees that $a \cdot b \neq 0$! Taking the contrapositive proves that if $a \cdot b = 0$, then $a = 0$ or $b = 0$.

**6. Multiplicative Cancellation Law:** We can prove this directly by induction on $a$ (or even more effortlessly in the next subsection as a one-line consequence of Trichotomy and order compatibility!). Let's give the direct inductive proof here so you can see how it mirrors additive cancellation: fix $c \neq 0$ and induct on $a \in \mathbb{N}$, letting $P(a)$ be the statement *"for all $b \in \mathbb{N}$, if $a \cdot c = b \cdot c$, then $a = b$."*
- **Base Case ($a = 0$):** Suppose $0 \cdot c = b \cdot c$ for some $b \in \mathbb{N}$. Then $b \cdot c = 0$. Since $c \neq 0$, the **No Zero Divisors** law (Part 5) forces $b = 0 = a$. Thus $P(0)$ holds.
- **Inductive Step:** Suppose $P(a)$ holds for some $a \in \mathbb{N}$, and suppose $S(a) \cdot c = b \cdot c$ for some $b \in \mathbb{N}$. Could $b = 0$? If $b = 0$, then $S(a) \cdot c = 0 \cdot c = 0$, which by No Zero Divisors (since $c \neq 0$) forces $S(a) = 0$, violating Peano Axiom 3! Thus $b \neq 0$, so by Existence of Predecessors, $b = S(m)$ for some $m \in \mathbb{N}$. Now our equation $S(a) \cdot c = S(m) \cdot c$ expands via the Left Successor Multiplication Rule to
  $$
  (a \cdot c) + c = (m \cdot c) + c
  $$
  By the **Additive Cancellation Law**, we cancel $+ c$ from both sides to obtain $a \cdot c = m \cdot c$. Applying our inductive hypothesis $P(a)$ yields $a = m$, and hence $S(a) = S(m) = b$. This completes the induction! $\blacksquare$

## Order on the Natural Numbers

With addition and multiplication in place, how do we define the inequality relation $a \le b$ on $\mathbb{N}$?

Think about what "$a \le b$" means when there are no negative numbers around: $b$ is at least as large as $a$ if and only if you can start at $a$ and reach $b$ by adding some natural number $k \in \mathbb{N}$ (which represents the non-negative gap between $a$ and $b$). For instance, $3 \le 7$ because $3 + 4 = 7$ for $4 \in \mathbb{N}$, whereas $7 \not\le 3$ because there is no natural number $k \in \mathbb{N}$ satisfying $7 + k = 3$.

> [!abstract] Definition: Order on $\mathbb{N}$
> Let $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$.
> 1. We say that $a$ is **less than or equal to** $b$, written $a \le b$ (or $b \ge a$), if there exists some $k \in \mathbb{N}$ such that
>    $$
>    a + k = b
>    $$
> 2. We say that $a$ is **strictly less than** $b$, written $a < b$ (or $b > a$), if $a \le b$ and $a \neq b$.

Let's immediately check an equivalent, super-convenient characterization of strict inequality $a < b$:
- If $a < b$, then $a \le b$ gives $a + k = b$ for some $k \in \mathbb{N}$. Could $k = 0$? If $k = 0$, then $a + 0 = b \implies a = b$, contradicting $a \neq b$! Thus $k \neq 0$, which by Existence of Predecessors means $k = S(m)$ for some $m \in \mathbb{N}$.
- Conversely, if $a + S(m) = b$ for some $m \in \mathbb{N}$, then $a \le b$. Could $a = b$? If $a = b$, then $a + S(m) = a = a + 0$, and cancelling $a$ by the Additive Cancellation Law gives $S(m) = 0$, violating Peano Axiom 3! Thus $a \neq b$, so $a < b$.

Let's highlight this equivalence because we will use it constantly:
$$
a < b \iff \text{there exists } m \in \mathbb{N} \text{ such that } a + S(m) = b \iff S(a) \le b
$$
(where the last equivalence follows because $a + S(m) = S(a + m) = S(a) + m$).

> [!example] Example: Discreteness of the Natural Numbers (No Number Between $n$ and $S(n)$)
> Prove that for every $n \in \mathbb{N}$:
> 1. $0 \le n$.
> 2. $n < S(n)$.
> 3. There is **no** natural number $x \in \mathbb{N}$ strictly between $n$ and $S(n)$—that is, if $n < x$, then $S(n) \le x$ (so we can never have $n < x < S(n)$).

**Solution:**
1. Since $0 + n = n$ for $n \in \mathbb{N}$, the definition of $\le$ immediately gives $0 \le n$.
2. Since $n + S(0) = S(n + 0) = S(n)$, the characterization of strict inequality ($a + S(m) = b$ with $m = 0$) immediately gives $n < S(n)$.
3. Suppose $x \in \mathbb{N}$ satisfies $n < x$. By our characterization of strict inequality, there exists $m \in \mathbb{N}$ such that
   $$
   n + S(m) = x
   $$
   Using the Recursive Step and Left Successor Rule of addition, we can rewrite the left side as
   $$
   S(n) + m = S(n + m) = n + S(m) = x
   $$
   Because $S(n) + m = x$ with $m \in \mathbb{N}$, the definition of $\le$ says directly that $S(n) \le x$! (Consequently, if we also had $x < S(n)$, then $S(n) \le x$ and $x \le S(n)$ would force $x = S(n)$ by antisymmetry below, contradicting $x < S(n)$.) In other words, stepping from $n$ to $S(n)$ is a minimum possible discrete jump of size $1$: there are no natural numbers hiding in the gap between $n$ and $n + 1$. $\blacksquare$

Now let's state and prove the four crowning order properties of $\mathbb{N}$. Pay special attention to two terms that may be new to you:
- **Trichotomy Law:** Given any two natural numbers $a$ and $b$, you can always compare them, and *exactly one* of the three possibilities $a < b$, $a = b$, or $a > b$ is true. (By contrast, subset inclusion $\subseteq$ on $\mathcal{P}(X)$ does *not* satisfy trichotomy when $|X| \ge 2$, because $\{1\}$ and $\{2\}$ are neither equal nor subsets of one another!)
- **The Well-Ordering Principle:** Every non-empty subset $E \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ has a **least element** (a minimum). Notice how special this is to $\mathbb{N}$: in the integers $\mathbb{Z}$, the subset of negative integers $\{-1, -2, -3, \dots\}$ has no least element; in the positive rationals $\mathbb{Q}^+$ or positive reals $\mathbb{R}^+$, the open interval $(0, 1) = \{x > 0 \mid x < 1\}$ has no least element (for any $x \in (0, 1)$, $x/2$ is even smaller!). In $\mathbb{N}$, however, you cannot descend endlessly below a natural number without hitting bottom in finitely many steps.

> [!theorem] Fundamental Order Properties of $\mathbb{N}$
> For all $a, b, c \in \mathbb{N}$:
> 1. **Partial Order Axioms:**
>    - *Reflexivity:* $a \le a$.
>    - *Antisymmetry:* If $a \le b$ and $b \le a$, then $a = b$.
>    - *Transitivity:* If $a \le b$ and $b \le c$, then $a \le c$ (and if $a < b$ and $b \le c$, or $a \le b$ and $b < c$, then $a < c$).
> 2. **Trichotomy Law (Total Order):** For any $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$, **exactly one** of the following three relations holds:
>    $$
>    a < b, \qquad a = b, \qquad \text{or} \qquad b < a
>    $$
> 3. **Order Compatibility with Arithmetic:**
>    - *Additive Compatibility:* $a \le b \iff a + c \le b + c$, and $a < b \iff a + c < b + c$.
>    - *Multiplicative Compatibility:* If $c \neq 0$, then $a < b \iff a \cdot c < b \cdot c$.
> 4. **The Well-Ordering Principle:** Every non-empty subset $E \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ has a **least element** (a minimum)—that is, there exists a unique element $m \in E$ such that $m \le x$ for all $x \in E$.

**Proof:**
**1. Partial Order Axioms:**
- *Reflexivity ($a \le a$):* Since $a + 0 = a$ with $0 \in \mathbb{N}$, we have $a \le a$.
- *Antisymmetry ($a \le b \text{ and } b \le a \implies a = b$):* Suppose $a \le b$ and $b \le a$. Then there exist $k, l \in \mathbb{N}$ such that $a + k = b$ and $b + l = a$. Substituting $b = a + k$ into the second equation and using associativity of addition gives
  $$
  a + (k + l) = (a + k) + l = b + l = a = a + 0
  $$
  By the Additive Cancellation Law, we cancel $a$ from both sides to get
  $$
  k + l = 0
  $$
  By our earlier lemma on sums equal to zero in $\mathbb{N}$, $k + l = 0$ forces $k = 0$ and $l = 0$. Therefore $b = a + k = a + 0 = a$.
- *Transitivity ($a \le b \text{ and } b \le c \implies a \le c$):* Suppose $a + k = b$ and $b + l = c$ for some $k, l \in \mathbb{N}$. Then
  $$
  a + (k + l) = (a + k) + l = b + l = c
  $$
  Since $k + l \in \mathbb{N}$, we have $a \le c$. Moreover, if $a < b$ (so $k \neq 0$) or $b < c$ (so $l \neq 0$), then $k + l \neq 0$ by our lemma on sums equal to zero, so $a < c$.

**2. Trichotomy Law:**
First, let's check that **at most one** of $a < b$, $a = b$, or $b < a$ can hold:
- By the very definition of strict inequality ($x < y \iff x \le y \text{ and } x \neq y$), neither $a < b$ nor $b < a$ can hold simultaneously with $a = b$.
- Could both $a < b$ and $b < a$ hold? If so, then $a \le b$ and $b \le a$, which by Antisymmetry forces $a = b$, contradicting $a \neq b$!

Now let's prove that **at least one** of the three relations holds by fixing $a \in \mathbb{N}$ and inducting on $b \in \mathbb{N}$:
- **Base Case ($b = 0$):** By Example 1 above, $0 \le a$ for every $a \in \mathbb{N}$. By definition of $<$, $0 \le a$ means either $a = 0$ or $0 < a$. Thus trichotomy holds between $a$ and $0$.
- **Inductive Step:** Suppose trichotomy holds between $a$ and $b$, so either $a < b$, $a = b$, or $b < a$. We must compare $a$ with $S(b)$:
  - *Case 1 ($a < b$ or $a = b$, i.e., $a \le b$):* Since $a \le b$ and $b < S(b)$ (as shown in our Example above), Transitivity gives $a < S(b)$.
  - *Case 2 ($b < a$):* As we proved in our Example above (no natural number lies strictly between $b$ and $S(b)$), $b < a$ implies $S(b) \le a$. By definition of $<$, having $S(b) \le a$ means either $S(b) = a$ or $S(b) < a$.

  In all cases, at least one of $a < S(b)$, $a = S(b)$, or $S(b) < a$ holds. By Induction, trichotomy holds for all $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$.

**3. Order Compatibility with Arithmetic:**
- *Additive Compatibility:* Suppose $a \le b$, so $a + k = b$ for some $k \in \mathbb{N}$. Adding $c$ to both sides and using commutativity and associativity of addition gives
  $$
  (a + c) + k = (a + k) + c = b + c
  $$
  which means $a + c \le b + c$. Conversely, if $a + c \le b + c$, then $(a + c) + k = b + c$ for some $k \in \mathbb{N}$, so $(a + k) + c = b + c$, and the Additive Cancellation Law cancels $+ c$ to give $a + k = b$, i.e., $a \le b$. Moreover, $k \neq 0$ in both directions if and only if the inequality is strict, so $a < b \iff a + c < b + c$.
- *Multiplicative Compatibility:* Suppose $c \neq 0$. If $a < b$, then $a + k = b$ for some $k \in \mathbb{N}$ with $k \neq 0$. Multiplying both sides by $c$ and using Distributivity gives
  $$
  (a \cdot c) + (k \cdot c) = (a + k) \cdot c = b \cdot c
  $$
  Since $k \neq 0$ and $c \neq 0$, the **No Zero Divisors** law guarantees $k \cdot c \neq 0$. Therefore $(a \cdot c) + (k \cdot c) = b \cdot c$ with $k \cdot c \neq 0$, which means $a \cdot c < b \cdot c$!
  Conversely, suppose $a \cdot c < b \cdot c$ with $c \neq 0$. By Trichotomy between $a$ and $b$, we must have $a < b$, $a = b$, or $b < a$. If $a = b$, then $a \cdot c = b \cdot c$, impossible; if $b < a$, then the forward direction we just proved gives $b \cdot c < a \cdot c$, also impossible by Trichotomy! Thus we must have $a < b$.

**4. The Well-Ordering Principle:**
How do we use Mathematical Induction to prove that every non-empty subset $E \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ has a minimum element? Here is the key insight: suppose $E \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ is a subset that has **no** least element. We will prove by induction that **no natural number can belong to $E$**, forcing $E = \emptyset$!

Wait: if we simply tried to induct on the statement $Q(n)$: "$n \notin E$," the inductive step $Q(n) \implies Q(S(n))$ wouldn't have enough information—knowing that the single number $n$ is not in $E$ doesn't tell us whether $S(n)$ is the smallest element of $E$, because some earlier number $0, 1, \dots, n - 1$ might be in $E$! To know that $S(n)$ would be the very first element of $E$, we need to know that **none of the numbers from $0$ up to $n$** are in $E$.

So, let $E \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ be a subset with *no* least element, and define $P(n)$ to be the cumulative statement:
$$
P(n): \quad \text{``For every } k \in \mathbb{N} \text{ with } k \le n, \text{ we have } k \notin E.\text{''}
$$
Let's prove $P(n)$ holds for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$ by Mathematical Induction:
- **Base Case ($n = 0$):** If $k \le 0$, then since $0 \le k$ always holds, Antisymmetry gives $k = 0$. So $P(0)$ simply asserts that $0 \notin E$. Could $0 \in E$? Since $0 \le x$ for every $x \in \mathbb{N}$ (and thus for every $x \in E$), if $0$ belonged to $E$, then $0$ would be a least element of $E$! That contradicts our assumption that $E$ has no least element. Hence $0 \notin E$, and $P(0)$ is true.
- **Inductive Step:** Suppose $P(n)$ holds for some $n \in \mathbb{N}$, meaning no number $k \le n$ belongs to $E$. To prove $P(S(n))$, we must show that no number $k \le S(n)$ belongs to $E$. By Trichotomy and our Example above (no number lies strictly between $n$ and $S(n)$), any $k \le S(n)$ satisfies either $k \le n$ or $k = S(n)$. Since we already know $k \notin E$ for all $k \le n$ by the inductive hypothesis $P(n)$, we only need to check that $S(n) \notin E$.

  Could $S(n) \in E$? Take any element $x \in E$. Because $P(n)$ tells us that no element of $E$ can satisfy $x \le n$, Trichotomy forces $n < x$, which as we proved in our Example above implies $S(n) \le x$! Thus $S(n) \le x$ for every $x \in E$. If $S(n)$ belonged to $E$, then $S(n)$ would be a least element of $E$—again contradicting our assumption that $E$ has no least element! Therefore $S(n) \notin E$, so $P(S(n))$ holds.

By Mathematical Induction, $P(n)$ holds for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$. In particular, taking $k = n$ (since $n \le n$) shows that $n \notin E$ for every $n \in \mathbb{N}$, so $E = \emptyset$. Taking the contrapositive, every non-empty subset $E \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ must have a least element! (And this least element is unique by Antisymmetry: if $m, m' \in E$ are both least elements, then $m \le m'$ and $m' \le m$, so $m = m'$.) $\blacksquare$

> [!note] Strong Induction and Fermat's Method of Infinite Descent
> Notice the clever trick we just used in the proof of the Well-Ordering Principle: instead of inducting on a single statement $Q(n)$, we inducted on the cumulative statement $P(n)$ asserting that $Q(k)$ holds for **all $k \le n$**. This technique is so widespread that it has its own name—**Strong Induction** (which you will formalize in Problem 0.2.2).
>
> Equally important is the contrapositive way the Well-Ordering Principle is used in practice, famously called **Fermat's Method of Infinite Descent**: to prove that no natural number $n \in \mathbb{N}$ can satisfy a "bad" property (or have a non-trivial solution to an equation like $m^2 = 2n^2$), you consider the set $E \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ of all counterexamples. If $E$ were non-empty, the Well-Ordering Principle guarantees that $E$ would possess a **smallest counterexample** $m_0 \in E$. You then use algebra to construct an even smaller counterexample $m_1 \in E$ with $m_1 < m_0$, contradicting the minimality of $m_0$ and proving $E = \emptyset$!

## 0.2 Exercises

### Problem 0.2.1: Logical Independence of the Peano Axioms via Pathological Models

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> To see why none of the Peano Axioms can be omitted, construct an explicit set $X$, a distinguished element $0_X \in X$, and a function $S_X: X \to X$ for each of the following scenarios, and prove that your construction satisfies the required axioms while violating the target axiom:
> 1. **Failure of Axiom 3 Alone:** Construct a model $(X, 0_X, S_X)$ that satisfies Peano Axioms 1, 2, 4, and 5, but violates Axiom 3 (i.e., $S_X(n) = 0_X$ for some $n \in X$).
> 2. **Failure of Axiom 4 Alone:** Construct a model $(X, 0_X, S_X)$ that satisfies Peano Axioms 1, 2, 3, and 5, but violates Axiom 4 (i.e., $S_X$ is not injective).
> 3. **Failure of Axiom 5 (Induction) Alone:** Construct a model $(X, 0_X, S_X)$ that satisfies Peano Axioms 1, 2, 3, and 4, but violates the Principle of Mathematical Induction.

**Solution:**
**1. Failure of Axiom 3 Alone (A Finite Looping Clock):**
Let $X = \{0, 1\}$, choose the distinguished starting element $0_X = 0$, and define $S_X: X \to X$ by
$$
S_X(0) = 1 \quad \text{and} \quad S_X(1) = 0
$$
*(In fact, even the one-element set $X = \{0\}$ with $S_X(0) = 0$ works, or any cyclic clock $\{0, 1, \dots, k - 1\}$ with $S_X(n) = (n + 1) \bmod k$! Let's verify all the axioms for $X = \{0, 1\}$:)*
- **Axiom 1 ($0_X \in X$):** Clearly $0 \in \{0, 1\}$.
- **Axiom 2 (Closure under $S_X$):** For every $n \in X$, $S_X(n) \in \{0, 1\} = X$.
- **Violation of Axiom 3:** Since $S_X(1) = 0 = 0_X$, zero *is* the successor of $1$, so Axiom 3 fails.
- **Axiom 4 (Injectivity of $S_X$):** The two distinct elements $0 \neq 1$ have distinct outputs $S_X(0) = 1 \neq 0 = S_X(1)$, so $S_X(n) = S_X(m) \implies n = m$ holds.
- **Axiom 5 (Induction):** Suppose $P \subseteq X$ satisfies $0 \in P$ and $(n \in P \implies S_X(n) \in P)$. Since $0 \in P$, the inductive step with $n = 0$ forces $S_X(0) = 1 \in P$. Thus $\{0, 1\} \subseteq P$, so $P = X$. Hence Induction holds!

**2. Failure of Axiom 4 Alone (A Line Collapsing into a Loop):**
Let $X = \{0, 1\}$, choose $0_X = 0$, and define $S_X: X \to X$ by
$$
S_X(0) = 1 \quad \text{and} \quad S_X(1) = 1
$$
Visually, $0$ steps to $1$, and then $1$ loops onto itself forever ($0 \to 1 \to 1 \to \dots$). Let's check each axiom:
- **Axiom 1 ($0_X \in X$):** Clearly $0 \in \{0, 1\}$.
- **Axiom 2 (Closure under $S_X$):** Every output of $S_X$ is $1 \in X$.
- **Axiom 3 ($S_X(n) \neq 0_X$):** For every $n \in X$, $S_X(n) = 1 \neq 0$, so $0$ is never a successor! Thus Axiom 3 holds.
- **Violation of Axiom 4:** We have $S_X(0) = 1 = S_X(1)$ even though $0 \neq 1$. Thus $S_X$ is not injective, violating Axiom 4.
- **Axiom 5 (Induction):** Suppose $P \subseteq X$ contains $0$ and is closed under $S_X$. Since $0 \in P$, closure under $S_X$ forces $S_X(0) = 1 \in P$, so $P = \{0, 1\} = X$. Thus Induction holds!

**3. Failure of Axiom 5 Alone (Adding an Unreachable "Rogue" Chain):**
To satisfy Axioms 1–4 while breaking Induction, we need all the usual natural numbers $0, 1, 2, \dots$ plus some extra "unreachable" elements where $S_X$ is still injective and never hits $0_X$. A clean way to build this using two disjoint copies of $\mathbb{N}$ is to set
$$
X = \mathbb{N} \times \{0, 1\} = \{(n, 0) \mid n \in \mathbb{N}\} \cup \{(n, 1) \mid n \in \mathbb{N}\}
$$
Think of $X$ as two parallel infinite tracks: the "standard track" $\mathbb{N} \times \{0\}$ and a disconnected "rogue shadow track" $\mathbb{N} \times \{1\}$. Set the starting element to be $0_X = (0, 0)$ on the standard track, and define $S_X: X \to X$ to step forward along whichever track an element sits on:
$$
S_X(n, i) = (S(n), i) \quad \text{for all } n \in \mathbb{N} \text{ and } i \in \{0, 1\}
$$
(Notice that $(0, 1)$ on the rogue track has no predecessor under $S_X$, which is completely allowed by Peano Axioms 1–4! Remember, Axioms 1–4 do *not* require every non-zero element to have a predecessor—that was a theorem we proved *using* Axiom 5!) Let's check that $(X, 0_X, S_X)$ satisfies Axioms 1–4 while violating Axiom 5:
- **Axiom 1 ($0_X \in X$):** $0_X = (0, 0) \in \mathbb{N} \times \{0, 1\}$.
- **Axiom 2 (Closure under $S_X$):** For any $(n, i) \in X$, $S_X(n, i) = (S(n), i) \in \mathbb{N} \times \{0, 1\} = X$.
- **Axiom 3 ($S_X(x) \neq 0_X$):** For any $(n, i) \in X$, the first coordinate of $S_X(n, i) = (S(n), i)$ is $S(n) \neq 0$ (by Peano Axiom 3 on $\mathbb{N}$), so $(S(n), i) \neq (0, 0) = 0_X$.
- **Axiom 4 (Injectivity of $S_X$):** Suppose $S_X(n, i) = S_X(m, j)$, meaning $(S(n), i) = (S(m), j)$. Comparing coordinates gives $S(n) = S(m)$ and $i = j$. By Peano Axiom 4 on $\mathbb{N}$, $S(n) = S(m) \implies n = m$, so $(n, i) = (m, j)$. Thus $S_X$ is injective!
- **Violation of Axiom 5 (Induction):** Consider the subset $P = \mathbb{N} \times \{0\} \subsetneq X$ consisting only of the standard track.
  - *Base Case:* $0_X = (0, 0) \in P$.
  - *Inductive Step:* If $(n, 0) \in P$, then $S_X(n, 0) = (S(n), 0) \in P$.

  Thus $P$ satisfies both hypotheses of Mathematical Induction, yet $P \neq X$ because $(0, 1) \in X \setminus P$! (Alternatively, if you also want every element other than $0_X$ to have a predecessor, you can take the second component to be a single looping element $\omega$ with $S_X(\omega) = \omega$, setting $X = \mathbb{N} \cup \{\omega\}$, $0_X = 0$, $S_X(n) = S(n)$ for $n \in \mathbb{N}$, and $S_X(\omega) = \omega$; since $\omega \neq 0$ and $S(n) \neq \omega$, $S_X$ is still injective and never hits $0$, while $P = \mathbb{N} \subsetneq X$ still violates Induction!) $\blacksquare$

### Problem 0.2.2: Logical Equivalence of Induction, Strong Induction, and Well-Ordering

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $\mathbb{N}$ be equipped with $0$, $S$, addition, and the order $\le$. We proved in the text that the Principle of Mathematical Induction (Peano Axiom 5) implies the Well-Ordering Principle. Complete the logical equivalence cycle among the three major induction principles by proving:
> 1. **Induction $\implies$ Strong Induction:** Prove that if $Q(n)$ is a statement about natural numbers such that for every $n \in \mathbb{N}$,
>    $$
>    (\forall k \in \mathbb{N},\; k < n \implies Q(k)) \implies Q(n)
>    $$
>    then $Q(n)$ holds for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$. *(Explain why no separate base case $Q(0)$ needs to be stated in this formulation, though checking $n = 0$ still occurs automatically!)*
> 2. **Strong Induction $\implies$ Well-Ordering Principle:** Using Strong Induction directly, give a concise proof that every non-empty subset $E \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ has a least element.
> 3. **Well-Ordering Principle $\implies$ Induction:** Assuming Peano Axioms 1–4, the Existence of Predecessors ($n \neq 0 \implies \exists m \in \mathbb{N},\; S(m) = n$), the order properties of $\le$, and the Well-Ordering Principle on $\mathbb{N}$, prove that Peano Axiom 5 (Mathematical Induction) holds.

**Solution:**
**1. Proof that Induction $\implies$ Strong Induction:**
Suppose $Q(n)$ satisfies the Strong Induction hypothesis: for every $n \in \mathbb{N}$, if $Q(k)$ holds for all $k < n$, then $Q(n)$ holds. (Notice what happens when we plug $n = 0$ into this hypothesis: since there are *no* natural numbers $k \in \mathbb{N}$ satisfying $k < 0$, the premise "$\forall k < 0,\; Q(k)$" is **vacuously true**, so the hypothesis for $n = 0$ automatically requires that $Q(0)$ is true!)

To deduce that $Q(n)$ holds for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$ using ordinary Mathematical Induction (Peano Axiom 5), define the cumulative statement $P(n)$ by
$$
P(n): \quad \text{``For all } k \in \mathbb{N} \text{ with } k < n, \text{ the statement } Q(k) \text{ is true.''}
$$
Let's prove $P(n)$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$ by ordinary Induction:
- **Base Case ($n = 0$):** As noted above, there does not exist any $k \in \mathbb{N}$ with $k < 0$ (since $0 \le k$ for all $k \in \mathbb{N}$). Therefore, the statement $P(0)$ ("for all $k < 0$, $Q(k)$ holds") is vacuously true!
- **Inductive Step ($P(n) \implies P(S(n))$):** Suppose $P(n)$ holds for some $n \in \mathbb{N}$, meaning $Q(k)$ is true for all $k < n$. By the Strong Induction hypothesis applied at $n$, having $Q(k)$ true for all $k < n$ immediately implies that **$Q(n)$ is true**! Now take any $k \in \mathbb{N}$ with $k < S(n)$. Since there is no natural number strictly between $n$ and $S(n)$, having $k < S(n)$ means $k \le n$, so either $k < n$ (in which case $Q(k)$ holds by $P(n)$) or $k = n$ (in which case $Q(n)$ holds, as we just deduced). Thus $Q(k)$ holds for all $k < S(n)$, which is precisely $P(S(n))$!

By Peano Axiom 5, $P(n)$ holds for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$. In particular, for any $n \in \mathbb{N}$, since $n < S(n)$ and $P(S(n))$ holds, we conclude that $Q(n)$ is true!

**2. Proof that Strong Induction $\implies$ Well-Ordering Principle:**
Let $E \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ be a subset that has **no** least element; we want to show $E = \emptyset$. Let $Q(n)$ be the statement "$n \notin E$." Take any $n \in \mathbb{N}$, and assume that $Q(k)$ holds for all $k < n$ (meaning $k \notin E$ for all $k < n$). Could $n \in E$? By Trichotomy, since no element of $E$ is strictly less than $n$, every element $x \in E$ must satisfy $n \le x$. Thus, if $n$ belonged to $E$, then $n$ would be a least element of $E$, contradicting our assumption that $E$ has no least element! Hence $n \notin E$, i.e., $Q(n)$ holds. By Strong Induction, $Q(n)$ holds for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$, so $E = \emptyset$. Taking the contrapositive, every non-empty subset $E \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ has a least element.

**3. Proof that Well-Ordering Principle $\implies$ Induction:**
Suppose $P \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ is a subset satisfying:
- **Base Case:** $0 \in P$, and
- **Inductive Step:** For every $m \in \mathbb{N}$, if $m \in P$, then $S(m) \in P$.

We want to prove $P = \mathbb{N}$. Suppose for contradiction that $P \neq \mathbb{N}$, and consider the set of "counterexamples" (elements of $\mathbb{N}$ where $P$ fails):
$$
E := \mathbb{N} \setminus P = \{n \in \mathbb{N} \mid n \notin P\}
$$
Because $P \neq \mathbb{N}$, the subset $E \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ is **non-empty**. By the **Well-Ordering Principle**, $E$ possesses a least element $n_0 \in E$ (so $n_0 \notin P$, and $n_0 \le x$ for all $x \in E$).
- Could $n_0 = 0$? No, because $0 \in P$ by the Base Case, whereas $n_0 \in E = \mathbb{N} \setminus P$.
- Since $n_0 \neq 0$, the Existence of Predecessors guarantees that there exists $m \in \mathbb{N}$ such that $S(m) = n_0$.
- Because $m + S(0) = S(m) = n_0$, we have $m < S(m) = n_0$. Since $n_0$ is the *least* element of $E$ and $m < n_0$, the predecessor $m$ **cannot belong to $E$**!
- Because $m \in \mathbb{N}$ and $m \notin E = \mathbb{N} \setminus P$, we must have $m \in P$.
- Applying the Inductive Step of $P$ to $m \in P$ forces $S(m) \in P$, which means $n_0 \in P$!

This contradicts $n_0 \in E = \mathbb{N} \setminus P$. Therefore $E$ must be empty, which proves $P = \mathbb{N}$. $\blacksquare$

### Problem 0.2.3: Euclidean Division Algorithm from Peano Arithmetic

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Using only addition, multiplication, order, and the Well-Ordering Principle on $\mathbb{N}$ (without appealing to negative numbers, subtraction, or fractions), prove that for any $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$ with $b \neq 0$, there exist **unique** natural numbers $q, r \in \mathbb{N}$ (called the **quotient** and **remainder**, respectively) such that
> $$
> a = b \cdot q + r \quad \text{and} \quad 0 \le r < b
> $$

**Solution:**
In elementary school, you found the quotient $q$ and remainder $r$ when dividing $a$ by $b$ by repeatedly subtracting $b$ from $a$ until what remained was strictly less than $b$. Since we do not have subtraction in $\mathbb{N}$, how can we express the set of all possible remainders using only addition and multiplication?

**Part 1: Existence (via the Well-Ordering Principle).**
A natural number $r \in \mathbb{N}$ is a candidate remainder after pulling out some multiple $b \cdot q$ from $a$ precisely when $a = b \cdot q + r$ for some $q \in \mathbb{N}$. Define the set $E \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ of all such candidate remainders:
$$
E := \{r \in \mathbb{N} \mid \text{there exists } q \in \mathbb{N} \text{ such that } a = b \cdot q + r\}
$$
First, let's verify that $E$ is **non-empty**: if we choose $q = 0 \in \mathbb{N}$ and $r = a \in \mathbb{N}$, then
$$
b \cdot 0 + a = 0 + a = a
$$
so $a \in E$, and thus $E \neq \emptyset$.

Because $E$ is a non-empty subset of $\mathbb{N}$, the **Well-Ordering Principle** guarantees that $E$ has a **least element** $r \in E$. By definition of $E$, there exists $q \in \mathbb{N}$ such that
$$
a = b \cdot q + r
$$
We already know $0 \le r$ (since $0 \le x$ for all $x \in \mathbb{N}$). We must show that $r < b$.

Suppose for contradiction that $r < b$ is false. By the **Trichotomy Law**, since $r < b$ does not hold, we must have $b \le r$. By definition of $\le$ on $\mathbb{N}$, there exists $r' \in \mathbb{N}$ such that
$$
b + r' = r
$$
Let's substitute $r = b + r'$ into our equation $a = b \cdot q + r$ and use associativity of addition and the Recursive Step of multiplication:
$$
\begin{aligned}
a &= b \cdot q + (b + r') \\
  &= (b \cdot q + b) + r' \\
  &= b \cdot S(q) + r'
\end{aligned}
$$
Look at what this equation says: $a = b \cdot S(q) + r'$ with $S(q) \in \mathbb{N}$, which means **$r'$ also belongs to the set $E$**! However, since $b \neq 0$ and $r' + b = b + r' = r$, the definition of strict inequality gives
$$
r' < r
$$
Thus $r' \in E$ is strictly smaller than $r$, contradicting the fact that $r$ is the *least* element of $E$! Therefore $b \le r$ is impossible, so $0 \le r < b$, completing the proof of existence. *(Note: One can also prove existence directly by induction on $a$!)*

**Part 2: Uniqueness.**
Suppose $(q_1, r_1)$ and $(q_2, r_2)$ are two pairs of natural numbers satisfying
$$
a = b \cdot q_1 + r_1 \quad (0 \le r_1 < b) \qquad \text{and} \qquad a = b \cdot q_2 + r_2 \quad (0 \le r_2 < b)
$$
We want to prove $q_1 = q_2$ and $r_1 = r_2$ without using negative numbers. First, let's ask: could $q_1 \neq q_2$? By Trichotomy, if $q_1 \neq q_2$, then one of them is strictly smaller than the other; by symmetry, we may assume without loss of generality that $q_1 < q_2$.
- Since $q_1 < q_2$, there exists a non-zero natural number $k = S(m) \in \mathbb{N}$ such that $q_1 + k = q_2$ (with $k \ge 1$, meaning $1 + m = k$).
- Substituting $q_2 = q_1 + k$ into $b \cdot q_1 + r_1 = a = b \cdot q_2 + r_2$ and using Distributivity gives
  $$
  b \cdot q_1 + r_1 = b \cdot (q_1 + k) + r_2 = b \cdot q_1 + (b \cdot k + r_2)
  $$
- By the **Additive Cancellation Law**, we cancel $b \cdot q_1$ from both sides to obtain
  $$
  r_1 = b \cdot k + r_2
  $$
- Now since $k = S(m)$, we have $b \cdot k = b \cdot S(m) = b \cdot m + b$, so
  $$
  r_1 = b + (b \cdot m + r_2)
  $$
- By definition of $\le$, this says $b \le r_1$! But by Trichotomy, $b \le r_1$ directly contradicts our hypothesis that $r_1 < b$!

Thus $q_1 < q_2$ (and similarly $q_2 < q_1$) is impossible, so Trichotomy forces
$$
q_1 = q_2
$$
Finally, substituting $q_1 = q_2$ into $b \cdot q_1 + r_1 = b \cdot q_2 + r_2$ gives $b \cdot q_1 + r_1 = b \cdot q_1 + r_2$, and cancelling $b \cdot q_1$ via the Additive Cancellation Law yields $r_1 = r_2$. $\blacksquare$

### Problem 0.2.4: Categoricity of the Peano Axioms (Uniqueness of $\mathbb{N}$ Up to Canonical Isomorphism)

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> At the start of Section 0.2, we saw one set-theoretic construction of the natural numbers (Von Neumann ordinals: $0 = \emptyset$, $S(n) = n \cup \{n\}$), while Ernst Zermelo proposed a different construction ($0 = \emptyset$, $S(n) = \{n\}$). Why doesn't it matter which construction we use, as long as it satisfies the five Peano Axioms?
>
> Call any triple $(N', 0', S')$ consisting of a set $N'$, an element $0' \in N'$, and a function $S': N' \to N'$ a **Peano system** if it satisfies all five Peano Axioms (with $N'$, $0'$, and $S'$ in place of $\mathbb{N}$, $0$, and $S$). Let $(\mathbb{N}, 0, S)$ and $(N', 0', S')$ be any two Peano systems, with addition ($+$, $+'$), multiplication ($\cdot$, $\cdot'$), and order ($\le$, $\le'$) defined recursively on each system. Prove Dedekind's **Categoricity Theorem**:
> 1. **Existence and Uniqueness of the Successor-Preserving Map:** Using the Recursion Theorem, prove that there exists a **unique** function $\Phi: \mathbb{N} \to N'$ such that
>    $$
>    \Phi(0) = 0' \quad \text{and} \quad \Phi(S(n)) = S'(\Phi(n)) \quad \text{for all } n \in \mathbb{N}
>    $$
> 2. **Bijectivity (Canonical Isomorphism):** By applying the Recursion Theorem in the reverse direction from $(N', 0', S')$ to $(\mathbb{N}, 0, S)$ (or by direct induction on $\mathbb{N}$ and $N'$), prove that $\Phi: \mathbb{N} \to N'$ is a **bijection**.
> 3. **Automatic Preservation of Arithmetic and Order:** Prove that for all $n, m \in \mathbb{N}$, the map $\Phi$ automatically preserves addition, multiplication, and order:
>    $$
>    \begin{aligned}
>    \Phi(n + m) &= \Phi(n) +' \Phi(m) \\
>    \Phi(n \cdot m) &= \Phi(n) \cdot' \Phi(m) \\
>    n \le m &\iff \Phi(n) \le' \Phi(m)
>    \end{aligned}
>    $$

**Solution:**
In Linear Algebra, two vector spaces $V$ and $W$ are called *isomorphic* if there is a bijection $\Phi: V \to W$ that preserves vector addition and scalar multiplication—meaning $V$ and $W$ are algebraically identical up to relabeling their elements. Dedekind's Categoricity Theorem shows something even stronger for the Peano Axioms: any two Peano systems are isomorphic via a **unique** relabeling map $\Phi$!

**1. Existence and Uniqueness of $\Phi: \mathbb{N} \to N'$:**
Apply the **Recursion Theorem on $\mathbb{N}$** with target set $X = N'$, starting element $a = 0' \in N'$, and stepping function $g: N' \to N'$ given by $g(y) = S'(y)$ (i.e., $G(n, y) = S'(y)$). The Recursion Theorem immediately guarantees the existence of a **unique** function $\Phi: \mathbb{N} \to N'$ satisfying:
- **Base Step:** $\Phi(0) = 0'$, and
- **Recursive Step:** $\Phi(S(n)) = S'(\Phi(n))$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$.

**2. Proof that $\Phi$ is a Bijection:**
Because $(N', 0', S')$ is *also* a Peano system (meaning it satisfies all five Peano Axioms, and therefore the Recursion Theorem holds on $N'$ by the exact same proof as on $\mathbb{N}$!), we can apply the Recursion Theorem on $N'$ with target set $\mathbb{N}$, starting element $0 \in \mathbb{N}$, and stepping function $S: \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N}$. This gives a unique function $\Psi: N' \to \mathbb{N}$ satisfying
$$
\Psi(0') = 0 \quad \text{and} \quad \Psi(S'(y)) = S(\Psi(y)) \quad \text{for all } y \in N'
$$
Let's show that $\Psi \circ \Phi = \mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{N}}$ and $\Phi \circ \Psi = \mathrm{id}_{N'}$ (so $\Phi$ and $\Psi$ are two-sided inverses of each other!).
- Consider the composite map $h := \Psi \circ \Phi: \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N}$. Let's check how $h$ behaves on $0$ and $S(n)$:
  $$
  \begin{aligned}
  h(0) &= \Psi(\Phi(0)) = \Psi(0') = 0 \\
  h(S(n)) &= \Psi(\Phi(S(n))) = \Psi(S'(\Phi(n))) = S(\Psi(\Phi(n))) = S(h(n))
  \end{aligned}
  $$
  Notice that the identity function $\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{N}}: \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N}$ defined by $\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{N}}(n) = n$ *also* satisfies $\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{N}}(0) = 0$ and $\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{N}}(S(n)) = S(n) = S(\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{N}}(n))$! By the **uniqueness** clause of the Recursion Theorem on $\mathbb{N}$ (or by an immediate two-line induction on $n \in \mathbb{N}$), we must have $h = \mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{N}}$, i.e., $\Psi(\Phi(n)) = n$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$.
- By complete symmetry, the composite map $\Phi \circ \Psi: N' \to N'$ satisfies $(\Phi \circ \Psi)(0') = 0'$ and $(\Phi \circ \Psi)(S'(y)) = S'((\Phi \circ \Psi)(y))$, so by uniqueness on $N'$ (or induction on $N'$), $\Phi(\Psi(y)) = y$ for all $y \in N'$.

Since $\Phi$ has a two-sided inverse $\Psi$, Theorem 0.1 (Characterization of Invertible Functions) guarantees that $\Phi: \mathbb{N} \to N'$ is a **bijection**!

**3. Preservation of Addition, Multiplication, and Order:**
- **Addition ($\Phi(n + m) = \Phi(n) +' \Phi(m)$):** Fix $n \in \mathbb{N}$ and induct on $m \in \mathbb{N}$.
  - *Base Case ($m = 0$):* Using the Base Step of addition in both $\mathbb{N}$ and $N'$ along with $\Phi(0) = 0'$,
    $$
    \Phi(n + 0) = \Phi(n) = \Phi(n) +' 0' = \Phi(n) +' \Phi(0)
    $$
  - *Inductive Step:* Suppose $\Phi(n + m) = \Phi(n) +' \Phi(m)$ holds for some $m \in \mathbb{N}$. Using the Recursive Step of addition in both systems and the property $\Phi(S(k)) = S'(\Phi(k))$:
    $$
    \begin{aligned}
    \Phi(n + S(m)) &= \Phi(S(n + m)) && \text{(by Recursive Step of } + \text{ on } \mathbb{N}\text{)} \\
                   &= S'(\Phi(n + m)) && \text{(since } \Phi \circ S = S' \circ \Phi\text{)} \\
                   &= S'(\Phi(n) +' \Phi(m)) && \text{(by the inductive hypothesis)} \\
                   &= \Phi(n) +' S'(\Phi(m)) && \text{(by Recursive Step of } +' \text{ on } N'\text{)} \\
                   &= \Phi(n) +' \Phi(S(m)) && \text{(since } S'(\Phi(m)) = \Phi(S(m))\text{)}
    \end{aligned}
    $$
    By Induction, $\Phi(n + m) = \Phi(n) +' \Phi(m)$ for all $n, m \in \mathbb{N}$.

- **Multiplication ($\Phi(n \cdot m) = \Phi(n) \cdot' \Phi(m)$):** Fix $n \in \mathbb{N}$ and induct on $m \in \mathbb{N}$.
  - *Base Case ($m = 0$):* Using the Base Step of multiplication in both systems and $\Phi(0) = 0'$,
    $$
    \Phi(n \cdot 0) = \Phi(0) = 0' = \Phi(n) \cdot' 0' = \Phi(n) \cdot' \Phi(0)
    $$
  - *Inductive Step:* Suppose $\Phi(n \cdot m) = \Phi(n) \cdot' \Phi(m)$ holds for some $m \in \mathbb{N}$. Using the Recursive Step of multiplication in both systems and the fact that $\Phi$ preserves addition:
    $$
    \begin{aligned}
    \Phi(n \cdot S(m)) &= \Phi((n \cdot m) + n) && \text{(by Recursive Step of } \cdot \text{ on } \mathbb{N}\text{)} \\
                       &= \Phi(n \cdot m) +' \Phi(n) && \text{(since } \Phi \text{ preserves addition)} \\
                       &= (\Phi(n) \cdot' \Phi(m)) +' \Phi(n) && \text{(by the inductive hypothesis)} \\
                       &= \Phi(n) \cdot' S'(\Phi(m)) && \text{(by Recursive Step of } \cdot' \text{ on } N'\text{)} \\
                       &= \Phi(n) \cdot' \Phi(S(m)) && \text{(since } S'(\Phi(m)) = \Phi(S(m))\text{)}
    \end{aligned}
    $$
    By Induction, $\Phi(n \cdot m) = \Phi(n) \cdot' \Phi(m)$ for all $n, m \in \mathbb{N}$.

- **Order ($n \le m \iff \Phi(n) \le' \Phi(m)$):**
  - $(\implies)$ If $n \le m$, then there exists $k \in \mathbb{N}$ such that $n + k = m$. Applying $\Phi$ and using preservation of addition gives
    $$
    \Phi(n) +' \Phi(k) = \Phi(n + k) = \Phi(m)
    $$
    Since $\Phi(k) \in N'$, this says by definition of $\le'$ on $N'$ that $\Phi(n) \le' \Phi(m)$.
  - $(\impliedby)$ Conversely, suppose $\Phi(n) \le' \Phi(m)$, so there exists $y \in N'$ such that $\Phi(n) +' y = \Phi(m)$. Because $\Phi: \mathbb{N} \to N'$ is surjective (Part 2), there exists $k \in \mathbb{N}$ such that $\Phi(k) = y$. Then
    $$
    \Phi(n + k) = \Phi(n) +' \Phi(k) = \Phi(n) +' y = \Phi(m)
    $$
    Because $\Phi$ is injective (Part 2), $\Phi(n + k) = \Phi(m)$ forces $n + k = m$, which means $n \le m$. $\blacksquare$

### Problem 0.2.5: Recursive Definition of Exponentiation, Its Algebraic Laws, and Fermat's Method of Infinite Descent

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Just as multiplication is repeated addition, **exponentiation** $a^b$ on $\mathbb{N}$ is repeated multiplication: for any fixed base $a \in \mathbb{N}$, we define $a^b$ recursively on the exponent $b \in \mathbb{N}$ by setting $1 := S(0)$ and declaring:
> 1. **Base Step:** $a^0 := 1$. *(Notice that this defines $0^0 = 1$ in Peano arithmetic, which makes all recursive identities hold without exception at $0$!)*
> 2. **Recursive Step:** $a^{S(b)} := a^b \cdot a$ for all $b \in \mathbb{N}$.
>
> Using this recursive definition and the properties of Peano arithmetic established in Section 0.2, prove the following results:
> 1. **The Three Laws of Exponents:** Prove by induction that for all $a, b, c \in \mathbb{N}$:
>    - *Product of Powers:* $a^{b + c} = a^b \cdot a^c$.
>    - *Power of a Power:* $(a^b)^c = a^{b \cdot c}$.
>    - *Power of a Product:* $(a \cdot b)^c = a^c \cdot b^c$.
> 2. **Parity of Squares in $\mathbb{N}$:** Call a natural number $n \in \mathbb{N}$ **even** if $n = 2q$ for some $q \in \mathbb{N}$, and **odd** if $n = 2q + 1$ for some $q \in \mathbb{N}$ (where $2 := S(1)$). Using the Euclidean Division Algorithm (Problem 0.2.3) with $b = 2$, prove that every natural number $n \in \mathbb{N}$ is either even or odd (never both), and prove that $n^2$ is even if and only if $n$ is even.
> 3. **Fermat's Method of Infinite Descent ($m^2 = 2n^2$ Has No Non-Zero Solutions):** Using the Well-Ordering Principle on $\mathbb{N}$, prove that there do **not** exist any non-zero natural numbers $m, n \in \mathbb{N}$ ($m \neq 0, n \neq 0$) such that
>    $$
>    m^2 = 2 n^2
>    $$
>    *(In Section 0.4, this exact result inside $\mathbb{N}$ will immediately prove that $\sqrt{2}$ is not a rational number!)*

**Solution:**
**1. Proof of the Three Laws of Exponents:**
First, note a quick helper identity for $1^c$: by induction on $c$, $1^0 = 1$, and if $1^c = 1$, then $1^{S(c)} = 1^c \cdot 1 = 1 \cdot 1 = 1$, so $1^c = 1$ for all $c \in \mathbb{N}$. Also, $a^1 = a^{S(0)} = a^0 \cdot a = 1 \cdot a = a$.

- **Product of Powers ($a^{b + c} = a^b \cdot a^c$):** Fix $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$ and induct on $c \in \mathbb{N}$.
  - *Base Case ($c = 0$):* Since $b + 0 = b$ and $a^0 = 1$, we have
    $$
    a^{b + 0} = a^b = a^b \cdot 1 = a^b \cdot a^0
    $$
  - *Inductive Step:* Suppose $a^{b + c} = a^b \cdot a^c$ holds for some $c \in \mathbb{N}$. Then for $S(c)$, using the Recursive Steps of addition and exponentiation along with associativity of multiplication:
    $$
    \begin{aligned}
    a^{b + S(c)} &= a^{S(b + c)} && \text{(by the Recursive Step of addition)} \\
                 &= a^{b + c} \cdot a && \text{(by the Recursive Step of exponentiation)} \\
                 &= (a^b \cdot a^c) \cdot a && \text{(by the inductive hypothesis)} \\
                 &= a^b \cdot (a^c \cdot a) && \text{(by associativity of multiplication)} \\
                 &= a^b \cdot a^{S(c)} && \text{(by the Recursive Step of exponentiation)}
    \end{aligned}
    $$
    By Induction, $a^{b + c} = a^b \cdot a^c$ holds for all $a, b, c \in \mathbb{N}$.

- **Power of a Power ($(a^b)^c = a^{b \cdot c}$):** Fix $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$ and induct on $c \in \mathbb{N}$.
  - *Base Case ($c = 0$):* Since $b \cdot 0 = 0$, we have
    $$
    (a^b)^0 = 1 = a^0 = a^{b \cdot 0}
    $$
  - *Inductive Step:* Suppose $(a^b)^c = a^{b \cdot c}$ holds for some $c \in \mathbb{N}$. Using the Recursive Steps of exponentiation and multiplication together with the Product of Powers law we just proved:
    $$
    \begin{aligned}
    (a^b)^{S(c)} &= (a^b)^c \cdot a^b && \text{(by the Recursive Step of exponentiation)} \\
                 &= a^{b \cdot c} \cdot a^b && \text{(by the inductive hypothesis)} \\
                 &= a^{(b \cdot c) + b} && \text{(by the Product of Powers law)} \\
                 &= a^{b \cdot S(c)} && \text{(by the Recursive Step of multiplication)}
    \end{aligned}
    $$
    By Induction, $(a^b)^c = a^{b \cdot c}$ holds for all $a, b, c \in \mathbb{N}$.

- **Power of a Product ($(a \cdot b)^c = a^c \cdot b^c$):** Fix $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$ and induct on $c \in \mathbb{N}$.
  - *Base Case ($c = 0$):* $(a \cdot b)^0 = 1 = 1 \cdot 1 = a^0 \cdot b^0$.
  - *Inductive Step:* Suppose $(a \cdot b)^c = a^c \cdot b^c$ holds for some $c \in \mathbb{N}$. Using the Recursive Step of exponentiation together with associativity and commutativity of multiplication:
    $$
    \begin{aligned}
    (a \cdot b)^{S(c)} &= (a \cdot b)^c \cdot (a \cdot b) && \text{(by the Recursive Step of exponentiation)} \\
                       &= (a^c \cdot b^c) \cdot (a \cdot b) && \text{(by the inductive hypothesis)} \\
                       &= a^c \cdot (b^c \cdot (a \cdot b)) && \text{(by associativity of multiplication)} \\
                       &= a^c \cdot ((b^c \cdot a) \cdot b) && \text{(by associativity of multiplication)} \\
                       &= a^c \cdot ((a \cdot b^c) \cdot b) && \text{(by commutativity of multiplication, } b^c \cdot a = a \cdot b^c\text{)} \\
                       &= (a^c \cdot a) \cdot (b^c \cdot b) && \text{(by associativity of multiplication)} \\
                       &= a^{S(c)} \cdot b^{S(c)} && \text{(by the Recursive Step of exponentiation)}
    \end{aligned}
    $$
    By Induction, $(a \cdot b)^c = a^c \cdot b^c$ holds for all $a, b, c \in \mathbb{N}$.

**2. Parity of Squares in $\mathbb{N}$:**
Applying the Euclidean Division Algorithm (Problem 0.2.3) to any $n \in \mathbb{N}$ with divisor $b = 2 = S(1) \neq 0$, there exist unique $q, r \in \mathbb{N}$ such that $n = 2q + r$ and $0 \le r < 2 = S(1)$. Since there is no natural number strictly between $0$ and $1 = S(0)$ or strictly between $1$ and $2 = S(1)$, the condition $0 \le r < S(1)$ means $r \le 1$, which leaves only two possibilities: $r = 0$ (so $n = 2q$ is **even**) or $r = 1$ (so $n = 2q + 1$ is **odd**). Uniqueness of the remainder $r$ in Problem 0.2.3 ensures that no natural number can be both even and odd!

Now let's relate the parity of $n^2 = n^{S(1)} = n^1 \cdot n = n \cdot n$ to the parity of $n$:
- $(\impliedby)$ If $n$ is even, then $n = 2q$ for some $q \in \mathbb{N}$. Then
  $$
  n^2 = (2q)^2 = 2^2 \cdot q^2 = (2 \cdot 2) q^2 = 2(2q^2)
  $$
  Since $2q^2 \in \mathbb{N}$, $n^2$ is even.
- $(\implies)$ To prove that "$n^2$ is even $\implies n$ is even," we prove the contrapositive: suppose $n$ is *not* even (so $n$ is odd), and let's show $n^2$ is odd (and hence not even).
