Since $n$ is odd, $n = 2q + 1$ for some $q \in \mathbb{N}$. Expanding $n^2 = (2q + 1) \cdot (2q + 1)$ using the distributive, associative, and commutative laws of addition and multiplication gives
  $$
  \begin{aligned}
  n^2 &= (2q + 1)(2q + 1) \\
      &= (2q + 1)(2q) + (2q + 1) \cdot 1 \\
      &= 2(2q^2 + q) + 2q + 1 \\
      &= 2(2q^2 + 2q) + 1
  \end{aligned}
  $$
  Because $2q^2 + 2q \in \mathbb{N}$, this expresses $n^2$ in the form $2k + 1$, so $n^2$ is odd (and by uniqueness of remainder cannot be even!). Taking the contrapositive shows that if $n^2$ is even, then $n$ must be even.

**3. Fermat's Method of Infinite Descent for $m^2 = 2n^2$:**
Suppose for contradiction that there exist non-zero natural numbers $m, n \in \mathbb{N}$ ($m \neq 0, n \neq 0$) satisfying $m^2 = 2n^2$. Define $E \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ to be the set of all non-zero natural numbers $m$ that appear on the left-hand side of such a relation:
$$
E := \{m \in \mathbb{N} \mid m \neq 0 \text{ and there exists } n \in \mathbb{N} \text{ with } n \neq 0 \text{ such that } m^2 = 2n^2\}
$$
Under our contradiction hypothesis, $E$ is a **non-empty** subset of $\mathbb{N}$. By the **Well-Ordering Principle**, $E$ has a **least element** $m_0 \in E$. By definition of $E$, $m_0 \neq 0$ and there exists some $n_0 \in \mathbb{N}$ with $n_0 \neq 0$ such that
$$
m_0^2 = 2 n_0^2
$$
Now watch Fermat's infinite descent in action:
1. Because $m_0^2 = 2(n_0^2)$, the number $m_0^2$ is **even**.
2. By Part 2, since $m_0^2$ is even, **$m_0$ itself must be even**! Thus there exists $m_1 \in \mathbb{N}$ such that
   $$
   m_0 = 2 m_1
   $$
   Notice that since $m_0 \neq 0$, we must have $m_1 \neq 0$ (as $2 \cdot 0 = 0$).
3. Substituting $m_0 = 2m_1$ into $m_0^2 = 2n_0^2$ gives
   $$
   2 n_0^2 = (2m_1)^2 = (2 \cdot 2) m_1^2 = 2(2m_1^2)
   $$
4. Since $2 = S(1) \neq 0$, we can apply the **Multiplicative Cancellation Law** to cancel the factor of $2$ from both sides:
   $$
   n_0^2 = 2 m_1^2
   $$
5. Look at the equation $n_0^2 = 2 m_1^2$: since $n_0 \neq 0$ and $m_1 \neq 0$, this equation says that **$n_0$ also belongs to the set $E$**! (In fact, repeating one more step shows $n_0 = 2n_1$ gives $m_1^2 = 2n_1^2$, so $m_1 \in E$ as well!)
6. Let's compare $n_0 \in E$ (or $m_1 \in E$) with our supposed minimum element $m_0 \in E$: since $m_0 = 2m_1 = m_1 + m_1$ and $m_1 \neq 0$, the definition of strict inequality ($a + k = b$ with $k \neq 0$) immediately gives $m_1 < m_0$. Even more directly, in $n_0^2 = 2m_1^2$ and $m_0^2 = 2n_0^2 = n_0^2 + n_0^2$, since $n_0 \neq 0$ implies $n_0^2 \neq 0$ (No Zero Divisors), we have $n_0^2 < m_0^2$. By Trichotomy and multiplicative compatibility of order ($x \le y \implies x^2 \le y^2$), having $n_0^2 < m_0^2$ forces
   $$
   n_0 < m_0
   $$

Thus $n_0 \in E$ is a member of $E$ that is **strictly smaller** than the least element $m_0 \in E$—an outright contradiction! Therefore $E = \emptyset$, proving that no non-zero natural numbers $m, n \in \mathbb{N}$ can satisfy $m^2 = 2n^2$. $\blacksquare$

---

# 0.3 Constructing the Integers from the Naturals

## Why Extend the Natural Numbers?

The natural numbers $(\mathbb{N}, +, \cdot, \le)$ constructed in Section 0.2 form a remarkably clean algebraic system, yet they suffer from a glaring algebraic defect: **you cannot always subtract**. Given two natural numbers $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$, ask yourself when the linear equation
$$
b + x = a
$$
can be solved for $x \in \mathbb{N}$. As we saw when defining order on $\mathbb{N}$, a solution $x \in \mathbb{N}$ exists if and only if $b \le a$. The moment $a < b$—for instance, in the innocent-looking equation $5 + x = 2$—no natural number $x$ can ever make the equation true, because adding $x \in \mathbb{N}$ to $5$ only produces numbers greater than or equal to $5$.

In Linear Algebra, you saw how useful it is for every vector $\mathbf{v}$ in a vector space to have an **additive inverse** $-\mathbf{v}$ satisfying $\mathbf{v} + (-\mathbf{v}) = \mathbf{0}$. Having additive inverses lets us solve any vector equation $\mathbf{b} + \mathbf{x} = \mathbf{a}$ effortlessly by adding $-\mathbf{b}$ to both sides: $\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{a} + (-\mathbf{b})$. In $\mathbb{N}$, only $0$ has an additive inverse (since $a + b = 0$ in $\mathbb{N}$ forces $a = 0$ and $b = 0$).

To cure this defect, we want to enlarge $\mathbb{N}$ into a richer number system—the **integers** $\mathbb{Z}$—by adjoining formal differences $a - b$ for *all* pairs of natural numbers $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$, thereby guaranteeing that every element has an additive inverse and every equation $b + x = a$ has a unique solution.

## Equivalence Classes of Formal Differences

Wait a second: how can we define a new number as "$a - b$" when the minus sign "$-$" and negative numbers do not exist yet? Writing $2 - 5 = -3$ would be circular if our goal is to *construct* $-3$ and subtraction from scratch using only $\mathbb{N}$!

Here is where the set-theoretic toolkit from Section 0.1 rescues us. Even though the operation $a - b$ does not exist in $\mathbb{N}$, the **ordered pair** $(a, b) \in \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$ is a completely legal set-theoretic object! We can think of the ordered pair $(a, b)$ as a *formal difference*—a bookkeeping record that stands for our *intended* difference $a - b$:
- **Financial Analogy:** Think of $(a, b)$ as an account ledger recording **$a$ dollars in cash (assets)** and **$b$ dollars in debt (liabilities)**. Even if a person only knows how to count non-negative whole dollars in $\mathbb{N}$, they can still write down a pair $(a, b)$ of natural numbers on a ledger.
- **Linear Algebra Analogy:** Recall how a displacement vector in $\mathbb{R}^n$ is determined by an ordered pair of points $(P, Q)$, representing the arrow from tail $Q$ to head $P$ (which we write as $P - Q$). Many different pairs of points $(P, Q)$ and $(P', Q')$ represent the exact same displacement vector if one arrow is just a parallel translation of the other.

Similarly, in our ledger analogy, many different pairs $(a, b) \in \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$ represent the exact same net balance! Having $\$5$ in cash and $\$2$ in debt gives the exact same net worth as having $\$3$ in cash and $\$0$ in debt, while having $\$2$ in cash and $\$5$ in debt represents the same net worth as having $\$0$ in cash and $\$3$ in debt, or $\$10$ in cash and $\$13$ in debt.

How do we tell when two pairs $(a, b)$ and $(c, d)$ represent the same intended difference *without* using subtraction? Let's do a quick scratchpad calculation in the future world where subtraction already exists:
$$
a - b = c - d \iff a + d = b + c
$$
Look closely at the equation on the right-hand side:
$$
a + d = b + c
$$
Every single symbol in $a + d = b + c$ uses **only addition of natural numbers**, which we have already constructed and proved rigorous in Section 0.2! No subtraction, no negative numbers, no circularity. Just as we bundled parallel arrows into a single vector in Linear Algebra, we now bundle all pairs $(a, b)$ that represent the same net difference into a single **equivalence class** using the relation $(a, b) \sim (c, d) \iff a + d = b + c$.

> [!abstract] Definition: The Equivalence Relation for Integers
> Define a binary relation $\sim$ on $\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$ by declaring that for any $(a, b), (c, d) \in \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$:
> $$
> (a, b) \sim (c, d) \iff a + d = b + c
> $$
> The set of **integers**, denoted $\mathbb{Z}$, is the quotient set of $\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$ by this equivalence relation:
> $$
> \mathbb{Z} := (\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}) / {\sim}
> $$
> For brevity, we write $[a, b]$ instead of $[(a, b)]$ for the equivalence class of the ordered pair $(a, b)$ in $\mathbb{Z}$:
> $$
> [a, b] := \{(c, d) \in \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N} : a + d = b + c\}
> $$

In plain English, an integer is not a single pair $(a, b)$, but rather the *entire infinite family* $[a, b]$ of all pairs $(c, d) \in \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$ that maintain the same net balance as $(a, b)$. Before we have the right to call $\mathbb{Z}$ a quotient set, we must verify that $\sim$ genuinely satisfies the three axioms of an equivalence relation from Section 0.1.

> [!theorem] Equivalence of Formal Differences
> The relation $\sim$ on $\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$ defined by $(a, b) \sim (c, d) \iff a + d = b + c$ is an equivalence relation.

**Proof:**
Let $(a, b), (c, d), (e, f) \in \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$. We check reflexivity, symmetry, and transitivity using the algebraic laws of $\mathbb{N}$ established in Section 0.2:
1. **Reflexivity:** By the commutativity of addition in $\mathbb{N}$, we have $a + b = b + a$. By the definition of $\sim$, this means $(a, b) \sim (a, b)$.
2. **Symmetry:** Suppose $(a, b) \sim (c, d)$, which means $a + d = b + c$ in $\mathbb{N}$. Since equality in $\mathbb{N}$ is symmetric and addition is commutative, we can rewrite this as $c + b = d + a$, which is the exact condition for $(c, d) \sim (a, b)$.
3. **Transitivity:** Suppose $(a, b) \sim (c, d)$ and $(c, d) \sim (e, f)$. Unpacking the definition of $\sim$ gives two equations in $\mathbb{N}$:
   $$
   a + d = b + c \quad \text{and} \quad c + f = d + e
   $$
   We want to show that $(a, b) \sim (e, f)$, i.e., $a + f = b + e$. Notice that $c$ and $d$ act as "middlemen" linking $(a, b)$ to $(e, f)$. Adding the two equations together in $\mathbb{N}$ and rearranging terms via associativity and commutativity of addition in $\mathbb{N}$ gives:
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   (a + d) + (c + f) &= (b + c) + (d + e) \\
   (a + f) + (c + d) &= (b + e) + (c + d)
   \end{aligned}
   $$
   Now look at the term $(c + d)$ appearing on both sides. Even though we cannot subtract $(c + d)$ in $\mathbb{N}$, Section 0.2 gave us the **Additive Cancellation Law** in $\mathbb{N}$ ($x + z = y + z \implies x = y$)! Canceling $(c + d)$ from both sides yields
   $$
   a + f = b + e
   $$
   which proves that $(a, b) \sim (e, f)$. $\blacksquare$

> [!example] Example: Visualizing Equivalence Classes in $\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$
> List three explicit ordered-pair representatives belonging to the equivalence class $[5, 2]$, three belonging to $[2, 5]$, and three belonging to $[0, 0]$. How do these equivalence classes look geometrically if we plot $\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$ as a grid of points in the first quadrant?

**Solution:**
1. For $[5, 2]$ (which represents the intended integer $5 - 2 = +3$), a pair $(c, d)$ belongs to $[5, 2]$ if and only if $5 + d = 2 + c$, or $c = d + 3$. Choosing $d = 0, 1, 4$ gives the representatives $(3, 0), (4, 1), (7, 4) \in [5, 2]$. Indeed, $[5, 2] = \{(3, 0), (4, 1), (5, 2), (6, 3), \dots\}$.
2. For $[2, 5]$ (which represents the intended integer $2 - 5 = -3$), a pair $(c, d)$ belongs to $[2, 5]$ if and only if $2 + d = 5 + c$, or $d = c + 3$. Choosing $c = 0, 1, 4$ gives the representatives $(0, 3), (1, 4), (4, 7) \in [2, 5]$. Thus $[2, 5] = \{(0, 3), (1, 4), (2, 5), (3, 6), \dots\}$.
3. For $[0, 0]$ (which represents the intended integer $0 - 0 = 0$), $(c, d) \in [0, 0] \iff 0 + d = 0 + c \iff c = d$. Thus $[0, 0] = \{(0, 0), (1, 1), (2, 2), (3, 3), \dots\}$.

Geometrically, if we plot the pairs $(a, b) \in \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$ as lattice points in the plane, each equivalence class $[a, b]$ consists of all lattice points lying on a single **$45^\circ$ diagonal ray** (of slope $+1$) satisfying $b - a = \text{constant}$! Every diagonal ray hits either the horizontal axis at a unique point $(n, 0)$ (positive integers), the origin $(0, 0)$ (zero), or the vertical axis at a unique point $(0, n)$ (negative integers). $\blacksquare$

## Integer Arithmetic and Well-Definedness

Now that we have the set $\mathbb{Z} = (\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}) / {\sim}$, how should we define addition and multiplication of two equivalence classes $[a, b]$ and $[c, d]$?

To discover the right rules, let's return to our scratchpad and pretend for a moment that $[a, b]$ is $a - b$ and $[c, d]$ is $c - d$:
- **Intended Sum:** If we combine two accounts—one with $\$a$ cash and $\$b$ debt, and another with $\$c$ cash and $\$d$ debt—our total cash is $a + c$ and our total debt is $b + d$. Algebraically,
  $$
  (a - b) + (c - d) = (a + c) - (b + d)
  $$
  which corresponds to the ordered pair $(a + c, b + d)$.
- **Intended Product:** If we expand the product $(a - b)(c - d)$ using standard foil/distributivity rules on our scratchpad, we get
  $$
  (a - b)(c - d) = ac - ad - bc + bd = (ac + bd) - (ad + bc)
  $$
  which corresponds to the ordered pair $(ac + bd, ad + bc)$.

Notice the magic: the pairs $(a + c, b + d)$ and $(ac + bd, ad + bc)$ are built exclusively out of addition and multiplication of natural numbers! We now promote these scratchpad formulas into our official definitions.

> [!abstract] Definition: Addition and Multiplication on $\mathbb{Z}$
> For any two integers $[a, b], [c, d] \in \mathbb{Z}$, we define their **sum** and **product** by:
> $$
> \begin{aligned}
> [a, b] + [c, d] &:= [a + c, b + d] \\
> [a, b] \cdot [c, d] &:= [ac + bd, ad + bc]
> \end{aligned}
> $$

Hold on! Whenever we define an operation on equivalence classes by picking specific **representatives** $(a, b) \in [a, b]$ and $(c, d) \in [c, d]$ and computing a formula with those coordinates, we face a crucial logical obligation that we first met in Section 0.1: **Is the operation well-defined?**

### What Does "Well-Defined" Mean?

Suppose Alice wants to compute the product of the integer $-3$ and the integer $+2$. She represents $-3$ as the pair $(2, 5)$ and $+2$ as $(4, 2)$. Meanwhile, Bob represents the *exact same* integers $-3$ and $+2$ using different pairs from the same equivalence classes: he picks $(0, 3) \in [2, 5]$ and $(2, 0) \in [4, 2]$. If Alice's formula produces an equivalence class that is *different* from Bob's equivalence class, then our "definition" of multiplication is broken—it would depend on the arbitrary whim of which representative you picked rather than on the equivalence classes themselves!

To say that an operation on equivalence classes is **well-defined** means that **the output equivalence class is independent of the choice of input representatives**: whenever $(a, b) \sim (a', b')$ and $(c, d) \sim (c', d')$, the formula applied to $(a, b)$ and $(c, d)$ must land in the exact same equivalence class as the formula applied to $(a', b')$ and $(c', d')$. Let's prove that our definitions pass this test.

> [!theorem] Well-Definedness of Integer Arithmetic
> Addition and multiplication on $\mathbb{Z}$ are well-defined: if $[a, b] = [a', b']$ and $[c, d] = [c', d']$ in $\mathbb{Z}$, then
> $$
> [a + c, b + d] = [a' + c', b' + d'] \quad \text{and} \quad [ac + bd, ad + bc] = [a'c' + b'd', a'd' + b'c']
> $$

**Proof:**
Suppose $[a, b] = [a', b']$ and $[c, d] = [c', d']$, so $(a, b) \sim (a', b')$ and $(c, d) \sim (c', d')$. Unpacking the definition of $\sim$ gives two equations in $\mathbb{N}$:
$$
a + b' = b + a' \quad \text{and} \quad c + d' = d + c'
$$

**1. Well-Definedness of Addition:**
We must show that $(a + c, b + d) \sim (a' + c', b' + d')$, which by definition of $\sim$ means showing $(a + c) + (b' + d') = (b + d) + (a' + c')$. Adding our two hypothesis equations in $\mathbb{N}$ and regrouping terms using associativity and commutativity of addition in $\mathbb{N}$, we obtain:
$$
\begin{aligned}
(a + c) + (b' + d') &= (a + b') + (c + d') \\
                    &= (b + a') + (d + c') \\
                    &= (b + d) + (a' + c')
\end{aligned}
$$
Thus $(a + c, b + d) \sim (a' + c', b' + d')$, so $[a + c, b + d] = [a' + c', b' + d']$.

**2. Well-Definedness of Multiplication:**
We must show that $(ac + bd, ad + bc) \sim (a'c' + b'd', a'd' + b'c')$. Instead of changing both $(a, b)$ and $(c, d)$ simultaneously (which creates a messy tangle of cross-terms), we can change **one factor at a time**! First we show that replacing $(a, b)$ by $(a', b')$ while holding $(c, d)$ fixed gives
$$
(ac + bd, ad + bc) \sim (a'c + b'd, a'd + b'c)
$$
By the exact same argument (and commutativity of multiplication in $\mathbb{N}$), replacing $(c, d)$ by $(c', d')$ while holding $(a', b')$ fixed gives
$$
(a'c + b'd, a'd + b'c) \sim (a'c' + b'd', a'd' + b'c')
$$
Transitivity of $\sim$ will then immediately link the first pair to the third!

To verify $(ac + bd, ad + bc) \sim (a'c + b'd, a'd + b'c)$, we need to check that the outer/inner sums match:
$$
(ac + bd) + (a'd + b'c) = (ad + bc) + (a'c + b'd)
$$
Notice how we can factor $c$ and $d$ on the left-hand side so that $a + b'$ and $b + a'$ appear! Using distributivity, associativity, and commutativity in $\mathbb{N}$, together with the hypothesis $a + b' = b + a'$, we compute:
$$
\begin{aligned}
(ac + bd) + (a'd + b'c) &= (ac + b'c) + (bd + a'd) \\
                        &= (a + b')c + (b + a')d \\
                        &= (b + a')c + (a + b')d \\
                        &= (bc + a'c) + (ad + b'd) \\
                        &= (ad + bc) + (a'c + b'd)
\end{aligned}
$$
Therefore $(ac + bd, ad + bc) \sim (a'c + b'd, a'd + b'c)$, and by transitivity multiplication on $\mathbb{Z}$ is well-defined. $\blacksquare$

### Algebraic Structure: Commutative Rings and Integral Domains

What have we gained by moving from $\mathbb{N}$ to $\mathbb{Z}$? In $\mathbb{N}$, we couldn't reverse addition. In $\mathbb{Z}$, reversing an integer $[a, b]$ (cash $a$, debt $b$) is as simple as swapping its coordinates to $[b, a]$ (cash $b$, debt $a$)! Indeed, adding $[a, b]$ and $[b, a]$ yields:
$$
[a, b] + [b, a] = [a + b, b + a] = [0, 0]
$$
because $(a + b) + 0 = (b + a) + 0$ shows $(a + b, b + a) \sim (0, 0)$.

To describe the algebraic properties of number systems like $\mathbb{N}$, $\mathbb{Z}$, $\mathbb{Q}$, and $\mathbb{R}$ concisely, mathematicians use a few standard terms from abstract algebra. Since you may not have taken an abstract algebra course yet, let's introduce these terms clearly right now—they are just compact labels for familiar rules of arithmetic that you have used in Calculus and Linear Algebra:
- **Commutative Monoid:** A set $M$ equipped with an associative, commutative binary operation (say $+$) and a neutral identity element $0 \in M$ satisfying $x + 0 = x$ for all $x \in M$. For example, $(\mathbb{N}, +, 0)$ and $(\mathbb{N}, \cdot, 1)$ are commutative monoids. They do *not* require inverses.
- **Abelian Group (Commutative Group):** A commutative monoid $(G, +, 0)$ in which every element $x \in G$ also possesses an **inverse** $-x \in G$ satisfying $x + (-x) = 0$. ("Abelian", named after Niels Henrik Abel, simply means the operation is commutative: $x + y = y + x$.) Every vector space in Linear Algebra, such as $(\mathbb{R}^n, +, \mathbf{0})$, is an abelian group under vector addition!
- **Commutative Ring (with Identity):** A set $R$ equipped with *two* operations, addition ($+$) and multiplication ($\cdot$), such that:
  1. $(R, +, 0_R)$ is an **abelian group** (associative, commutative, additive identity $0_R$, and every $x$ has an additive inverse $-x$).
  2. $(R, \cdot, 1_R)$ is a **commutative monoid** (associative, commutative, multiplicative identity $1_R$).
  3. Multiplication **distributes** over addition: $x \cdot (y + z) = x \cdot y + x \cdot z$.
   Notice that in a ring, we have additive inverses (so we can always subtract), but we do *not* require multiplicative inverses (so we cannot necessarily divide!).
- **Zero Divisors and Integral Domains:** In linear algebra, you learned a shocking fact about $2 \times 2$ matrices: two non-zero matrices can multiply together to give the zero matrix! For example,
  $$
  \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{pmatrix} \begin{pmatrix} 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{pmatrix}
  $$
  Non-zero elements $x \neq 0$ and $y \neq 0$ whose product is $x \cdot y = 0$ are called **zero divisors**. When a ring has zero divisors, you cannot cancel a non-zero factor $z$ from an equation $xz = yz$! A commutative ring with $1_R \neq 0_R$ that has **no zero divisors**—meaning $x \cdot y = 0_R$ forces $x = 0_R$ or $y = 0_R$—is called an **integral domain** (named specifically because the integers $\mathbb{Z}$ are the prototype!).

Let's now state and prove that $(\mathbb{Z}, +, \cdot)$ is a commutative ring and an integral domain.

> [!theorem] Algebraic Structure of $\mathbb{Z}$ (Commutative Ring and Integral Domain)
> Define the additive identity $0_{\mathbb{Z}} := [0, 0]$, the multiplicative identity $1_{\mathbb{Z}} := [1, 0]$, and for each integer $x = [a, b] \in \mathbb{Z}$, define its **negation** (additive inverse) by $-x := [b, a]$. (Note that $-[a, b]$ is well-defined because $(a, b) \sim (a', b') \implies a + b' = b + a' \implies b + a' = a + b' \implies (b, a) \sim (b', a')$.) Then for all $x, y, z \in \mathbb{Z}$:
> 1. **Additive Abelian Group:**
>    - *Associativity of $+$:* $(x + y) + z = x + (y + z)$.
>    - *Commutativity of $+$:* $x + y = y + x$.
>    - *Additive Identity:* $x + 0_{\mathbb{Z}} = x$.
>    - *Additive Inverses:* $x + (-x) = 0_{\mathbb{Z}}$.
> 2. **Multiplicative Commutative Monoid & Distributivity:**
>    - *Associativity of $\cdot$:* $(x \cdot y) \cdot z = x \cdot (y \cdot z)$.
>    - *Commutativity of $\cdot$:* $x \cdot y = y \cdot x$.
>    - *Multiplicative Identity:* $x \cdot 1_{\mathbb{Z}} = x$, with $1_{\mathbb{Z}} \neq 0_{\mathbb{Z}}$.
>    - *Distributivity:* $x \cdot (y + z) = x \cdot y + x \cdot z$.
> 3. **No Zero Divisors (Integral Domain):** If $x \cdot y = 0_{\mathbb{Z}}$, then $x = 0_{\mathbb{Z}}$ or $y = 0_{\mathbb{Z}}$. Equivalently, **Multiplicative Cancellation** holds: if $x \cdot z = y \cdot z$ and $z \neq 0_{\mathbb{Z}}$, then $x = y$.

**Proof:**
Let $x = [a, b]$, $y = [c, d]$, and $z = [e, f]$ be arbitrary integers in $\mathbb{Z}$.
1. **Additive Abelian Group Laws:**
   - *Associativity and Commutativity of $+$:* By definition of addition on $\mathbb{Z}$ and the associativity and commutativity of addition in $\mathbb{N}$,
     $$
     \begin{aligned}
     (x + y) + z &= [(a + c) + e, (b + d) + f] = [a + (c + e), b + (d + f)] = x + (y + z) \\
     x + y &= [a + c, b + d] = [c + a, d + b] = y + x
     \end{aligned}
     $$
   - *Additive Identity:* $x + 0_{\mathbb{Z}} = [a, b] + [0, 0] = [a + 0, b + 0] = [a, b] = x$.
   - *Additive Inverses:* $x + (-x) = [a, b] + [b, a] = [a + b, b + a] = [0, 0] = 0_{\mathbb{Z}}$, since $(a + b) + 0 = (b + a) + 0$ in $\mathbb{N}$.

2. **Multiplicative Monoid Laws & Distributivity:**
   - *Commutativity of $\cdot$:* Using commutativity of $+$ and $\cdot$ in $\mathbb{N}$,
     $$
     x \cdot y = [ac + bd, ad + bc] = [ca + db, cb + da] = [ca + db, da + cb] = y \cdot x
     $$
   - *Associativity of $\cdot$:* Computing both groupings in $\mathbb{N}$ gives:
     $$
     \begin{aligned}
     (x \cdot y) \cdot z &= [ac + bd, ad + bc] \cdot [e, f] \\
                         &= [(ac + bd)e + (ad + bc)f, (ac + bd)f + (ad + bc)e] \\
                         &= [ace + bde + adf + bcf, acf + bdf + ade + bce] \\
                         &= [a(ce + df) + b(cf + de), a(cf + de) + b(ce + df)] \\
                         &= [a, b] \cdot [ce + df, cf + de] = x \cdot (y \cdot z)
     \end{aligned}
     $$
   - *Multiplicative Identity:* $x \cdot 1_{\mathbb{Z}} = [a, b] \cdot [1, 0] = [a \cdot 1 + b \cdot 0, a \cdot 0 + b \cdot 1] = [a, b] = x$. Moreover, $1_{\mathbb{Z}} = [1, 0] \neq [0, 0] = 0_{\mathbb{Z}}$ because $1 + 0 \neq 0 + 0$ in $\mathbb{N}$.
   - *Distributivity:* Using distributivity and commutativity in $\mathbb{N}$,
     $$
     \begin{aligned}
     x \cdot (y + z) &= [a, b] \cdot [c + e, d + f] \\
                     &= [a(c + e) + b(d + f), a(d + f) + b(c + e)] \\
                     &= [(ac + bd) + (ae + bf), (ad + bc) + (af + be)] \\
                     &= [ac + bd, ad + bc] + [ae + bf, af + be] = x \cdot y + x \cdot z
     \end{aligned}
     $$

3. **No Zero Divisors:** We will give a remarkably clean one-line proof of this in the next subsection right after establishing the canonical form of integers via Trichotomy, and we will also explore a direct coordinate proof in Problem 0.3.3. $\blacksquare$

> [!note] Subtraction and Unique Solutions to Linear Equations
> Once every element $y \in \mathbb{Z}$ has an additive inverse $-y$, **subtraction** is no longer a mysterious primitive operation—we simply *define* subtraction as adding the additive inverse:
> $$
> x - y := x + (-y)
> $$
> Better yet, the original defect of $\mathbb{N}$ is completely resolved: for *any* $a, b \in \mathbb{Z}$, the equation $b + x = a$ now has a **unique** solution in $\mathbb{Z}$, namely $x = a - b = a + (-b)$. (Indeed, $b + (a + (-b)) = a + (b + (-b)) = a + 0_{\mathbb{Z}} = a$, and if $b + x = a$, adding $-b$ to both sides forces $x = a + (-b)$.)

## Embedding the Naturals and Ordering the Integers

Pause and reflect on a subtle set-theoretic point. A natural number $n \in \mathbb{N}$ (such as the von Neumann ordinal $3 = \{0, 1, 2\}$) and an integer in $\mathbb{Z}$ (which is an infinite equivalence class $[3, 0] = \{(3, 0), (4, 1), (5, 2), \dots\}$ of pairs of natural numbers) are completely different set-theoretic objects! Strictly speaking, $\mathbb{N}$ is not literally a subset of $(\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}) / {\sim}$. How, then, are we justified in writing $\mathbb{N} \subset \mathbb{Z}$ and treating natural numbers as integers?

In mathematics, whenever we construct a larger system $B$ to extend an existing system $A$, we connect $A$ to $B$ via a **canonical embedding**—an injective (one-to-one) function $\iota: A \hookrightarrow B$ that **preserves all algebraic and order structure**.
- **Linear Algebra Analogy:** Think of the plane $\mathbb{R}^2$ and three-dimensional space $\mathbb{R}^3$. Strictly speaking, an ordered pair $(x, y) \in \mathbb{R}^2$ is not an ordered triple $(x, y, z) \in \mathbb{R}^3$. However, the map $\iota: \mathbb{R}^2 \hookrightarrow \mathbb{R}^3$ defined by $\iota(x, y) = (x, y, 0)$ is an injective linear transformation ($\iota(\mathbf{u} + \mathbf{v}) = \iota(\mathbf{u}) + \iota(\mathbf{v})$ and $\iota(c\mathbf{u}) = c\iota(\mathbf{u})$) that maps $\mathbb{R}^2$ onto the $xy$-plane inside $\mathbb{R}^3$. Because this copy of $\mathbb{R}^2$ inside $\mathbb{R}^3$ behaves identically to $\mathbb{R}^2$ in every algebraic and geometric way, we harmlessly identify $(x, y)$ with $(x, y, 0)$.

In exact analogy with $(x, y) \mapsto (x, y, 0)$, how should we embed a natural number $n \in \mathbb{N}$ into $\mathbb{Z}$? In our ledger analogy, a natural number $n$ corresponds to having **$n$ dollars in cash and $0$ dollars in debt**, which is the equivalence class $[n, 0]$!

> [!theorem] Canonical Embedding of $\mathbb{N}$ into $\mathbb{Z}$ and Trichotomy Classification
> Define the **canonical embedding** $\iota: \mathbb{N} \hookrightarrow \mathbb{Z}$ by
> $$
> \iota(n) := [n, 0] \quad \text{for all } n \in \mathbb{N}
> $$
> Then:
> 1. **Injectivity:** For all $n, m \in \mathbb{N}$, $\iota(n) = \iota(m) \iff n = m$.
> 2. **Homomorphism (Structure Preservation):** For all $n, m \in \mathbb{N}$,
>    $$
>    \iota(n + m) = \iota(n) + \iota(m), \quad \iota(n \cdot m) = \iota(n) \cdot \iota(m), \quad \iota(0) = 0_{\mathbb{Z}}, \quad \iota(1) = 1_{\mathbb{Z}}
>    $$
> 3. **Canonical Form of Integers:** For every integer $x = [a, b] \in \mathbb{Z}$, **exactly one** of the following three cases holds:
>    - **Positive Integer:** There exists a unique non-zero $n \in \mathbb{N}$ ($n > 0$) such that $x = [n, 0] = \iota(n)$.
>    - **Zero:** $x = [0, 0] = 0_{\mathbb{Z}}$.
>    - **Negative Integer:** There exists a unique non-zero $n \in \mathbb{N}$ ($n > 0$) such that $x = [0, n] = -[n, 0] = -\iota(n)$.

**Proof:**
1. **Injectivity:** Suppose $\iota(n) = \iota(m)$, so $[n, 0] = [m, 0]$. By definition of $\sim$, this means $n + 0 = 0 + m$ in $\mathbb{N}$, which simplifies to $n = m$.
2. **Structure Preservation:** For any $n, m \in \mathbb{N}$, applying the definitions of addition and multiplication on $\mathbb{Z}$ gives:
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   \iota(n) + \iota(m) &= [n, 0] + [m, 0] = [n + m, 0 + 0] = [n + m, 0] = \iota(n + m) \\
   \iota(n) \cdot \iota(m) &= [n, 0] \cdot [m, 0] = [n \cdot m + 0 \cdot 0, n \cdot 0 + 0 \cdot m] = [nm, 0] = \iota(n \cdot m)
   \end{aligned}
   $$
   By definition, $\iota(0) = [0, 0] = 0_{\mathbb{Z}}$ and $\iota(1) = [1, 0] = 1_{\mathbb{Z}}$.
3. **Canonical Form:** Let $x = [a, b] \in \mathbb{Z}$ with $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$. By the **Trichotomy Law** on $\mathbb{N}$ (Section 0.2), exactly one of three order relationships holds between $a$ and $b$:
   - **Case 1 ($a > b$):** By the definition of strict order on $\mathbb{N}$, there exists a unique non-zero $n \in \mathbb{N}$ such that $a = b + n$. This equation can be written as $a + 0 = b + n$, which means $(a, b) \sim (n, 0)$. Hence $x = [a, b] = [n, 0] = \iota(n)$.
   - **Case 2 ($a = b$):** Then $a + 0 = b + 0$, so $(a, b) \sim (0, 0)$, which means $x = [0, 0] = 0_{\mathbb{Z}}$.
   - **Case 3 ($a < b$):** By definition of strict order on $\mathbb{N}$, there exists a unique non-zero $n \in \mathbb{N}$ such that $b = a + n$. This can be written as $a + n = b + 0$, so $(a, b) \sim (0, n)$. Hence $x = [a, b] = [0, n] = -[n, 0] = -\iota(n)$.

   Finally, these three cases are mutually exclusive: $[n, 0] = [0, 0]$ would force $n = 0$; $[0, n] = [0, 0]$ would force $n = 0$; and $[n, 0] = [0, m]$ for non-zero $n, m \in \mathbb{N}$ would force $n + m = 0$ in $\mathbb{N}$, which is impossible when $n, m > 0$. $\blacksquare$

Because $\iota: \mathbb{N} \hookrightarrow \mathbb{Z}$ embeds $\mathbb{N}$ as an exact algebraic replica inside $\mathbb{Z}$, we henceforth **identify** each natural number $n \in \mathbb{N}$ with its image $\iota(n) = [n, 0] \in \mathbb{Z}$, and we write $-n$ for $-\iota(n) = [0, n]$. Notice too that for any $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$,
$$
\iota(a) - \iota(b) = [a, 0] + (-[b, 0]) = [a, 0] + [0, b] = [a, b]
$$
So the equivalence class $[a, b]$ genuinely *is* the difference $a - b$ inside $\mathbb{Z}$!

With this canonical three-way classification in hand, we can immediately complete the proof of **Part 3 of the Algebraic Structure Theorem (No Zero Divisors)**: if $x, y \in \mathbb{Z}$ are both non-zero, then $x$ is either $[n, 0]$ or $[0, n]$ for some non-zero $n \in \mathbb{N}$, and $y$ is either $[m, 0]$ or $[0, m]$ for some non-zero $m \in \mathbb{N}$. Multiplying them in any of the four sign combinations gives either $[nm, 0]$ or $[0, nm]$. Since $n \neq 0$ and $m \neq 0$ in $\mathbb{N}$, their product $nm \neq 0$ in $\mathbb{N}$, so neither $[nm, 0]$ nor $[0, nm]$ can equal $[0, 0] = 0_{\mathbb{Z}}$! And once we know $\mathbb{Z}$ has no zero divisors, **Multiplicative Cancellation** follows immediately: if $xz = yz$ with $z \neq 0$, then $(x - y)z = 0$, which forces $x - y = 0$, so $x = y$.

### Ordering the Integers

How should we extend the order relation $\le$ and $<$ from $\mathbb{N}$ to $\mathbb{Z}$? In the world where subtraction exists, $a - b \le c - d$ is equivalent to $a + d \le b + c$, and $x < y$ simply means that the difference $y - x$ is a positive integer ($[k, 0]$ for some non-zero $k \in \mathbb{N}$). Let's check that these two perspectives agree and turn them into a formal definition.

> [!abstract] Definition: Order on $\mathbb{Z}$
> Let $x = [a, b]$ and $y = [c, d]$ be integers in $\mathbb{Z}$.
> 1. We define $x \le y$ (read "$x$ is less than or equal to $y$") if and only if
>    $$
>    a + d \le b + c \quad \text{in } \mathbb{N}
>    $$
>    Equivalently, $x \le y$ if and only if $y - x = [n, 0] = \iota(n)$ for some $n \in \mathbb{N}$.
> 2. We define $x < y$ (or $y > x$) if and only if
>    $$
>    a + d < b + c \quad \text{in } \mathbb{N}
>    $$
>    Equivalently, $x < y$ if and only if $y - x = [k, 0] = \iota(k)$ for some non-zero $k \in \mathbb{N}$ ($k > 0$).
> 3. An integer $x \in \mathbb{Z}$ is called **positive** if $x > 0_{\mathbb{Z}}$ and **negative** if $x < 0_{\mathbb{Z}}$.

Let's verify that the coordinate condition $a + d < b + c$ in $\mathbb{N}$ and the difference condition $y - x = [k, 0]$ ($k > 0$) are indeed identical, and that this order makes $\mathbb{Z}$ into what algebraists call an **ordered integral domain**.

> [!theorem] Properties of the Order on $\mathbb{Z}$
> The order relations $\le$ and $<$ on $\mathbb{Z}$ are well-defined and extend the order on $\mathbb{N}$ (meaning $n \le m \text{ in } \mathbb{N} \iff \iota(n) \le \iota(m) \text{ in } \mathbb{Z}$). Furthermore, for all $x, y, z \in \mathbb{Z}$:
> 1. **Trichotomy Law:** Exactly one of the relations $x < y$, $x = y$, or $x > y$ holds.
> 2. **Transitivity:** If $x < y$ and $y < z$, then $x < z$.
> 3. **Additive Compatibility (Translation Invariance):** If $x < y$, then $x + z < y + z$.
> 4. **Multiplicative Compatibility:** If $x < y$ and $z > 0_{\mathbb{Z}}$, then $x \cdot z < y \cdot z$. Conversely, if $x < y$ and $z < 0_{\mathbb{Z}}$, then $x \cdot z > y \cdot z$ (multiplying by a negative reverses the inequality!).

**Proof:**
First, let's check why the coordinate condition and the difference condition are equivalent for $x = [a, b]$ and $y = [c, d]$. By definition of subtraction on $\mathbb{Z}$,
$$
y - x = [c, d] + (-[a, b]) = [c, d] + [b, a] = [c + b, d + a]
$$
By the Canonical Form Theorem, the integer $y - x = [c + b, d + a]$ equals $[k, 0]$ for some non-zero $k \in \mathbb{N}$ if and only if its first coordinate is strictly greater than its second coordinate in $\mathbb{N}$, that is, $c + b > d + a$, or $a + d < b + c$ in $\mathbb{N}$! Because subtraction $y - x$ is already proved to be well-defined on $\mathbb{Z}$, the condition "$y - x = [k, 0]$ for some non-zero $k \in \mathbb{N}$" does not depend on representatives at all, so $<$ and $\le$ are automatically **well-defined**! And for $n, m \in \mathbb{N}$, $\iota(n) \le \iota(m) \iff [n, 0] \le [m, 0] \iff n + 0 \le 0 + m \iff n \le m$, so the order on $\mathbb{Z}$ faithfully extends the order on $\mathbb{N}$.

Now the four order properties become effortless consequences of the difference characterization $x < y \iff y - x = [k, 0]$ for some $k \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$:
1. **Trichotomy:** Apply the three-way Canonical Form classification to the single integer $y - x \in \mathbb{Z}$:
   - Either $y - x = [k, 0]$ for a unique non-zero $k \in \mathbb{N}$ (which means $x < y$),
   - Or $y - x = [0, 0] = 0_{\mathbb{Z}}$ (which means $x = y$),
   - Or $y - x = [0, k] = -[k, 0]$ for a unique non-zero $k \in \mathbb{N}$, so $x - y = -(y - x) = [k, 0]$ (which means $y < x$, i.e., $x > y$).
2. **Transitivity:** If $x < y$ and $y < z$, then $y - x = [k, 0]$ and $z - y = [m, 0]$ for some non-zero $k, m \in \mathbb{N}$. Then
   $$
   z - x = (z - y) + (y - x) = [m, 0] + [k, 0] = [m + k, 0]
   $$
   Since $m, k > 0$ in $\mathbb{N}$, their sum $m + k > 0$ in $\mathbb{N}$, so $x < z$.
3. **Additive Compatibility:** If $x < y$, then $(y + z) - (x + z) = y - x = [k, 0]$ with $k > 0$, so $x + z < y + z$.
4. **Multiplicative Compatibility:** Suppose $x < y$, so $y - x = [k, 0]$ with $k \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$.
   - If $z > 0_{\mathbb{Z}}$, then $z = [m, 0]$ for some $m \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$. By distributivity,
     $$
     yz - xz = (y - x)z = [k, 0] \cdot [m, 0] = [km, 0]
     $$
     Since $k, m > 0$ in $\mathbb{N}$, $km > 0$ in $\mathbb{N}$, which proves $xz < yz$.
   - If $z < 0_{\mathbb{Z}}$, then $z = [0, m]$ for some $m \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$, so $-z = [m, 0] > 0_{\mathbb{Z}}$. Then
     $$
     xz - yz = (y - x)(-z) = [k, 0] \cdot [m, 0] = [km, 0]
     $$
     with $km > 0$ in $\mathbb{N}$, which means $yz < xz$ (i.e., $xz > yz$). $\blacksquare$

## 0.3 Exercises

### Problem 0.3.1: Units of $\mathbb{Z}$ and the Grothendieck Cancellation Criterion

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> 1. Let $(M, +)$ be any **commutative monoid** (a set $M$ equipped with an associative, commutative binary operation $+$ and an identity element $0 \in M$ satisfying $m + 0 = m$ for all $m \in M$). Suppose we attempt to mimic our construction of $\mathbb{Z}$ by defining a binary relation $\sim$ on $M \times M$ via
>    $$
>    (a, b) \sim (c, d) \iff a + d = b + c
>    $$
>    Prove that $\sim$ is an equivalence relation on $M \times M$ **if and only if** $(M, +)$ satisfies the **cancellation law** ($x + z = y + z \implies x = y$ for all $x, y, z \in M$). Then show that for an arbitrary commutative monoid $(M, +)$ where cancellation might fail, the modified relation $\approx$ on $M \times M$ defined by
>    $$
>    (a, b) \approx (c, d) \iff \exists k \in M \text{ such that } a + d + k = b + c + k
>    $$
>    is *always* an equivalence relation (whose quotient $(M \times M)/{\approx}$ is an abelian group called the **Grothendieck group** of $M$), and prove that the canonical map $\iota: M \to (M \times M)/{\approx}$ given by $\iota(a) = [a, 0]$ is injective if and only if cancellation holds in $M$.
> 2. In a commutative ring $R$ with multiplicative identity $1_R$, an element $u \in R$ is called a **unit** (or **invertible element**) if it possesses a multiplicative inverse in $R$, meaning there exists $v \in R$ such that $u \cdot v = 1_R$. Using the order and canonical form of elements in $\mathbb{Z}$, prove that the only units of the ring $\mathbb{Z}$ are $u = 1_{\mathbb{Z}} = [1, 0]$ and $u = -1_{\mathbb{Z}} = [0, 1]$.

**Solution:**
**Part 1 (The Grothendieck Cancellation Criterion):**
First, let us prove that $(a, b) \sim (c, d) \iff a + d = b + c$ is an equivalence relation on $M \times M$ if and only if $(M, +)$ satisfies the cancellation law.
- **($\impliedby$) Suppose cancellation holds in $M$:** Reflexivity ($a + b = b + a$) and symmetry ($a + d = b + c \implies c + b = d + a$) hold in *any* commutative monoid. For transitivity, suppose $(a, b) \sim (c, d)$ and $(c, d) \sim (e, f)$, so $a + d = b + c$ and $c + f = d + e$. Adding these equations and regrouping via associativity and commutativity in $M$ gives
  $$
  (a + f) + (c + d) = (a + d) + (c + f) = (b + c) + (d + e) = (b + e) + (c + d)
  $$
  By the cancellation law in $M$, we may cancel the common term $z = c + d$ to obtain $a + f = b + e$, so $(a, b) \sim (e, f)$. Thus $\sim$ is an equivalence relation.
- **($\implies$) Suppose $\sim$ is an equivalence relation on $M \times M$:** We must prove that for any $x, y, z \in M$, if $x + z = y + z$, then $x = y$. Notice that the hypothesis $x + z = y + z$ can be rewritten as:
  $$
  \begin{aligned}
  x + z = 0 + (y + z) = (0 + y) + z &\implies (x, 0) \sim (y + z, z) \\
  (y + z) + 0 = z + y &\implies (y + z, z) \sim (y, 0)
  \end{aligned}
  $$
  Wait—let's check those two equivalences carefully! By definition of $\sim$:
  - $(x, 0) \sim (z, z)$ is false unless $x = 0$, so instead let's pair $x$ with $0$ and $z$ with $z$ properly: we want $(x, 0) \sim (x + z, z)$ and $(x + z, z) \sim (y, 0)$! Let's check:
    1. Does $(x, 0) \sim (x + z, z)$? Yes, because $x + z = 0 + (x + z)$.
    2. Does $(x + z, z) \sim (y, 0)$? Let's test the definition of $\sim$: the outer sum is $(x + z) + 0 = x + z$, and the inner sum is $z + y = y + z$. By our hypothesis $x + z = y + z$, these are equal! Thus $(x + z, z) \sim (y, 0)$.
  Since $\sim$ is assumed to be transitive, $(x, 0) \sim (x + z, z)$ and $(x + z, z) \sim (y, 0)$ imply $(x, 0) \sim (y, 0)$. Unpacking the definition of $(x, 0) \sim (y, 0)$ gives $x + 0 = 0 + y$, which by the identity axiom of $M$ simplifies to $x = y$! This proves the cancellation law in $M$.

Next, let us verify that the modified relation $(a, b) \approx (c, d) \iff \exists k \in M \text{ such that } a + d + k = b + c + k$ is an equivalence relation on $M \times M$ even without cancellation:
- **Reflexivity:** Taking $k = 0 \in M$, we have $a + b + 0 = b + a + 0$ by commutativity, so $(a, b) \approx (a, b)$.
- **Symmetry:** If $(a, b) \approx (c, d)$, there exists $k \in M$ with $a + d + k = b + c + k$. By commutativity, $c + b + k = d + a + k$, so $(c, d) \approx (a, b)$ using the same witness $k$.
- **Transitivity:** Suppose $(a, b) \approx (c, d)$ (witnessed by $k_1 \in M$ with $a + d + k_1 = b + c + k_1$) and $(c, d) \approx (e, f)$ (witnessed by $k_2 \in M$ with $c + f + k_2 = d + e + k_2$). Instead of canceling $c + d$, we simply absorb $c + d + k_1 + k_2$ into our new witness! Define $k_3 := c + d + k_1 + k_2 \in M$. Then:
  $$
  \begin{aligned}
  a + f + k_3 &= a + f + (c + d + k_1 + k_2) \\
              &= (a + d + k_1) + (c + f + k_2) \\
              &= (b + c + k_1) + (d + e + k_2) \\
              &= b + e + (c + d + k_1 + k_2) = b + e + k_3
  \end{aligned}
  $$
  Thus $(a, b) \approx (e, f)$ with witness $k_3 \in M$.

Finally, let us show that $\iota: M \to (M \times M)/{\approx}$ given by $\iota(a) = [a, 0]$ is injective if and only if cancellation holds in $M$:
- By definition of $\approx$, for any $x, y \in M$,
  $$
  \iota(x) = \iota(y) \iff (x, 0) \approx (y, 0) \iff \exists k \in M \text{ such that } x + 0 + k = 0 + y + k \iff \exists k \in M \text{ such that } x + k = y + k
  $$
- If cancellation holds in $M$, then $x + k = y + k \implies x = y$, so $\iota(x) = \iota(y) \implies x = y$ ($\iota$ is injective).
- Conversely, if $\iota$ is injective and $x + z = y + z$ for some $x, y, z \in M$, then $z \in M$ witnesses $(x, 0) \approx (y, 0)$, so $\iota(x) = \iota(y)$, which by injectivity of $\iota$ forces $x = y$.

**Part 2 (Units of $\mathbb{Z}$):**
Suppose $u, v \in \mathbb{Z}$ satisfy $u \cdot v = 1_{\mathbb{Z}} = [1, 0]$.
First, neither $u$ nor $v$ can be $0_{\mathbb{Z}} = [0, 0]$, because for any $z \in \mathbb{Z}$, $0_{\mathbb{Z}} \cdot z = [0, 0]$ whereas $1_{\mathbb{Z}} = [1, 0] \neq [0, 0]$.
By the Canonical Form / Trichotomy Theorem on $\mathbb{Z}$, each of $u$ and $v$ is either positive or negative:
- **Case 1 ($u > 0_{\mathbb{Z}}$):** Since $u \cdot v = 1_{\mathbb{Z}} > 0_{\mathbb{Z}}$, $v$ cannot be negative (if $v < 0_{\mathbb{Z}}$, then multiplying $v < 0_{\mathbb{Z}}$ by $u > 0_{\mathbb{Z}}$ gives $u \cdot v < 0_{\mathbb{Z}}$, a contradiction). Thus $v > 0_{\mathbb{Z}}$ as well. By the canonical form of positive integers, there exist non-zero natural numbers $n, m \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$ such that
  $$
  u = [n, 0] \quad \text{and} \quad v = [m, 0]
  $$
  Multiplying them gives
  $$
  [1, 0] = u \cdot v = [n, 0] \cdot [m, 0] = [nm, 0]
  $$
  By injectivity of the canonical embedding $\iota$, this implies $nm = 1$ in $\mathbb{N}$. Since $n, m \in \mathbb{N} \setminus \{0\}$, we have $n \ge 1$ and $m \ge 1$. If $n > 1$, then $n = 1 + k$ for some $k \ge 1$, so $nm = (1 + k)m = m + km \ge 1 + 1 = 2 > 1$, a contradiction. Therefore $n = 1$ (and $m = 1$), which means $u = [1, 0] = 1_{\mathbb{Z}}$.
- **Case 2 ($u < 0_{\mathbb{Z}}$):** Then $-u > 0_{\mathbb{Z}}$ and $(-u)(-v) = u \cdot v = 1_{\mathbb{Z}}$ (since $(-u)(-v) = [n, 0] \cdot [m, 0] = [0, n] \cdot [0, m]$). Applying Case 1 to the positive integer $-u$ shows that $-u = [1, 0]$, which means $u = -[1, 0] = [0, 1] = -1_{\mathbb{Z}}$ (with inverse $v = -1_{\mathbb{Z}}$ since $[0, 1] \cdot [0, 1] = [0 \cdot 0 + 1 \cdot 1, 0 \cdot 1 + 1 \cdot 0] = [1, 0]$).

Thus $u = 1_{\mathbb{Z}} = [1, 0]$ and $u = -1_{\mathbb{Z}} = [0, 1]$ are the only units in $\mathbb{Z}$. $\blacksquare$

### Problem 0.3.2: Universal Property of the Integers

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> In Linear Algebra, a linear transformation $T: V \to W$ is completely and uniquely determined once you specify its values on a basis of $V$. In abstract algebra, the integers $\mathbb{Z}$ enjoy an analogous **universal property**: $\mathbb{Z}$ is the "most general" (or *initial*) commutative ring extending $\mathbb{N}$.
>
> To state this precisely, let $\iota: \mathbb{N} \hookrightarrow \mathbb{Z}$ be the canonical embedding $\iota(n) = [n, 0]$. Suppose $(R, +_R, \cdot_R, 0_R, 1_R)$ is any commutative ring, and let $\varphi: \mathbb{N} \to R$ be a function that preserves the monoid structures of $\mathbb{N}$, meaning that for all $n, m \in \mathbb{N}$:
> $$
> \varphi(0) = 0_R, \quad \varphi(1) = 1_R, \quad \varphi(n + m) = \varphi(n) +_R \varphi(m), \quad \text{and} \quad \varphi(n \cdot m) = \varphi(n) \cdot_R \varphi(m)
> $$
> Prove that there exists a **unique** **ring homomorphism** $\Phi: \mathbb{Z} \to R$—that is, a function satisfying $\Phi(x + y) = \Phi(x) +_R \Phi(y)$, $\Phi(x \cdot y) = \Phi(x) \cdot_R \Phi(y)$, $\Phi(0_{\mathbb{Z}}) = 0_R$, and $\Phi(1_{\mathbb{Z}}) = 1_R$ for all $x, y \in \mathbb{Z}$—such that $\Phi \circ \iota = \varphi$ (meaning $\Phi([n, 0]) = \varphi(n)$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$).

**Solution:**
This is an "existence and uniqueness" proof, which we tackle in two clear stages: first we show that *if* such a ring homomorphism $\Phi$ exists, there is only one possible formula it can have (**Uniqueness**); second, we use that forced formula to define $\Phi$ and verify that it is well-defined and satisfies all the axioms of a ring homomorphism (**Existence**).

**Stage 1: Uniqueness (Why the Formula is Forced)**
Suppose $\Phi: \mathbb{Z} \to R$ is any ring homomorphism satisfying $\Phi \circ \iota = \varphi$. First note that any ring homomorphism automatically preserves additive inverses: for any $z \in \mathbb{Z}$,
$$
\Phi(z) +_R \Phi(-z) = \Phi(z + (-z)) = \Phi(0_{\mathbb{Z}}) = 0_R
$$
Adding the additive inverse $-\Phi(z)$ in the ring $R$ to both sides yields $\Phi(-z) = -\Phi(z)$.

Now let $x = [a, b] \in \mathbb{Z}$ be any integer, where $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$. We already observed that in $\mathbb{Z}$, every equivalence class $[a, b]$ decomposes as the difference of two embedded natural numbers:
$$
[a, b] = [a, 0] + [0, b] = [a, 0] + (-[b, 0]) = \iota(a) + (-\iota(b))
$$
Since $\Phi$ must preserve addition and additive inverses, and must satisfy $\Phi(\iota(n)) = \varphi(n)$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$, applying $\Phi$ to both sides forces:
$$
\begin{aligned}
\Phi([a, b]) &= \Phi(\iota(a) + (-\iota(b))) \\
             &= \Phi(\iota(a)) +_R \Phi(-\iota(b)) \\
             &= \Phi(\iota(a)) -_R \Phi(\iota(b)) \\
             &= \varphi(a) -_R \varphi(b)
\end{aligned}
$$
where $u -_R v := u +_R (-v)$ in the ring $R$. Because the value of $\Phi([a, b])$ is completely determined by $\varphi(a)$ and $\varphi(b)$ for every $[a, b] \in \mathbb{Z}$, at most one such ring homomorphism $\Phi$ can exist!

**Stage 2: Existence (Well-Definedness and Homomorphism Properties)**
Motivated by Stage 1, we now *define* $\Phi: \mathbb{Z} \to R$ by the formula
$$
\Phi([a, b]) := \varphi(a) -_R \varphi(b) = \varphi(a) +_R (-\varphi(b)) \quad \text{for all } [a, b] \in \mathbb{Z}
$$
1. **Well-Definedness of $\Phi$:** Because our formula uses a specific representative $(a, b)$ of the equivalence class $[a, b]$, we must check that if $[a, b] = [a', b']$, then $\varphi(a) -_R \varphi(b) = \varphi(a') -_R \varphi(b')$ in $R$.
   Suppose $[a, b] = [a', b']$, so $a + b' = b + a'$ in $\mathbb{N}$. Applying $\varphi$ to both sides and using the hypothesis that $\varphi$ preserves addition on $\mathbb{N}$, we get:
   $$
   \varphi(a) +_R \varphi(b') = \varphi(a + b') = \varphi(b + a') = \varphi(b) +_R \varphi(a')
   $$
   Now add $(-\varphi(b)) +_R (-\varphi(b'))$ to both sides in the abelian group $(R, +_R)$:
   $$
   \varphi(a) -_R \varphi(b) = \varphi(a') -_R \varphi(b')
   $$
   Thus $\Phi([a, b]) = \Phi([a', b'])$, proving that $\Phi$ is well-defined!

2. **Extension Property ($\Phi \circ \iota = \varphi$):** For any $n \in \mathbb{N}$,
   $$
   \Phi(\iota(n)) = \Phi([n, 0]) = \varphi(n) -_R \varphi(0) = \varphi(n) -_R 0_R = \varphi(n)
   $$
   In particular, $\Phi(0_{\mathbb{Z}}) = \Phi(\iota(0)) = \varphi(0) = 0_R$ and $\Phi(1_{\mathbb{Z}}) = \Phi(\iota(1)) = \varphi(1) = 1_R$.

3. **Preservation of Addition:** For any $[a, b], [c, d] \in \mathbb{Z}$, using the additive homomorphism property of $\varphi$ and the abelian group laws of $(R, +_R)$, we have:
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   \Phi([a, b] + [c, d]) &= \Phi([a + c, b + d]) \\
                         &= \varphi(a + c) -_R \varphi(b + d) \\
                         &= (\varphi(a) +_R \varphi(c)) -_R (\varphi(b) +_R \varphi(d)) \\
                         &= (\varphi(a) -_R \varphi(b)) +_R (\varphi(c) -_R \varphi(d)) \\
                         &= \Phi([a, b]) +_R \Phi([c, d])
   \end{aligned}
   $$

4. **Preservation of Multiplication:** For any $[a, b], [c, d] \in \mathbb{Z}$, using the fact that $\varphi$ preserves both $+$ and $\cdot$ on $\mathbb{N}$ along with the ring laws in $R$ (including distributivity in $R$, which implies $u(-v) = (-u)v = -(uv)$ and $(-u)(-v) = uv$ in any ring $R$), we compute:
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   \Phi([a, b] \cdot [c, d]) &= \Phi([ac + bd, ad + bc]) \\
                             &= \varphi(ac + bd) -_R \varphi(ad + bc) \\
                             &= \big(\varphi(a)\varphi(c) +_R \varphi(b)\varphi(d)\big) -_R \big(\varphi(a)\varphi(d) +_R \varphi(b)\varphi(c)\big) \\
                             &= \varphi(a)\big(\varphi(c) -_R \varphi(d)\big) -_R \varphi(b)\big(\varphi(c) -_R \varphi(d)\big) \\
                             &= \big(\varphi(a) -_R \varphi(b)\big) \cdot_R \big(\varphi(c) -_R \varphi(d)\big) \\
                             &= \Phi([a, b]) \cdot_R \Phi([c, d])
   \end{aligned}
   $$
This completes the proof of both existence and uniqueness. $\blacksquare$

### Problem 0.3.3: The Law of Signs and Direct Coordinate Proof of No Zero Divisors

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> 1. (**The Law of Signs from Equivalence Classes**) Everyone learns in elementary school that "a negative times a negative is a positive," or $(-x)(-y) = x \cdot y$. Using *only* the equivalence-class definitions of negation $-[a, b] := [b, a]$ and multiplication $[a, b] \cdot [c, d] := [ac + bd, ad + bc]$ on $\mathbb{Z}$ (together with the arithmetic laws of $\mathbb{N}$), prove directly that for all $x, y \in \mathbb{Z}$:
>    $$
>    (-x) \cdot y = -(x \cdot y) = x \cdot (-y) \quad \text{and} \quad (-x) \cdot (-y) = x \cdot y
>    $$
>    In particular, verify that $(-1_{\mathbb{Z}}) \cdot x = -x$ for all $x \in \mathbb{Z}$.
> 2. (**Direct Coordinate Proof that $\mathbb{Z}$ is an Integral Domain**) In the text, we proved that $\mathbb{Z}$ has no zero divisors by splitting integers into three cases using Trichotomy. Give an alternative, direct coordinate proof that if $[a, b] \cdot [c, d] = [0, 0]$ and $[a, b] \neq [0, 0]$, then $[c, d] = [0, 0]$, by assuming without loss of generality that $a > b$ (so $a = b + k$ for some non-zero $k \in \mathbb{N}$), substituting into the equivalence $(ac + bd, ad + bc) \sim (0, 0)$, and using the additive and multiplicative cancellation laws of $\mathbb{N}$.

**Solution:**
**Part 1 (The Law of Signs from Equivalence Classes):**
Let $x = [a, b]$ and $y = [c, d]$ be arbitrary integers in $\mathbb{Z}$, where $a, b, c, d \in \mathbb{N}$. By definition of negation on $\mathbb{Z}$, we have
$$
-x = -[a, b] = [b, a] \quad \text{and} \quad -y = -[c, d] = [d, c]
$$
First, let us compute $-(x \cdot y)$, $(-x) \cdot y$, and $x \cdot (-y)$ directly from the definition of multiplication on $\mathbb{Z}$ and the commutativity of addition in $\mathbb{N}$:
$$
\begin{aligned}
-(x \cdot y) &= -[ac + bd, ad + bc] = [ad + bc, ac + bd] \\
(-x) \cdot y &= [b, a] \cdot [c, d] = [bc + ad, bd + ac] = [ad + bc, ac + bd] \\
x \cdot (-y) &= [a, b] \cdot [d, c] = [ad + bc, ac + bd]
\end{aligned}
$$
All three expressions evaluate to the exact same ordered pair $[ad + bc, ac + bd]$, which proves $(-x) \cdot y = -(x \cdot y) = x \cdot (-y)$!

Next, let us compute $(-x) \cdot (-y)$ directly:
$$
\begin{aligned}
(-x) \cdot (-y) &= [b, a] \cdot [d, c] \\
                &= [bd + ac, bc + ad] \\
                &= [ac + bd, ad + bc] = x \cdot y
\end{aligned}
$$
where the third equality simply uses the commutativity of addition in $\mathbb{N}$ ($bd + ac = ac + bd$ and $bc + ad = ad + bc$). Notice how transparent the "mystery" of $(-x)(-y) = xy$ becomes when viewed through formal differences: swapping the coordinates of *both* $[a, b]$ and $[c, d]$ swaps $a \leftrightarrow b$ and $c \leftrightarrow d$, which leaves the "same-side" sum $ac + bd$ in the first slot and the "cross-side" sum $ad + bc$ in the second slot!

Finally, since $-1_{\mathbb{Z}} = -[1, 0] = [0, 1]$, we have for any $x = [a, b] \in \mathbb{Z}$:
$$
(-1_{\mathbb{Z}}) \cdot x = [0, 1] \cdot [a, b] = [0 \cdot a + 1 \cdot b, 0 \cdot b + 1 \cdot a] = [b, a] = -x
$$

**Part 2 (Direct Coordinate Proof of No Zero Divisors):**
Suppose $[a, b] \cdot [c, d] = [0, 0]$ with $[a, b] \neq [0, 0]$. Unpacking the definition of multiplication and equivalence in $\mathbb{Z}$ gives:
$$
[ac + bd, ad + bc] = [0, 0] \iff (ac + bd) + 0 = (ad + bc) + 0 \iff ac + bd = ad + bc \quad \text{in } \mathbb{N}
$$
Since $[a, b] \neq [0, 0]$, we have $a \neq b$ in $\mathbb{N}$. By Trichotomy on $\mathbb{N}$, either $a > b$ or $a < b$.
- Suppose first that $a > b$. Then there exists a non-zero $k \in \mathbb{N}$ ($k \neq 0$) such that $a = b + k$. Substituting $a = b + k$ into the equation $ac + bd = ad + bc$ and expanding via distributivity in $\mathbb{N}$ gives:
  $$
  \begin{aligned}
  (b + k)c + bd &= (b + k)d + bc \\
  bc + kc + bd &= bd + kd + bc \\
  kc + (bc + bd) &= kd + (bc + bd)
  \end{aligned}
  $$
  By the **Additive Cancellation Law** in $\mathbb{N}$, we can cancel the common natural number $(bc + bd)$ from both sides to obtain:
  $$
  kc = kd
  $$
  Since $k \in \mathbb{N}$ is non-zero ($k \neq 0$), the **Multiplicative Cancellation Law** in $\mathbb{N}$ (Section 0.2) allows us to cancel $k$ from both sides, yielding
  $$
  c = d
  $$
  Therefore $c + 0 = d + 0$, which means $(c, d) \sim (0, 0)$, so $[c, d] = [0, 0]$!
- If instead $a < b$, then $-[a, b] = [b, a]$ satisfies $b > a$ and $[b, a] \cdot [c, d] = -([a, b] \cdot [c, d]) = -[0, 0] = [0, 0]$, so the exact same argument with $a$ and $b$ swapped yields $c = d$ and $[c, d] = [0, 0]$. $\blacksquare$

### Problem 0.3.4: Discreteness of $\mathbb{Z}$ and Well-Ordering of Bounded-Below Subsets

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Unlike the natural numbers $\mathbb{N}$, the set of all integers $\mathbb{Z}$ is **not** well-ordered by $\le$ (for instance, the set $\mathbb{Z}$ itself has no least element because $x - 1 < x$ for every $x \in \mathbb{Z}$). However, $\mathbb{Z}$ inherits both the "step-by-step" discreteness of $\mathbb{N}$ and a powerful version of the Well-Ordering Principle for subsets that have a floor!
> 1. (**Discreteness of $\mathbb{Z}$**) Prove that for every integer $x \in \mathbb{Z}$, there is **no** integer $z \in \mathbb{Z}$ satisfying $x < z < x + 1_{\mathbb{Z}}$. (That is, $x + 1_{\mathbb{Z}}$ is the immediate successor of $x$ in the order on $\mathbb{Z}$.)
> 2. (**Well-Ordering of Bounded-Below Subsets of $\mathbb{Z}$**) A subset $S \subseteq \mathbb{Z}$ is said to be **bounded below** in $\mathbb{Z}$ if there exists an integer $m \in \mathbb{Z}$ (called a **lower bound** of $S$) such that $m \le s$ for all $s \in S$. Prove that every non-empty subset $S \subseteq \mathbb{Z}$ that is bounded below has a unique **least element** (or **minimum**) $s_0 \in S$ satisfying $s_0 \le s$ for all $s \in S$.
> 3. (**Existence of Maximums for Bounded-Above Subsets**) State the analogous definition of a subset $T \subseteq \mathbb{Z}$ being **bounded above** in $\mathbb{Z}$, and prove (by considering the set of negations $-T := \{-t : t \in T\}$) that every non-empty subset $T \subseteq \mathbb{Z}$ that is bounded above has a unique **greatest element** (or **maximum**) $t_0 \in T$ satisfying $t \le t_0$ for all $t \in T$.

**Solution:**
**Part 1 (Discreteness of $\mathbb{Z}$):**
Suppose for the sake of contradiction that for some $x \in \mathbb{Z}$, there exists an integer $z \in \mathbb{Z}$ such that
$$
x < z < x + 1_{\mathbb{Z}}
$$
Using the **Additive Compatibility** of the order on $\mathbb{Z}$, we can add $-x$ to each part of this compound inequality to translate it back to $0_{\mathbb{Z}}$:
$$
0_{\mathbb{Z}} = x + (-x) < z - x < (x + 1_{\mathbb{Z}}) + (-x) = 1_{\mathbb{Z}}
$$
By the definition of strict order on $\mathbb{Z}$, since $z - x > 0_{\mathbb{Z}}$, there exists a non-zero natural number $k \in \mathbb{N}$ ($k > 0$) such that $z - x = \iota(k) = [k, 0]$. Furthermore, since $1_{\mathbb{Z}} = \iota(1) = [1, 0]$ and the embedding $\iota: \mathbb{N} \hookrightarrow \mathbb{Z}$ preserves order ($\iota(a) < \iota(b) \iff a < b$ in $\mathbb{N}$), the inequality $0_{\mathbb{Z}} < z - x < 1_{\mathbb{Z}}$ translates into
$$
\iota(0) < \iota(k) < \iota(1) \implies 0 < k < 1 \quad \text{in } \mathbb{N}
$$
However, as proved in Section 0.2, $1 = 0^+$ is the immediate successor of $0$ in $\mathbb{N}$, so every non-zero natural number $k \in \mathbb{N}$ satisfies $k \ge 1$! No natural number $k$ satisfies $0 < k < 1$. This contradiction proves that no integer $z$ can exist strictly between $x$ and $x + 1_{\mathbb{Z}}$.

**Part 2 (Well-Ordering of Bounded-Below Subsets of $\mathbb{Z}$):**
Let $S \subseteq \mathbb{Z}$ be a non-empty subset that is bounded below, and let $m \in \mathbb{Z}$ be a lower bound of $S$, so $m \le s$ for all $s \in S$.
Our strategy is to **shift $S$ into the natural numbers $\mathbb{N}$** so that we can invoke the Well-Ordering Principle of $\mathbb{N}$ from Section 0.2!

For every $s \in S$, since $m \le s$, the difference $s - m$ satisfies $s - m \ge 0_{\mathbb{Z}}$. By the definition of $\le$ on $\mathbb{Z}$, every integer $\ge 0_{\mathbb{Z}}$ is of the form $\iota(n) = [n, 0]$ for a unique $n \in \mathbb{N}$. Therefore, we can define a subset $A \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ by collecting all these shifted natural numbers:
$$
A := \{n \in \mathbb{N} : \iota(n) = s - m \text{ for some } s \in S\}
$$
Let's check the hypotheses of the Well-Ordering Principle of $\mathbb{N}$ for $A$:
1. $A \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ by construction.
2. Since $S$ is non-empty, there is at least one element $s_1 \in S$; then $s_1 - m \ge 0_{\mathbb{Z}}$ gives a unique $n_1 \in \mathbb{N}$ with $\iota(n_1) = s_1 - m$, so $n_1 \in A$ and $A$ is non-empty!

By the **Well-Ordering Principle of $\mathbb{N}$** (Section 0.2), the non-empty subset $A \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ has a unique least element $n_0 \in A$, satisfying $n_0 \le n$ for all $n \in A$.
Now we shift $n_0$ back to $S$! Since $n_0 \in A$, the integer
$$
s_0 := m + \iota(n_0)
$$
belongs to $S$ (since $s_0 - m = \iota(n_0)$ matches the unique element $s \in S$ with $s - m = \iota(n_0)$). To see that $s_0$ is the least element of $S$, take any $s \in S$. Then $s - m = \iota(n)$ for some $n \in A$. Since $n_0 \le n$ in $\mathbb{N}$ and $\iota$ preserves order, we have $\iota(n_0) \le \iota(n)$ in $\mathbb{Z}$. Adding $m$ to both sides via Additive Compatibility yields:
$$
s_0 = m + \iota(n_0) \le m + \iota(n) = m + (s - m) = s
$$
Finally, $s_0$ is unique because if $s_0' \in S$ were another least element of $S$, then $s_0 \le s_0'$ and $s_0' \le s_0$, which by Trichotomy on $\mathbb{Z}$ forces $s_0 = s_0'$.

**Part 3 (Existence of Maximums for Bounded-Above Subsets):**
A subset $T \subseteq \mathbb{Z}$ is **bounded above** in $\mathbb{Z}$ if there exists an integer $M \in \mathbb{Z}$ (called an **upper bound** of $T$) such that $t \le M$ for all $t \in T$.
Suppose $T \subseteq \mathbb{Z}$ is non-empty and bounded above by $M \in \mathbb{Z}$. Consider the reflected set of negations:
$$
S := -T = \{-t : t \in T\} \subseteq \mathbb{Z}
$$
1. Since $T$ is non-empty, $S = -T$ is also non-empty.
2. For every element $-t \in S$ (where $t \in T$), we have $t \le M$. Multiplying this inequality by $-1_{\mathbb{Z}} < 0_{\mathbb{Z}}$ (or adding $-t - M$ to both sides) reverses the inequality to give
   $$
   -M \le -t
   $$
   Thus $-M \in \mathbb{Z}$ is a **lower bound** for the non-empty set $S = -T$!

By **Part 2**, the non-empty bounded-below set $S = -T$ has a unique least element $s_0 \in S$. By definition of $S$, $s_0 = -t_0$ for a unique element $t_0 \in T$, and for every $t \in T$ we have $-t \in S$, so:
$$
-t_0 = s_0 \le -t
$$
Multiplying both sides by $-1_{\mathbb{Z}}$ (or adding $t + t_0$ to both sides) reverses the inequality to yield
$$
t \le t_0 \quad \text{for all } t \in T
$$
Thus $t_0 \in T$ is the unique greatest element (maximum) of $T$. $\blacksquare$

---

# 0.4 Constructing the Rational Numbers from the Integers

## Why Extend the Integers?

In Section 0.3, passing from the natural numbers $\mathbb{N}$ to the integers $\mathbb{Z}$ cured the impossibility of subtraction. Within $\mathbb{Z}$, we can add, subtract, and multiply freely, and every linear equation of the form $b + x = a$ has a unique integer solution $x = a - b$. In algebraic language, $\mathbb{Z}$ is a **commutative ring** with no **zero divisors** (meaning that if $a \cdot b = 0$ in $\mathbb{Z}$, then either $a = 0$ or $b = 0$).

Yet the integers still suffer from a second fundamental algebraic limitation: **you cannot always divide**. Suppose we are given two integers $p, q \in \mathbb{Z}$ with $q \neq 0$, and we want to solve the linear equation
$$
q \cdot x = p
$$
Unless $p$ happens to be an exact integer multiple of $q$, no integer $x \in \mathbb{Z}$ satisfies this equation! For instance, the simple equation $2 \cdot x = 1$ has no solution in $\mathbb{Z}$. Put another way, a non-zero integer $q \in \mathbb{Z}$ has a **multiplicative inverse** (an element $q^{-1} \in \mathbb{Z}$ satisfying $q \cdot q^{-1} = 1$) if and only if $q = 1$ or $q = -1$. Every other non-zero integer lacks a reciprocal inside $\mathbb{Z}$.

Why does this matter for Calculus and Linear Algebra? Think back to Gaussian elimination in Linear Algebra: to eliminate a pivot entry $q \neq 0$, you must divide the pivot row by $q$—that is, multiply by $q^{-1}$. You could not even perform row reduction or define vector spaces over $\mathbb{Z}$ because scalars in Linear Algebra must permit division by any non-zero number! Similarly, in Calculus, difference quotients like $\frac{f(x + h) - f(x)}{h}$ require dividing by arbitrarily small non-zero increments $h$.

How do we build a larger number system where division by any non-zero integer is always possible? We apply the exact same philosophical blueprint that took us from $\mathbb{N}$ to $\mathbb{Z}$:
- In Section 0.3, when we lacked subtraction $a - b$, we represented an intended difference as an ordered pair $(a, b) \in \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$ and glued together pairs that represented the same difference.
- Now, when we lack division $p / q$, we represent an intended fraction as an **ordered pair** $(p, q)$ of integers where the second entry $q$ (the intended denominator) is non-zero, and we glue together pairs that represent the same ratio using an equivalence relation!

## Equivalence Classes of Formal Fractions

Let $\mathbb{Z}^* := \mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0\}$ denote the set of all non-zero integers. A **formal fraction** is simply an ordered pair $(p, q) \in \mathbb{Z} \times \mathbb{Z}^*$, where we think of $p \in \mathbb{Z}$ as the numerator and $q \in \mathbb{Z}^*$ as the non-zero denominator.

Before we have constructed the rational numbers, we do not yet know what the division bar in $\frac{p}{q}$ means formally. So how can we tell when two formal pairs $(p, q)$ and $(r, s)$ in $\mathbb{Z} \times \mathbb{Z}^*$ are *trying* to represent the same fraction? In elementary school, you learned that $\frac{1}{2} = \frac{2}{4} = \frac{-3}{-6}$ because cross-multiplying produces equal integers:
$$
\frac{p}{q} = \frac{r}{s} \iff p \cdot s = q \cdot r
$$
Look closely at the right-hand side: the equation $p \cdot s = q \cdot r$ uses **only multiplication of integers**, which we have already rigorously defined and proved associative, commutative, and distributive in Section 0.3! This allows us to define equivalence of formal fractions without any circularity.

> [!abstract] Definition: The Equivalence Relation for Rationals
> Let $\mathbb{Z}^* := \mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0\}$ be the set of non-zero integers. We define a binary relation $\sim$ on the Cartesian product $\mathbb{Z} \times \mathbb{Z}^*$ by declaring that for any $(p, q), (r, s) \in \mathbb{Z} \times \mathbb{Z}^*$:
> $$
> (p, q) \sim (r, s) \iff p \cdot s = q \cdot r
> $$
> The set of **rational numbers**, denoted $\mathbb{Q}$ (for *quotient*), is the quotient set of $\mathbb{Z} \times \mathbb{Z}^*$ modulo this equivalence relation:
> $$
> \mathbb{Q} := (\mathbb{Z} \times \mathbb{Z}^*) / {\sim}
> $$
> We denote the equivalence class $[(p, q)] \in \mathbb{Q}$ of a pair $(p, q) \in \mathbb{Z} \times \mathbb{Z}^*$ either by $[p, q]$ or by the familiar fraction notation $\frac{p}{q}$ (or $p / q$).

In other words, a single rational number is not just one pair of integers—it is an *entire infinite equivalence class* of pairs that all share the same ratio. For example, the rational number $\frac{1}{2}$ is literally the set
$$
\frac{1}{2} = [1, 2] = \{(p, q) \in \mathbb{Z} \times \mathbb{Z}^* : 2p = q\} = \{(1, 2), (-1, -2), (2, 4), (-2, -4), (3, 6), \dots\}
$$

Let us verify that $\sim$ is genuinely an equivalence relation on $\mathbb{Z} \times \mathbb{Z}^*$. As you read the proof below, pay special attention to the proof of **transitivity**: watch how the requirement $s \neq 0$ and the fact that $\mathbb{Z}$ has no zero divisors (proved in Section 0.3) save the day!

> [!theorem] Equivalence of Formal Fractions
> The binary relation $\sim$ on $\mathbb{Z} \times \mathbb{Z}^*$ defined by $(p, q) \sim (r, s) \iff p \cdot s = q \cdot r$ is an equivalence relation.

**Proof:**
Let $(p, q), (r, s), (t, u) \in \mathbb{Z} \times \mathbb{Z}^*$. By definition of $\mathbb{Z}^*$, we know that the denominators are all non-zero: $q \neq 0$, $s \neq 0$, and $u \neq 0$.
1. **Reflexivity:** For any $(p, q) \in \mathbb{Z} \times \mathbb{Z}^*$, commutativity of integer multiplication gives $p \cdot q = q \cdot p$, which means $(p, q) \sim (p, q)$.
2. **Symmetry:** Suppose $(p, q) \sim (r, s)$, so $p \cdot s = q \cdot r$. By symmetry of equality and commutativity of multiplication in $\mathbb{Z}$, we have $r \cdot q = s \cdot p$, which means $(r, s) \sim (p, q)$.
3. **Transitivity:** Suppose $(p, q) \sim (r, s)$ and $(r, s) \sim (t, u)$. By definition, we have two integer equations:
   $$
   p \cdot s = q \cdot r \quad \text{and} \quad r \cdot u = s \cdot t
   $$
   We want to prove that $p \cdot u = q \cdot t$. To link the two equations together, multiply both sides of the first equation $p \cdot s = q \cdot r$ by $u$, and use the associativity and commutativity of multiplication in $\mathbb{Z}$ along with the second equation $r \cdot u = s \cdot t$:
   $$
   (p \cdot u) \cdot s = (p \cdot s) \cdot u = (q \cdot r) \cdot u = q \cdot (r \cdot u) = q \cdot (s \cdot t) = (q \cdot t) \cdot s
   $$
   Subtracting $(q \cdot t) \cdot s$ from both sides and using the distributive law in $\mathbb{Z}$, we obtain:
   $$
   (p \cdot u - q \cdot t) \cdot s = 0
   $$
   Now comes the critical step: because $(r, s) \in \mathbb{Z} \times \mathbb{Z}^*$, the middle denominator satisfies $s \neq 0$. Because $\mathbb{Z}$ has **no zero divisors**, a product of two integers can be zero only if at least one factor is zero. Since $s \neq 0$, we are forced to conclude that
   $$
   p \cdot u - q \cdot t = 0 \implies p \cdot u = q \cdot t
   $$
   Therefore $(p, q) \sim (t, u)$, establishing transitivity. $\blacksquare$

> [!warning] Caution: Why Zero Cannot Be a Denominator
> Look at what would happen in the transitivity proof if we had been careless and defined $\sim$ on all of $\mathbb{Z} \times \mathbb{Z}$, allowing $(0, 0)$ into our set of pairs! Since $p \cdot 0 = q \cdot 0$ simplifies to $0 = 0$ for *every* pair $(p, q)$, every formal fraction $(p, q)$ would be equivalent to $(0, 0)$:
> $$
> (1, 2) \sim (0, 0) \quad \text{and} \quad (0, 0) \sim (3, 4)
> $$
> If $\sim$ were transitive, this would force $(1, 2) \sim (3, 4)$, or $\frac{1}{2} = \frac{3}{4}$! In fact, every fraction would become equivalent to every other fraction, collapsing the entire rational number system into a single trivial equivalence class. Restricting denominators to $\mathbb{Z}^* = \mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0\}$ and using the absence of zero divisors in $\mathbb{Z}$ is what keeps distinct ratios separate.

## Rational Arithmetic and Field Structure

How should we add and multiply two rational numbers $[p, q]$ and $[r, s]$? In elementary arithmetic, we add two fractions $\frac{p}{q}$ and $\frac{r}{s}$ by putting them over a common denominator $q \cdot s$:
$$
\frac{p}{q} + \frac{r}{s} = \frac{p \cdot s + q \cdot r}{q \cdot s}
$$
And we multiply two fractions by multiplying their numerators and denominators straight across:
$$
\frac{p}{q} \cdot \frac{r}{s} = \frac{p \cdot r}{q \cdot s}
$$
Notice a crucial sanity check before we turn these into formal definitions: if $q \in \mathbb{Z}^*$ and $s \in \mathbb{Z}^*$, is the new denominator $q \cdot s$ guaranteed to be in $\mathbb{Z}^*$? Yes! Because $q \neq 0$ and $s \neq 0$, the no-zero-divisors property of $\mathbb{Z}$ guarantees that $q \cdot s \neq 0$. Thus both $(ps + qr, qs)$ and $(pr, qs)$ are legitimate elements of $\mathbb{Z} \times \mathbb{Z}^*$.

> [!abstract] Definition: Addition and Multiplication on $\mathbb{Q}$
> For any two rational numbers $[p, q], [r, s] \in \mathbb{Q}$, we define their **sum** and **product** by:
> $$
> \begin{aligned}
> [p, q] + [r, s] &:= [p \cdot s + q \cdot r, \, q \cdot s] \\
> [p, q] \cdot [r, s] &:= [p \cdot r, \, q \cdot s]
> \end{aligned}
> $$

Just as in Section 0.3, because we defined addition and multiplication on *equivalence classes* by picking specific *representatives* $(p, q)$ and $(r, s)$, we are obligated to prove that these operations are **well-defined**—that is, if you pick different representatives $(p', q') \sim (p, q)$ and $(r', s') \sim (r, s)$ (say $[2, 4]$ instead of $[1, 2]$), the resulting sum and product land in the exact same equivalence class.

> [!theorem] Well-Definedness of Rational Arithmetic
> Addition and multiplication on $\mathbb{Q}$ are well-defined: if $(p, q) \sim (p', q')$ and $(r, s) \sim (r', s')$ in $\mathbb{Z} \times \mathbb{Z}^*$, then
> $$
> [ps + qr, qs] = [p's' + q'r', q's'] \quad \text{and} \quad [pr, qs] = [p'r', q's']
> $$

**Proof:**
Suppose $(p, q) \sim (p', q')$ and $(r, s) \sim (r', s')$. By definition of $\sim$, we are given two integer equations:
$$
p q' = q p' \quad \text{and} \quad r s' = s r'
$$
**1. Well-Definedness of Addition:**
To show that $(ps + qr, qs) \sim (p's' + q'r', q's')$, we must verify the cross-multiplication identity
$$
(ps + qr)(q's') = (qs)(p's' + q'r')
$$
Starting from the left-hand side, we expand using distributivity in $\mathbb{Z}$, rearrange factors using associativity and commutativity in $\mathbb{Z}$, and substitute $pq' = qp'$ and $rs' = sr'$:
$$
\begin{aligned}
(ps + qr)(q's') &= (ps)(q's') + (qr)(q's') \\
                &= (pq')(ss') + (qq')(rs') \\
                &= (qp')(ss') + (qq')(sr') \\
                &= (qs)(p's') + (qs)(q'r') \\
                &= (qs)(p's' + q'r')
\end{aligned}
$$
Thus $(ps + qr, qs) \sim (p's' + q'r', q's')$, so addition on $\mathbb{Q}$ is well-defined.

**2. Well-Definedness of Multiplication:**
To show that $(pr, qs) \sim (p'r', q's')$, we must verify that $(pr)(q's') = (qs)(p'r')$. Using associativity and commutativity of multiplication in $\mathbb{Z}$ along with $pq' = qp'$ and $rs' = sr'$, we compute:
$$
(pr)(q's') = (pq')(rs') = (qp')(sr') = (qs)(p'r')
$$
Thus $(pr, qs) \sim (p'r', q's')$, so multiplication on $\mathbb{Q}$ is well-defined. $\blacksquare$

> [!example] Example: Cancellation of Common Factors ($[a \cdot k, b \cdot k] = [a, b]$)
> Use the definition of $\sim$ on $\mathbb{Z} \times \mathbb{Z}^*$ to prove the familiar **cancellation law for fractions**: for any $a \in \mathbb{Z}$ and any $b, k \in \mathbb{Z}^*$,
> $$
> [a \cdot k, b \cdot k] = [a, b]
> $$
> Explain how this rule reconciles our formal definition of addition $[p, q] + [r, q] = [pq + qr, q^2]$ for fractions with the *same* denominator $q$ with the elementary school rule $\frac{p}{q} + \frac{r}{q} = \frac{p + r}{q}$.

**Solution:**
First, since $b \neq 0$ and $k \neq 0$, the absence of zero divisors in $\mathbb{Z}$ ensures $b \cdot k \neq 0$, so $(a \cdot k, b \cdot k) \in \mathbb{Z} \times \mathbb{Z}^*$. To check that $(a \cdot k, b \cdot k) \sim (a, b)$, we cross-multiply and use associativity and commutativity in $\mathbb{Z}$:
$$
(a \cdot k) \cdot b = a \cdot (k \cdot b) = a \cdot (b \cdot k) = (b \cdot k) \cdot a
$$
By definition of $\sim$, this means $(a \cdot k, b \cdot k) \sim (a, b)$, or $[a \cdot k, b \cdot k] = [a, b]$.

Now suppose we add two fractions $[p, q]$ and $[r, q]$ that already share the same denominator $q \in \mathbb{Z}^*$. Our formal definition of addition gives
$$
[p, q] + [r, q] = [p \cdot q + q \cdot r, \, q \cdot q] = [(p + r) \cdot q, \, q \cdot q]
$$
Applying the cancellation law $[a \cdot k, b \cdot k] = [a, b]$ with $a = p + r$, $b = q$, and $k = q \neq 0$, we immediately obtain
$$
[(p + r) \cdot q, \, q \cdot q] = [p + r, q]
$$
which is the familiar common-denominator rule $\frac{p}{q} + \frac{r}{q} = \frac{p + r}{q}$! $\blacksquare$

### Embedding the Integers and Solving Division

How do the integers $\mathbb{Z}$ sit inside our newly constructed rational numbers $\mathbb{Q}$? Just as we embedded $\mathbb{N}$ into $\mathbb{Z}$ via $n \mapsto [n, 0]$, we can embed $\mathbb{Z}$ into $\mathbb{Q}$ by placing every integer over the denominator $1$:
$$
\iota: \mathbb{Z} \hookrightarrow \mathbb{Q}, \qquad \iota(n) := [n, 1] = \frac{n}{1}
$$
Let us check that $\iota$ is a faithful, structure-preserving copy of $\mathbb{Z}$ inside $\mathbb{Q}$:
- **Injectivity:** If $\iota(n) = \iota(m)$, then $[n, 1] = [m, 1]$, which means $n \cdot 1 = 1 \cdot m$, so $n = m$.
- **Preservation of Addition and Multiplication:** For all $n, m \in \mathbb{Z}$,
  $$
  \begin{aligned}
  \iota(n) + \iota(m) &= [n, 1] + [m, 1] = [n \cdot 1 + 1 \cdot m, \, 1 \cdot 1] = [n + m, 1] = \iota(n + m) \\
  \iota(n) \cdot \iota(m) &= [n, 1] \cdot [m, 1] = [n \cdot m, \, 1 \cdot 1] = [nm, 1] = \iota(n \cdot m)
  \end{aligned}
  $$
In particular, the **additive identity** (zero) of $\mathbb{Q}$ is $0_{\mathbb{Q}} := [0, 1]$ (and in fact $[0, q] = [0, 1]$ for every $q \in \mathbb{Z}^*$, since $0 \cdot 1 = q \cdot 0$), the **multiplicative identity** (one) of $\mathbb{Q}$ is $1_{\mathbb{Q}} := [1, 1]$ (and $[q, q] = [1, 1]$ for every $q \in \mathbb{Z}^*$), and the **additive inverse** (negative) of $[p, q]$ is $-[p, q] := [-p, q]$, since
$$
[p, q] + [-p, q] = [pq + q(-p), q^2] = [0, q^2] = [0, 1] = 0_{\mathbb{Q}}
$$

Best of all, we have completely cured the division defect of $\mathbb{Z}$! When is a rational number $x = [p, q] \in \mathbb{Q}$ non-zero? We have $[p, q] = [0, 1] \iff p \cdot 1 = q \cdot 0 \iff p = 0$. Therefore, $x = [p, q] \neq 0_{\mathbb{Q}}$ if and only if its numerator satisfies $p \neq 0$ (i.e., $p \in \mathbb{Z}^*$). Because $p \in \mathbb{Z}^*$, we are legally allowed to flip the pair upside down so that $p$ sits in the denominator! The flipped fraction $x^{-1} := [q, p] \in \mathbb{Q}$ is the **multiplicative inverse** (reciprocal) of $[p, q]$, because
$$
[p, q] \cdot [q, p] = [p \cdot q, \, q \cdot p] = [pq, pq] = [1, 1] = 1_{\mathbb{Q}}
$$
Moreover, for any integers $p \in \mathbb{Z}$ and $q \in \mathbb{Z}^*$, the formal fraction $[p, q]$ is literally the unique solution in $\mathbb{Q}$ to the linear equation $\iota(q) \cdot x = \iota(p)$, because
$$
[q, 1] \cdot [p, q] = [qp, 1 \cdot q] = [p \cdot q, 1 \cdot q] = [p, 1]
$$
From now on, we identify each integer $n \in \mathbb{Z}$ with its image $[n, 1] \in \mathbb{Q}$ and write $0$ and $1$ for $[0, 1]$ and $[1, 1]$.

## Ordered Fields and Positivity

With the construction of $\mathbb{Q}$, we have arrived at our very first example of a **field**—and when equipped with its natural ordering, an **ordered field**.

If the word *field* sounds slightly abstract, you have actually worked with fields extensively in **Linear Algebra**! Remember that whenever you define a vector space $V$, you must specify a *field of scalars* $F$ (in Linear Algebra, usually $F = \mathbb{R}$ or $F = \mathbb{C}$, or sometimes $\mathbb{Q}$). Why did Linear Algebra require scalars to come from a field rather than just from the integers $\mathbb{Z}$? Because solving systems of linear equations, row-reducing matrices, and scaling vectors to unit length all require that you can **add, subtract, multiply, and divide by any non-zero scalar**, and that these operations obey the familiar associative, commutative, and distributive laws. Any algebraic system with two operations ($+$ and $\cdot$) satisfying those exact rules is called a **field**.

Because both the rational numbers $\mathbb{Q}$ and the real numbers $\mathbb{R}$ (which we will construct in Section 0.5) are **ordered fields**, stating the axioms of a field and an ordered field right now gives us a huge strategic advantage: *every single algebraic and inequality rule we prove from these axioms will automatically hold in both $\mathbb{Q}$ and $\mathbb{R}$ (and in any other ordered field)!*

> [!abstract] Definition: Field
> A **field** is a set $F$ equipped with two binary operations $+: F \times F \to F$ (called **addition**) and $\cdot: F \times F \to F$ (called **multiplication**) satisfying the following nine axioms:
> 1. **Additive Axioms:**
>    - *(A1) Associativity of Addition:* $(x + y) + z = x + (y + z)$ for all $x, y, z \in F$.
>    - *(A2) Commutativity of Addition:* $x + y = y + x$ for all $x, y \in F$.
>    - *(A3) Additive Identity:* There exists an element $0 \in F$ such that $x + 0 = x$ for all $x \in F$.
>    - *(A4) Additive Inverses:* For every $x \in F$, there exists an element $-x \in F$ such that $x + (-x) = 0$.
> 2. **Multiplicative Axioms:**
>    - *(M1) Associativity of Multiplication:* $(x \cdot y) \cdot z = x \cdot (y \cdot z)$ for all $x, y, z \in F$.
>    - *(M2) Commutativity of Multiplication:* $x \cdot y = y \cdot x$ for all $x, y \in F$.
>    - *(M3) Multiplicative Identity:* There exists an element $1 \in F$ with $1 \neq 0$ such that $x \cdot 1 = x$ for all $x \in F$.
>    - *(M4) Multiplicative Inverses:* For every $x \in F$ with $x \neq 0$, there exists an element $x^{-1} \in F$ such that $x \cdot x^{-1} = 1$.
> 3. **Distributive Law:**
>    - *(D) Distributivity:* $x \cdot (y + z) = x \cdot y + x \cdot z$ for all $x, y, z \in F$.

In any field $F$, we write $x - y$ as shorthand for $x + (-y)$, and for $y \neq 0$, we write $\frac{x}{y}$ or $x / y$ as shorthand for $x \cdot y^{-1}$.

> [!note] Theoretical Remark: Why Do We Require $1 \neq 0$ in Axiom (M3)?
> You might wonder why Axiom (M3) explicitly stipulates that $1 \neq 0$. Remember that in any ring or field, $0 \cdot x = 0$ for all $x \in F$ (because $0 \cdot x = (0 + 0) \cdot x = 0 \cdot x + 0 \cdot x$, and subtracting $0 \cdot x$ gives $0 = 0 \cdot x$). If $1 = 0$ were allowed, then for every element $x \in F$ we would have
> $$
> x = x \cdot 1 = x \cdot 0 = 0
> $$
> forcing $F = \{0\}$ to be the degenerate one-point set (the "zero ring"), where division is meaningless. Requiring $1 \neq 0$ ensures that every field has at least two distinct elements ($0$ and $1$).

Next, what makes a field an **ordered field**? In Calculus, you don't just add and multiply numbers—you compare their sizes using inequalities ($<$ and $>$) and manipulate those inequalities by adding the same quantity to both sides or multiplying both sides by a positive number. An **ordered field** is simply a field $F$ equipped with a strict order relation $<$ that is compatible with addition and multiplication in the exact way you use in Calculus.

> [!abstract] Definition: Ordered Field
> An **ordered field** is a field $F$ equipped with a binary relation $<$ on $F$ satisfying the following four order axioms:
