> 1. *(O1) Trichotomy:* For every $x, y \in F$, **exactly one** of the following three relations holds:
>    $$
>    x < y, \qquad x = y, \qquad \text{or} \qquad y < x
>    $$
> 2. *(O2) Transitivity:* For all $x, y, z \in F$, if $x < y$ and $y < z$, then $x < z$.
> 3. *(O3) Additive Compatibility (Translation Invariance):* For all $x, y, z \in F$, if $x < y$, then $x + z < y + z$.
> 4. *(O4) Multiplicative Compatibility (Positivity Preservation):* For all $x, y \in F$, if $0 < x$ and $0 < y$, then $0 < x \cdot y$.

In any ordered field $F$, we write $x > y$ to mean $y < x$, we write $x \le y$ to mean "$x < y$ or $x = y$," and we call an element $x \in F$ **positive** if $x > 0$ and **negative** if $x < 0$. Notice how Axioms (O3) and (O1)–(O2) mean that the entire order relation $<$ is completely determined by which elements are positive: adding $-x$ to both sides of $x < y$ via (O3) shows that
$$
x < y \iff 0 < y - x
$$
That is, $x < y$ means precisely that the difference $y - x$ is positive!

### Ordering the Rational Numbers $\mathbb{Q}$

How do we define positivity and order on our newly constructed set of rational numbers $\mathbb{Q}$?

Notice first that every rational number $[p, q] \in \mathbb{Q}$ can be written with a **positive integer denominator**. Why? Because for any $q \in \mathbb{Z}^*$, trichotomy in $\mathbb{Z}$ tells us either $q > 0$ or $q < 0$. If $q < 0$, then $-q > 0$, and since $[-p, -q] = [p \cdot (-1), q \cdot (-1)] = [p, q]$ by our cancellation law, we can always replace $[p, q]$ by $[-p, -q]$ so that the denominator is positive!

When a fraction $[p, q]$ is written with a positive denominator $q > 0$, it should be positive ($[p, q] > 0$) if and only if its numerator is positive ($p > 0$). What if we don't want to bother rewriting $[p, q]$ with a positive denominator first? Notice that a fraction $\frac{p}{q}$ is positive when $p$ and $q$ have the *same sign* (both positive or both negative)—which in $\mathbb{Z}$ is equivalent to saying that their product $p \cdot q$ is a positive integer ($p \cdot q > 0$)!

> [!abstract] Definition: Positivity and Order on $\mathbb{Q}$
> A rational number $x = [p, q] \in \mathbb{Q}$ is said to be **positive**, written $x > 0$, if
> $$
> p \cdot q > 0 \quad \text{in } \mathbb{Z}
> $$
> (Equivalently, if $q > 0$, then $[p, q] > 0 \iff p > 0$ in $\mathbb{Z}$.) For any two rational numbers $x, y \in \mathbb{Q}$, we define the strict order relation $<$ by
> $$
> x < y \iff y - x > 0
> $$

Let us verify that this order on $\mathbb{Q}$ is well-defined and turns $\mathbb{Q}$ into an ordered field, and let us simultaneously prove the workhorse inequality rules that hold in **every** ordered field $F$.

> [!theorem] $\mathbb{Q}$ Is an Ordered Field, and Fundamental Laws of Any Ordered Field
> Equipped with addition $+$, multiplication $\cdot$, and the order relation $<$ defined above, $(\mathbb{Q}, +, \cdot, <)$ is an **ordered field**. Moreover, in *any* ordered field $(F, +, \cdot, <)$, the following properties hold for all $x, y, z \in F$:
> 1. **Sign Reversal under Negation:** $x > 0 \iff -x < 0$, and more generally, $x < y \iff -y < -x$.
> 2. **Multiplication by Positives and Negatives:**
>    - If $x < y$ and $z > 0$, then $x \cdot z < y \cdot z$.
>    - If $x < y$ and $z < 0$, then $x \cdot z > y \cdot z$ (multiplying by a negative reverses inequalities).
> 3. **Positivity of Squares and of $1$:** For every non-zero $x \in F \setminus \{0\}$, its square is strictly positive:
>    $$
>    x^2 > 0
>    $$
>    In particular, the multiplicative identity is strictly positive: $1 > 0$ (and hence $-1 < 0$).
> 4. **Positivity and Reversal of Reciprocals:** If $x > 0$, then $x^{-1} > 0$. Moreover, if $0 < x < y$, then
>    $$
>    0 < y^{-1} < x^{-1}
>    $$

**Proof:**
First let us verify that $(\mathbb{Q}, +, \cdot, <)$ is an ordered field. The field axioms (A1)–(A4), (M1)–(M4), and (D) follow directly from the commutative ring axioms of $\mathbb{Z}$ together with our verification that $0_{\mathbb{Q}} = [0, 1]$, $1_{\mathbb{Q}} = [1, 1] \neq [0, 1]$, $-[p, q] = [-p, q]$, and $[p, q]^{-1} = [q, p]$ for $p \neq 0$. For instance, to verify distributivity $[p, q] \cdot ([r, s] + [t, u]) = [p, q] \cdot [r, s] + [p, q] \cdot [t, u]$, notice that if we represent the three rationals with a common denominator $d = qsu \in \mathbb{Z}^*$ as $[A, d], [B, d], [C, d]$, distributivity in $\mathbb{Q}$ reduces immediately to distributivity in $\mathbb{Z}$.

To check that positivity on $\mathbb{Q}$ is well-defined, suppose $[p, q] = [p', q']$. By writing both representatives with positive denominators (replacing $(p, q)$ by $(-p, -q)$ if $q < 0$), we may assume $q > 0$ and $q' > 0$. Since $p q' = q p'$ in $\mathbb{Z}$ and $q, q' > 0$, the order laws of $\mathbb{Z}$ imply that $p > 0 \iff p q' > 0 \iff q p' > 0 \iff p' > 0$. Thus positivity is independent of the representative. Furthermore, if $x = [p, q]$ and $y = [r, s]$ are written with $q > 0$ and $s > 0$, then $qs > 0$, and $y - x = [qr - ps, qs] > 0 \iff qr - ps > 0 \iff ps < qr$ in $\mathbb{Z}$. From this, trichotomy (O1), transitivity (O2), additive compatibility (O3), and multiplicative compatibility (O4) on $\mathbb{Q}$ follow immediately from the corresponding order properties of $\mathbb{Z}$.

Now let $(F, +, \cdot, <)$ be **any** ordered field, and let us deduce properties (1)–(4) purely from the axioms:
1. **Sign Reversal:** Suppose $x > 0$ (i.e., $0 < x$). Adding $-x$ to both sides using Axiom (O3) gives $0 + (-x) < x + (-x)$, which simplifies via (A3) and (A4) to $-x < 0$. Conversely, if $-x < 0$, adding $x$ to both sides gives $0 < x$. More generally, if $x < y$, adding $(-x) + (-y)$ to both sides gives $x + (-x) + (-y) < y + (-x) + (-y)$, which simplifies to $-y < -x$.
2. **Multiplication by Positives and Negatives:** Suppose $x < y$. Adding $-x$ to both sides via (O3) gives $y - x > 0$.
   - If $z > 0$, then since $y - x > 0$ and $z > 0$, Axiom (O4) gives $(y - x) \cdot z > 0$. By distributivity, $yz - xz > 0$, and adding $xz$ to both sides yields $xz < yz$.
   - If $z < 0$, then by Part 1 we have $-z > 0$. Since $y - x > 0$ and $-z > 0$, Axiom (O4) gives $(y - x) \cdot (-z) > 0$. Expanding via distributivity gives $xz - yz > 0$, and adding $yz$ to both sides yields $xz > yz$.
3. **Positivity of Squares and of $1$:** Let $x \in F$ with $x \neq 0$. By Trichotomy (O1), either $x > 0$ or $x < 0$:
   - **Case 1 ($x > 0$):** By Axiom (O4) applied to $x > 0$ and $x > 0$, we have $x^2 = x \cdot x > 0$.
   - **Case 2 ($x < 0$):** By Part 1, $-x > 0$. Applying Axiom (O4) to $-x > 0$ and $-x > 0$ gives $(-x) \cdot (-x) > 0$. Since $(-x)(-x) = x^2$ in any ring/field, we again have $x^2 > 0$.
   Finally, by Axiom (M3), $1 \neq 0$, and $1^2 = 1 \cdot 1 = 1$. Applying $x^2 > 0$ to $x = 1 \neq 0$ immediately proves that $1 = 1^2 > 0$!
4. **Positivity and Reversal of Reciprocals:** Suppose $x > 0$. Could $x^{-1} \le 0$? Since $x \cdot x^{-1} = 1 \neq 0$, we cannot have $x^{-1} = 0$. If $x^{-1} < 0$, then multiplying $x^{-1} < 0$ by $x > 0$ (using Part 2) would give $1 = x \cdot x^{-1} < 0 \cdot x = 0$, contradicting $1 > 0$ from Part 3! By trichotomy, we must have $x^{-1} > 0$.
   Now suppose $0 < x < y$. Then $x > 0$ and $y > 0$, so $x^{-1} > 0$ and $y^{-1} > 0$, and by Axiom (O4) their product is positive: $x^{-1} y^{-1} > 0$. Multiplying both sides of $x < y$ by the positive element $x^{-1} y^{-1} > 0$ (via Part 2) yields
   $$
   x \cdot (x^{-1} y^{-1}) < y \cdot (x^{-1} y^{-1}) \implies y^{-1} < x^{-1}
   $$
   Combining this with $y^{-1} > 0$ gives $0 < y^{-1} < x^{-1}$. $\blacksquare$

> [!note] Theoretical Remark: Why the Complex Numbers $\mathbb{C}$ Cannot Be an Ordered Field
> Look at Property 3 of our theorem above: in **every** ordered field $F$, squares of non-zero elements are strictly positive ($x^2 > 0$ for all $x \neq 0$), and $1 > 0$, so $-1 < 0$.
>
> Recall from Linear Algebra that the complex numbers $\mathbb{C} = \{a + bi : a, b \in \mathbb{R}\}$ form a wonderful field in which $i^2 = -1$. People sometimes ask: *"Why don't we write inequalities like $i > 0$ or $1 + i < 2 + 3i$ for complex numbers?"* Property 3 gives the devastating answer: **it is mathematically impossible to define an order relation $<$ on $\mathbb{C}$ that makes $(\mathbb{C}, +, \cdot, <)$ into an ordered field!** Why? Because if such an order $<$ existed on $\mathbb{C}$, then since $i \neq 0$, Property 3 would force $i^2 > 0$, meaning $-1 > 0$. Yet Property 3 also forces $1 = 1^2 > 0$, and adding $-1$ gives $0 > -1$, contradicting Trichotomy (O1)! Thus, the price we pay in $\mathbb{C}$ for gaining a square root of $-1$ is the permanent loss of compatible ordering.

### Density of the Rationals: Why $\mathbb{Q}$ Looks Continuous at First Glance

Between the integers $0$ and $1$, there are no other integers—$\mathbb{Z}$ consists of isolated, discrete stepping stones. What about $\mathbb{Q}$? In $\mathbb{Q}$ (and in fact in any ordered field $F$), because $1 > 0$, we have $2 := 1 + 1 > 1 + 0 = 1 > 0$, so $2$ is a positive element with a positive reciprocal $2^{-1} = \frac{1}{2} > 0$. Consequently, given *any* two elements $r, s \in \mathbb{Q}$ with $r < s$, we can form their **arithmetic midpoint** (average) $m := \frac{r + s}{2} \in \mathbb{Q}$ and observe that
$$
r = \frac{r + r}{2} < \frac{r + s}{2} < \frac{s + s}{2} = s
$$
Wait—think about what that means! Between $r$ and $s$, there is the rational midpoint $m_1 = \frac{r + s}{2}$. Between $r$ and $m_1$, there is another rational midpoint $m_2 = \frac{r + m_1}{2}$. Continuing forever, between any two distinct rational numbers—no matter how microscopically close together they are—there are **infinitely many** other rational numbers! We say that $\mathbb{Q}$ is **densely ordered**.

## The Incompleteness of the Rationals

At this stage in our journey, let us pause and ask an honest question: **Why isn't $\mathbb{Q}$ good enough for doing Calculus and Real Analysis?**

After all, $\mathbb{Q}$ is an ordered field: we can add, subtract, multiply, divide by any non-zero number, and compare sizes with $<$. Moreover, as we just saw, the rationals are densely packed along the number line with no visible gaps of positive width between them. Why on earth do we need to go through the trouble of constructing the real numbers $\mathbb{R}$ in Section 0.5?

The shocking answer—first discovered in geometric disguise by the ancient Pythagoreans around 500 BCE—is that despite being densely packed, **the rational number line $\mathbb{Q}$ is riddled with infinitely many microscopic "pinhole gaps"**!

Think of the simplest geometric figure imaginable: a unit square whose sides have length $1$. By the Pythagorean Theorem, the diagonal of this unit square has a well-defined geometric length $x$ satisfying
$$
x^2 = 1^2 + 1^2 = 2
$$
Or think of Calculus: consider the continuous polynomial function $f(x) = x^2 - 2$. At $x = 1$, the graph is below the $x$-axis ($f(1) = -1 < 0$); at $x = 2$, the graph is above the $x$-axis ($f(2) = 2 > 0$). For the Intermediate Value Theorem of Calculus to hold, the curve $y = x^2 - 2$ *must* cross the $x$-axis at some point $x \in (1, 2)$ where $x^2 = 2$. Yet if our universe of numbers is only $\mathbb{Q}$, the curve passes right through a phantom pinhole in the $x$-axis without ever hitting a rational point, because **no rational number has a square equal to $2$**!

> [!theorem] Irrationality of $\sqrt{2}$ (No Rational Square Equals $2$)
> There is no rational number $x \in \mathbb{Q}$ such that $x^2 = 2$.

**Proof:**
Suppose for the sake of contradiction that there exists a rational number $x \in \mathbb{Q}$ such that $x^2 = 2$. Since $(-x)^2 = x^2 = 2$ and $0^2 = 0 \neq 2$, we may assume $x > 0$, so we can write $x = \frac{p}{q}$ where $p, q \in \mathbb{N}^+$ are positive integers.

Furthermore, we can choose the representative fraction $\frac{p}{q}$ in **lowest terms**—meaning that $p$ and $q$ have **no common integer factors greater than $1$** (in particular, $p$ and $q$ are not both even). Why is this guaranteed? Consider the non-empty set of all possible positive integer denominators for $x$, namely $D := \{q \in \mathbb{N}^+ : \text{there exists } p \in \mathbb{N}^+ \text{ with } x = p / q\}$. By the **Well-Ordering Principle** on $\mathbb{N}$ (proved in Section 0.2), $D$ has a smallest element $q_0 \in \mathbb{N}^+$, with corresponding numerator $p_0 \in \mathbb{N}^+$. If $p_0$ and $q_0$ were both divisible by $2$, say $p_0 = 2a$ and $q_0 = 2b$, then $x = \frac{2a}{2b} = \frac{a}{b}$ would have a strictly smaller positive denominator $b = q_0 / 2 < q_0$, contradicting the minimality of $q_0$! Thus $p$ and $q$ cannot both be even.

Now, squaring $x = \frac{p}{q}$ gives $\frac{p^2}{q^2} = 2$, and multiplying both sides by $q^2$ yields the integer equation
$$
p^2 = 2q^2
$$
Recall that an integer $n \in \mathbb{Z}$ is **even** if $n = 2k$ for some $k \in \mathbb{Z}$, and **odd** if $n = 2k + 1$ for some $k \in \mathbb{Z}$. The equation $p^2 = 2q^2$ tells us that $p^2$ is even. Could $p$ itself be odd? If $p$ were odd, say $p = 2k + 1$, then expanding $p^2$ would give
$$
p^2 = (2k + 1)^2 = 4k^2 + 4k + 1 = 2(2k^2 + 2k) + 1
$$
which is odd! Since $p^2$ is even, $p$ cannot be odd; therefore, **$p$ must be even**.

Because $p$ is even, we can write $p = 2m$ for some integer $m \in \mathbb{N}^+$. Substituting $p = 2m$ back into our equation $p^2 = 2q^2$ gives
$$
(2m)^2 = 2q^2 \implies 4m^2 = 2q^2
$$
Dividing both sides by $2$ (i.e., writing $2(2m^2 - q^2) = 0$ and using the fact that $\mathbb{Z}$ has no zero divisors and $2 \neq 0$), we obtain
$$
q^2 = 2m^2
$$
Look at that equation: it has the exact same form as $p^2 = 2q^2$! It says that $q^2$ is even, which by the exact same reasoning forces **$q$ to be even** as well.

We have proved that **both $p$ and $q$ are even**, directly contradicting the fact that $\frac{p}{q}$ was chosen in lowest terms so that $p$ and $q$ are not both even! Therefore, no rational number $x \in \mathbb{Q}$ can satisfy $x^2 = 2$. $\blacksquare$

### Upper Bounds, Supremums, and the Least Upper Bound Property

Wait a minute: if we are currently living inside the universe $\mathbb{Q}$, the number $\sqrt{2}$ *does not exist yet*! So how can we describe or detect the "hole" at $\sqrt{2}$ **intrinsically**—using only elements and subsets of $\mathbb{Q}$ itself, without cheating and mentioning a phantom number $\sqrt{2}$ from the outside?

Here is the brilliant geometric insight of Richard Dedekind: even though the point $\sqrt{2}$ is missing from $\mathbb{Q}$, the rational numbers that lie to the *left* of the hole, namely
$$
A = \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p > 0 \text{ and } p^2 < 2\}
$$
and the rational numbers that lie to the *right* of the hole, namely
$$
B = \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p > 0 \text{ and } p^2 > 2\}
$$
are completely legitimate subsets of $\mathbb{Q}$! As you march through the elements of $A$ from left to right ($1, 1.4, 1.41, 1.414, 1.4142, \dots$), the elements of $A$ are fenced in on the right by all the elements of $B$ (such as $2, 1.5, 1.42, \dots$). Yet if you look for the **tightest possible right-hand fencepost**—the *least* upper bound—where the set $A$ stops and the set $B$ begins, **that fencepost is missing from $\mathbb{Q}$**!

Because this notion of a "tightest fencepost" (called the **supremum** or **least upper bound**) is the single most important concept in all of Real Analysis—the foundational bedrock on which limits, continuity, derivatives, and Riemann integrals are built—let us define it carefully and unpack every detail.

> [!abstract] Definition: Boundedness, Supremum, Infimum, and the Least Upper Bound Property
> Let $(S, <)$ be any ordered set (such as $\mathbb{Q}$ or $\mathbb{R}$) and let $E \subseteq S$ be a subset.
> 1. **Upper Bound:** We say that $E$ is **bounded above** if there exists an element $\beta \in S$ such that
>    $$
>    x \le \beta \quad \text{for all } x \in E
>    $$
>    Any such element $\beta \in S$ is called an **upper bound** of $E$.
> 2. **Supremum (Least Upper Bound):** Suppose $E \subseteq S$ is bounded above. An element $\alpha \in S$ is called the **least upper bound** or **supremum** of $E$, written $\alpha = \sup E$, if it satisfies two conditions:
>    - *(i) $\alpha$ is an upper bound of $E$:* $x \le \alpha$ for all $x \in E$.
>    - *(ii) $\alpha$ is the smallest of all upper bounds of $E$:* If $\gamma \in S$ is any element with $\gamma < \alpha$, then $\gamma$ is **not** an upper bound of $E$ (meaning there exists at least one $x \in E$ such that $\gamma < x \le \alpha$). Equivalently, for every upper bound $\beta \in S$ of $E$, we have $\alpha \le \beta$.
> 3. **Lower Bound and Infimum (Greatest Lower Bound):** Similarly, $E$ is **bounded below** if there exists $\beta \in S$ such that $\beta \le x$ for all $x \in E$ (called a **lower bound** of $E$). If $E$ is bounded below, an element $\alpha \in S$ is called the **greatest lower bound** or **infimum** of $E$, written $\alpha = \inf E$, if $\alpha$ is a lower bound of $E$ and $\beta \le \alpha$ for every lower bound $\beta$ of $E$.
> 4. **Least Upper Bound Property (Dedekind Completeness):** An ordered set $(S, <)$ is said to have the **Least Upper Bound Property** (or to be **Dedekind complete**) if **every** non-empty subset $E \subseteq S$ that is bounded above in $S$ possesses a supremum $\sup E \in S$.

Let us pause to make sure the intuition behind $\sup E$ is crystal clear:
- **An upper bound $\beta$ is a ceiling above the set $E$:** No element of $E$ can rise above $\beta$. If a set $E$ has one upper bound $\beta$, it automatically has infinitely many upper bounds ($\beta + 1, \beta + 2, \beta + 100, \dots$), because raising a ceiling keeps it above $E$.
- **The supremum $\alpha = \sup E$ is the lowest possible ceiling:** Imagine lowering a horizontal ceiling down from above toward the set $E$ until the ceiling *just touches* the top of $E$ and cannot be lowered any further without bumping into an element of $E$. That resting height is $\alpha = \sup E$! Condition (ii) says precisely that if you lower the ceiling even slightly to $\gamma < \alpha$, some element $x \in E$ pokes above $\gamma$ ($x > \gamma$).
- **Uniqueness of the Supremum:** Can a set $E$ have two different supremums $\alpha_1$ and $\alpha_2$? Never! Since $\alpha_1$ is the least upper bound and $\alpha_2$ is an upper bound, $\alpha_1 \le \alpha_2$; symmetrically, $\alpha_2 \le \alpha_1$, so $\alpha_1 = \alpha_2$.
- **Does $\sup E$ have to belong to the set $E$ itself?** **No!** This is the most common beginner pitfall, so let us illustrate it with a concrete example right away.

> [!example] Example: Maximum vs. Supremum on Open and Closed Rational Intervals
> Inside the ordered set $S = \mathbb{Q}$, consider the two subsets
> $$
> E_1 := \{x \in \mathbb{Q} : 0 \le x \le 1\} \quad \text{and} \quad E_2 := \{x \in \mathbb{Q} : 0 \le x < 1\}
> $$
> Find $\sup E_1$ and $\sup E_2$ in $\mathbb{Q}$, and determine whether each supremum belongs to the respective set.

**Solution:**
1. **For $E_1 = \{x \in \mathbb{Q} : 0 \le x \le 1\}$:**
   - First, by definition of $E_1$, every $x \in E_1$ satisfies $x \le 1$, so $1 \in \mathbb{Q}$ is an upper bound of $E_1$.
   - Second, could any $\gamma < 1$ be an upper bound of $E_1$? No, because the element $x = 1$ belongs to $E_1$ itself, and $1 > \gamma$!
   Thus $\sup E_1 = 1$, and in this case $\sup E_1 \in E_1$ (whenever $\sup E \in E$, it is simply the **maximum** or **largest element** of $E$, written $\max E$).
2. **For $E_2 = \{x \in \mathbb{Q} : 0 \le x < 1\}$:**
   - Notice first that $E_2$ has **no largest element (no maximum)**: if someone claims $p \in E_2$ is the largest element of $E_2$, then since $p < 1$, the midpoint $q = \frac{p + 1}{2} \in \mathbb{Q}$ satisfies $p < q < 1$, so $q \in E_2$ is strictly larger than $p$!
   - Even though $E_2$ has no maximum, does $E_2$ still have a **supremum** in $\mathbb{Q}$? Let us check $\alpha = 1 \in \mathbb{Q}$:
     - *(i)* Every $x \in E_2$ satisfies $x < 1$, so $x \le 1$; hence $1$ is an upper bound of $E_2$.
     - *(ii)* Suppose $\gamma \in \mathbb{Q}$ satisfies $\gamma < 1$. Is $\gamma$ an upper bound of $E_2$? If $\gamma < 0$, then $0 \in E_2$ satisfies $0 > \gamma$. If $0 \le \gamma < 1$, then the midpoint $x := \frac{\gamma + 1}{2} \in \mathbb{Q}$ satisfies $0 \le \gamma < x < 1$, so $x \in E_2$ pokes strictly above $\gamma$ ($x > \gamma$). Thus no $\gamma < 1$ is an upper bound of $E_2$!
   Therefore, $\sup E_2 = 1$ in $\mathbb{Q}$, even though $1 \notin E_2$! $\blacksquare$

### Proving That $\mathbb{Q}$ Fails the Least Upper Bound Property

Notice why $E_2 = \{x \in \mathbb{Q} : 0 \le x < 1\}$ still had a supremum in $\mathbb{Q}$: even though the right endpoint $1$ was excluded from $E_2$, the number $1$ itself *exists in the ambient space $\mathbb{Q}$* to serve as the least upper bound $\sup E_2 = 1$.

Now look at what happens when we slice $\mathbb{Q}$ at the missing point $\sqrt{2}$ instead of at $1$! Let us prove rigorously—using only rational arithmetic, without ever assuming the existence of $\mathbb{R}$ or $\sqrt{2}$—that the set of positive rationals whose square is less than $2$ is non-empty and bounded above in $\mathbb{Q}$, yet **has no supremum in $\mathbb{Q}$**.

> [!theorem] Failure of the Least Upper Bound Property in $\mathbb{Q}$
> Consider the two subsets of positive rational numbers:
> $$
> A := \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p > 0 \text{ and } p^2 < 2\}, \qquad B := \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p > 0 \text{ and } p^2 > 2\}
> $$
> Then $A$ is non-empty and bounded above in $\mathbb{Q}$, but:
> 1. $A$ has **no largest element** (for every $p \in A$, there exists $q \in A$ with $p < q$).
> 2. $B$ has **no smallest element** (for every $p \in B$, there exists $q \in B$ with $q < p$).
> 3. $A$ has **no supremum in $\mathbb{Q}$** (and $B$ has no infimum in $\mathbb{Q}$). Consequently, the ordered field $\mathbb{Q}$ **fails the Least Upper Bound Property**.

**Proof:**
First let us verify that $A$ is non-empty and bounded above in $\mathbb{Q}$:
- Since $1 \in \mathbb{Q}$ satisfies $1 > 0$ and $1^2 = 1 < 2$, we have $1 \in A$, so $A \neq \emptyset$.
- Similarly, $2 \in \mathbb{Q}$ satisfies $2 > 0$ and $2^2 = 4 > 2$, so $2 \in B$. In fact, **every element $b \in B$ is an upper bound for $A$**: if $a \in A$ and $b \in B$, then $a^2 < 2 < b^2$, so $b^2 - a^2 = (b - a)(b + a) > 0$. Since $a > 0$ and $b > 0$, we have $b + a > 0$, which forces $b - a > 0$, i.e., $a < b$. In particular, $2$ is an upper bound for $A$.

Now how do we prove that $A$ has no largest element and $B$ has no smallest element? If we are given a positive rational $p \in A$ (so $p^2 < 2$), we need an explicit algebraic formula that takes $p$ and produces a *slightly larger* rational $q > p$ whose square is *still* less than $2$ ($q^2 < 2$). Where can we find such a formula?

Here is the intuition behind the magic formula we are about to write down: if $p^2$ is slightly less than $2$, how much do we need to add to $p$, say $q = p + h$ with $h > 0$, so that $(p + h)^2 = p^2 + 2ph + h^2 < 2$? Since $p < 2$ for all $p \in A$, if $h < 1$ we can roughly bound $2ph + h^2$ by $(p + 2)h$, and setting $(p + 2)h = 2 - p^2$ suggests choosing the step size
$$
h = \frac{2 - p^2}{p + 2} = -\frac{p^2 - 2}{p + 2}
$$
Motivated by this, for **any** positive rational $p \in \mathbb{Q}$ ($p > 0$), let us define a new rational number $q \in \mathbb{Q}$ by
$$
q := p - \frac{p^2 - 2}{p + 2} = \frac{p(p + 2) - (p^2 - 2)}{p + 2} = \frac{2p + 2}{p + 2}
$$
Notice immediately from the simplified expression $q = \frac{2p + 2}{p + 2}$ that because $p > 0$, both numerator $2p + 2 > 0$ and denominator $p + 2 > 0$ are positive rationals, so $q \in \mathbb{Q}$ and $q > 0$.

Now let us compute how far $q^2$ is from $2$ by calculating $q^2 - 2$:
$$
\begin{aligned}
q^2 - 2 &= \left(\frac{2p + 2}{p + 2}\right)^2 - 2 \\
        &= \frac{(4p^2 + 8p + 4) - 2(p^2 + 4p + 4)}{(p + 2)^2} \\
        &= \frac{4p^2 + 8p + 4 - 2p^2 - 8p - 8}{(p + 2)^2} \\
        &= \frac{2(p^2 - 2)}{(p + 2)^2}
\end{aligned}
$$
Look at the two boxed relationships we have just established for all $p > 0$:
$$
q - p = -\frac{p^2 - 2}{p + 2} \qquad \text{and} \qquad q^2 - 2 = \frac{2(p^2 - 2)}{(p + 2)^2}
$$
Since $p + 2 > 0$ and $(p + 2)^2 > 0$, the signs of $q - p$ and $q^2 - 2$ are completely controlled by the sign of $p^2 - 2$:
1. **Proof that $A$ has no largest element:** Suppose $p \in A$. Then $p > 0$ and $p^2 - 2 < 0$.
   - Because $p^2 - 2 < 0$ and $p + 2 > 0$, the first equation $q - p = -\frac{p^2 - 2}{p + 2}$ tells us that $q - p > 0$, so **$q > p$**.
   - Because $p^2 - 2 < 0$ and $(p + 2)^2 > 0$, the second equation $q^2 - 2 = \frac{2(p^2 - 2)}{(p + 2)^2}$ tells us that $q^2 - 2 < 0$, so $q^2 < 2$, which means **$q \in A$**!
   Thus, for every $p \in A$, we have constructed $q \in A$ with $q > p$. Therefore, $A$ has no largest element.
2. **Proof that $B$ has no smallest element:** Suppose $p \in B$. Then $p > 0$ and $p^2 - 2 > 0$.
   - Because $p^2 - 2 > 0$ and $p + 2 > 0$, the first equation $q - p = -\frac{p^2 - 2}{p + 2}$ tells us that $q - p < 0$, so **$q < p$**.
   - Because $p^2 - 2 > 0$ and $(p + 2)^2 > 0$, the second equation $q^2 - 2 = \frac{2(p^2 - 2)}{(p + 2)^2}$ tells us that $q^2 - 2 > 0$, so $q^2 > 2$, which means **$q \in B$**!
   Thus, for every $p \in B$, we have constructed $q \in B$ with $q < p$. Therefore, $B$ has no smallest element.
3. **Proof that $\sup A$ does not exist in $\mathbb{Q}$:** Suppose for the sake of contradiction that $A$ has a supremum $\alpha = \sup A \in \mathbb{Q}$. Since $1 \in A$ and $\alpha$ is an upper bound of $A$, we must have $\alpha \ge 1 > 0$, so $\alpha$ is a positive rational number. By Trichotomy (O1) in $\mathbb{Q}$, exactly one of $\alpha^2 < 2$, $\alpha^2 = 2$, or $\alpha^2 > 2$ holds:
   - By the Irrationality of $\sqrt{2}$, we know $\alpha^2 = 2$ is **impossible** for $\alpha \in \mathbb{Q}$!
   - **If $\alpha^2 < 2$ (so $\alpha \in A$):** By Part 1, since $A$ has no largest element, there exists an element $q \in A$ with $\alpha < q$. But that means an element $q \in A$ exceeds $\alpha$, contradicting Requirement (i) that $\alpha$ is an upper bound of $A$!
   - **If $\alpha^2 > 2$ (so $\alpha \in B$):** By Part 2, since $B$ has no smallest element, there exists an element $q \in B$ with $q < \alpha$. But as we proved at the beginning, *every* element of $B$ (including $q \in B$) is an upper bound for $A$! Thus $q$ is an upper bound of $A$ that is strictly smaller than $\alpha$ ($q < \alpha$), contradicting Requirement (ii) that $\alpha$ is the *least* upper bound of $A$!

In every case we reach a contradiction. Therefore, the set $A$ has **no supremum in $\mathbb{Q}$**, proving that $\mathbb{Q}$ fails the Least Upper Bound Property. $\blacksquare$

> [!example] Example: Iterating the Rational Approximation Recipe for $\sqrt{2}$
> Starting with $p_0 = 1 \in A$, apply the formula $p_{n+1} = \frac{2p_n + 2}{p_n + 2}$ twice to compute $p_1$ and $p_2$, and verify directly that $1 = p_0 < p_1 < p_2$ and $p_0^2 < p_1^2 < p_2^2 < 2$. Similarly, starting with $q_0 = 2 \in B$, compute $q_1$ and $q_2$, and verify that $2 = q_0 > q_1 > q_2$ and $q_0^2 > q_1^2 > q_2^2 > 2$.

**Solution:**
1. **Approximating from below in $A$ (starting at $p_0 = 1$):**
   - For $n = 0$, substituting $p_0 = 1$ gives
     $$
     p_1 = \frac{2(1) + 2}{1 + 2} = \frac{4}{3} \approx 1.3333\dots
     $$
     Notice that $p_1 = \frac{4}{3} > 1 = p_0$, and $p_1^2 = \frac{16}{9} = 1.\overline{7} < \frac{18}{9} = 2$, so $p_1 \in A$.
   - For $n = 1$, substituting $p_1 = \frac{4}{3}$ gives
     $$
     p_2 = \frac{2(4/3) + 2}{(4/3) + 2} = \frac{8/3 + 6/3}{4/3 + 6/3} = \frac{14}{10} = \frac{7}{5} = 1.4
     $$
     Notice that $p_2 = \frac{7}{5} = \frac{21}{15} > \frac{20}{15} = \frac{4}{3} = p_1$, and $p_2^2 = \frac{49}{25} = 1.96 < \frac{50}{25} = 2$, so $p_2 \in A$.
   Thus $1 < \frac{4}{3} < \frac{7}{5}$ and $1 < \frac{16}{9} < \frac{49}{25} < 2$.
2. **Approximating from above in $B$ (starting at $q_0 = 2$):**
   - For $n = 0$, substituting $q_0 = 2$ gives
     $$
     q_1 = \frac{2(2) + 2}{2 + 2} = \frac{6}{4} = \frac{3}{2} = 1.5
     $$
     Notice that $q_1 = \frac{3}{2} < 2 = q_0$, and $q_1^2 = \frac{9}{4} = 2.25 > 2$, so $q_1 \in B$.
   - For $n = 1$, substituting $q_1 = \frac{3}{2}$ gives
     $$
     q_2 = \frac{2(3/2) + 2}{(3/2) + 2} = \frac{5}{7/2} = \frac{10}{7} \approx 1.42857\dots
     $$
     Notice that $q_2 = \frac{10}{7} = \frac{20}{14} < \frac{21}{14} = \frac{3}{2} = q_1$, and $q_2^2 = \frac{100}{49} > \frac{98}{49} = 2$, so $q_2 \in B$.
   Thus $2 > \frac{3}{2} > \frac{10}{7}$ and $4 > \frac{9}{4} > \frac{100}{49} > 2$. Notice how the elements of $A$ ($1 < \frac{4}{3} < \frac{7}{5} < \dots$) and the elements of $B$ ($\dots < \frac{10}{7} < \frac{3}{2} < 2$) pinch tighter and tighter toward the missing pinhole at $\sqrt{2} \approx 1.41421356\dots$ without ever meeting in $\mathbb{Q}$! $\blacksquare$

> [!summary] Summary: Where We Stand and Where We Are Going
> By taking equivalence classes of formal fractions $(p, q) \in \mathbb{Z} \times \mathbb{Z}^*$, we constructed the **rational numbers $\mathbb{Q}$**, our prototype of an **ordered field**. Yet $\mathbb{Q}$ fails the **Least Upper Bound Property**: bounded sets like $A = \{p \in \mathbb{Q}^+ : p^2 < 2\}$ have no supremum in $\mathbb{Q}$ because $\mathbb{Q}$ is full of irrational holes.
>
> In **Section 0.5**, we will turn this exact defect into our salvation: following Richard Dedekind, we will define a **real number** to *be* the left-hand slice (a **Dedekind cut**) determined by a position along the rational line, thereby plugging every single hole in $\mathbb{Q}$ at once and constructing the **complete ordered field $\mathbb{R}$**!

## 0.4 Exercises

### Problem 0.4.1: Every Ordered Field Contains a Unique Canonical Copy of $\mathbb{Q}$

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $(F, +, \cdot, <)$ be any ordered field with additive identity $0_F$ and multiplicative identity $1_F$. Recall that an injective map $\phi: \mathbb{Q} \to F$ is called an **order-preserving field homomorphism** (or **ordered field embedding**) if for all $x, y \in \mathbb{Q}$:
> - $\phi(x + y) = \phi(x) + \phi(y)$ and $\phi(x \cdot y) = \phi(x) \cdot \phi(y)$,
> - $\phi(0_{\mathbb{Q}}) = 0_F$ and $\phi(1_{\mathbb{Q}}) = 1_F$, and
> - $x < y \implies \phi(x) < \phi(y)$ in $F$.
>
> Define a map $\chi: \mathbb{N} \to F$ recursively using the Peano successor operation $S(n) = n + 1$ by setting $\chi(0) := 0_F$ and $\chi(S(n)) := \chi(n) + 1_F$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$.
> 1. Prove by mathematical induction that for all $n, m \in \mathbb{N}$, $\chi(n + m) = \chi(n) + \chi(m)$, $\chi(n \cdot m) = \chi(n) \cdot \chi(m)$, and if $n < m$ in $\mathbb{N}$, then $\chi(n) < \chi(m)$ in $F$. Deduce that $\chi(n) > 0_F$ (and hence $\chi(n) \neq 0_F$) for all $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$, and explain why this proves that **no finite field can ever be made into an ordered field**.
> 2. Prove that $\chi$ extends uniquely to an injective, strictly order-preserving field homomorphism $\tilde{\chi}: \mathbb{Q} \hookrightarrow F$ given by
>    $$
>    \tilde{\chi}([p, q]) := (\chi(a) - \chi(b)) \cdot (\chi(c) - \chi(d))^{-1}
>    $$
>    where $p = [a, b] \in \mathbb{Z}$ and $q = [c, d] \in \mathbb{Z}^*$ are represented as equivalence classes of natural numbers from Section 0.3. (This proves that $\mathbb{Q}$ is the **prime ordered field**: every ordered field contains a unique canonical copy of $\mathbb{Q}$ inside it!)

**Solution:**
**Part 1: Induction on $\mathbb{N}$ and Impossibility of Finite Ordered Fields.**
First, let us verify by induction on $m \in \mathbb{N}$ that $\chi(n + m) = \chi(n) + \chi(m)$ for any fixed $n \in \mathbb{N}$:
- **Base Case ($m = 0$):** Using $n + 0 = n$ in $\mathbb{N}$ and $\chi(0) = 0_F$, we have $\chi(n + 0) = \chi(n) = \chi(n) + 0_F = \chi(n) + \chi(0)$.
- **Inductive Step:** Suppose $\chi(n + m) = \chi(n) + \chi(m)$ holds for $m$. Then for $S(m)$, using the recursive definition $n + S(m) = S(n + m)$ in $\mathbb{N}$ and associativity of addition (A1) in $F$:
  $$
  \chi(n + S(m)) = \chi(S(n + m)) = \chi(n + m) + 1_F = (\chi(n) + \chi(m)) + 1_F = \chi(n) + (\chi(m) + 1_F) = \chi(n) + \chi(S(m))
  $$
Thus $\chi(n + m) = \chi(n) + \chi(m)$ for all $n, m \in \mathbb{N}$.

Next, let us verify by induction on $m \in \mathbb{N}$ that $\chi(n \cdot m) = \chi(n) \cdot \chi(m)$ for any fixed $n \in \mathbb{N}$:
- **Base Case ($m = 0$):** We have $\chi(n \cdot 0) = \chi(0) = 0_F = \chi(n) \cdot 0_F = \chi(n) \cdot \chi(0)$.
- **Inductive Step:** Suppose $\chi(n \cdot m) = \chi(n) \cdot \chi(m)$ holds for $m$. Using $n \cdot S(m) = n \cdot m + n$ in $\mathbb{N}$, our additive property $\chi(A + B) = \chi(A) + \chi(B)$, and distributivity (D) in $F$:
  $$
  \chi(n \cdot S(m)) = \chi(n \cdot m + n) = \chi(n \cdot m) + \chi(n) = \chi(n) \cdot \chi(m) + \chi(n) \cdot 1_F = \chi(n) \cdot (\chi(m) + 1_F) = \chi(n) \cdot \chi(S(m))
  $$
Thus $\chi(n \cdot m) = \chi(n) \cdot \chi(m)$ for all $n, m \in \mathbb{N}$.

Now let us prove by induction on $k \in \mathbb{N}^+$ that $\chi(k) > 0_F$ for every positive natural number $k \in \mathbb{N}^+$:
- **Base Case ($k = 1 = S(0)$):** We have $\chi(1) = \chi(0) + 1_F = 0_F + 1_F = 1_F$. As we proved in Section 0.4, in *every* ordered field $F$, $1_F = 1_F^2 > 0_F$. Thus $\chi(1) > 0_F$.
- **Inductive Step:** Suppose $\chi(k) > 0_F$ for some $k \in \mathbb{N}^+$. Since $1_F > 0_F$, adding $\chi(k)$ to $0_F < 1_F$ via Axiom (O3) gives $\chi(k) < \chi(k) + 1_F = \chi(S(k))$. By transitivity (O2), $0_F < \chi(k) < \chi(S(k))$, so $\chi(S(k)) > 0_F$.

Consequently, whenever $n < m$ in $\mathbb{N}$, by the definition of order on $\mathbb{N}$ (Section 0.2) there exists $k \in \mathbb{N}^+$ such that $m = n + k$. Applying $\chi$ gives $\chi(m) = \chi(n + k) = \chi(n) + \chi(k)$. Since $\chi(k) > 0_F$, adding $\chi(n)$ to both sides of $0_F < \chi(k)$ via (O3) yields $\chi(n) < \chi(n) + \chi(k) = \chi(m)$.

Because $n < m \implies \chi(n) < \chi(m)$, the map $\chi: \mathbb{N} \to F$ is **strictly increasing** and hence **injective** (if $n \neq m$, then either $n < m$ so $\chi(n) < \chi(m)$, or $m < n$ so $\chi(m) < \chi(n)$; in either case $\chi(n) \neq \chi(m)$ by Trichotomy). Since $\mathbb{N}$ is an infinite set and $\chi: \mathbb{N} \to F$ is injective, $F$ must contain the infinitely many distinct elements $\chi(0) < \chi(1) < \chi(2) < \dots$, which proves that **every ordered field is infinite** (no finite field like $\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}$ can ever be made into an ordered field!).

**Part 2: Unique Extension to $\mathbb{Z}$ and $\mathbb{Q}$.**
First we extend $\chi$ to a map $\hat{\chi}: \mathbb{Z} \to F$ by setting $\hat{\chi}([a, b]) := \chi(a) - \chi(b)$ for each integer $p = [a, b] \in \mathbb{Z}$ (where $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$).
- **Well-Definedness on $\mathbb{Z}$:** If $[a, b] = [a', b']$ in $\mathbb{Z}$, then $a + b' = b + a'$ in $\mathbb{N}$. Applying $\chi$ and using $\chi(x + y) = \chi(x) + \chi(y)$ gives $\chi(a) + \chi(b') = \chi(b) + \chi(a')$, and subtracting $\chi(b) + \chi(b')$ in $F$ yields $\chi(a) - \chi(b) = \chi(a') - \chi(b')$.
- **Ring Homomorphism and Order Preservation on $\mathbb{Z}$:** For $p = [a, b]$ and $r = [c, d]$, routine algebra in $F$ using the additive and multiplicative laws of $\chi$ shows $\hat{\chi}(p + r) = \hat{\chi}(p) + \hat{\chi}(r)$ and $\hat{\chi}(p \cdot r) = \hat{\chi}(p) \cdot \hat{\chi}(r)$. Moreover, if $p > 0$ in $\mathbb{Z}$ (meaning $a > b$ in $\mathbb{N}$), then $\chi(a) > \chi(b)$ in $F$ by Part 1, so $\hat{\chi}(p) = \chi(a) - \chi(b) > 0_F$. In particular, for every non-zero integer $q = [c, d] \in \mathbb{Z}^*$, we have $\hat{\chi}(q) = \chi(c) - \chi(d) \neq 0_F$ in $F$, so its multiplicative inverse $(\chi(c) - \chi(d))^{-1} = \hat{\chi}(q)^{-1}$ exists in $F$!

Now we define $\tilde{\chi}: \mathbb{Q} \to F$ for any rational $[p, q] \in \mathbb{Q}$ (with $p = [a, b] \in \mathbb{Z}$ and $q = [c, d] \in \mathbb{Z}^*$) by
$$
\tilde{\chi}([p, q]) := \hat{\chi}(p) \cdot \hat{\chi}(q)^{-1} = (\chi(a) - \chi(b)) \cdot (\chi(c) - \chi(d))^{-1}
$$
- **Well-Definedness on $\mathbb{Q}$:** Suppose $[p, q] = [p', q']$ in $\mathbb{Q}$, so $p \cdot q' = q \cdot p'$ in $\mathbb{Z}$. Applying $\hat{\chi}$ gives $\hat{\chi}(p) \hat{\chi}(q') = \hat{\chi}(q) \hat{\chi}(p')$ in $F$. Multiplying both sides by $\hat{\chi}(q)^{-1} \hat{\chi}(q')^{-1}$ in $F$ yields $\hat{\chi}(p) \hat{\chi}(q)^{-1} = \hat{\chi}(p') \hat{\chi}(q')^{-1}$, so $\tilde{\chi}$ is well-defined.
- **Homomorphism Properties:** For any $[p, q], [r, s] \in \mathbb{Q}$, using the field axioms of $F$ and the fact that $\hat{\chi}$ preserves $+$ and $\cdot$ on $\mathbb{Z}$:
  $$
  \begin{aligned}
  \tilde{\chi}([p, q] + [r, s]) &= \tilde{\chi}([ps + qr, qs]) \\
                                &= \hat{\chi}(ps + qr) \cdot \hat{\chi}(qs)^{-1} \\
                                &= (\hat{\chi}(p)\hat{\chi}(s) + \hat{\chi}(q)\hat{\chi}(r)) \cdot \hat{\chi}(q)^{-1} \hat{\chi}(s)^{-1} \\
                                &= \hat{\chi}(p)\hat{\chi}(q)^{-1} + \hat{\chi}(r)\hat{\chi}(s)^{-1} = \tilde{\chi}([p, q]) + \tilde{\chi}([r, s])
  \end{aligned}
  $$
  and similarly
  $$
  \tilde{\chi}([p, q] \cdot [r, s]) = \hat{\chi}(pr) \cdot \hat{\chi}(qs)^{-1} = (\hat{\chi}(p)\hat{\chi}(q)^{-1}) \cdot (\hat{\chi}(r)\hat{\chi}(s)^{-1}) = \tilde{\chi}([p, q]) \cdot \tilde{\chi}([r, s])
  $$
- **Strict Order Preservation and Injectivity:** If $x = [p, q] > 0$ in $\mathbb{Q}$ with $q > 0$ and $p > 0$ in $\mathbb{Z}$, then $\hat{\chi}(p) > 0_F$ and $\hat{\chi}(q) > 0_F$ in $F$. Since the reciprocal of a positive element in an ordered field is positive, $\hat{\chi}(q)^{-1} > 0_F$, and by Axiom (O4) we have $\tilde{\chi}(x) = \hat{\chi}(p) \hat{\chi}(q)^{-1} > 0_F$. Thus for any $x < y$ in $\mathbb{Q}$, $y - x > 0 \implies \tilde{\chi}(y) - \tilde{\chi}(x) = \tilde{\chi}(y - x) > 0_F \implies \tilde{\chi}(x) < \tilde{\chi}(y)$, which also implies $\tilde{\chi}$ is injective.
- **Uniqueness:** Any field homomorphism $\phi: \mathbb{Q} \to F$ must satisfy $\phi(0_{\mathbb{Q}}) = 0_F$ and $\phi(1_{\mathbb{Q}}) = 1_F$. By induction on $n \in \mathbb{N}$, $\phi(n) = \phi(n - 1 + 1) = \phi(n - 1) + 1_F$, so $\phi$ must agree with $\chi$ on $\mathbb{N}$. Because $\phi(a - b) = \phi(a) - \phi(b)$, $\phi$ must agree with $\hat{\chi}$ on $\mathbb{Z}$. Finally, because $\phi(p / q) = \phi(p \cdot q^{-1}) = \phi(p) \cdot \phi(q)^{-1}$, $\phi$ is forced to equal $\tilde{\chi}$ on all of $\mathbb{Q}$. $\blacksquare$

### Problem 0.4.2: A Non-Archimedean Ordered Field with Infinitesimals

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> An ordered field $F$ is said to satisfy the **Archimedean Property** if for every element $x \in F$, there exists a natural number $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$ (viewed inside $F$ via $n = \underbrace{1_F + \dots + 1_F}_{n \text{ times}}$) such that $n > x$. Equivalently, for every positive element $\varepsilon > 0_F$ in $F$, there exists $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$ such that $0_F < \frac{1}{n} < \varepsilon$.
>
> To see why the Archimedean Property requires the Least Upper Bound Property (which we will prove in Section 0.5) and **cannot** be proved from the Ordered Field Axioms (A1–A4, M1–M4, D, O1–O4) alone, let $\mathbb{Q}(t)$ denote the **field of rational functions** in a formal variable $t$ with rational coefficients. Specifically, elements of $\mathbb{Q}(t)$ are equivalence classes of formal quotients of polynomials
> $$
> f(t) = \frac{P(t)}{Q(t)} = \frac{a_n t^n + a_{n-1} t^{n-1} + \dots + a_0}{b_m t^m + b_{m-1} t^{m-1} + \dots + b_0}
> $$
> where $a_i, b_j \in \mathbb{Q}$ and the leading coefficient of the denominator is non-zero ($b_m \neq 0$), under the equivalence relation $\frac{P(t)}{Q(t)} = \frac{R(t)}{S(t)} \iff P(t) S(t) = Q(t) R(t)$ as polynomials.
> 1. Define a non-zero rational function $f(t) = \frac{P(t)}{Q(t)} \in \mathbb{Q}(t)$ (written with leading coefficients $a_n \neq 0$ and $b_m \neq 0$) to be **positive** ($f > 0$) if the product of the leading coefficients satisfies $a_n b_m > 0$ in $\mathbb{Q}$, and define $f < g \iff g - f > 0$. Prove that $(\mathbb{Q}(t), +, \cdot, <)$ satisfies all axioms of an **ordered field**. (Intuitively, $f(t) > 0$ means $f(t)$ is eventually positive as $t \to +\infty$.)
> 2. Consider the rational function $\varepsilon(t) := \frac{1}{t} \in \mathbb{Q}(t)$. Prove that for every positive rational number $r \in \mathbb{Q}^+$ (and in particular for $r = \frac{1}{n}$ for every $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$),
>    $$
>    0 < \varepsilon(t) < r
>    $$
>    Explain why this makes $\varepsilon(t) = \frac{1}{t}$ a positive **infinitesimal** (and $t$ an **infinitely large** element), and deduce that $\mathbb{Q}(t)$ violates both the Archimedean Property and the Least Upper Bound Property.

**Solution:**
**Part 1: Verification that $(\mathbb{Q}(t), +, \cdot, <)$ Is an Ordered Field.**
Just as we constructed $\mathbb{Q}$ from the integral domain $\mathbb{Z}$ by taking equivalence classes of pairs $(p, q) \in \mathbb{Z} \times \mathbb{Z}^*$, the ring of polynomials $\mathbb{Q}[t]$ is a commutative ring with no zero divisors (if $P(t)$ has leading term $a_n t^n$ with $a_n \neq 0$ and $Q(t)$ has leading term $b_m t^m$ with $b_m \neq 0$, then $P(t) Q(t)$ has leading term $a_n b_m t^{n+m}$ with $a_n b_m \neq 0$). Therefore, the exact same fraction-field construction and proofs from Section 0.4 show that $\mathbb{Q}(t)$ is a field under standard addition $\frac{P}{Q} + \frac{R}{S} = \frac{PS + QR}{QS}$ and multiplication $\frac{P}{Q} \cdot \frac{R}{S} = \frac{PR}{QS}$.

Now let us check that the positivity condition $f = \frac{P}{Q} > 0 \iff a_n b_m > 0$ in $\mathbb{Q}$ is well-defined and satisfies the order axioms (O1)–(O4):
- **Well-Definedness of Positivity:** Suppose $\frac{P(t)}{Q(t)} = \frac{R(t)}{S(t)}$ where $P, Q, R, S$ are non-zero polynomials with leading terms $a_n t^n$, $b_m t^m$, $c_k t^k$, and $d_l t^l$ ($a_n, b_m, c_k, d_l \in \mathbb{Q} \setminus \{0\}$). Then $P(t) S(t) = Q(t) R(t)$ as polynomials. Equating the leading coefficients on both sides gives
  $$
  a_n d_l = b_m c_k \quad \text{in } \mathbb{Q}
  $$
  Multiplying both sides by the positive rational number $b_m d_l$ (Wait: $b_m d_l$ might not be positive, so let us multiply by $b_m c_k$ or note that in the ordered field $\mathbb{Q}$, $a_n d_l = b_m c_k \implies (a_n b_m)(c_k d_l) = (a_n d_l)(b_m c_k) = (b_m c_k)^2 > 0$). Because $(a_n b_m)(c_k d_l) > 0$ in $\mathbb{Q}$, the two products $a_n b_m$ and $c_k d_l$ must have the exact same sign in $\mathbb{Q}$: $a_n b_m > 0 \iff c_k d_l > 0$! Thus positivity is well-defined on equivalence classes.
- **Verification of (O1)–(O4):** Notice that if we multiply numerator and denominator of $f = \frac{P}{Q}$ by $b_m \neq 0$, the new denominator has leading coefficient $b_m^2 > 0$. In fact, over any common denominator $Q(t)$ whose leading coefficient $b_m$ is positive ($b_m > 0$), a rational function $\frac{P(t)}{Q(t)}$ is positive if and only if the numerator polynomial $P(t)$ has a positive leading coefficient ($a_n > 0$).
  1. *(O1) Trichotomy:* For any $f, g \in \mathbb{Q}(t)$, consider $h = g - f = \frac{P(t)}{Q(t)}$ written with denominator leading coefficient $b_m > 0$. Either $P(t)$ is the zero polynomial (in which case $f = g$), or $P(t) \neq 0$ has a non-zero leading coefficient $a_n \in \mathbb{Q} \setminus \{0\}$. By trichotomy in $\mathbb{Q}$, either $a_n > 0$ (so $g - f > 0$, meaning $f < g$) or $a_n < 0$ (so $f - g = \frac{-P(t)}{Q(t)} > 0$, meaning $g < f$), and these three cases are mutually exclusive.
  2. *(O2) Transitivity and (O3) Additive Compatibility:* Suppose $f_1 > 0$ and $f_2 > 0$. Putting $f_1$ and $f_2$ over a common denominator $Q(t)$ with positive leading coefficient $b_m > 0$, say $f_1 = \frac{P_1(t)}{Q(t)}$ and $f_2 = \frac{P_2(t)}{Q(t)}$, both $P_1(t) = a_n t^n + \dots$ and $P_2(t) = c_k t^k + \dots$ have positive leading coefficients $a_n > 0$ and $c_k > 0$. What is the leading coefficient of $P_1(t) + P_2(t)$?
     - If $n > k$, the leading term of $P_1 + P_2$ is $a_n t^n$ with $a_n > 0$.
     - If $k > n$, the leading term of $P_1 + P_2$ is $c_k t^k$ with $c_k > 0$.
     - If $n = k$, the leading term of $P_1 + P_2$ is $(a_n + c_n) t^n$, and since $a_n > 0$ and $c_n > 0$ in $\mathbb{Q}$, we have $a_n + c_n > 0$ (in particular, $a_n + c_n \neq 0$, so the leading terms cannot cancel out!).
     In all three cases, $P_1 + P_2$ has a positive leading coefficient, so $f_1 + f_2 > 0$. Thus, if $f < g$ and $g < h$, then $(g - f) > 0$ and $(h - g) > 0 \implies h - f = (h - g) + (g - f) > 0 \implies f < h$, establishing (O2). And if $f < g$, then $(g + h) - (f + h) = g - f > 0 \implies f + h < g + h$, establishing (O3).
  3. *(O4) Multiplicative Compatibility:* If $f = \frac{P}{Q} > 0$ (so $a_n b_m > 0$) and $g = \frac{R}{S} > 0$ (so $c_k d_l > 0$), then $f \cdot g = \frac{PR}{QS}$ has numerator leading coefficient $a_n c_k$ and denominator leading coefficient $b_m d_l$. Since $(a_n c_k)(b_m d_l) = (a_n b_m)(c_k d_l) > 0$ in $\mathbb{Q}$ by (O4) in $\mathbb{Q}$, we have $f \cdot g > 0$.
Thus $(\mathbb{Q}(t), +, \cdot, <)$ is a genuine ordered field!

**Part 2: Infinitesimals and Failure of the Archimedean and Least Upper Bound Properties.**
Consider $\varepsilon(t) := \frac{1}{t} \in \mathbb{Q}(t)$, where the numerator is $P(t) = 1$ (degree $0$, leading coefficient $a_0 = 1$) and the denominator is $Q(t) = 1 \cdot t + 0$ (degree $1$, leading coefficient $b_1 = 1$).
- First, since $a_0 b_1 = 1 \cdot 1 = 1 > 0$ in $\mathbb{Q}$, we have $\varepsilon(t) = \frac{1}{t} > 0$.
- Second, let $r \in \mathbb{Q}^+$ be any positive rational number (identified with the constant rational function $\frac{r}{1} \in \mathbb{Q}(t)$). Let us compute the difference $r - \varepsilon(t)$:
  $$
  r - \varepsilon(t) = \frac{r}{1} - \frac{1}{t} = \frac{r \cdot t - 1}{1 \cdot t}
  $$
  Look at the numerator polynomial $P(t) = r t - 1$: its degree is $1$ and its leading coefficient is $a_1 = r > 0$. The denominator polynomial $Q(t) = 1 \cdot t$ has degree $1$ and leading coefficient $b_1 = 1 > 0$. Since the product of the leading coefficients is $a_1 b_1 = r \cdot 1 = r > 0$ in $\mathbb{Q}$, our definition of positivity in $\mathbb{Q}(t)$ says that
  $$
  r - \varepsilon(t) > 0 \implies \varepsilon(t) < r
  $$
Therefore, for **every** positive rational number $r \in \mathbb{Q}^+$ (and in particular for $r = \frac{1}{n}$ for every $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$), we have
$$
0 < \varepsilon(t) < \frac{1}{n}
$$
This makes $\varepsilon(t) = \frac{1}{t}$ a **positive infinitesimal**: it is strictly greater than $0$, yet strictly smaller than every positive fraction $\frac{1}{n}$! Taking reciprocals via Property 4 of ordered fields ($0 < \frac{1}{t} < \frac{1}{n} \implies t > n$), we see simultaneously that $t \in \mathbb{Q}(t)$ is an **infinitely large element** that is strictly greater than every natural number $n \in \mathbb{N}$!

Consequently:
1. **Violation of the Archimedean Property:** Because $t > n$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$ (and $0 < \frac{1}{t} < \frac{1}{n}$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$), $\mathbb{Q}(t)$ violates the Archimedean Property.
2. **Violation of the Least Upper Bound Property:** Consider the subset $\mathbb{N} \subseteq \mathbb{Q}(t)$ of natural numbers inside $\mathbb{Q}(t)$. Clearly $\mathbb{N}$ is non-empty ($1 \in \mathbb{N}$), and because $n < t$ for every $n \in \mathbb{N}$, the element $t \in \mathbb{Q}(t)$ is an **upper bound** for $\mathbb{N}$! Could $\mathbb{N}$ have a supremum $\alpha = \sup \mathbb{N} \in \mathbb{Q}(t)$? If $\alpha = \sup \mathbb{N}$ existed, then since $n + 1 \in \mathbb{N}$ for every $n \in \mathbb{N}$, we would have $n + 1 \le \alpha$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$, which Rearranges to $n \le \alpha - 1$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$. That means $\alpha - 1 < \alpha$ would ALSO be an upper bound for $\mathbb{N}$, contradicting the requirement that $\alpha$ is the *least* upper bound of $\mathbb{N}$! Thus $\sup \mathbb{N}$ does not exist in $\mathbb{Q}(t)$, proving that $\mathbb{Q}(t)$ fails the Least Upper Bound Property. $\blacksquare$

### Problem 0.4.3: Diophantine Repulsion of Rationals Away from $\sqrt{2}$

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Although $\mathbb{Q}$ is dense in $\mathbb{R}$ (so there are rational numbers arbitrarily close to $\sqrt{2}$), rational numbers $\frac{p}{q}$ with a given denominator $q \in \mathbb{N}^+$ cannot get *too* close to $\sqrt{2}$: they experience an algebraic "Diophantine repulsion" away from $\sqrt{2}$ controlled by $\frac{1}{q^2}$. (Here we work inside an ordered field containing $\mathbb{Q}$ and a positive element $\sqrt{2}$ satisfying $(\sqrt{2})^2 = 2$, and we define $|x| := x$ if $x \ge 0$ and $|x| := -x$ if $x < 0$.)
> 1. Prove that for every rational number $\frac{p}{q} \in \mathbb{Q}$ (with $p \in \mathbb{Z}$ and $q \in \mathbb{N}^+$), the integer $p^2 - 2q^2$ is non-zero, and deduce that
>    $$
>    \left| \frac{p^2}{q^2} - 2 \right| \ge \frac{1}{q^2}
>    $$
> 2. Using this lower bound and the factorization $\frac{p^2}{q^2} - 2 = \left(\frac{p}{q} - \sqrt{2}\right)\left(\frac{p}{q} + \sqrt{2}\right)$, prove that for all rational numbers $\frac{p}{q} \in (1, 2)$,
>    $$
>    \left| \frac{p}{q} - \sqrt{2} \right| > \frac{1}{4q^2}
>    $$

**Solution:**
**Part 1: Integer Gap and Lower Bound on $\left|\frac{p^2}{q^2} - 2\right|$.**
Let $\frac{p}{q} \in \mathbb{Q}$ with $p \in \mathbb{Z}$ and $q \in \mathbb{N}^+$. Since $p$ and $q$ are integers, the quantity $N := p^2 - 2q^2$ is also an **integer** ($N \in \mathbb{Z}$). Could $N = 0$? If $p^2 - 2q^2 = 0$, then dividing by $q^2 > 0$ would give $\left(\frac{p}{q}\right)^2 = 2$, contradicting the Irrationality of $\sqrt{2}$!

Therefore, $N = p^2 - 2q^2$ is a **non-zero integer** ($N \in \mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0\}$). Now recall a fundamental property of the integers $\mathbb{Z}$ (proved from the discrete order of $\mathbb{N}$ in Sections 0.2 and 0.3): there is no integer strictly between $0$ and $1$, so every positive integer satisfies $k \ge 1$. Since $|N| = |p^2 - 2q^2|$ is a positive integer, we must have
$$
|p^2 - 2q^2| \ge 1
$$
Multiplying both sides of this inequality by the positive rational $q^{-2} = \frac{1}{q^2} > 0$, we obtain
$$
\left| \frac{p^2 - 2q^2}{q^2} \right| \ge \frac{1}{q^2} \implies \left| \frac{p^2}{q^2} - 2 \right| \ge \frac{1}{q^2}
$$
**Part 2: Lower Bound on the Distance $\left|\frac{p}{q} - \sqrt{2}\right|$.**
Suppose $\frac{p}{q} \in (1, 2)$, meaning $1 < \frac{p}{q} < 2$. First let us bound $\sqrt{2}$: since $(\sqrt{2})^2 = 2 < 4 = 2^2$ and $\sqrt{2} > 0$, we must have $\sqrt{2} < 2$ (because if $\sqrt{2} \ge 2$, squaring would give $2 \ge 4$, a contradiction). Adding the two strict inequalities $\frac{p}{q} < 2$ and $\sqrt{2} < 2$ using Axiom (O3) gives
$$
0 < \frac{p}{q} + \sqrt{2} < 2 + 2 = 4
$$
By Property 4 of ordered fields (taking reciprocals of positive elements reverses strict inequalities), $0 < \frac{p}{q} + \sqrt{2} < 4$ implies
$$
\frac{1}{\frac{p}{q} + \sqrt{2}} > \frac{1}{4}
$$
Now, using the difference-of-squares identity $x^2 - y^2 = (x - y)(x + y)$ in our ordered field with $x = \frac{p}{q}$ and $y = \sqrt{2}$, we can factor
$$
\left| \frac{p^2}{q^2} - 2 \right| = \left| \frac{p}{q} - \sqrt{2} \right| \cdot \left( \frac{p}{q} + \sqrt{2} \right)
$$
Combining this factorization with the inequality $\left|\frac{p^2}{q^2} - 2\right| \ge \frac{1}{q^2}$ from Part 1, we have
$$
\left| \frac{p}{q} - \sqrt{2} \right| \cdot \left( \frac{p}{q} + \sqrt{2} \right) \ge \frac{1}{q^2}
$$
Multiplying both sides by the positive reciprocal $\left(\frac{p}{q} + \sqrt{2}\right)^{-1} > \frac{1}{4}$ and noting that $\left|\frac{p}{q} - \sqrt{2}\right| > 0$, we conclude that
$$
\left| \frac{p}{q} - \sqrt{2} \right| \ge \frac{1}{q^2 \left( \frac{p}{q} + \sqrt{2} \right)} > \frac{1}{4q^2}
$$
In other words, a fraction with denominator $q$ (such as $q = 100$) can never get closer to $\sqrt{2}$ than $\frac{1}{4q^2}$ ($\frac{1}{40{,}000}$), no matter how cleverly you choose the numerator $p$! $\blacksquare$

### Problem 0.4.4: The Rational Root Theorem and Irrationality of $\sqrt{6}$ and $\sqrt{2} + \sqrt{3}$

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Our proof that $\sqrt{2} \notin \mathbb{Q}$ is a special case of a much broader algebraic phenomenon governing polynomial equations with integer coefficients. Recall from elementary number theory that two integers $p, q \in \mathbb{Z}$ are **coprime** (or **relatively prime**, meaning $\gcd(p, q) = 1$) if no prime number divides both $p$ and $q$, and **Euclid's Lemma** states that if a prime $d$ divides a product $a \cdot b$ ( enabling us to deduce that if $\gcd(p, q) = 1$ and $q$ divides $p \cdot k$, then $q$ must divide $k$).
> 1. **The Rational Root Theorem:** Let $P(x) = c_n x^n + c_{n-1} x^{n-1} + \dots + c_1 x + c_0$ be a polynomial with integer coefficients $c_0, c_1, \dots, c_n \in \mathbb{Z}$, where $c_n \neq 0$ and $c_0 \neq 0$. Prove that if $x = \frac{p}{q} \in \mathbb{Q}$ (written in lowest terms with $p \in \mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0\}$, $q \in \mathbb{N}^+$, and $\gcd(p, q) = 1$) is a rational root of $P(x) = 0$, then **$p$ divides the constant term $c_0$** and **$q$ divides the leading coefficient $c_n$**.
> 2. **Monic Integer Polynomials:** Deduce that if a monic polynomial with integer coefficients ($c_n = 1$) has a rational root $x \in \mathbb{Q}$, then $x$ must actually be an **integer** ($x \in \mathbb{Z}$) that divides $c_0$. Use this to prove in two lines that there is no rational number $x \in \mathbb{Q}$ satisfying $x^2 = 6$ or $x^3 = 5$.
> 3. **Irrationality of $\sqrt{2} + \sqrt{3}$:** Suppose $x$ is an element of an ordered field extending $\mathbb{Q}$ that contains positive elements $\sqrt{2}$ and $\sqrt{3}$ satisfying $(\sqrt{2})^2 = 2$ and $(\sqrt{3})^2 = 3$, and let $\alpha := \sqrt{2} + \sqrt{3}$. By squaring $\alpha$ twice, find a monic degree-$4$ polynomial $P(x) = x^4 + c_3 x^3 + c_2 x^2 + c_1 x + c_0$ with integer coefficients such that $P(\alpha) = 0$, and use the Rational Root Theorem to prove that $\sqrt{2} + \sqrt{3}$ is irrational ($\alpha \notin \mathbb{Q}$).

**Solution:**
**Part 1: Proof of the Rational Root Theorem.**
Suppose $x = \frac{p}{q} \in \mathbb{Q}$ with $\gcd(p, q) = 1$ satisfies $P\left(\frac{p}{q}\right) = 0$:
$$
c_n \left(\frac{p}{q}\right)^n + c_{n-1} \left(\frac{p}{q}\right)^{n-1} + \dots + c_1 \left(\frac{p}{q}\right) + c_0 = 0
$$
Multiplying both sides of this equation by $q^n \neq 0$ clears all denominators and gives the integer equation
$$
c_n p^n + c_{n-1} p^{n-1} q + \dots + c_1 p q^{n-1} + c_0 q^n = 0
$$
We can rearrange this single equation in two ways to isolate the two end terms:
- **First, isolate $c_0 q^n$:** Subtracting $c_0 q^n$ and factoring $p$ out of all remaining terms on the left-hand side gives
  $$
  p \cdot \left( c_n p^{n-1} + c_{n-1} p^{n-2} q + \dots + c_1 q^{n-1} \right) = -c_0 q^n
  $$
  Because the expression in parentheses is an integer, this shows that **$p$ divides $c_0 q^n$**. Since $\gcd(p, q) = 1$, $p$ shares no prime factors with $q$, and therefore $\gcd(p, q^n) = 1$. By Euclid's Lemma, since $p$ divides $q^n \cdot c_0$ and $\gcd(p, q^n) = 1$, **$p$ must divide $c_0$**!
- **Second, isolate $c_n p^n$:** Subtracting all terms except $c_n p^n$ and factoring $q$ out of the right-hand side gives
  $$
  c_n p^n = -q \cdot \left( c_{n-1} p^{n-1} + \dots + c_1 p q^{n-2} + c_0 q^{n-1} \right)
  $$
  Because the expression in parentheses is an integer, this shows that **$q$ divides $c_n p^n$**. Since $\gcd(p, q) = 1$, we have $\gcd(q, p^n) = 1$. By Euclid's Lemma, since $q$ divides $p^n \cdot c_n$ and $\gcd(q, p^n) = 1$, **$q$ must divide $c_n$**!

**Part 2: Monic Integer Polynomials and Irrationality of $\sqrt{6}$ and $\sqrt[3]{5}$.**
Suppose $P(x) = x^n + c_{n-1} x^{n-1} + \dots + c_0$ is **monic**, meaning its leading coefficient is $c_n = 1$. If $x = \frac{p}{q} \in \mathbb{Q}$ (with $q \in \mathbb{N}^+$ and $\gcd(p, q) = 1$) is a rational root of $P(x) = 0$, then by Part 1, the positive integer $q$ must divide $c_n = 1$. The only positive integer that divides $1$ is $q = 1$! Therefore, $x = \frac{p}{1} = p \in \mathbb{Z}$ must be an **integer**, and by Part 1 this integer $p$ must divide $c_0$.
- **Why $x^2 = 6$ has no rational solution:** A rational solution to $x^2 = 6$ is a rational root of the monic polynomial $x^2 - 6 = 0$. By our corollary, any rational root must be an integer $x \in \mathbb{Z}$. Since $x \mapsto x^2$ is strictly increasing on positive integers and $2^2 = 4 < 6 < 9 = 3^2$, no integer satisfies $x^2 = 6$, so $\sqrt{6} \notin \mathbb{Q}$!
- **Why $x^3 = 5$ has no rational solution:** Similarly, any rational root of the monic polynomial $x^3 - 5 = 0$ must be an integer $x \in \mathbb{Z}$. Since $1^3 = 1 < 5 < 8 = 2^3$, no integer satisfies $x^3 = 5$, so $\sqrt[3]{5} \notin \mathbb{Q}$!

**Part 3: Irrationality of $\alpha = \sqrt{2} + \sqrt{3}$.**
Let $\alpha = \sqrt{2} + \sqrt{3}$. Squaring both sides once gives
$$
\alpha^2 = (\sqrt{2} + \sqrt{3})^2 = 2 + 2\sqrt{6} + 3 = 5 + 2\sqrt{6}
$$
To eliminate the remaining radical $\sqrt{6}$, isolate $2\sqrt{6}$ on the right-hand side by subtracting $5$, and then square both sides a second time:
$$
(\alpha^2 - 5)^2 = (2\sqrt{6})^2 \implies \alpha^4 - 10\alpha^2 + 25 = 4 \cdot 6 = 24
$$
Subtracting $24$ from both sides shows that $\alpha = \sqrt{2} + \sqrt{3}$ is a root of the monic integer polynomial
$$
P(x) := x^4 - 10x^2 + 1 = 0
$$
By the Rational Root Theorem (Part 2), since $P(x)$ is monic with constant term $c_0 = 1$, the *only* possible rational roots of $P(x) = 0$ are integers $p \in \mathbb{Z}$ that divide $c_0 = 1$, namely $p = 1$ or $p = -1$! Checking both candidates directly gives
$$
P(1) = 1^4 - 10(1)^2 + 1 = -8 \neq 0 \quad \text{and} \quad P(-1) = (-1)^4 - 10(-1)^2 + 1 = -8 \neq 0
$$
(Alternatively, since $\sqrt{2} > 1$ and $\sqrt{3} > 1$, we have $\alpha = \sqrt{2} + \sqrt{3} > 2$, so $\alpha$ cannot equal $\pm 1$.) Since $P(x) = 0$ has **no rational roots whatsoever** and $P(\alpha) = 0$, we conclude that $\alpha = \sqrt{2} + \sqrt{3} \notin \mathbb{Q}$. $\blacksquare$

### Problem 0.4.5: Adjoining $\sqrt{2}$ to $\mathbb{Q}$ — The Quadratic Ordered Field $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})$ Still Fails Completeness

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> When we discovered that $\mathbb{Q}$ fails the Least Upper Bound Property because it lacks a square root of $2$, a natural question arises: *"Instead of building the massive system of all real numbers via Dedekind cuts, what if we simply patch the $\sqrt{2}$ hole algebraically by adjoining a formal symbol $\sqrt{2}$ (satisfying $(\sqrt{2})^2 = 2$) to $\mathbb{Q}$?"*
>
> To test this idea rigorously without assuming the prior existence of $\mathbb{R}$, define the set $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2}) := \mathbb{Q} \times \mathbb{Q}$, where we write an ordered pair $(a, b) \in \mathbb{Q} \times \mathbb{Q}$ suggestively as $a + b\sqrt{2}$, and define addition and multiplication on $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})$ by
> $$
> \begin{aligned}
> (a + b\sqrt{2}) + (c + d\sqrt{2}) &:= (a + c) + (b + d)\sqrt{2} \\
> (a + b\sqrt{2}) \cdot (c + d\sqrt{2}) &:= (ac + 2bd) + (ad + bc)\sqrt{2}
> \end{aligned}
> $$
> 1. Prove that for every non-zero element $x = a + b\sqrt{2} \in \mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2}) \setminus \{0 + 0\sqrt{2}\}$ (meaning $(a, b) \neq (0, 0)$ in $\mathbb{Q} \times \mathbb{Q}$), the rational number $N(x) := a^2 - 2b^2 \in \mathbb{Q}$ (called the **norm** of $x$) is non-zero, and use this to prove that $x$ has a multiplicative inverse in $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})$ given by "rationalizing the denominator":
>    $$
>    (a + b\sqrt{2})^{-1} = \frac{a}{a^2 - 2b^2} + \left(\frac{-b}{a^2 - 2b^2}\right)\sqrt{2}
>    $$
>    Verify that the element $\omega := 0 + 1\sqrt{2} \in \mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})$ satisfies $\omega^2 = 2 + 0\sqrt{2}$!
> 2. Prove that there is no element $x = a + b\sqrt{2} \in \mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})$ such that $x^2 = 3 + 0\sqrt{2}$. Conclude that if $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})$ is ordered by $a + b\sqrt{2} > 0 \iff a + b\sqrt{2}$ lies in the positive cone $\{x \in \mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2}) : (a > 0, b \ge 0) \text{ or } (a > 0, b < 0, a^2 > 2b^2) \text{ or } (a \le 0, b > 0, 2b^2 > a^2)\}$, the subset $E := \{x \in \mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2}) : x > 0 \text{ and } x^2 < 3\}$ is non-empty and bounded above in $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})$ but has **no supremum in $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})$**!

**Solution:**
**Part 1: Non-Vanishing of the Norm $a^2 - 2b^2$, Multiplicative Inverses, and $\omega^2 = 2$.**
Let $x = a + b\sqrt{2} \in \mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})$ be a non-zero element, meaning $a, b \in \mathbb{Q}$ are not both zero. Could the norm $N(x) = a^2 - 2b^2$ equal $0$ in $\mathbb{Q}$?
- If $b = 0$, then since $(a, b) \neq (0, 0)$, we must have $a \neq 0$, so $a^2 - 2(0)^2 = a^2 > 0$ in $\mathbb{Q}$.
- If $b \neq 0$, suppose for the sake of contradiction that $a^2 - 2b^2 = 0$. Dividing by $b^2 \neq 0$ in the field $\mathbb{Q}$ gives $\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)^2 = 2$, where $\frac{a}{b} \in \mathbb{Q}$, which directly contradicts the Irrationality of $\sqrt{2}$ in $\mathbb{Q}$!

Therefore, $a^2 - 2b^2 \neq 0$ in $\mathbb{Q}$ for every non-zero element $x = a + b\sqrt{2} \in \mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})$. Because $\mathbb{Q}$ is a field and $a^2 - 2b^2 \neq 0$, we can divide by $a^2 - 2b^2$ in $\mathbb{Q}$ to form the element
$$
y := \frac{a}{a^2 - 2b^2} + \left(\frac{-b}{a^2 - 2b^2}\right)\sqrt{2} \in \mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})
$$
Let us multiply $x = a + b\sqrt{2}$ by $y$ using the formal multiplication rule $(a + b\sqrt{2})(c + d\sqrt{2}) = (ac + 2bd) + (ad + bc)\sqrt{2}$:
$$
\begin{aligned}
(a + b\sqrt{2}) \cdot y &= \left( a \cdot \frac{a}{a^2 - 2b^2} + 2b \cdot \frac{-b}{a^2 - 2b^2} \right) + \left( a \cdot \frac{-b}{a^2 - 2b^2} + b \cdot \frac{a}{a^2 - 2b^2} \right)\sqrt{2} \\
                        &= \frac{a^2 - 2b^2}{a^2 - 2b^2} + \frac{-ab + ab}{a^2 - 2b^2}\sqrt{2} \\
                        &= 1 + 0\sqrt{2}
\end{aligned}
$$
Since $1 + 0\sqrt{2}$ is the multiplicative identity of $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})$ (and routine verification confirms associativity, commutativity, additive inverses $(-a) + (-b)\sqrt{2}$, and distributivity directly from $\mathbb{Q}$), $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})$ is a **field**! Moreover, for the element $\omega = 0 + 1\sqrt{2}$, the multiplication definition gives
$$
\omega^2 = (0 + 1\sqrt{2}) \cdot (0 + 1\sqrt{2}) = (0 \cdot 0 + 2 \cdot 1 \cdot 1) + (0 \cdot 1 + 1 \cdot 0)\sqrt{2} = 2 + 0\sqrt{2}
$$
So $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})$ genuinely contains a square root of $2$!

**Part 2: Why $\sqrt{3} \notin \mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})$ and Why $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})$ Still Fails the Least Upper Bound Property.**
Suppose for the sake of contradiction that there exists an element $x = a + b\sqrt{2} \in \mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})$ (with $a, b \in \mathbb{Q}$) such that $x^2 = 3 + 0\sqrt{2}$. Expanding $x^2$ using the multiplication law on $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})$ gives
$$
(a + b\sqrt{2})^2 = (a^2 + 2b^2) + (2ab)\sqrt{2} = 3 + 0\sqrt{2}
$$
Because elements of $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})$ are ordered pairs $(A, B) \in \mathbb{Q} \times \mathbb{Q}$, equating the two coordinates gives a system of two rational equations:
$$
a^2 + 2b^2 = 3 \quad \text{and} \quad 2ab = 0
$$
Since $2 \neq 0$ in the field $\mathbb{Q}$, the second equation $2ab = 0$ forces either $a = 0$ or $b = 0$:
- **Case 1 ($b = 0$):** Substituting $b = 0$ into $a^2 + 2b^2 = 3$ gives $a^2 = 3$ for some rational number $a \in \mathbb{Q}$. By the Rational Root Theorem (Problem 0.4.4, Part 2), any rational root of the monic polynomial $a^2 - 3 = 0$ must be an integer $a \in \mathbb{Z}$, which is impossible since $1^2 = 1 < 3 < 4 = 2^2$.
- **Case 2 ($a = 0$):** Substituting $a = 0$ into $a^2 + 2b^2 = 3$ gives $2b^2 = 3$ for some rational number $b \in \mathbb{Q}$. Multiplying both sides by $2$ gives $(2b)^2 = 4b^2 = 6$, which means the rational number $r := 2b \in \mathbb{Q}$ satisfies $r^2 = 6$, contradicting Problem 0.4.4, Part 2 (where we proved no rational square equals $6$)!

In both cases we reach a contradiction, so **no element $x \in \mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})$ satisfies $x^2 = 3$**.

Consequently, consider the subset $E = \{x \in \mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2}) : x > 0 \text{ and } x^2 < 3\}$. Clearly $1 \in E$ (so $E$ is non-empty) and $2$ is an upper bound for $E$ (since $x > 0$ and $x^2 < 3 < 2^2 \implies x < 2$). Can $E$ have a supremum $\alpha = \sup E \in \mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})$? We can apply the **exact same rational-function approximation recipe** from our proof of the failure of the Least Upper Bound Property in $\mathbb{Q}$, merely replacing $2$ by $3$: for any positive $p \in \mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})$, define
$$
q := p - \frac{p^2 - 3}{p + 2} = \frac{2p + 3}{p + 2} \in \mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})
$$
Then $q > 0$, and simple algebra in the field $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})$ shows that
$$
q^2 - 3 = \left(\frac{2p + 3}{p + 2}\right)^2 - 3 = \frac{(4p^2 + 12p + 9) - 3(p^2 + 4p + 4)}{(p + 2)^2} = \frac{p^2 - 3}{(p + 2)^2}
$$
Therefore:
- If $\alpha \in \mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})$ satisfies $\alpha > 0$ and $\alpha^2 < 3$ ($\alpha \in E$), then $q > \alpha$ and $q^2 < 3$ ($q \in E$), so $\alpha$ cannot be an upper bound of $E$.
- If $\alpha \in \mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})$ satisfies $\alpha > 0$ and $\alpha^2 > 3$, then $q < \alpha$ and $q^2 > 3$ (so $q$ is still an upper bound of $E$ smaller than $\alpha$), so $\alpha$ cannot be the *least* upper bound of $E$.
- And we just proved that $\alpha^2 = 3$ is impossible in $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})$!

Thus $\sup E$ does not exist in $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})$. Patching individual algebraic holes one at a time ($\sqrt{2}$, then $\sqrt{3}$, then $\sqrt{5}, \dots$) can never achieve Dedekind completeness cleanly; we need the holistic analytical construction of **Dedekind cuts** in Section 0.5! $\blacksquare$

---

# 0.5 Constructing the Real Numbers via Dedekind Cuts

## The Geometry and Definition of a Dedekind Cut

In Calculus I and II, you used the real number line $\mathbb{R}$ every single day. Whenever you applied the **Intermediate Value Theorem (IVT)** to conclude that a continuous function $f(x) = x^2 - 2$ changing sign from $f(1) = -1 < 0$ to $f(2) = 2 > 0$ must cross zero at some point $c \in (1, 2)$, or whenever you invoked the **Extreme Value Theorem (EVT)** or **Mean Value Theorem (MVT)**, you relied on a geometric faith: the number line has *no microscopic pinholes*.

Yet as we saw at the end of Section 0.4, the rational numbers $\mathbb{Q}$ are riddled with gaps! The set $A = \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p > 0 \text{ and } p^2 < 2\}$ is non-empty and bounded above in $\mathbb{Q}$, but it has no least upper bound in $\mathbb{Q}$. If you graph $f(x) = x^2 - 2$ strictly over the rational domain $\mathbb{Q}$, the curve passes right through the $x$-axis at the missing spot $\sqrt{2}$ without ever touching a rational point. Without completeness, the Intermediate Value Theorem, the Extreme Value Theorem, the Mean Value Theorem, and the convergence of power series in Calculus II all collapse!

How can we rigorously *construct* the real numbers $\mathbb{R}$ out of $\mathbb{Q}$ without assuming in advance that irrational numbers already exist? Look closely at the gap where $\sqrt{2}$ ought to live. Even though $\sqrt{2}$ does not exist inside $\mathbb{Q}$, the set of all rational numbers *strictly to the left of the gap*,
$$
\alpha = \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p \le 0 \text{ or } p^2 < 2\}
$$
is a completely concrete, well-defined subset of $\mathbb{Q}$ whose description uses only rational numbers and rational arithmetic!

In 1872, the German mathematician Richard Dedekind had a stroke of genius. Suppose you have any point $x$—rational or irrational—on the imagined continuous number line. That point $x$ slices the rational numbers $\mathbb{Q}$ into two disjoint pieces: the **left ray** of all rationals strictly less than $x$, and the **right ray** of all rationals greater than or equal to $x$. Since two different points on the line always have a rational number between them, every point $x$ determines a *unique* left ray of rationals. Dedekind turned this observation upside down: **if we don't yet have the point $x$, why not *define* a real number to BE its open left ray $\alpha \subset \mathbb{Q}$?**

To make this work using only the language of $\mathbb{Q}$, we must characterize which subsets $\alpha \subset \mathbb{Q}$ look like an open left ray $(-\infty, x) \cap \mathbb{Q}$:
1. **Non-Triviality:** An open left ray is neither empty (there are rationals to the left of $x$) nor all of $\mathbb{Q}$ (there are rationals to the right of $x$). Thus $\alpha \neq \emptyset$ and $\alpha \neq \mathbb{Q}$.
2. **Downward Closure (Left-Ray Geometry):** If a rational $p$ belongs to the left ray $\alpha$, then every rational $q < p$ to the left of $p$ must also belong to $\alpha$. This prevents $\alpha$ from having gaps or disconnected islands on the left.
3. **No Greatest Element (Open Right Endpoint):** Why should we require that $\alpha$ have *no largest rational element*? Suppose we want to represent the rational number $2$ as a left ray. In $\mathbb{Q}$, we could consider the open ray $\{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p < 2\}$ (which has no largest element) or the closed ray $\{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p \le 2\}$ (which has largest element $2$). By contrast, at an *irrational* gap like $\sqrt{2}$, there is no rational number sitting at the boundary, so the left ray $\alpha = \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p \le 0 \text{ or } p^2 < 2\}$ *automatically* has no largest element! To ensure that every real number is represented by a *single, unique* subset $\alpha \subset \mathbb{Q}$ and that rational and irrational cuts behave uniformly, we forbid $\alpha$ from containing a largest rational element. Every cut behaves like an open interval $(-\infty, x) \cap \mathbb{Q}$: for any $p \in \alpha$, you can always nudge slightly to the right and find another rational $r \in \alpha$ with $p < r$.

These three geometric requirements define a **Dedekind cut**.

> [!abstract] Definition: Dedekind Cut and the Real Numbers $\mathbb{R}$
> A subset $\alpha \subset \mathbb{Q}$ is called a **Dedekind cut** (or simply a **cut**) if it satisfies the following three axioms:
> 1. **Non-Triviality:** $\alpha \neq \emptyset$ and $\alpha \neq \mathbb{Q}$.
> 2. **Downward Closure:** If $p \in \alpha$ and $q \in \mathbb{Q}$ with $q < p$, then $q \in \alpha$.
> 3. **No Greatest Element:** If $p \in \alpha$, then there exists some $r \in \alpha$ such that $p < r$.
>
> The set of **real numbers**, denoted $\mathbb{R}$, is defined to be the collection of all Dedekind cuts of $\mathbb{Q}$:
> $$
> \mathbb{R} := \{\alpha \subset \mathbb{Q} : \alpha \text{ is a Dedekind cut}\}
> $$

Before we do anything else, let's unpack how to think about elements of $\mathbb{R}$. Under this definition, a single real number $\alpha \in \mathbb{R}$ is literally a **subset of $\mathbb{Q}$** ($\alpha \subset \mathbb{Q}$), while a subset of real numbers $A \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ is a **family of subsets of $\mathbb{Q}$**! Let's record two immediate consequences of the definition that we will use constantly:
- **Every element inside $\alpha$ is strictly smaller than every rational outside $\alpha$:** If $p \in \alpha$ and $q \in \mathbb{Q} \setminus \alpha$ (written $q \notin \alpha$), then $p < q$. Why? By Trichotomy in $\mathbb{Q}$, if $p < q$ failed, we would have $q \le p$. If $q = p$, then $q \in \alpha$, a contradiction; if $q < p$, then Downward Closure (Property 2) would force $q \in \alpha$, again a contradiction! Thus $\alpha$ lies completely to the left of its complement $\mathbb{Q} \setminus \alpha$.
- **Rational Cuts ($r^*$):** Every rational number $r \in \mathbb{Q}$ embeds into $\mathbb{R}$ as the **rational cut** $r^* \in \mathbb{R}$ consisting of all rationals strictly less than $r$:
  $$
  r^* := \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p < r\}
  $$
  Let's verify that $r^*$ satisfies all three properties of a Dedekind cut:
  1. *Non-Triviality:* Since $r - 1 < r$, we have $r - 1 \in r^*$, so $r^* \neq \emptyset$. Since $r \not< r$, we have $r \notin r^*$, so $r^* \neq \mathbb{Q}$.
  2. *Downward Closure:* If $p \in r^*$ (meaning $p < r$) and $q \in \mathbb{Q}$ satisfies $q < p$, then $q < r$ by transitivity of $<$ in $\mathbb{Q}$, so $q \in r^*$.
  3. *No Greatest Element:* If $p \in r^*$ (so $p < r$), the rational midpoint $s = \frac{p + r}{2} \in \mathbb{Q}$ satisfies $p < \frac{p + r}{2} < r$, so $s \in r^*$ and $p < s$.

What about a cut that is *not* of the form $r^*$ for any $r \in \mathbb{Q}$? We call such a cut an **irrational cut**. Let's verify in a concrete worked example that our motivating set for $\sqrt{2}$ is a genuine Dedekind cut.

> [!example] Example: Constructing the Irrational Cut for $\sqrt{2}$
> Define the subset $\alpha \subset \mathbb{Q}$ by
> $$
> \alpha := \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p \le 0 \text{ or } p^2 < 2\}
> $$
> Prove that $\alpha$ is a Dedekind cut.

**Solution:**
We verify each of the three defining axioms of a Dedekind cut:
1. **Non-Triviality ($\alpha \neq \emptyset$ and $\alpha \neq \mathbb{Q}$):** The rational number $1 \in \mathbb{Q}$ satisfies $1^2 = 1 < 2$, so $1 \in \alpha$ and $\alpha \neq \emptyset$. On the other hand, $2 \in \mathbb{Q}$ satisfies $2 > 0$ and $2^2 = 4 \not< 2$, so $2 \notin \alpha$ and $\alpha \neq \mathbb{Q}$.
2. **Downward Closure:** Suppose $p \in \alpha$ and $q \in \mathbb{Q}$ with $q < p$. We must show $q \in \alpha$.
   - If $q \le 0$, then $q \in \alpha$ immediately by the definition of $\alpha$.
   - If $q > 0$, then since $0 < q < p$, we also have $p > 0$. Because $p \in \alpha$ and $p > 0$, it must be that $p^2 < 2$. Since $0 < q < p$ in the ordered field $\mathbb{Q}$, squaring preserves the strict inequality: $q^2 < p^2 < 2$. Thus $q \in \alpha$.
3. **No Greatest Element:** Suppose $p \in \alpha$; we must find some $r \in \alpha$ with $p < r$.
   - If $p \le 0$, we can simply choose $r = 1 \in \alpha$, which satisfies $p \le 0 < 1 = r$.
   - If $p > 0$, then $p^2 < 2$. This is the same inequality we analyzed in Section 0.4! Let's show how to nudge $p$ slightly upward by a small positive rational $h \in (0, 1)$ so that $r = p + h$ still satisfies $r^2 < 2$. For any $h \in \mathbb{Q}$ with $0 < h < 1$, we have $h^2 < h$, so we can bound $(p + h)^2$ linearly in $h$:
     $$
     \begin{aligned}
     (p + h)^2 &= p^2 + 2ph + h^2 \\
               &< p^2 + 2ph + h \\
               &= p^2 + (2p + 1)h
     \end{aligned}
     $$
     To ensure that $(p + h)^2 < 2$, it suffices to choose $h \in (0, 1)$ small enough that $p^2 + (2p + 1)h < 2$, which means $h < \frac{2 - p^2}{2p + 1}$. Specifically, since $2 - p^2 > 0$ and $2p + 1 > 0$, we can pick the concrete rational number
     $$
     h := \frac{1}{2} \min\left(1, \frac{2 - p^2}{2p + 1}\right) \in \mathbb{Q}
     $$
     Then $0 < h < 1$ and $(p + h)^2 < p^2 + (2p + 1)h < 2$. Setting $r = p + h \in \mathbb{Q}$ gives $r > p > 0$ and $r^2 < 2$, so $r \in \alpha$ is strictly larger than $p$.

Having verified all three axioms, we conclude that $\alpha \in \mathbb{R}$ is a valid Dedekind cut! $\blacksquare$

## Ordering the Real Numbers and the Least Upper Bound Property

Here is where Dedekind cuts shine brighter than any other construction of $\mathbb{R}$. If a real number $\alpha$ is the open left ray of all rationals to its left, what does it mean geometrically for one real number $\alpha$ to lie to the left of another real number $\beta$? It means the left ray $\alpha$ is contained inside the left ray $\beta$!

In other words, **numerical inequality $\le$ between real numbers is simply set inclusion $\subseteq$ between Dedekind cuts**.

> [!abstract] Definition: Order on $\mathbb{R}$
> For any two real numbers (cuts) $\alpha, \beta \in \mathbb{R}$, we define
> $$
> \alpha \le \beta \iff \alpha \subseteq \beta
> $$
> We define $\alpha < \beta$ (and equivalently $\beta > \alpha$) if $\alpha$ is a **proper subset** of $\beta$, written $\alpha \subsetneq \beta$—meaning $\alpha \subseteq \beta$ and $\alpha \neq \beta$ (so every rational in $\alpha$ is in $\beta$, and there exists at least one rational $p \in \beta$ such that $p \notin \alpha$).

Wait—for arbitrary subsets of $\mathbb{Q}$, set inclusion $\subseteq$ is only a *partial* order (for instance, $\{1, 2\}$ and $\{2, 3\}$ are neither subsets of one another). Why does $\subseteq$ become a **total order** (satisfying Trichotomy) when restricted to Dedekind cuts? Because every Dedekind cut is a downward-closed ray extending all the way to $-\infty$! If one ray $\beta$ reaches even a single rational number $p$ that the other ray $\alpha$ does not reach ($p \in \beta \setminus \alpha$), then downward closure forces $\beta$ to contain *everything* to the left of $p$—which includes the entire cut $\alpha$!

Even better, because $\alpha \le \beta$ means $\alpha \subseteq \beta$, finding the **least upper bound ($\sup A$)** of a bounded family of cuts $A \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ means finding the *smallest cut $\gamma$ that contains every cut $\alpha \in A$ as a subset*. In naive set theory (Section 0.1), what is the smallest set that contains every set $\alpha$ in a family $A$? **Their union $\bigcup_{\alpha \in A} \alpha$!**

Let's prove both of these foundational facts rigorously.

> [!theorem] Order Properties and Completeness (Least Upper Bound Property) of $\mathbb{R}$
> 1. **Total Order (Trichotomy and Transitivity):** The relation $<$ on $\mathbb{R}$ is an order on $\mathbb{R}$:
>    - **Trichotomy:** For any $\alpha, \beta \in \mathbb{R}$, exactly one of $\alpha < \beta$, $\alpha = \beta$, or $\beta < \alpha$ holds.
>    - **Transitivity:** For any $\alpha, \beta, \gamma \in \mathbb{R}$, if $\alpha < \beta$ and $\beta < \gamma$, then $\alpha < \gamma$.
> 2. **Least Upper Bound Property (Dedekind Completeness):** Every non-empty subset $A \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ that is bounded above in $\mathbb{R}$ has a supremum $\gamma = \sup A \in \mathbb{R}$, given explicitly by the set union of all cuts in $A$:
>    $$
>    \gamma = \bigcup_{\alpha \in A} \alpha
>    $$

**Proof:**
**1. Trichotomy and Transitivity:**
Let $\alpha, \beta, \gamma \in \mathbb{R}$ be cuts.
- *Transitivity:* Suppose $\alpha < \beta$ and $\beta < \gamma$, meaning $\alpha \subsetneq \beta$ and $\beta \subsetneq \gamma$. For any $p \in \alpha$, we have $p \in \beta$ (since $\alpha \subseteq \beta$) and hence $p \in \gamma$ (since $\beta \subseteq \gamma$), so $\alpha \subseteq \gamma$. Moreover, since $\beta \subsetneq \gamma$, there exists a rational $q \in \gamma \setminus \beta$. Because $q \notin \beta$ and $\alpha \subseteq \beta$, we also have $q \notin \alpha$. Thus $q \in \gamma \setminus \alpha$, proving $\alpha \subsetneq \gamma$, i.e., $\alpha < \gamma$.
- *Trichotomy:* By the definition of set equality ($\alpha = \beta \iff \alpha \subseteq \beta \text{ and } \beta \subseteq \alpha$), at most one of the three relations $\alpha \subsetneq \beta$, $\alpha = \beta$, or $\beta \subsetneq \alpha$ can hold. To see that at least one holds, suppose $\alpha \subsetneq \beta$ and $\alpha = \beta$ both fail. Then $\alpha \not\subseteq \beta$, which means there exists some rational $p \in \alpha$ such that $p \notin \beta$. We claim that $\beta \subsetneq \alpha$. Take *any* element $q \in \beta$. Since $q \in \beta$ and $p \notin \beta$, our fundamental observation about cuts tells us that $q < p$. Now, since $p \in \alpha$ and $q < p$, the Downward Closure of $\alpha$ forces $q \in \alpha$! Because $q \in \beta$ was arbitrary, this proves $\beta \subseteq \alpha$. Furthermore, since $p \in \alpha$ and $p \notin \beta$, we have $\beta \neq \alpha$, so $\beta \subsetneq \alpha$, which means $\beta < \alpha$.

**2. Least Upper Bound Property:**
