Let $A \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ be a non-empty collection of cuts, and suppose $A$ is bounded above in $\mathbb{R}$ by some cut $\beta \in \mathbb{R}$ (meaning $\alpha \le \beta$, or $\alpha \subseteq \beta$, for all $\alpha \in A$). Define the subset $\gamma \subset \mathbb{Q}$ to be the union of all cuts belonging to the family $A$:
$$
\gamma := \bigcup_{\alpha \in A} \alpha = \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : \exists \alpha \in A \text{ such that } p \in \alpha\}
$$
We must first verify that $\gamma$ is a legitimate real number ($\gamma \in \mathbb{R}$) by checking the three Dedekind cut axioms:
- *Non-Triviality ($\gamma \neq \emptyset$ and $\gamma \neq \mathbb{Q}$):* Since $A$ is non-empty, we can pick at least one cut $\alpha_0 \in A$. Because $\alpha_0$ is a cut, $\alpha_0 \neq \emptyset$, so $\gamma \supseteq \alpha_0$ is also non-empty. Next, because $\beta$ is an upper bound for $A$, we have $\alpha \subseteq \beta$ for every $\alpha \in A$, which implies that their union satisfies $\gamma = \bigcup_{\alpha \in A} \alpha \subseteq \beta$. Since $\beta$ is a cut, $\beta \neq \mathbb{Q}$, so there exists a rational $q \in \mathbb{Q} \setminus \beta$. Because $\gamma \subseteq \beta$, we also have $q \notin \gamma$, which proves $\gamma \neq \mathbb{Q}$.
- *Downward Closure:* Suppose $p \in \gamma$ and $q \in \mathbb{Q}$ with $q < p$. By the definition of $\gamma = \bigcup_{\alpha \in A} \alpha$, there exists some cut $\alpha_1 \in A$ such that $p \in \alpha_1$. Since $\alpha_1$ is a Dedekind cut and $q < p \in \alpha_1$, Downward Closure of $\alpha_1$ gives $q \in \alpha_1$. Therefore $q \in \bigcup_{\alpha \in A} \alpha = \gamma$.
- *No Greatest Element:* Suppose $p \in \gamma$. Again, by definition of the union, $p \in \alpha_1$ for some cut $\alpha_1 \in A$. Because $\alpha_1$ is a Dedekind cut, it has no greatest element, so there exists a rational $r \in \alpha_1$ with $p < r$. Since $\alpha_1 \subseteq \gamma$, this same rational satisfies $r \in \gamma$ and $p < r$.

Thus $\gamma \in \mathbb{R}$ is a valid Dedekind cut! Finally, we check the two defining conditions of the supremum $\gamma = \sup A$:
- *$\gamma$ is an upper bound for $A$:* For every $\alpha \in A$, elementary set theory gives $\alpha \subseteq \bigcup_{\alpha' \in A} \alpha' = \gamma$, which by definition of order on $\mathbb{R}$ means $\alpha \le \gamma$ for all $\alpha \in A$.
- *$\gamma$ is the least upper bound for $A$:* Let $\delta \in \mathbb{R}$ be any cut strictly smaller than $\gamma$ ($\delta < \gamma$, i.e., $\delta \subsetneq \gamma$). Then there exists a rational number $p \in \gamma$ such that $p \notin \delta$. Since $p \in \gamma = \bigcup_{\alpha \in A} \alpha$, there exists some cut $\alpha_1 \in A$ such that $p \in \alpha_1$. Now we have $p \in \alpha_1$ and $p \notin \delta$, which means $\alpha_1 \not\subseteq \delta$, or by Trichotomy, $\delta < \alpha_1$. Because $\delta$ is strictly smaller than an element $\alpha_1 \in A$, $\delta$ cannot be an upper bound for $A$. Equivalently, every upper bound of $A$ must be $\ge \gamma$.

This proves that $\gamma = \sup A \in \mathbb{R}$. $\blacksquare$

> [!note] Why Dedekind Cuts Make Completeness Effortless
> Pause and admire how naturally that proof unfolded! In other constructions of $\mathbb{R}$ (such as equivalence classes of Cauchy sequences), proving the Least Upper Bound Property requires a multi-page nested-interval or diagonalization argument. With Dedekind cuts, because we defined $\alpha \le \beta$ as $\alpha \subseteq \beta$, the Least Upper Bound Property is essentially a tautology of set theory: the union $\bigcup_{\alpha \in A} \alpha$ of any bounded family of downward-closed open left rays is still a downward-closed open left ray, and it is automatically the smallest set containing all of them!

## Addition of Dedekind Cuts

Now that $(\mathbb{R}, <)$ is a complete ordered set, we must equip $\mathbb{R}$ with addition and multiplication and verify the ordered field axioms from Section 0.4.

How should we define the sum $\alpha + \beta$ of two cuts $\alpha, \beta \in \mathbb{R}$? Intuitively, if $\alpha$ consists of all rationals $r < x$ and $\beta$ consists of all rationals $s < y$, then the sums $r + s$ sweep out all rationals strictly less than $x + y$. So we define $\alpha + \beta$ to be the set of all pairwise sums $r + s$ with $r \in \alpha$ and $s \in \beta$! And what should the additive identity (zero) of $\mathbb{R}$ be? Naturally, the rational cut $0^* = \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p < 0\}$ consisting of all negative rationals.

What about the **additive inverse (negation)** $-\alpha$ of a cut $\alpha \in \mathbb{R}$? Here we must tread carefully!

> [!warning] Caution: Why the Naive Formula $\{p \in \mathbb{Q} : -p \notin \alpha\}$ Fails for Negation
> Suppose $\alpha$ represents a real number $x$. A rational $p$ should belong to $-\alpha$ when $p < -x$, which is equivalent to $-p > x$. It is very tempting to translate "$-p > x$" into "$-p \notin \alpha$" and define $-\alpha$ naively as $\{p \in \mathbb{Q} : -p \notin \alpha\}$.
>
> Let's test that naive formula on the rational cut $\alpha = 2^* = \{q \in \mathbb{Q} : q < 2\}$. If $p \in \mathbb{Q}$, when is $-p \notin 2^*$?
> $$
> -p \notin 2^* \iff -p \ge 2 \iff p \le -2
> $$
> Look at the resulting set: $\{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p \le -2\}$. **It contains the boundary rational $p = -2$ as a greatest element**, violating Property 3 of a Dedekind cut! Why did this happen? Because for a rational cut $\alpha = 2^*$, the complement $\mathbb{Q} \setminus 2^* = [2, \infty) \cap \mathbb{Q}$ has a *smallest element* (namely $2$ itself, sitting right on the boundary). When we reflect $\mathbb{Q} \setminus \alpha$ across $0$ by negating its elements, that smallest element $2 \in \mathbb{Q} \setminus \alpha$ becomes a *largest element* $-2$!
>
> How do we exclude that single boundary rational without knowing in advance whether $\alpha$ is a rational or irrational cut? We require that $-p$ not merely lie outside $\alpha$, but lie **strictly inside the complement $\mathbb{Q} \setminus \alpha$ with a positive rational safety margin $r > 0$**—meaning there exists some $r \in \mathbb{Q}$ with $r > 0$ such that $-p - r \notin \alpha$! If $\alpha = 2^*$, then for $p = -2$, any $r > 0$ gives $-(-2) - r = 2 - r < 2$, which *is* in $2^*$, so $p = -2$ is excluded! Meanwhile, for any $p < -2$, choosing $r = \frac{-2 - p}{2} > 0$ gives $-p - r > 2$, so $-p - r \notin 2^*$, and every $p < -2$ is included.

With that crucial geometric insight in place, we can state the formal definitions of addition, zero, and negation on $\mathbb{R}$.

> [!abstract] Definition: Addition, Zero, and Negation of Cuts
> Let $\alpha, \beta \in \mathbb{R}$ be Dedekind cuts.
> 1. **Sum ($\alpha + \beta$):** The sum of $\alpha$ and $\beta$ is the set of all pairwise rational sums:
>    $$
>    \alpha + \beta := \{r + s : r \in \alpha \text{ and } s \in \beta\}
>    $$
> 2. **Additive Identity ($0^*$):** The zero element of $\mathbb{R}$ is the rational cut of all negative rationals:
>    $$
>    0^* := \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p < 0\}
>    $$
> 3. **Additive Inverse ($-\alpha$):** The negation of $\alpha$ is the set of all rationals $p$ such that $-p$ lies strictly above the cut $\alpha$ by some positive rational margin $r > 0$:
>    $$
>    -\alpha := \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : \exists r \in \mathbb{Q} \text{ with } r > 0 \text{ such that } -p - r \notin \alpha\}
>    $$

To prove that $\alpha + (-\alpha) = 0^*$, we need a simple, intuitive tool that captures the fact that a Dedekind cut $\alpha$ leaves no "wide gap" between $\alpha$ and its complement $\mathbb{Q} \setminus \alpha$: given any tiny rational step size $d > 0$, we can always find a rational $r \in \alpha$ and a rational $s \notin \alpha$ that straddle the boundary of $\alpha$ within distance $d$ of each other! Let's prove the full additive group structure of $\mathbb{R}$ and see how this straddling idea works in action.

> [!theorem] Additive Group Structure of $\mathbb{R}$ and Order Compatibility
> For all $\alpha, \beta, \gamma \in \mathbb{R}$:
> 1. **Closure:** $\alpha + \beta \in \mathbb{R}$ and $-\alpha \in \mathbb{R}$ are valid Dedekind cuts.
> 2. **Abelian Group Axioms (A1–A4):**
>    - **Commutativity (A1):** $\alpha + \beta = \beta + \alpha$.
>    - **Associativity (A2):** $(\alpha + \beta) + \gamma = \alpha + (\beta + \gamma)$.
>    - **Additive Identity (A3):** $\alpha + 0^* = \alpha$.
>    - **Additive Inverse (A4):** $\alpha + (-\alpha) = 0^*$.
> 3. **Order Preservation under Addition (O3):** If $\alpha < \beta$, then $\alpha + \gamma < \beta + \gamma$.

**Proof:**
**1. Closure of $\alpha + \beta$:**
Let $\alpha, \beta \in \mathbb{R}$. We check the three Dedekind cut axioms for $\alpha + \beta$:
- *Non-Triviality:* Since $\alpha \neq \emptyset$ and $\beta \neq \emptyset$, we can pick $r_0 \in \alpha$ and $s_0 \in \beta$; then $r_0 + s_0 \in \alpha + \beta$, so $\alpha + \beta \neq \emptyset$. Next, since $\alpha \neq \mathbb{Q}$ and $\beta \neq \mathbb{Q}$, we can pick $p \notin \alpha$ and $q \notin \beta$. For every $r \in \alpha$ and $s \in \beta$, we have $r < p$ and $s < q$, so $r + s < p + q$. Thus $p + q$ cannot be written as $r + s$, meaning $p + q \notin \alpha + \beta$ and $\alpha + \beta \neq \mathbb{Q}$.
- *Downward Closure:* Suppose $x \in \alpha + \beta$ and $y \in \mathbb{Q}$ with $y < x$. By definition of $\alpha + \beta$, we can write $x = r + s$ with $r \in \alpha$ and $s \in \beta$. Since $y < r + s$, subtracting $s$ gives $y - s < r$. Because $r \in \alpha$ and $\alpha$ is downward closed, $y - s \in \alpha$. Therefore, $y = (y - s) + s$ is the sum of an element of $\alpha$ and an element of $\beta$, so $y \in \alpha + \beta$.
- *No Greatest Element:* Suppose $x = r + s \in \alpha + \beta$ with $r \in \alpha$ and $s \in \beta$. Because $\alpha$ has no greatest element, there exists $r' \in \alpha$ with $r < r'$. Then $r' + s \in \alpha + \beta$ and $x = r + s < r' + s$.

**2. Commutativity (A1), Associativity (A2), and Identity (A3):**
For any $r \in \alpha, s \in \beta, t \in \gamma$, commutativity $r + s = s + r$ and associativity $(r + s) + t = r + (s + t)$ in $\mathbb{Q}$ immediately imply $\alpha + \beta = \beta + \alpha$ and $(\alpha + \beta) + \gamma = \alpha + (\beta + \gamma)$ as sets.

To prove $\alpha + 0^* = \alpha$, we prove mutual set inclusion ($\alpha + 0^* \subseteq \alpha$ and $\alpha \subseteq \alpha + 0^*$):
- *($\alpha + 0^* \subseteq \alpha$):* Let $x \in \alpha + 0^*$, so $x = r + s$ with $r \in \alpha$ and $s \in 0^*$ (meaning $s < 0$). Because $s < 0$, we have $x = r + s < r$. By Downward Closure of $\alpha$, $r \in \alpha$ and $x < r$ imply $x \in \alpha$. Thus $\alpha + 0^* \subseteq \alpha$.
- *($\alpha \subseteq \alpha + 0^*$):* Let $x \in \alpha$. Because $\alpha$ has no greatest element, there exists some rational $r \in \alpha$ with $x < r$. Set $s := x - r \in \mathbb{Q}$. Since $x < r$, we have $s = x - r < 0$, which means $s \in 0^*$. Therefore, $x = r + (x - r) = r + s \in \alpha + 0^*$. Thus $\alpha \subseteq \alpha + 0^*$, and we conclude $\alpha + 0^* = \alpha$.

**3. Closure of $-\alpha$ and Additive Inverse $\alpha + (-\alpha) = 0^*$ (A4):**
First let's check that $-\alpha$ is a Dedekind cut:
- *Non-Triviality:* Since $\alpha \neq \mathbb{Q}$, pick some $s \notin \alpha$, and set $p := -s - 1 \in \mathbb{Q}$. Choosing $r = 1 > 0$ gives $-p - r = (s + 1) - 1 = s \notin \alpha$, so $p \in -\alpha$ and $-\alpha \neq \emptyset$. Next, since $\alpha \neq \emptyset$, pick some $q \in \alpha$. Could $-q$ belong to $-\alpha$? For any $r > 0$, we have $-(-q) - r = q - r < q \in \alpha$, so by Downward Closure of $\alpha$, $-(-q) - r \in \alpha$. Thus no $r > 0$ can satisfy $-(-q) - r \notin \alpha$, meaning $-q \notin -\alpha$ and $-\alpha \neq \mathbb{Q}$.
- *Downward Closure:* Suppose $p \in -\alpha$ and $q \in \mathbb{Q}$ with $q < p$. Since $p \in -\alpha$, there exists $r > 0$ such that $-p - r \notin \alpha$. Because $q < p$, we have $-q > -p$, so $-q - r > -p - r$. Since $-p - r \notin \alpha$, no larger rational can be in $\alpha$ either, so $-q - r \notin \alpha$. Thus $q \in -\alpha$.
- *No Greatest Element:* Suppose $p \in -\alpha$, so there exists $r > 0$ with $-p - r \notin \alpha$. Split the safety margin $r$ in half! Set $p' := p + \frac{r}{2} \in \mathbb{Q}$ (so $p < p'$) and $r' := \frac{r}{2} > 0$. Then
  $$
  -p' - r' = -\left(p + \frac{r}{2}\right) - \frac{r}{2} = -p - r \notin \alpha
  $$
  Thus $p' \in -\alpha$ with $p < p'$, proving $-\alpha$ has no greatest element.

Now we prove $\alpha + (-\alpha) = 0^*$ by mutual inclusion:
- *($\alpha + (-\alpha) \subseteq 0^*$):* Let $x \in \alpha + (-\alpha)$, so $x = q + p$ for some $q \in \alpha$ and $p \in -\alpha$. By definition of $-\alpha$, there exists $r > 0$ such that $-p - r \notin \alpha$. Since $-p > -p - r \notin \alpha$, we certainly have $-p \notin \alpha$. Because $q \in \alpha$ and $-p \notin \alpha$, every element of $\alpha$ is strictly less than every element outside $\alpha$, giving $q < -p$. Adding $p$ to both sides yields $x = q + p < 0$, so $x \in 0^*$. Thus $\alpha + (-\alpha) \subseteq 0^*$.
- *($0^* \subseteq \alpha + (-\alpha)$):* Let $v \in 0^*$ be any negative rational ($v < 0$). We want to write $v = q + p$ with $q \in \alpha$ and $p \in -\alpha$. Notice that $q + p = v \iff p = v - q \iff -p = -v + q$, so we want $q \in \alpha$ while $q + (-v)$ lies strictly outside $\alpha$ with a positive rational margin! Let $d := \frac{-v}{2} \in \mathbb{Q}$; since $v < 0$, we have $d > 0$ and $-v = 2d$. Pick any rational $a_0 \in \alpha$ and any rational $b_0 \notin \alpha$ (so $a_0 < b_0$). Consider the arithmetic progression of rationals stepping upward from $a_0$ in increments of $d$:
  $$
  a_0, \quad a_0 + d, \quad a_0 + 2d, \quad a_0 + 3d, \quad \dots
  $$
  Because $\mathbb{Q}$ is Archimedean (for a positive rational $d = m/k$ and $b_0 - a_0 = u/w$, choosing a natural number $N > kw(b_0 - a_0)$ gives $a_0 + Nd > b_0$), eventually $a_0 + Nd > b_0 \notin \alpha$, so $a_0 + Nd \notin \alpha$. By the Well-Ordering Principle of $\mathbb{N}$, there is a *smallest* positive integer $n + 1 \in \mathbb{N}^+$ such that $a_0 + (n + 1)d \notin \alpha$, which means its predecessor $q := a_0 + nd$ still belongs to $\alpha$! Thus we have:
  $$
  q := a_0 + nd \in \alpha, \qquad a_0 + (n + 1)d \notin \alpha
  $$
  Now define $p := -a_0 - (n + 2)d \in \mathbb{Q}$. Let's check that $p \in -\alpha$: using the positive margin $r = d > 0$, we compute
  $$
  -p - d = a_0 + (n + 2)d - d = a_0 + (n + 1)d \notin \alpha
  $$
  By definition of $-\alpha$, this guarantees $p \in -\alpha$! Finally, adding $q \in \alpha$ and $p \in -\alpha$ gives
  $$
  \begin{aligned}
  q + p &= (a_0 + nd) + (-a_0 - (n + 2)d) \\
        &= -2d \\
        &= v
  \end{aligned}
  $$
  Thus $v = q + p \in \alpha + (-\alpha)$, proving $0^* \subseteq \alpha + (-\alpha)$ and hence $\alpha + (-\alpha) = 0^*$.

**4. Order Preservation under Addition (O3):**
Suppose $\alpha < \beta$ (so $\alpha \subsetneq \beta$) and let $\gamma \in \mathbb{R}$. First, for any $x \in \alpha + \gamma$, we can write $x = r + t$ with $r \in \alpha$ and $t \in \gamma$. Since $\alpha \subseteq \beta$, we also have $r \in \beta$, so $x = r + t \in \beta + \gamma$. This shows $\alpha + \gamma \subseteq \beta + \gamma$ (i.e., $\alpha + \gamma \le \beta + \gamma$). Could equality $\alpha + \gamma = \beta + \gamma$ hold? If $\alpha + \gamma = \beta + \gamma$, then adding $-\gamma$ to both sides and using Associativity (A2), Inverse (A4), and Identity (A3) would give
$$
\alpha = \alpha + 0^* = \alpha + (\gamma + (-\gamma)) = (\alpha + \gamma) + (-\gamma) = (\beta + \gamma) + (-\gamma) = \beta + (\gamma + (-\gamma)) = \beta + 0^* = \beta
$$
which contradicts $\alpha \subsetneq \beta$! Therefore $\alpha + \gamma \subsetneq \beta + \gamma$, meaning $\alpha + \gamma < \beta + \gamma$. $\blacksquare$

## Multiplication of Dedekind Cuts and the Complete Ordered Field

Having mastered addition, you might expect that we can define the product $\alpha \cdot \beta$ of two Dedekind cuts by simply taking all pairwise products $\{r \cdot s : r \in \alpha \text{ and } s \in \beta\}$. Stop and test that idea for a moment—**it fails catastrophically!**

Why does naive pairwise multiplication fail while pairwise addition worked? Because every Dedekind cut $\alpha$ is a left ray extending all the way to $-\infty$, so **every cut $\alpha$ contains arbitrarily large negative rationals** (such as $-10, -1000, -10^6 \in \alpha$). Under addition, adding two negative numbers gives an even more negative number, which stays safely to the left. Under multiplication, however, **the product of two negative numbers is positive**! Even if $\alpha = 2^*$ and $\beta = 3^*$, both $2^*$ and $3^*$ contain $-1000$, so their pairwise product set $\{r \cdot s : r \in 2^*, s \in 3^*\}$ would contain $(-1000)(-1000) = +1{,}000{,}000$, and in fact would equal the entire set $\mathbb{Q}$!

How do we fix this sign-flip problem? Think about how you multiply real numbers in everyday algebra: you first multiply their **positive absolute values** (where no negative numbers exist to flip the inequality!), and then you attach the appropriate $+$ or $-$ sign according to the standard rules of signs!
- Specifically, when are two cuts $\alpha, \beta \in \mathbb{R}$ **positive**? When $\alpha > 0^*$ and $\beta > 0^*$—meaning $0^* \subsetneq \alpha$ and $0^* \subsetneq \beta$, which simply says that $\alpha$ and $\beta$ contain positive rational numbers $r > 0$ and $s > 0$!
- If $\alpha > 0^*$ and $\beta > 0^*$, then $\alpha \cdot \beta$ should also be a positive cut ($> 0^*$). So $\alpha \cdot \beta$ must contain all non-positive rationals $p \le 0$ (to be downward closed), together with all products $r \cdot s$ of **positive** rationals $r \in \alpha$ ($r > 0$) and $s \in \beta$ ($s > 0$).
- Once $\alpha \cdot \beta$ is defined for positive cuts $\alpha, \beta > 0^*$, we can define $\alpha \cdot \beta$ for negative cuts by noting that if $\alpha < 0^*$, then $-\alpha > 0^*$ (since adding $-\alpha$ to $\alpha < 0^*$ gives $0^* = \alpha + (-\alpha) < 0^* + (-\alpha) = -\alpha$).

And what about the **multiplicative inverse (reciprocal)** $\alpha^{-1}$ of a positive cut $\alpha > 0^*$? Just as we had to include a positive additive margin $r > 0$ in $-\alpha$ so that $-p - r \notin \alpha$ (avoiding a greatest element when $\alpha = 2^*$), when taking reciprocals of a positive cut $\alpha > 0^*$ we must require that for a positive rational $p > 0$ to belong to $\alpha^{-1}$, its reciprocal $\frac{1}{p}$ must lie **strictly outside $\alpha$ with a positive rational margin $r > 0$** (meaning $\frac{1}{p} - r \notin \alpha$). Otherwise, for $\alpha = 2^*$, the set $\{p > 0 : \frac{1}{p} \notin 2^*\} = \{p > 0 : p \le \frac{1}{2}\}$ would contain $p = \frac{1}{2}$ as a greatest element!

> [!abstract] Definition: Multiplication, One, and Reciprocals of Dedekind Cuts
> 1. **Product of Positive Cuts:** For any two positive cuts $\alpha > 0^*$ and $\beta > 0^*$, we define their product $\alpha \cdot \beta$ to be:
>    $$
>    \alpha \cdot \beta := \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p \le 0 \text{ or } (\exists r \in \alpha, s \in \beta \text{ with } r > 0, s > 0 \text{ such that } p = r \cdot s)\}
>    $$
> 2. **Extension to All Cuts via Sign Rules:** For arbitrary cuts $\alpha, \beta \in \mathbb{R}$, we define:
>    $$
>    \alpha \cdot \beta := \begin{cases}
>    0^* & \text{if } \alpha = 0^* \text{ or } \beta = 0^* \\
>    -\big((-\alpha) \cdot \beta\big) & \text{if } \alpha < 0^* \text{ and } \beta > 0^* \\
>    -\big(\alpha \cdot (-\beta)\big) & \text{if } \alpha > 0^* \text{ and } \beta < 0^* \\
>    (-\alpha) \cdot (-\beta) & \text{if } \alpha < 0^* \text{ and } \beta < 0^*
>    \end{cases}
>    $$
> 3. **Multiplicative Identity ($1^*$):** We define the multiplicative identity of $\mathbb{R}$ to be the rational cut $1^* := \{q \in \mathbb{Q} : q < 1\}$.
> 4. **Multiplicative Inverse ($\alpha^{-1}$):** For any positive cut $\alpha > 0^*$, we define its reciprocal $\alpha^{-1}$ by:
>    $$
>    \alpha^{-1} := \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p \le 0\} \cup \left\{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p > 0 \text{ and } \exists r \in \mathbb{Q} \text{ with } r > 0 \text{ such that } \frac{1}{p} - r \notin \alpha\right\}
>    $$
>    For any negative cut $\alpha < 0^*$ (where $-\alpha > 0^*$), we define $\alpha^{-1} := -\big((-\alpha)^{-1}\big)$.

Let's immediately test this definition on our irrational cut $\alpha = \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p \le 0 \text{ or } p^2 < 2\}$ from earlier and verify that $\alpha \cdot \alpha = 2^*$!

> [!example] Example: Verifying that the Cut $\alpha$ for $\sqrt{2}$ Satisfies $\alpha \cdot \alpha = 2^*$
> Let $\alpha = \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p \le 0 \text{ or } p^2 < 2\}$. Since $1 \in \alpha \setminus 0^*$, $\alpha > 0^*$ is a positive cut. Prove directly from the definition of cut multiplication that
> $$
> \alpha \cdot \alpha = 2^*
> $$

**Solution:**
We prove the set equality $\alpha \cdot \alpha = 2^*$ by showing mutual inclusion ($\alpha \cdot \alpha \subseteq 2^*$ and $2^* \subseteq \alpha \cdot \alpha$):
1. **($\alpha \cdot \alpha \subseteq 2^*$):** Let $p \in \alpha \cdot \alpha$. By definition of the product of positive cuts, either $p \le 0$ or $p = r \cdot s$ for some $r, s \in \alpha$ with $r > 0$ and $s > 0$.
   - If $p \le 0$, then $p < 2$, so $p \in 2^*$.
   - If $p = r \cdot s$ with $r, s \in \alpha$ and $r > 0, s > 0$, then by definition of $\alpha$, we have $r^2 < 2$ and $s^2 < 2$. Since $r, s > 0$, multiplying these two strict inequalities in $\mathbb{Q}$ gives $(rs)^2 = r^2 s^2 < 2 \cdot 2 = 4$. If $rs \ge 2$ were true, squaring would give $(rs)^2 \ge 4$, a contradiction! Therefore $p = rs < 2$, which means $p \in 2^*$.
   In both cases $p \in 2^*$, so $\alpha \cdot \alpha \subseteq 2^*$.
2. **($2^* \subseteq \alpha \cdot \alpha$):** Conversely, let $p \in 2^*$ (so $p < 2$).
   - If $p \le 0$, then $p \in \alpha \cdot \alpha$ directly by the first clause of the definition of $\alpha \cdot \beta$.
   - If $0 < p < 2$, we want to write $p = r \cdot s$ for some positive rationals $r, s \in \alpha$. Even better, if we can find a positive rational $r \in \alpha$ such that $r^2 > p$ (meaning $p < r^2 < 2$), then since $\alpha \cdot \alpha$ is downward closed (or directly by setting $s = \frac{p}{r} \in \mathbb{Q}$, which satisfies $0 < s < r \in \alpha \implies s \in \alpha$), we will have $p = r \cdot \left(\frac{p}{r}\right) = r \cdot s \in \alpha \cdot \alpha$!
   How do we find a rational $r > 0$ such that $p < r^2 < 2$? Notice that $\frac{p}{2} < 1$, and as $n \to \infty$, $(1 + \frac{1}{n})^2 \to 1$. More concretely, since $0 < p < 2$, the set of rationals in $\alpha$ has no greatest element, and in fact we can step from $1 \in \alpha$ in tiny rational increments $d > 0$ (where $d < \frac{2 - p}{5}$) up to the last step $r = 1 + nd \in \alpha$ before exiting $\alpha$ (so $(r + d)^2 \ge 2$). Since $r \in \alpha$ and $r \ge 1$ while $2 \le (r + d)^2 = r^2 + 2rd + d^2 < r^2 + 5d$ (as $r < 2$ and $d < 1$), we have $r^2 > 2 - 5d > p$! Setting $s := \frac{p}{r} > 0$, we have $s < r \in \alpha \implies s \in \alpha$ by Downward Closure, and thus $p = r \cdot s \in \alpha \cdot \alpha$.

Therefore $\alpha \cdot \alpha = 2^*$, so $\alpha$ is indeed a square root of $2^*$ in $\mathbb{R}$! $\blacksquare$

Now let's establish the culmination of Sections 0.4 and 0.5: equipped with $+, \cdot, <$, the set of Dedekind cuts $\mathbb{R}$ is a **complete ordered field**, and the rational numbers $\mathbb{Q}$ sit inside $\mathbb{R}$ via the canonical map $r \mapsto r^*$ in a way that preserves all addition, multiplication, and order!

> [!theorem] $\mathbb{R}$ Is a Complete Ordered Field Extending $\mathbb{Q}$
> 1. Equipped with $+, \cdot, <$ defined on Dedekind cuts, $\mathbb{R}$ is an **ordered field** that satisfies the **Least Upper Bound Property** (i.e., $\mathbb{R}$ is a **complete ordered field**).
> 2. The map $\iota: \mathbb{Q} \hookrightarrow \mathbb{R}$ defined by $\iota(r) = r^* = \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p < r\}$ is an **order-preserving field embedding**: it is injective, and for all $r, s \in \mathbb{Q}$,
>    $$
>    (r + s)^* = r^* + s^*, \qquad (r \cdot s)^* = r^* \cdot s^*, \qquad r < s \iff r^* < s^*
>    $$

**Proof:**
**1. Ordered Field Axioms for Multiplication:**
Let $\alpha, \beta, \gamma > 0^*$ be positive cuts. Notice that the positive rationals $\mathbb{Q}^+ = \{q \in \mathbb{Q} : q > 0\}$ under multiplication $(\mathbb{Q}^+, \cdot, 1, <)$ satisfy the exact same algebraic and order laws as $(\mathbb{Q}, +, 0, <)$ under addition:
- *Closure and Positivity (O4):* Since $\alpha > 0^*$ and $\beta > 0^*$, there exist $r_0 \in \alpha$ and $s_0 \in \beta$ with $r_0 > 0$ and $s_0 > 0$. Their product $r_0 s_0 > 0$ belongs to $\alpha \cdot \beta$ (and is not in $0^*$), so $\alpha \cdot \beta \neq \emptyset$ and $0^* \subsetneq \alpha \cdot \beta$ (which proves Order Axiom O4: $\alpha > 0^* \text{ and } \beta > 0^* \implies \alpha \cdot \beta > 0^*$). If $p \notin \alpha$ and $q \notin \beta$ (so $p > r_0 > 0$ and $q > s_0 > 0$), then every $r \in \alpha, s \in \beta$ with $r, s > 0$ satisfies $rs < pq$, so $pq \notin \alpha \cdot \beta$ and $\alpha \cdot \beta \neq \mathbb{Q}$. Downward closure holds because if $y < x \in \alpha \cdot \beta$ and $y > 0$, then $x = rs$ ($r \in \alpha, s \in \beta, r, s > 0$), so $y = \left(\frac{y}{s}\right) s$ where $0 < \frac{y}{s} < r \in \alpha \implies \frac{y}{s} \in \alpha$, hence $y \in \alpha \cdot \beta$. And $\alpha \cdot \beta$ has no greatest element because for $p \le 0$ we have $r_0 s_0 > 0 \ge p$, while for $p = rs > 0$ we can choose $r' \in \alpha$ with $r' > r > 0$, giving $r's > rs = p$ with $r's \in \alpha \cdot \beta$.
- *Commutativity (M1), Associativity (M2), Identity (M3), Inverse (M4), and Distributivity (D) for Positive Cuts:* Commutativity $rs = sr$, associativity $(rs)t = r(st)$, and distributivity $r(s + t) = rs + rt$ for positive rationals $r, s, t > 0$ immediately transfer to $\alpha \cdot \beta = \beta \cdot \alpha$, $(\alpha \cdot \beta) \cdot \gamma = \alpha \cdot (\beta \cdot \gamma)$, and $\alpha \cdot (\beta + \gamma) = (\alpha \cdot \beta) + (\alpha \cdot \gamma)$ for $\alpha, \beta, \gamma > 0^*$. For $\alpha \cdot 1^* = \alpha$ ($\alpha > 0^*$): if $r \in \alpha$ ($r > 0$) and $s \in 1^*$ ($0 < s < 1$), then $rs < r \implies rs \in \alpha$; conversely, if $x \in \alpha$ and $x > 0$, choosing $r \in \alpha$ with $x < r$ gives $x = r \cdot \left(\frac{x}{r}\right) \in \alpha \cdot 1^*$ since $0 < \frac{x}{r} < 1 \implies \frac{x}{r} \in 1^*$. The verification of $\alpha \cdot \alpha^{-1} = 1^*$ for $\alpha > 0^*$ follows the exact same straddling argument as $\alpha + (-\alpha) = 0^*$ (replacing additive steps $a_0 + nd$ by multiplicative steps $a_0 (1 + d)^n$, which you will carry out in full detail in Problem 0.5.2!). Finally, all identities extend from positive cuts to arbitrary cuts $\alpha, \beta, \gamma \in \mathbb{R}$ by routine inspection of sign cases in the piecewise definition of $\alpha \cdot \beta$.

**2. Order-Preserving Field Embedding $\iota: \mathbb{Q} \hookrightarrow \mathbb{R}$:**
Let $r, s \in \mathbb{Q}$.
- *Order Preservation and Injectivity:* If $r < s$, then $r \in s^*$ (since $r < s$) while $r \notin r^*$ (since $r \not< r$); also any $p \in r^*$ satisfies $p < r < s \implies p \in s^*$. Thus $r^* \subsetneq s^*$, i.e., $r^* < s^*$. Conversely, if $r^* < s^*$, then by Trichotomy in $\mathbb{Q}$ we cannot have $r = s$ (which gives $r^* = s^*$) or $s < r$ (which gives $s^* < r^*$), so $r < s$. In particular, $r^* = s^* \iff r = s$, so $\iota$ is injective.
- *Preservation of Addition ($r^* + s^* = (r + s)^*$):* If $x \in r^* + s^*$, then $x = p + q$ with $p < r$ and $q < s$, so $x = p + q < r + s \implies x \in (r + s)^*$. Conversely, if $x \in (r + s)^*$ (so $x < r + s$), let $h := \frac{(r + s) - x}{2} > 0$. Setting $p := r - h < r$ (so $p \in r^*$) and $q := s - h < s$ (so $q \in s^*$), we have
  $$
  p + q = (r - h) + (s - h) = (r + s) - 2h = x
  $$
  so $x \in r^* + s^*$. Thus $r^* + s^* = (r + s)^*$. Note that this also shows $(-r)^* + r^* = 0^*$, so $(-r)^* = -(r^*)$ by uniqueness of additive inverses.
- *Preservation of Multiplication ($r^* \cdot s^* = (r \cdot s)^*$):* Suppose first that $r > 0$ and $s > 0$. If $x \in r^* \cdot s^*$, then either $x \le 0 < rs$ or $x = pq$ with $0 < p < r$ and $0 < q < s$, so $x = pq < rs \implies x \in (rs)^*$. Conversely, if $x \in (rs)^*$, then for $x \le 0$ we have $x \in r^* \cdot s^*$ by definition; for $0 < x < rs$, let $t := \frac{x}{rs} \in (0, 1)$, and choose a rational $u \in (t, 1)$ (for instance $u = \frac{t + 1}{2}$). Then $p := r u \in (0, r) \subset r^*$ and $q := s \left(\frac{t}{u}\right) \in (0, s) \subset s^*$ are positive rationals in $r^*$ and $s^*$ whose product is $pq = rs u \left(\frac{t}{u}\right) = rst = x \in r^* \cdot s^*$. Thus $r^* \cdot s^* = (rs)^*$ for $r, s > 0$, and the remaining sign cases follow immediately from $(-r)^* = -(r^*)$. $\blacksquare$

> [!note] Dropping the Star Notation and Uniqueness of Complete Ordered Fields
> Because $\iota(r) = r^*$ embeds $\mathbb{Q}$ into $\mathbb{R}$ while preserving $+, \cdot, <$ identically, we now identify each rational number $r \in \mathbb{Q}$ with its rational cut $r^* \in \mathbb{R}$ and write $r$ instead of $r^*$ (so $\mathbb{Q} \subset \mathbb{R}$, $0^* = 0$, and $1^* = 1$), and we write real numbers as $x, y, z \in \mathbb{R}$ instead of $\alpha, \beta, \gamma$.
>
> Even better, we never have to look back at the internal set-theoretic scaffolding of Dedekind cuts again! Why? Because in Problem 0.5.5 at the end of this section, you will prove one of the most profound theorems in mathematics: **any two complete ordered fields $(\mathbb{F}_1, +_1, \cdot_1, <_1)$ and $(\mathbb{F}_2, +_2, \cdot_2, <_2)$ are uniquely order-isomorphic**. That is, there exists a *unique* bijection $\Phi: \mathbb{F}_1 \to \mathbb{F}_2$ that preserves addition ($\Phi(x +_1 y) = \Phi(x) +_2 \Phi(y)$), multiplication ($\Phi(x \cdot_1 y) = \Phi(x) \cdot_2 \Phi(y)$), and order ($x <_1 y \iff \Phi(x) <_2 \Phi(y)$).
>
> What does this mean conceptually? It means that **everything true about the real numbers follows purely from the Complete Ordered Field Axioms** (the field axioms, the order axioms, and the Least Upper Bound Property). Dedekind cuts served their purpose by proving that a complete ordered field actually *exists* without logical contradiction; from this moment onward, we work entirely from the axioms of a complete ordered field!

## Rewards of Completeness: The Archimedean Property, Density of $\mathbb{Q}$, and $n$-th Roots

Now that we stand on the solid axiomatic ground of a complete ordered field $\mathbb{R}$, let's reap three immediate, foundational rewards of the **Least Upper Bound Property**:
1. **The Archimedean Property:** $\mathbb{R}$ contains no "infinitely large" elements (every real $y$ is surpassed by some natural number $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$) and no non-zero "infinitesimals" (for every $\varepsilon > 0$, there is some $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$ with $0 < \frac{1}{n} < \varepsilon$).
2. **Density of $\mathbb{Q}$ in $\mathbb{R}$:** Between any two distinct real numbers $x < y$, no matter how microscopically close together they are, there exists a rational number $r \in \mathbb{Q}$ with $x < r < y$.
3. **Existence of Positive $n$-th Roots:** For every real $y > 0$ and every positive integer $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$, the equation $x^n = y$ has a unique positive real solution $x = \sqrt[n]{y} \in \mathbb{R}$.

Wait—isn't it obvious that $\mathbb{N} = \{1, 2, 3, \dots\}$ isn't bounded above in $\mathbb{R}$? In Calculus I, we take $\lim_{n \to \infty} \frac{1}{n} = 0$ for granted. Yet in modern algebra and analysis, one can construct **non-Archimedean ordered fields** (such as the field of rational functions $\mathbb{R}(t)$ ordered by dominance as $t \to \infty$, or Abraham Robinson's *hyperreal numbers* ${}^*\mathbb{R}$) that satisfy *all* the ordered field axioms (A1–A4, M1–M4, D, O1–O4), but also contain "infinite" elements $\omega$ larger than every natural number $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$, as well as positive "infinitesimals" $\varepsilon = \frac{1}{\omega} > 0$ smaller than $\frac{1}{n}$ for every $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$!

Why can't such infinite or infinitesimal elements exist in $\mathbb{R}$? Because **the Least Upper Bound Property forbids $\mathbb{N}^+$ from being bounded above**! Let's see how the proof works: if $\mathbb{N}^+$ (or the multiples $\{nx : n \in \mathbb{N}^+\}$ of a step $x > 0$) were bounded above, it would have a least upper bound $\alpha = \sup A$. Stepping backward from the ceiling $\alpha$ by one step $x$ gives $\alpha - x < \alpha$, which is no longer an upper bound, so some multiple $mx$ must exceed $\alpha - x$. But then the very next step $(m + 1)x = mx + x$ breaks right through the ceiling $\alpha$, a contradiction!

> [!theorem] The Archimedean Property of $\mathbb{R}$
> Let $x, y \in \mathbb{R}$.
> 1. **General Stepping Form:** If $x > 0$, then there exists a positive integer $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$ such that
>    $$
>    nx > y
>    $$
> 2. **Unboundedness of $\mathbb{N}$:** Taking $x = 1$, for every real number $y \in \mathbb{R}$ there exists $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$ such that $n > y$ (so $\mathbb{N}^+$ is not bounded above in $\mathbb{R}$).
> 3. **No Infinitesimals ($\frac{1}{n} < \varepsilon$):** For every real $\varepsilon > 0$, there exists $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$ such that
>    $$
>    0 < \frac{1}{n} < \varepsilon
>    $$

**Proof:**
**1. General Stepping Form ($nx > y$):**
Let $x, y \in \mathbb{R}$ with $x > 0$, and consider the non-empty subset $A \subset \mathbb{R}$ consisting of all positive integer multiples of $x$:
$$
A := \{nx : n \in \mathbb{N}^+\}
$$
Suppose, for the sake of contradiction, that the theorem is false—meaning $nx \le y$ for *every* $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$. Then $y$ is an upper bound for $A$ in $\mathbb{R}$.

Because $A$ is non-empty ($1 \cdot x \in A$) and bounded above by $y$, the **Least Upper Bound Property** of $\mathbb{R}$ guarantees that $A$ possesses a least upper bound (supremum):
$$
\alpha := \sup A \in \mathbb{R}
$$
Since $x > 0$, subtracting $x$ from $\alpha$ gives $\alpha - x < \alpha$. Now invoke the defining property of the *least* upper bound: because $\alpha - x$ is strictly smaller than $\alpha = \sup A$, the number $\alpha - x$ **cannot be an upper bound for $A$**! Therefore, there must exist at least one element of $A$—say $mx \in A$ for some positive integer $m \in \mathbb{N}^+$—that is strictly greater than $\alpha - x$:
$$
\alpha - x < mx
$$
Adding $x$ to both sides of this inequality (using Order Axiom O3) yields:
$$
\alpha < mx + x = (m + 1)x
$$
Look at the right-hand side: since $m \in \mathbb{N}^+$, its successor $m + 1$ is also a positive integer in $\mathbb{N}^+$, which means $(m + 1)x$ belongs to the set $A$! Thus we have found an element $(m + 1)x \in A$ that is strictly greater than $\alpha = \sup A$, contradicting the fact that $\alpha$ is an upper bound for $A$. Therefore, our assumption that $nx \le y$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$ must be false, and there exists some $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$ such that $nx > y$.

**2. Unboundedness of $\mathbb{N}$ and 3. No Infinitesimals:**
Setting $x = 1 > 0$ in Part (1) immediately yields $n = n \cdot 1 > y$ for any $y \in \mathbb{R}$. Similarly, given any $\varepsilon > 0$, applying Part (1) with $x = \varepsilon > 0$ and $y = 1$ yields some $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$ such that $n \varepsilon > 1$. Multiplying both sides by the positive real $(n)^{-1} > 0$ gives $0 < \frac{1}{n} < \varepsilon$. $\blacksquare$

Next, let's prove that the rational numbers $\mathbb{Q}$ are **dense in $\mathbb{R}$**: between any two real numbers $x < y$, there is a rational number $r = \frac{m}{n} \in \mathbb{Q}$.

How do we find a fraction $\frac{m}{n}$ trapped inside a tiny interval $(x, y)$? Think of $\frac{1}{n}$ as the spacing between tick marks on a ruler. If the interval $(x, y)$ has length $y - x > 0$ and is extremely narrow, our first job is to choose a denominator $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$ large enough that the tick spacing $\frac{1}{n}$ is smaller than the width $y - x$ of the interval! Algebraically,
$$
\frac{1}{n} < y - x \iff n(y - x) > 1 \iff ny - nx > 1
$$
Once we scale the interval $(x, y)$ up by a factor of $n$ to the interval $(nx, ny)$, its width is $ny - nx > 1$! And if an interval $(nx, ny)$ on the number line has width strictly greater than $1$, you cannot step from left to right in integer steps of size $1$ without landing on at least one integer $m \in \mathbb{Z}$ inside $(nx, ny)$! Dividing $nx < m < ny$ back by $n$ then hands us $x < \frac{m}{n} < y$. Let's write out this "Archimedean magnifying glass" argument rigorously.

> [!theorem] Density of $\mathbb{Q}$ in $\mathbb{R}$
> If $x, y \in \mathbb{R}$ and $x < y$, then there exists a rational number $r \in \mathbb{Q}$ such that
> $$
> x < r < y
> $$

**Proof:**
Since $x < y$, their difference is strictly positive: $y - x > 0$. By the **Archimedean Property** applied to $y - x > 0$ and $1$, there exists a positive integer $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$ such that
$$
n(y - x) > 1 \implies ny - nx > 1 \implies nx + 1 < ny
$$
We now need to show that there exists an integer $m \in \mathbb{Z}$ satisfying $nx < m \le nx + 1$. To find the *first* integer $m$ strictly to the right of $nx$, we apply the Archimedean Property twice more (with step size $1 > 0$) to find positive integers $m_1, m_2 \in \mathbb{N}^+$ such that $m_1 > nx$ and $m_2 > -nx$. Multiplying $m_2 > -nx$ by $-1$ gives $-m_2 < nx$, so $nx$ is trapped between two integers:
$$
-m_2 < nx < m_1
$$
Now consider the set of positive integers $S \subset \mathbb{N}^+$ defined by shifting the integers above $nx$ by $m_2$:
$$
S := \{k \in \mathbb{N}^+ : -m_2 + k > nx\}
$$
Because $-m_2 + (m_1 + m_2) = m_1 > nx$ and $m_1 + m_2 \in \mathbb{N}^+$, the set $S$ is a non-empty subset of $\mathbb{N}^+$. By the **Well-Ordering Principle** of $\mathbb{N}$ (Section 0.2), $S$ has a least element $k_0 \in S$. Define the integer
$$
m := -m_2 + k_0 \in \mathbb{Z}
$$
Let's check the two inequalities satisfied by $m$:
1. Since $k_0 \in S$, we have $m = -m_2 + k_0 > nx$.
2. What about the predecessor $m - 1 = -m_2 + (k_0 - 1)$? If $k_0 = 1$, then $m - 1 = -m_2 < nx$. If $k_0 > 1$, then $k_0 - 1 \in \mathbb{N}^+$ is strictly smaller than the least element $k_0$ of $S$, so $k_0 - 1 \notin S$, which means $m - 1 = -m_2 + (k_0 - 1) \le nx$. In either case, $m - 1 \le nx$, or equivalently $m \le nx + 1$.

Chaining our inequalities together with $nx + 1 < ny$ gives:
$$
nx < m \le nx + 1 < ny
$$
Since $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$ satisfies $n > 0$, multiplying the strict inequalities $nx < m < ny$ by $\frac{1}{n} > 0$ preserves the order:
$$
x < \frac{m}{n} < y
$$
Since $m \in \mathbb{Z}$ and $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$, the number $r := \frac{m}{n}$ is a rational number in $\mathbb{Q}$ strictly between $x$ and $y$. $\blacksquare$

> [!example] Example: Finding an Explicit Rational Between Two Close Reals via the Archimedean Magnifying Glass
> Use the constructive method of the proof of the Density of $\mathbb{Q}$ to find a rational number $r = \frac{m}{n}$ strictly between $x = \sqrt{2} \approx 1.4142\dots$ and $y = \sqrt{2} + 0.03$ (given only that $1.41 < \sqrt{2} < 1.42$).

**Solution:**
1. **Magnify the gap $y - x$ until it exceeds $1$:** Here the gap width is $y - x = 0.03 = \frac{3}{100}$. By the Archimedean Property, we choose a positive integer $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$ such that $n(y - x) > 1$, i.e., $n \cdot \frac{3}{100} > 1 \iff n > \frac{100}{3} = 33.\overline{3}$. Let's pick $n = 34$ (or for clean decimal arithmetic, $n = 100$). Let's test $n = 34$: then $n(y - x) = 34(0.03) = 1.02 > 1$.
2. **Locate the first integer $m > nx$:** Using $1.41 < \sqrt{2} < 1.42$ and $n = 100$ (so $n(y - x) = 3 > 1$), we have
   $$
   141 < 100 x < 142 \quad \text{and} \quad 144 < 100 y < 145
   $$
   The smallest integer $m$ strictly greater than $100x \in (141, 142)$ is $m = 142$.
3. **Divide by $n$:** Since $100x < 142 < 144 < 100y$, dividing by $n = 100$ gives the explicit rational number
   $$
   x < \frac{142}{100} = \frac{71}{50} < y
   $$
   (Indeed, $\frac{143}{100}$ and $\frac{144}{100}$ also work!) $\blacksquare$

Finally, let us come full circle to the equation $x^2 = 2$ (and more generally $x^n = y$ for any real $y > 0$ and positive integer $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$) that exposed the incompleteness of $\mathbb{Q}$ in Section 0.4. Using only the Complete Ordered Field Axioms of $\mathbb{R}$—without ever unpacking cuts again—let's prove that every positive real number $y > 0$ has a unique positive $n$-th root $x = \sqrt[n]{y} \in \mathbb{R}$!

Pay close attention to the proof technique below, because it is the **blueprint for almost every existence proof in Real Analysis** (including the proof of the Intermediate Value Theorem later in the course):
1. Define the set $E = \{t \in \mathbb{R} : t > 0 \text{ and } t^n < y\}$ of all positive real numbers that *undershoot* the target $y$.
2. Show that $E$ is non-empty and bounded above, so the **Least Upper Bound Property** produces a candidate solution $x := \sup E$.
3. Use a "small nudge" inequality to show that if $x^n < y$, you can nudge $x$ slightly upward to $x + h \in E$ (contradicting that $x$ is an upper bound of $E$), and if $x^n > y$, you can nudge $x$ slightly downward to an upper bound $x - k$ of $E$ (contradicting that $x$ is the *least* upper bound of $E$). By Trichotomy, only $x^n = y$ survives!

To make step (3) clean for any power $n \ge 1$, we use a simple algebraic bound for the difference of $n$-th powers $b^n - a^n$ when $0 < a < b$. Recall from algebra that $b^n - a^n$ factors as
$$
b^n - a^n = (b - a)\left(b^{n-1} + b^{n-2}a + b^{n-3}a^2 + \dots + a^{n-1}\right)
$$
Notice that the second factor is a sum of $n$ terms, and since $0 < a < b$, every single term $b^{n-1-k}a^k$ is strictly less than or equal to $b^{n-1}$! Replacing each of the $n$ terms by $b^{n-1}$ yields the fundamental polynomial increment bound:
$$
b^n - a^n \le (b - a) n b^{n-1} \quad \text{for all } 0 < a < b
$$
(Notice that from a Calculus I perspective, this is just the Mean Value Theorem $\frac{b^n - a^n}{b - a} = n c^{n-1} \le n b^{n-1}$ proved purely algebraically!)

> [!theorem] Existence and Uniqueness of Positive $n$-th Roots in $\mathbb{R}$
> For every real number $y > 0$ and every positive integer $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$, there exists a **unique** real number $x > 0$ such that
> $$
> x^n = y
> $$
> This unique positive real number $x$ is denoted by $\sqrt[n]{y}$ or $y^{1/n}$.

**Proof:**
**1. Uniqueness:**
Suppose $x_1, x_2 \in \mathbb{R}$ are positive reals with $0 < x_1 < x_2$. By repeated application of order preservation under positive multiplication (Axiom O4, proved by induction on $n$), we have $x_1^n < x_2^n$. Thus $x_1^n$ and $x_2^n$ cannot both equal $y$, so at most one positive real $x > 0$ can satisfy $x^n = y$.

**2. Existence via the Least Upper Bound Property:**
Define the subset $E \subset \mathbb{R}$ of all positive reals whose $n$-th power is strictly less than $y$:
$$
E := \{t \in \mathbb{R} : t > 0 \text{ and } t^n < y\}
$$
Let us first verify that $E$ is non-empty and bounded above in $\mathbb{R}$:
- *$E$ is non-empty:* We need a small positive real $t_0 > 0$ that is guaranteed to satisfy $t_0 < 1$ and $t_0 < y$ (whether $y$ is tiny like $0.001$ or huge like $1000$). Set
  $$
  t_0 := \frac{y}{1 + y}
  $$
  Since $y > 0$, we have $0 < y < 1 + y$, so dividing by $1 + y > 0$ gives $0 < t_0 < 1$. Also, since $1 + y > 1$, we have $t_0 = \frac{y}{1 + y} < y$. Because $0 < t_0 < 1$, multiplying by $t_0$ repeatedly decreases the value: $t_0^n \le t_0 < y$. Thus $t_0 \in E$, proving $E \neq \emptyset$.
- *$E$ is bounded above:* We need a large real $M$ guaranteed to exceed both $1$ and $y$. Set
  $$
  M := 1 + y
  $$
  Since $y > 0$, we have $M > 1$ and $M > y$. For any real $t > M$, we have $t > 1$ and $t > y$, so $t^n \ge t > y$, which implies $t \notin E$. By contraposition, every $t \in E$ satisfies $t \le M$, so $M = 1 + y$ is an upper bound for $E$.

Because $E \subset \mathbb{R}$ is non-empty and bounded above, the **Least Upper Bound Property** guarantees that $E$ has a supremum in $\mathbb{R}$:
$$
x := \sup E \in \mathbb{R}
$$
Note that since $t_0 \in E$ and $x$ is an upper bound of $E$, we have $x \ge t_0 > 0$, so $x > 0$.

By the Trichotomy Law in $\mathbb{R}$, exactly one of $x^n < y$, $x^n > y$, or $x^n = y$ holds. We now show that both $x^n < y$ and $x^n > y$ lead to contradictions using the algebraic bound $b^n - a^n \le (b - a) n b^{n-1}$ ($0 < a < b$):
- **Case 1: Suppose $x^n < y$.** Then the gap $y - x^n > 0$ is strictly positive. We want to nudge $x$ upward by a small positive amount $h \in (0, 1)$ so that $(x + h)^n < y$, which will put $x + h \in E$ and contradict $x = \sup E$! For any $h \in (0, 1)$, applying $b^n - a^n \le (b - a) n b^{n-1}$ with $a = x$ and $b = x + h < x + 1$ gives:
  $$
  \begin{aligned}
  (x + h)^n - x^n &\le \big((x + h) - x\big) n (x + h)^{n-1} \\
                  &= h n (x + h)^{n-1} \\
                  &< h n (x + 1)^{n-1}
  \end{aligned}
  $$
  To force $(x + h)^n - x^n < y - x^n$, we simply choose $h \in (0, 1)$ small enough that $h n (x + 1)^{n-1} < y - x^n$—for instance, set
  $$
  h := \frac{1}{2} \min\left(1, \frac{y - x^n}{n(x + 1)^{n-1}}\right) > 0
  $$
  Then $0 < h < 1$ and
  $$
  (x + h)^n - x^n < h n (x + 1)^{n-1} < y - x^n \implies (x + h)^n < y
  $$
  Since $x + h > x > 0$ and $(x + h)^n < y$, the number $x + h$ belongs to $E$! But $x + h > x$, contradicting the fact that $x = \sup E$ is an upper bound for $E$. Thus $x^n < y$ is impossible.
- **Case 2: Suppose $x^n > y$.** Then the excess $x^n - y > 0$ is strictly positive. We want to nudge $x$ downward by a small positive amount $k > 0$ so that $x - k$ is *still* an upper bound for $E$, contradicting that $x$ is the *least* upper bound of $E$! Specifically, define
  $$
  k := \frac{x^n - y}{n x^{n-1}}
  $$
  Since $x > 0$ and $x^n > y > 0$, we have $k > 0$, and also $k < \frac{x^n}{n x^{n-1}} = \frac{x}{n} \le x$, so $x - k > 0$. Now test *any* real number $t$ satisfying $t \ge x - k$:
  - If $t \ge x$, then $t^n \ge x^n > y$.
  - If $x - k \le t < x$, then $0 < x - t \le k$. Applying $b^n - a^n \le (b - a) n b^{n-1}$ with $a = t$ and $b = x$ gives:
    $$
    \begin{aligned}
    x^n - t^n &\le (x - t) n x^{n-1} \\
              &\le k n x^{n-1} \\
              &= \left(\frac{x^n - y}{n x^{n-1}}\right) n x^{n-1} \\
              &= x^n - y
    \end{aligned}
    $$
    Rearranging $x^n - t^n \le x^n - y$ immediately yields $t^n \ge y$!
  In both subcases, every $t \ge x - k$ satisfies $t^n \ge y$, which means $t \notin E$. By contraposition, every element $t \in E$ must satisfy $t < x - k$! Therefore, $x - k$ is an upper bound for $E$ that is strictly smaller than $x = \sup E$—contradicting the definition of the *least* upper bound! Thus $x^n > y$ is also impossible.

Since both $x^n < y$ and $x^n > y$ are impossible, Trichotomy forces $x^n = y$. $\blacksquare$

> [!summary] Summary of the Number System Pipeline ($\emptyset \to \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{Z} \to \mathbb{Q} \to \mathbb{R}$)
> Let's step back and admire the complete edifice we have constructed from nothing but the empty set $\emptyset$:
> 1. **Sets $\to \mathbb{N}$ (Counting & Induction):** Starting from $0 = \emptyset$ and $S(n) = n \cup \{n\}$, the **Peano Axioms** give us mathematical induction, recursive definitions of $+$ and $\cdot$, cancellation laws, no zero divisors, and the Well-Ordering Principle.
> 2. **$\mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{Z}$ (Additive Inverses / Subtraction):** Taking equivalence classes $[a, b]$ of formal differences $(a, b) \in \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$ under $a + d = b + c$ upgrades $\mathbb{N}$ to an ordered **integral domain** (commutative ring with identity and no zero divisors) where every additive equation $b + x = a$ is uniquely solvable.
> 3. **$\mathbb{Z} \to \mathbb{Q}$ (Multiplicative Inverses / Division):** Taking equivalence classes $[p, q]$ of formal fractions $(p, q) \in \mathbb{Z} \times \mathbb{Z}^*$ under $ps = qr$ upgrades $\mathbb{Z}$ to an **ordered field** where every linear equation $qx = p$ ($q \neq 0$) is uniquely solvable, though $\mathbb{Q}$ remains analytically incomplete (lacking $\sqrt{2}$ and failing the Least Upper Bound Property).
> 4. **$\mathbb{Q} \to \mathbb{R}$ (Analytical Completeness / Supremums & Limits):** Taking **Dedekind cuts** $\alpha \subset \mathbb{Q}$ seals every pinhole in $\mathbb{Q}$, producing the unique **complete ordered field** $\mathbb{R}$ in which every non-empty set bounded above has a least upper bound $\sup A = \bigcup_{\alpha \in A} \alpha$—unlocking $n$-th roots, the Archimedean Property, and the entire machinery of Calculus and Real Analysis!

## 0.5 Exercises

### Problem 0.5.1: Characterizing Rational vs. Irrational Cuts via the Upper Class

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> For any Dedekind cut $\alpha \in \mathbb{R}$, let $\alpha^c := \mathbb{Q} \setminus \alpha$ denote the **upper class** of rational numbers not belonging to $\alpha$. Prove that $\alpha$ is a **rational cut** (meaning $\alpha = r^* = \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p < r\}$ for some $r \in \mathbb{Q}$) **if and only if** the complement $\alpha^c$ possesses a smallest element in $\mathbb{Q}$ (meaning there exists $r \in \alpha^c$ such that $r \le q$ for all $q \in \alpha^c$).

**Solution:**
We prove both directions of the "if and only if" statement separately.

**1. Forward Direction ($\implies$):**
Suppose $\alpha$ is a rational cut, so there exists some rational number $r \in \mathbb{Q}$ such that
$$
\alpha = r^* = \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p < r\}
$$
Then the complement $\alpha^c = \mathbb{Q} \setminus \alpha$ is given by Trichotomy in $\mathbb{Q}$ as
$$
\alpha^c = \{q \in \mathbb{Q} : q \not< r\} = \{q \in \mathbb{Q} : q \ge r\}
$$
We claim that $r$ is the smallest element of $\alpha^c$:
- First, since $r \ge r$, we have $r \in \alpha^c$.
- Second, by definition of $\alpha^c$, every element $q \in \alpha^c$ satisfies $r \le q$.
Thus $\alpha^c$ possesses a smallest element in $\mathbb{Q}$, namely $r$.

**2. Reverse Direction ($\Longleftarrow$):**
Conversely, suppose that $\alpha \in \mathbb{R}$ is a Dedekind cut whose complement $\alpha^c = \mathbb{Q} \setminus \alpha$ has a smallest element $r \in \alpha^c$ (so $r \in \alpha^c$, and $r \le q$ for every $q \in \alpha^c$). We must prove that $\alpha = r^* = \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p < r\}$ by verifying mutual set inclusion ($\alpha \subseteq r^*$ and $r^* \subseteq \alpha$):
- **($\alpha \subseteq r^*$):** Let $p \in \alpha$. Since $r \in \alpha^c = \mathbb{Q} \setminus \alpha$, we have $r \notin \alpha$. As established for any Dedekind cut, every element inside $\alpha$ is strictly less than every rational outside $\alpha$ (for if $r \le p$, Downward Closure of $\alpha$ would force $r \in \alpha$, contradicting $r \notin \alpha$). Thus $p < r$, which means $p \in r^*$. Therefore $\alpha \subseteq r^*$.
- **($r^* \subseteq \alpha$):** Let $p \in r^*$, which means $p \in \mathbb{Q}$ and $p < r$. Could $p$ fail to belong to $\alpha$? Suppose for the sake of contradiction that $p \notin \alpha$, so $p \in \alpha^c$. Because $r$ is the *smallest* element of $\alpha^c$, every element of $\alpha^c$ must be $\ge r$, which forces $r \le p$—contradicting $p < r$! Therefore $p \in \alpha$ must hold, proving $r^* \subseteq \alpha$.

Combining $\alpha \subseteq r^*$ and $r^* \subseteq \alpha$ gives $\alpha = r^*$, so $\alpha$ is a rational cut. (Consequently, a cut $\alpha \in \mathbb{R}$ is an *irrational cut* if and only if $\alpha$ has no largest element and $\mathbb{Q} \setminus \alpha$ has no smallest element!) $\blacksquare$

### Problem 0.5.2: The Straddling Lemma for Dedekind Cuts and Multiplicative Inverses

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> 1. **Additive Straddling Lemma:** Let $\alpha \in \mathbb{R}$ be a Dedekind cut. Prove that for every positive rational number $\varepsilon \in \mathbb{Q}^+$, there exist rationals $r \in \alpha$ and $s \in \mathbb{Q} \setminus \alpha$ such that $s - r < \varepsilon$, and moreover $s$ can be chosen strictly greater than the boundary of $\alpha$ (meaning there exists $s' \notin \alpha$ with $s' < s$).
> 2. **Multiplicative Straddling & Inverse Verification:** Using Part (1), prove that for any positive cut $\alpha > 0^*$ and any rational $M > 1$, there exist positive rationals $r \in \alpha$ ($r > 0$) and $s \notin \alpha$ (with some $s' \notin \alpha$ satisfying $s' < s$) such that $\frac{s}{r} < M$. Use this to prove directly from the definition of $\alpha^{-1}$ and cut multiplication that $\alpha \cdot \alpha^{-1} = 1^*$.

**Solution:**
**1. Proof of the Additive Straddling Lemma:**
Let $\alpha \in \mathbb{R}$ be a Dedekind cut and let $\varepsilon \in \mathbb{Q}^+$ ($\varepsilon > 0$). Define the rational step size $d := \frac{\varepsilon}{3} > 0$. By Non-Triviality of $\alpha$ (Property 1), we can pick some rational $a_0 \in \alpha$ and some rational $b_0 \in \mathbb{Q} \setminus \alpha$ (so $a_0 < b_0$).

Consider the sequence of rational numbers $a_k := a_0 + kd$ for $k \in \mathbb{N}$. Since $\frac{b_0 - a_0}{d} \in \mathbb{Q}^+$, we can write $\frac{b_0 - a_0}{d} = \frac{u}{w}$ with $u, w \in \mathbb{N}^+$; then for the positive integer $N = u + 1 \in \mathbb{N}^+$, we have $N > u \ge \frac{u}{w} = \frac{b_0 - a_0}{d}$, so $a_N = a_0 + Nd > b_0$. Because $b_0 \notin \alpha$ and $a_N > b_0$, Downward Closure of $\alpha$ implies $a_N \notin \alpha$.

Thus the set $S := \{k \in \mathbb{N}^+ : a_0 + kd \notin \alpha\}$ is a non-empty subset of $\mathbb{N}^+$ (containing $N$). By the **Well-Ordering Principle** of $\mathbb{N}$, $S$ has a least element $n + 1 \in \mathbb{N}^+$ (where $n \in \mathbb{N}$). Because $n + 1$ is the *first* positive integer index where $a_0 + kd$ exits $\alpha$, we have:
- $r := a_0 + nd \in \alpha$ (for $n = 0$ this is $a_0 \in \alpha$; for $n \ge 1$ it holds by minimality of $n + 1$).
- $s' := a_0 + (n + 1)d \notin \alpha$.
- $s := a_0 + (n + 2)d \notin \alpha$ (since $s > s' \notin \alpha$).

Let's check all required properties of $r$ and $s$: we have $r \in \alpha$, $s \notin \alpha$, there exists $s' \notin \alpha$ with $s' < s$ (namely $s' = a_0 + (n + 1)d$), and their difference is
$$
s - r = \big(a_0 + (n + 2)d\big) - (a_0 + nd) = 2d = \frac{2}{3}\varepsilon < \varepsilon
$$
which completes the proof of Part (1).

**2. Multiplicative Straddling & Verification of $\alpha \cdot \alpha^{-1} = 1^*$:**
First, let $\alpha > 0^*$ be a positive cut and let $M \in \mathbb{Q}$ with $M > 1$. Since $\alpha > 0^*$, there exists some positive rational $r_0 \in \alpha$ with $r_0 > 0$. Set $\varepsilon := r_0 (M - 1) \in \mathbb{Q}^+$. Applying Part (1) to $\alpha$ and $\varepsilon > 0$ gives $r_1 \in \alpha$ and $s \notin \alpha$ (with $s' \notin \alpha$ satisfying $s' < s$) such that $s - r_1 < \varepsilon$. Define $r := \max(r_0, r_1) \in \alpha$. Then $r \ge r_0 > 0$, $r \ge r_1 \implies s - r \le s - r_1 < \varepsilon = r_0 (M - 1) \le r(M - 1)$, and dividing $s - r < r(M - 1)$ by $r > 0$ yields
$$
\frac{s}{r} - 1 < M - 1 \implies \frac{s}{r} < M
$$

Now let us prove $\alpha \cdot \alpha^{-1} = 1^*$ for $\alpha > 0^*$ by mutual set inclusion ($\alpha \cdot \alpha^{-1} \subseteq 1^*$ and $1^* \subseteq \alpha \cdot \alpha^{-1}$):
- **($\alpha \cdot \alpha^{-1} \subseteq 1^*$):** Let $x \in \alpha \cdot \alpha^{-1}$. By definition of the product of positive cuts, either $x \le 0$ (in which case $x < 1 \implies x \in 1^*$) or $x = q \cdot p$ for some $q \in \alpha$ ($q > 0$) and $p \in \alpha^{-1}$ ($p > 0$). By the definition of $\alpha^{-1}$, since $p \in \alpha^{-1}$ and $p > 0$, there exists $t \in \mathbb{Q}$ with $t > 0$ such that $\frac{1}{p} - t \notin \alpha$. Because $\frac{1}{p} > \frac{1}{p} - t \notin \alpha$, we have $\frac{1}{p} \notin \alpha$. Since $q \in \alpha$ and $\frac{1}{p} \notin \alpha$, every element inside $\alpha$ is strictly less than every element outside $\alpha$, so $q < \frac{1}{p}$. Multiplying both sides by $p > 0$ gives $x = q \cdot p < 1$, so $x \in 1^*$. Thus $\alpha \cdot \alpha^{-1} \subseteq 1^*$.
- **($1^* \subseteq \alpha \cdot \alpha^{-1}$):** Let $v \in 1^*$ (so $v < 1$).
  - If $v \le 0$, then $v \in \alpha \cdot \alpha^{-1}$ automatically by definition of the product of positive cuts.
  - If $0 < v < 1$, then its reciprocal satisfies $M := \frac{1}{v} \in \mathbb{Q}$ with $M > 1$. By our Multiplicative Straddling Lemma above, there exist $r \in \alpha$ ($r > 0$), $s \notin \alpha$, and $s' \notin \alpha$ with $s' < s$ such that $\frac{s}{r} < M = \frac{1}{v}$, which rearranges to $v < \frac{r}{s}$.
  Let's first check that $\frac{1}{s} \in \alpha^{-1}$: since $s > s' > r > 0$, we have $\frac{1}{s} > 0$, and setting the positive margin $t := s - s' > 0$ gives
  $$
  \frac{1}{1/s} - t = s - (s - s') = s' \notin \alpha
  $$
  By the definition of $\alpha^{-1}$, this proves $\frac{1}{s} \in \alpha^{-1}$! Next, since $r \in \alpha$ ($r > 0$) and $\frac{1}{s} \in \alpha^{-1}$ ($\frac{1}{s} > 0$), their product $r \cdot \left(\frac{1}{s}\right) = \frac{r}{s}$ belongs to $\alpha \cdot \alpha^{-1}$. Finally, because $v < \frac{r}{s}$ and $\alpha \cdot \alpha^{-1}$ is downward closed (or directly by writing $v = r \cdot \left(\frac{v}{r}\right)$ where $\frac{v}{r} < \frac{1}{s} \in \alpha^{-1} \implies \frac{v}{r} \in \alpha^{-1}$), we conclude that $v \in \alpha \cdot \alpha^{-1}$.

Thus $1^* \subseteq \alpha \cdot \alpha^{-1}$, completing the proof that $\alpha \cdot \alpha^{-1} = 1^*$. $\blacksquare$

### Problem 0.5.3: Dedekind's Closure Theorem (Taking Cuts of $\mathbb{R}$ Produces No New Numbers)

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Suppose we repeat Dedekind's construction on $\mathbb{R}$ itself: define a **cut of $\mathbb{R}$** to be a subset $L \subset \mathbb{R}$ such that (i) $L \neq \emptyset$ and $L \neq \mathbb{R}$, (ii) if $x \in L$ and $y \in \mathbb{R}$ with $y < x$, then $y \in L$, and (iii) if $x \in L$, there exists $z \in L$ with $x < z$. Prove that because $\mathbb{R}$ satisfies the Least Upper Bound Property, **every** cut $L$ of $\mathbb{R}$ is already of the form
> $$
> L = \{x \in \mathbb{R} : x < c\}
> $$
> for a unique real number $c \in \mathbb{R}$ (so applying the Dedekind cut construction a second time to $\mathbb{R}$ produces an ordered set canonically isomorphic to $\mathbb{R}$ itself, with no "hyper-irrational" gaps).

**Solution:**
Let $L \subset \mathbb{R}$ be any cut of $\mathbb{R}$ satisfying properties (i), (ii), and (iii).

**1. Existence of $c = \sup L \in \mathbb{R}$:**
By Property (i), $L$ is a non-empty subset of $\mathbb{R}$ ($L \neq \emptyset$), and $L \neq \mathbb{R}$, so there exists some real number $u \in \mathbb{R} \setminus L$. We claim that $u$ is an upper bound for $L$. Indeed, for any $x \in L$, we cannot have $u \le x$ (for if $u = x$, then $u \in L$; and if $u < x$, then Downward Closure (ii) of $L$ would force $u \in L$, contradicting $u \notin L$). By Trichotomy in $\mathbb{R}$, every $x \in L$ satisfies $x < u$.

Because $L \subset \mathbb{R}$ is non-empty and bounded above by $u$, the **Least Upper Bound Property** of $\mathbb{R}$ guarantees that $L$ has a supremum in $\mathbb{R}$:
$$
c := \sup L \in \mathbb{R}
$$

**2. Proof that $L = \{x \in \mathbb{R} : x < c\}$:**
We prove mutual set inclusion ($L \subseteq \{x \in \mathbb{R} : x < c\}$ and $\{x \in \mathbb{R} : x < c\} \subseteq L$):
- **($L \subseteq \{x \in \mathbb{R} : x < c\}$):** Let $x \in L$. By Property (iii) (No Greatest Element), there exists some $z \in L$ such that $x < z$. Since $z \in L$ and $c = \sup L$ is an upper bound for $L$, we have $z \le c$. Combining $x < z$ and $z \le c$ by transitivity yields $x < c$. Thus $L \subseteq \{x \in \mathbb{R} : x < c\}$.
- **($\{x \in \mathbb{R} : x < c\} \subseteq L$):** Conversely, let $x \in \mathbb{R}$ satisfy $x < c$. Because $c = \sup L$ is the *least* upper bound of $L$ and $x < c$, the number $x$ **cannot be an upper bound for $L$**! Therefore, there must exist some element $y \in L$ such that $x < y$. Now, since $y \in L$ and $x < y$, Downward Closure (Property ii) of $L$ forces $x \in L$! Thus $\{x \in \mathbb{R} : x < c\} \subseteq L$.

Combining both inclusions proves $L = \{x \in \mathbb{R} : x < c\}$.

**3. Uniqueness of $c \in \mathbb{R}$:**
Suppose $c_1, c_2 \in \mathbb{R}$ both satisfy $L = \{x \in \mathbb{R} : x < c_1\} = \{x \in \mathbb{R} : x < c_2\}$. If $c_1 \neq c_2$, say $c_1 < c_2$ without loss of generality, then $c_1$ belongs to $\{x \in \mathbb{R} : x < c_2\} = L$, which implies $c_1 \in \{x \in \mathbb{R} : x < c_1\}$, i.e., $c_1 < c_1$—a contradiction! Thus $c_1 = c_2$, so $c = \sup L$ is unique. $\blacksquare$

### Problem 0.5.4: The Floor Function $\lfloor x \rfloor$ and Density of the Irrational Numbers $\mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$ in $\mathbb{R}$

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> 1. **Existence and Uniqueness of the Floor Function $\lfloor x \rfloor$:** Using the Archimedean Property of $\mathbb{R}$ and the Well-Ordering Principle of $\mathbb{N}$, prove that for every real number $x \in \mathbb{R}$, there exists a **unique** integer $m \in \mathbb{Z}$ such that
>    $$
>    m \le x < m + 1
>    $$
>    This unique integer $m$ is called the **floor** (or **greatest integer part**) of $x$ and is denoted by $\lfloor x \rfloor$.
> 2. **Density of the Irrationals $\mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$ in $\mathbb{R}$:** Using the Density of $\mathbb{Q}$ in $\mathbb{R}$ and the fact that $\sqrt{2} \in \mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$ is irrational, prove that between any two real numbers $x, y \in \mathbb{R}$ with $x < y$, there exists an **irrational number** $z \in \mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$ such that
>    $$
>    x < z < y
>    $$

**Solution:**
**1. Existence and Uniqueness of $\lfloor x \rfloor$:**
Let $x \in \mathbb{R}$.
- *Uniqueness:* Suppose $m_1, m_2 \in \mathbb{Z}$ both satisfy $m_1 \le x < m_1 + 1$ and $m_2 \le x < m_2 + 1$. If $m_1 \neq m_2$, assume without loss of generality that $m_1 < m_2$. Since $m_1, m_2$ are integers with $m_2 - m_1 \in \mathbb{N}^+$, we have $m_2 - m_1 \ge 1$, or $m_1 + 1 \le m_2$. Chaining the inequalities gives
  $$
  x < m_1 + 1 \le m_2 \le x \implies x < x
  $$
  which contradicts Trichotomy! Thus at most one integer $m \in \mathbb{Z}$ can satisfy $m \le x < m + 1$.
- *Existence:* By the **Archimedean Property** of $\mathbb{R}$, there exist positive integers $N_1, N_2 \in \mathbb{N}^+$ such that $N_1 > x$ and $N_2 > -x$ (so $-N_2 < x < N_1$). Define the subset of positive integers:
  $$
  S := \{k \in \mathbb{N}^+ : -N_2 + k > x\}
  $$
  Notice that for $k = N_1 + N_2 \in \mathbb{N}^+$, we have $-N_2 + (N_1 + N_2) = N_1 > x$, so $S$ is a non-empty subset of $\mathbb{N}^+$. By the **Well-Ordering Principle** of $\mathbb{N}$, $S$ possesses a least element $k_0 \in S$. Now define the integer
  $$
  m := -N_2 + k_0 - 1 \in \mathbb{Z}
  $$
  Let's check both inequalities in $m \le x < m + 1$:
  - Since $k_0 \in S$, we have $m + 1 = -N_2 + k_0 > x$, so $x < m + 1$.
  - For $m = -N_2 + (k_0 - 1)$: if $k_0 = 1$, then $m = -N_2 < x$; if $k_0 > 1$, then $k_0 - 1 \in \mathbb{N}^+$ is strictly smaller than the least element $k_0$ of $S$, so $k_0 - 1 \notin S$, which means $m = -N_2 + (k_0 - 1) \le x$. In either case, $m \le x$.
  Thus $m \in \mathbb{Z}$ satisfies $m \le x < m + 1$.

**2. Density of the Irrational Numbers $\mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$ in $\mathbb{R}$:**
Let $x, y \in \mathbb{R}$ with $x < y$. By the Existence of Positive $n$-th Roots in $\mathbb{R}$ (with $y = 2$ and $n = 2$), there exists a unique positive real number $\sqrt{2} > 0$ in $\mathbb{R}$ satisfying $(\sqrt{2})^2 = 2$; and by our theorem from Section 0.4, $\sqrt{2} \notin \mathbb{Q}$.

Since $\sqrt{2} > 0$, its reciprocal $\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}$ is also positive, so multiplying the strict inequality $x < y$ by $\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}} > 0$ preserves the order:
$$
\frac{x}{\sqrt{2}} < \frac{y}{\sqrt{2}}
$$
Now apply the **Density of $\mathbb{Q}$ in $\mathbb{R}$** to the open interval $\left(\frac{x}{\sqrt{2}}, \frac{y}{\sqrt{2}}\right)$: there exists a rational number $r_1 \in \mathbb{Q}$ such that $\frac{x}{\sqrt{2}} < r_1 < \frac{y}{\sqrt{2}}$, and applying Density of $\mathbb{Q}$ a second time to $\left(r_1, \frac{y}{\sqrt{2}}\right)$ gives a second distinct rational $r_2 \in \mathbb{Q}$ with
$$
\frac{x}{\sqrt{2}} < r_1 < r_2 < \frac{y}{\sqrt{2}}
$$
Because $r_1 \neq r_2$, at least one of $r_1, r_2$ must be **non-zero** ($r \neq 0$). Let $r \in \{r_1, r_2\}$ be a non-zero rational ($r \in \mathbb{Q} \setminus \{0\}$) satisfying
$$
\frac{x}{\sqrt{2}} < r < \frac{y}{\sqrt{2}}
$$
Multiplying through by $\sqrt{2} > 0$ gives the real number $z := r \sqrt{2} \in \mathbb{R}$, which satisfies:
$$
x < r \sqrt{2} < y
$$
Finally, we verify that $z = r \sqrt{2}$ is **irrational** ($z \in \mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$): suppose for the sake of contradiction that $z \in \mathbb{Q}$. Since $r \in \mathbb{Q}$ and $r \neq 0$, and $\mathbb{Q}$ is a field closed under division by non-zero rationals, the quotient
$$
\sqrt{2} = \frac{z}{r}
$$
would have to belong to $\mathbb{Q}$—contradicting the irrationality of $\sqrt{2}$! Therefore $z = r \sqrt{2} \in \mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$ is an irrational number strictly between $x$ and $y$. $\blacksquare$

### Problem 0.5.5: Categorical Uniqueness of Complete Ordered Fields (Isomorphism of All Models of $\mathbb{R}$)

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $(\mathbb{F}_1, +_1, \cdot_1, <_1)$ and $(\mathbb{F}_2, +_2, \cdot_2, <_2)$ be any two **complete ordered fields** (ordered fields satisfying the Least Upper Bound Property). Because every ordered field contains a canonical copy of the rational numbers $\mathbb{Q}$ generated by its additive and multiplicative identities $0_{\mathbb{F}_i}$ and $1_{\mathbb{F}_i}$ ($i = 1, 2$), identify $\mathbb{Q} \subset \mathbb{F}_1$ and $\mathbb{Q} \subset \mathbb{F}_2$. For each $x \in \mathbb{F}_1$, define its lower rational cut $L_x := \{r \in \mathbb{Q} : r <_1 x\} \subset \mathbb{Q}$, and define a map $\Phi: \mathbb{F}_1 \to \mathbb{F}_2$ by taking the supremum in $\mathbb{F}_2$:
> $$
> \Phi(x) := \sup_{\mathbb{F}_2} L_x
> $$
> Prove the following:
> 1. For every $x \in \mathbb{F}_1$, the set $L_x$ is non-empty and bounded above in $\mathbb{F}_2$ (so $\Phi(x) \in \mathbb{F}_2$ is well-defined), $\Phi(q) = q$ for all $q \in \mathbb{Q}$, and for all $r \in \mathbb{Q}$ and $x \in \mathbb{F}_1$, $r <_1 x \iff r <_2 \Phi(x)$.
> 2. $\Phi: \mathbb{F}_1 \to \mathbb{F}_2$ is a **strictly order-preserving bijection** ($x <_1 y \iff \Phi(x) <_2 \Phi(y)$, and $\Phi$ is onto $\mathbb{F}_2$) that preserves addition ($\Phi(x +_1 y) = \Phi(x) +_2 \Phi(y)$) and multiplication ($\Phi(x \cdot_1 y) = \Phi(x) \cdot_2 \Phi(y)$).
> 3. **Uniqueness of the Isomorphism:** Prove that $\Phi$ is the **unique** order-preserving field isomorphism from $\mathbb{F}_1$ to $\mathbb{F}_2$ (in fact, any field homomorphism $\Psi: \mathbb{F}_1 \to \mathbb{F}_2$ between complete ordered fields automatically preserves order and satisfies $\Psi = \Phi$).

**Solution:**
Note that our proofs of the Archimedean Property and the Density of $\mathbb{Q}$ in Section 0.5 used *only* the Complete Ordered Field Axioms, so both $\mathbb{F}_1$ and $\mathbb{F}_2$ satisfy the Archimedean Property and the Density of $\mathbb{Q}$! To keep notation clean, we write $+, \cdot, <$ in both fields when clear from context, while subscripting $\sup_{\mathbb{F}_1}$ and $\sup_{\mathbb{F}_2}$.

**1. Well-Definedness of $\Phi(x)$ and Equivalence $r <_1 x \iff r <_2 \Phi(x)$:**
Let $x \in \mathbb{F}_1$. By the Archimedean Property in $\mathbb{F}_1$, there exist integers $m, n \in \mathbb{Z}$ (which belong to $\mathbb{Q}$) such that $m <_1 x <_1 n$.
- Since $m \in \mathbb{Q}$ and $m <_1 x$, we have $m \in L_x$, so $L_x \neq \emptyset$.
- For every $r \in L_x$, we have $r <_1 x <_1 n$ in $\mathbb{F}_1$. Because the order on $\mathbb{Q}$ is identical in $\mathbb{F}_1$ and $\mathbb{F}_2$, we have $r <_2 n$ in $\mathbb{F}_2$ for all $r \in L_x$. Thus $L_x$ is bounded above by $n$ in $\mathbb{F}_2$.
By the **Least Upper Bound Property** of $\mathbb{F}_2$, the supremum $\Phi(x) := \sup_{\mathbb{F}_2} L_x \in \mathbb{F}_2$ exists and is unique.

Now let $r \in \mathbb{Q}$ and $x \in \mathbb{F}_1$:
- If $r <_1 x$, then by Density of $\mathbb{Q}$ in $\mathbb{F}_1$ there exists $s \in \mathbb{Q}$ with $r <_1 s <_1 x$. Since $s \in L_x$ and $\Phi(x) = \sup_{\mathbb{F}_2} L_x$ is an upper bound of $L_x$ in $\mathbb{F}_2$, we have $r <_2 s \le_2 \Phi(x)$, so $r <_2 \Phi(x)$.
- Conversely, if $r <_2 \Phi(x) = \sup_{\mathbb{F}_2} L_x$, then $r$ is not an upper bound of $L_x$ in $\mathbb{F}_2$, so there exists some $s \in L_x$ with $r <_2 s$. Since $s \in L_x$ means $s <_1 x$, we get $r <_1 s <_1 x$, so $r <_1 x$.
Thus $r <_1 x \iff r <_2 \Phi(x)$ for all $r \in \mathbb{Q}$ and $x \in \mathbb{F}_1$. In particular, if $x = q \in \mathbb{Q}$, then $L_q = \{r \in \mathbb{Q} : r < q\}$ has $\sup_{\mathbb{F}_2} L_q = q$ (by Density of $\mathbb{Q}$ in $\mathbb{F}_2$), so $\Phi(q) = q$ for all $q \in \mathbb{Q}$.

**2. Order-Preserving Bijection and Homomorphism Properties:**
- *Strict Order Preservation and Injectivity:* Suppose $x <_1 y$ in $\mathbb{F}_1$. By Density of $\mathbb{Q}$ in $\mathbb{F}_1$, choose $r_1, r_2 \in \mathbb{Q}$ such that $x <_1 r_1 <_1 r_2 <_1 y$.
  - Since $x <_1 r_1$, every $s \in L_x$ satisfies $s <_1 x <_1 r_1$, so $r_1$ is an upper bound for $L_x$ in $\mathbb{F}_2$, giving $\Phi(x) = \sup_{\mathbb{F}_2} L_x \le_2 r_1$.
  - Since $r_2 <_1 y$, we have $r_2 \in L_y$, so $r_2 \le_2 \sup_{\mathbb{F}_2} L_y = \Phi(y)$.
  Combining these with $r_1 <_2 r_2$ yields $\Phi(x) \le_2 r_1 <_2 r_2 \le_2 \Phi(y)$, so $\Phi(x) <_2 \Phi(y)$. By Trichotomy, this proves $x <_1 y \iff \Phi(x) <_2 \Phi(y)$ and shows that $\Phi$ is injective.
- *Surjectivity:* Given any $w \in \mathbb{F}_2$, reverse the roles of $\mathbb{F}_1$ and $\mathbb{F}_2$: define $M_w := \{r \in \mathbb{Q} : r <_2 w\} \subset \mathbb{Q}$ and let $x := \sup_{\mathbb{F}_1} M_w \in \mathbb{F}_1$. By the exact same reasoning as Part (1), for any $r \in \mathbb{Q}$ we have $r <_1 x \iff r <_2 w$, which means $L_x = M_w$. Therefore $\Phi(x) = \sup_{\mathbb{F}_2} L_x = \sup_{\mathbb{F}_2} M_w = w$ (since every $w \in \mathbb{F}_2$ is the supremum of the rationals below it by Density of $\mathbb{Q}$ in $\mathbb{F}_2$). Thus $\Phi$ is a bijection!
- *Preservation of Addition ($\Phi(x + y) = \Phi(x) + \Phi(y)$):* Let $x, y \in \mathbb{F}_1$. For any $r \in L_x$ ($r <_1 x$) and $s \in L_y$ ($s <_1 y$), we have $r + s <_1 x + y$, so $r + s \in L_{x + y}$, which implies $r + s \le_2 \Phi(x + y)$ in $\mathbb{F}_2$. Fixing $s \in L_y$ and taking the supremum over $r \in L_x$ gives $\Phi(x) + s \le_2 \Phi(x + y)$, and then taking the supremum over $s \in L_y$ gives $\Phi(x) + \Phi(y) \le_2 \Phi(x + y)$. Could $\Phi(x) + \Phi(y) <_2 \Phi(x + y)$ hold? If so, by Density of $\mathbb{Q}$ in $\mathbb{F}_2$, there exists a rational $q \in \mathbb{Q}$ such that $\Phi(x) + \Phi(y) <_2 q <_2 \Phi(x + y)$. By Part (1), $q <_2 \Phi(x + y) \implies q <_1 x + y \implies q - x <_1 y$. Choose a rational $s \in \mathbb{Q}$ with $q - x <_1 s <_1 y$; then $r := q - s \in \mathbb{Q}$ satisfies $r <_1 x$ and $r + s = q$. By Part (1), $r <_1 x \implies r <_2 \Phi(x)$ and $s <_1 y \implies s <_2 \Phi(y)$, so $q = r + s <_2 \Phi(x) + \Phi(y)$, contradicting $\Phi(x) + \Phi(y) <_2 q$! Thus $\Phi(x + y) = \Phi(x) + \Phi(y)$, and in particular $\Phi(-x) = -\Phi(x)$.
- *Preservation of Multiplication ($\Phi(x \cdot y) = \Phi(x) \cdot \Phi(y)$):* Suppose first that $x >_1 0$ and $y >_1 0$ (so $\Phi(x) >_2 0$ and $\Phi(y) >_2 0$). For any positive rationals $r, s \in \mathbb{Q}^+$ with $0 <_1 r <_1 x$ and $0 <_1 s <_1 y$, we have $0 <_1 rs <_1 xy$, so $rs \le_2 \Phi(xy)$ in $\mathbb{F}_2$; taking supremums over positive rationals $r <_1 x$ and $s <_1 y$ gives $\Phi(x)\Phi(y) \le_2 \Phi(xy)$. Conversely, if $\Phi(x)\Phi(y) <_2 \Phi(xy)$, pick a rational $q \in \mathbb{Q}^+$ with $\Phi(x)\Phi(y) <_2 q <_2 \Phi(xy)$. Then $q <_1 xy \implies \frac{q}{x} <_1 y$; picking $s \in \mathbb{Q}^+$ with $\frac{q}{x} <_1 s <_1 y$ and setting $r := \frac{q}{s} \in \mathbb{Q}^+$ gives $0 <_1 r <_1 x$ and $0 <_1 s <_1 y$ with $rs = q$, forcing $q = rs <_2 \Phi(x)\Phi(y)$—a contradiction! Thus $\Phi(xy) = \Phi(x)\Phi(y)$ for $x, y >_1 0$, and the remaining sign cases follow immediately from $\Phi(0) = 0$ and $\Phi(-x) = -\Phi(x)$.

**3. Uniqueness of the Isomorphism:**
Let $\Psi: \mathbb{F}_1 \to \mathbb{F}_2$ be *any* field homomorphism (not even assumed *a priori* to preserve order!).
- First, $\Psi(0) = 0$ and $\Psi(1) = 1$, so by induction $\Psi(n) = n$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$, hence $\Psi(m) = m$ for all $m \in \mathbb{Z}$, and $\Psi(m/n) = \Psi(m)/\Psi(n) = m/n$, meaning **$\Psi(q) = q$ for every rational $q \in \mathbb{Q}$**!
- Second, why must $\Psi$ automatically preserve order? Suppose $x <_1 y$ in $\mathbb{F}_1$, so $y - x >_1 0$. By the Existence of Positive Square Roots in the complete ordered field $\mathbb{F}_1$ (proved in Section 0.5), there exists $u \in \mathbb{F}_1$ with $u >_1 0$ such that $y - x = u^2$. Applying the field homomorphism $\Psi$ gives
  $$
  \Psi(y) - \Psi(x) = \Psi(y - x) = \Psi(u^2) = \big(\Psi(u)\big)^2
  $$
  Since $u \neq 0$ and any non-zero homomorphism of fields is injective ($\ker \Psi = \{0\}$), we have $\Psi(u) \neq 0$ in $\mathbb{F}_2$, so its square in the ordered field $\mathbb{F}_2$ is strictly positive: $\big(\Psi(u)\big)^2 >_2 0$! Thus $\Psi(x) <_2 \Psi(y)$, proving that $\Psi$ is strictly order-preserving!
- Finally, for any $x \in \mathbb{F}_1$ and any $r, s \in \mathbb{Q}$ with $r <_1 x <_1 s$, applying $\Psi$ and using $\Psi(q) = q$ gives $r = \Psi(r) <_2 \Psi(x) <_2 \Psi(s) = s$. Since $r <_1 x$ was arbitrary, $\Psi(x)$ is an upper bound for $L_x$ in $\mathbb{F}_2$, so $\Phi(x) = \sup_{\mathbb{F}_2} L_x \le_2 \Psi(x)$. And if $\Phi(x) <_2 \Psi(x)$ held, Density of $\mathbb{Q}$ in $\mathbb{F}_2$ would give a rational $s \in \mathbb{Q}$ with $\Phi(x) <_2 s <_2 \Psi(x)$, which by Part (1) implies $x <_1 s$ and hence $\Psi(x) <_2 \Psi(s) = s$—contradicting $s <_2 \Psi(x)$! Therefore $\Psi(x) = \Phi(x)$ for all $x \in \mathbb{F}_1$. $\blacksquare$

---

# 0.6 Absolute Value, Distance, and the Extended Reals

With the real number system $\mathbb{R}$ constructed as a complete ordered field in Section 0.5, we have all the algebraic and order-theoretic machinery of the continuum in place. Now we need to assemble the daily analytical toolkit that you will use on virtually every page of a Real Analysis textbook: **absolute value**, **metric distance**, **the Triangle and Reverse Triangle Inequalities**, **$\varepsilon$-neighborhoods**, **the $\varepsilon$-Principle**, **the $\varepsilon$-characterization of supremums and infimums**, and the **extended real number system** $\overline{\mathbb{R}} = \mathbb{R} \cup \{-\infty, +\infty\}$.

## Absolute Value and the Triangle Inequality

The order relation $<$ tells us whether a number $x$ lies to the left or to the right of $y$ on the number line. However, analysis is fundamentally the study of **closeness**, **error bounds**, and **approximation**—we want to measure how far apart $x$ and $y$ are, regardless of which one is larger. The tool that converts order into unsigned magnitude and distance is the **absolute value**.

> [!abstract] Definition: Absolute Value and Distance on $\mathbb{R}$
> For any real number $x \in \mathbb{R}$, its **absolute value** (or **modulus**), denoted $|x|$, is defined piecewise by:
> $$
> |x| := \begin{cases}
> x & \text{if } x > 0 \\
> 0 & \text{if } x = 0 \\
> -x & \text{if } x < 0
> \end{cases}
> $$
> Equivalently, since $x \ge -x$ when $x \ge 0$ and $-x > x$ when $x < 0$, the absolute value is the maximum of $x$ and $-x$:
> $$
> |x| = \max\{x, -x\}
> $$
> Given any two real numbers $x, y \in \mathbb{R}$, the **Euclidean distance** between $x$ and $y$ is defined as:
> $$
> d(x, y) := |x - y|
> $$

Notice that when $x < 0$, its additive inverse $-x$ is strictly positive by the ordered field laws ($x < 0 \implies -x > 0$). Thus $|x|$ never "adds a minus sign to make a number negative"—it strips away the sign so that the output is always non-negative. Moreover, because $x^2 = (-x)^2 = |x|^2 \ge 0$ for every $x \in \mathbb{R}$, and because we proved in Section 0.5 that every non-negative real number has a unique non-negative square root, we can also write $|x| = \sqrt{x^2}$.

Why does almost every $\varepsilon$-$\delta$ continuity argument or sequence convergence proof in analysis revolve around an inequality of the form $|x - y| < \varepsilon$? Because of a fundamental algebraic equivalence that translates a single absolute-value inequality into a two-sided "sandwich" inequality—and because the absolute value obeys the **Triangle Inequality**, the single most important inequality in all of analysis.

> [!theorem] Fundamental Properties of Absolute Value and the Triangle Inequality
> For all $x, y, z, c \in \mathbb{R}$:
> 1. **Non-Negativity and Definiteness:** $|x| \ge 0$, with $|x| = 0$ if and only if $x = 0$.
> 2. **Symmetry and Multiplicativity:** $|-x| = |x|$ and $|x \cdot y| = |x| \cdot |y|$. Furthermore, if $y \neq 0$, then $\left|\frac{x}{y}\right| = \frac{|x|}{|y|}$.
> 3. **Sandwich Equivalence:** For any $c \ge 0$,
>    $$
>    |x| \le c \iff -c \le x \le c
>    $$
>    and for any $c > 0$,
>    $$
>    |x| < c \iff -c < x < c
>    $$
>    In particular, taking $c = |x|$ yields the universal bound $-|x| \le x \le |x|$.
> 4. **The Triangle Inequality:**
>    $$
>    |x + y| \le |x| + |y|
>    $$
>    More generally, replacing $y$ with $-y$ gives $|x - y| \le |x| + |y|$.
> 5. **The Reverse Triangle Inequality:**
>    $$
>    \big| |x| - |y| \big| \le |x - y|
>    $$
> 6. **Metric Properties of Distance:** The distance function $d(x, y) = |x - y|$ satisfies the three axioms of a **metric** on $\mathbb{R}$:
>    - **Positivity and Definiteness:** $d(x, y) \ge 0$, and $d(x, y) = 0 \iff x = y$.
>    - **Symmetry:** $d(x, y) = d(y, x)$.
>    - **Metric Triangle Inequality:**
>      $$
>      d(x, z) \le d(x, y) + d(y, z) \quad \text{i.e.,} \quad |x - z| \le |x - y| + |y - z|
>      $$

**Proof:**
Let's prove each property step by step from the ordered field axioms.

- **Proof of (1) and (2):** By trichotomy, either $x > 0$ (so $|x| = x > 0$), $x = 0$ (so $|x| = 0$), or $x < 0$ (so $|x| = -x > 0$). Thus $|x| \ge 0$, and $|x| = 0 \iff x = 0$. Since $-(-x) = x$, swapping $x$ and $-x$ in the piecewise definition leaves the set $\{x, -x\}$ unchanged, so $|-x| = |x|$. For multiplicativity, one checks the sign cases of $x$ and $y$: for instance, if $x < 0$ and $y > 0$, then $x \cdot y < 0$, so $|x \cdot y| = -(x \cdot y) = (-x) \cdot y = |x| \cdot |y|$; the remaining sign combinations follow identically. If $y \neq 0$, applying multiplicativity to $x = \frac{x}{y} \cdot y$ gives $|x| = \left|\frac{x}{y}\right| \cdot |y|$, and dividing by $|y| > 0$ yields $\left|\frac{x}{y}\right| = \frac{|x|}{|y|}$.

- **Proof of (3) (Sandwich Equivalence):** Since $|x| = \max\{x, -x\}$, we always have $x \le |x|$ and $-x \le |x|$.
  - **($\implies$):** Suppose $|x| \le c$. By transitivity of $\le$, we obtain both $x \le |x| \le c$ and $-x \le |x| \le c$. Multiplying $-x \le c$ by $-1$ reverses the inequality to $x \ge -c$. Combining $x \ge -c$ and $x \le c$ gives $-c \le x \le c$.
