  - **($\Longleftarrow$):** Conversely, suppose $-c \le x \le c$. Then $x \le c$, and multiplying $-c \le x$ by $-1$ gives $-x \le c$. Because $|x|$ is equal to either $x$ or $-x$, and both are at most $c$, we conclude $|x| \le c$.
  The strict inequality version ($|x| < c \iff -c < x < c$) follows by the exact same argument with $<$ in place of $\le$.

- **Proof of (4) (The Triangle Inequality):** Applying the universal sandwich bound from Property (3) to $x$ and $y$ individually gives:
  $$
  -|x| \le x \le |x| \qquad \text{and} \qquad -|y| \le y \le |y|
  $$
  Adding these two compatible inequalities term-by-term preserves the order relation:
  $$
  -(|x| + |y|) \le x + y \le |x| + |y|
  $$
  Now look at the structure of this inequality: it has the form $-c \le x + y \le c$ with $c = |x| + |y| \ge 0$. Applying the $(\Longleftarrow)$ direction of the Sandwich Equivalence (Property 3) immediately converts this into
  $$
  |x + y| \le |x| + |y|
  $$
  Replacing $y$ by $-y$ and using $|-y| = |y|$ yields $|x - y| = |x + (-y)| \le |x| + |-y| = |x| + |y|$.

- **Proof of (5) (The Reverse Triangle Inequality):** Often in analysis we need a *lower* bound on $|x - y|$ rather than an upper bound. To relate $|x|$ and $|y|$ to $|x - y|$, we use the "add and subtract" trick: write $x = (x - y) + y$ and apply the Triangle Inequality:
  $$
  |x| = |(x - y) + y| \le |x - y| + |y|
  $$
  Subtracting $|y|$ from both sides isolates the difference of magnitudes:
  $$
  |x| - |y| \le |x - y|
  $$
  By symmetry, swapping the roles of $x$ and $y$ gives $|y| - |x| \le |y - x| = |x - y|$. Multiplying by $-1$ rewrites this second inequality as
  $$
  -|x - y| \le |x| - |y|
  $$
  Combining both bounds gives $-|x - y| \le |x| - |y| \le |x - y|$, which by Property (3) is equivalent to $\big| |x| - |y| \big| \le |x - y|$.

- **Proof of (6) (Metric Properties of Distance):** For $d(x, y) = |x - y|$, positivity and definiteness follow from Property (1) since $|x - y| = 0 \iff x - y = 0 \iff x = y$. Symmetry follows from Property (2): $d(x, y) = |x - y| = |-(y - x)| = |y - x| = d(y, x)$. Finally, to prove the metric triangle inequality, we use the "insert an intermediate point $y$" trick—writing $x - z = (x - y) + (y - z)$—and apply Property (4):
  $$
  |x - z| = |(x - y) + (y - z)| \le |x - y| + |y - z|
  $$
  This completes the proof. $\blacksquare$

Let's pause to appreciate the geometric and linear-algebraic meaning of these properties. In **Linear Algebra**, you studied the Euclidean norm $\|\mathbf{u}\|$ of a vector $\mathbf{u} \in \mathbb{R}^n$, which measures the length of $\mathbf{u}$ from the origin, and the distance $\|\mathbf{u} - \mathbf{v}\|$ between two vectors, which obeys the vector Triangle Inequality $\|\mathbf{u} + \mathbf{v}\| \le \|\mathbf{u}\| + \|\mathbf{v}\|$. On the one-dimensional real line $\mathbb{R} = \mathbb{R}^1$, the absolute value $|x|$ is precisely the norm of $x$, and $d(x, y) = |x - y|$ is the distance between $x$ and $y$.

Why is Property (6) called the **metric triangle inequality**, even though $x, y, z$ all lie on a single flat line rather than forming a two-dimensional triangle? In higher mathematics, *any* set $X$ equipped with a distance function $d : X \times X \to [0, \infty)$ satisfying positivity/definiteness, symmetry, and $d(x, z) \le d(x, y) + d(y, z)$ is called a **metric space**. Intuitively, the inequality $|x - z| \le |x - y| + |y - z|$ says that the direct distance from $x$ to $z$ is never greater than the detour distance obtained by stopping at an intermediate point $y$ along the way. In practically every proof about limits or continuity, whenever you know that $x$ is close to $y$ and $y$ is close to $z$, you bridge the gap from $x$ to $z$ by adding and subtracting $y$: $x - z = (x - y) + (y - z)$.

Similarly, the **Reverse Triangle Inequality** $\big| |x| - |y| \big| \le |x - y|$ tells us that *the difference between the distances of $x$ and $y$ from the origin cannot exceed the distance between $x$ and $y$ themselves*. Whenever you need to bound a denominator away from zero—say, proving that if $x$ is close to $a \neq 0$, then $|x|$ cannot get dangerously close to $0$—you invoke the Reverse Triangle Inequality in the form $|x| \ge |a| - |x - a|$.

### $\varepsilon$-Neighborhoods and Punctured Neighborhoods

In Calculus, you often spoke informally of "$x$ being sufficiently close to $a$." Using distance and the Sandwich Equivalence, we can package this geometric notion of closeness into a clean set-theoretic definition.

> [!abstract] Definition: $\varepsilon$-Neighborhood and Punctured $\varepsilon$-Neighborhood
> Let $a \in \mathbb{R}$ be a point (called the **center**) and let $\varepsilon > 0$ be a strictly positive real number (called the **radius**).
> 1. The **$\varepsilon$-neighborhood** (or **open ball of radius $\varepsilon$**) around $a$ is the set of all real numbers whose distance from $a$ is strictly less than $\varepsilon$:
>    $$
>    V_\varepsilon(a) := \{x \in \mathbb{R} : |x - a| < \varepsilon\} = (a - \varepsilon, a + \varepsilon)
>    $$
> 2. The **punctured (or deleted) $\varepsilon$-neighborhood** around $a$ is obtained by removing the center point $a$ itself:
>    $$
>    V_\varepsilon^*(a) := V_\varepsilon(a) \setminus \{a\} = \{x \in \mathbb{R} : 0 < |x - a| < \varepsilon\} = (a - \varepsilon, a) \cup (a, a + \varepsilon)
>    $$

Let's verify why $\{x \in \mathbb{R} : |x - a| < \varepsilon\}$ equals the open interval $(a - \varepsilon, a + \varepsilon)$. By the Sandwich Equivalence (Property 3),
$$
\begin{aligned}
|x - a| < \varepsilon &\iff -\varepsilon < x - a < \varepsilon \\
&\iff a - \varepsilon < x < a + \varepsilon \\
&\iff x \in (a - \varepsilon, a + \varepsilon)
\end{aligned}
$$
Whenever you read the inequality $|x - a| < \varepsilon$ in an analysis proof, your mind should immediately see a symmetric open window $(a - \varepsilon, a + \varepsilon)$ centered at $a$ of half-width $\varepsilon$ (and total width $2\varepsilon$). And whenever you see $0 < |x - a| < \varepsilon$—as in the definition of a limit $\lim_{x \to a} f(x) = L$ from Calculus—the extra condition $0 < |x - a|$ simply means $|x - a| \neq 0$, i.e., $x \neq a$, so $x$ lies in the punctured neighborhood $V_\varepsilon^*(a)$.

> [!example] Example: Bounding a Rational Expression Near a Point Using the Reverse Triangle Inequality
> Suppose $x \in \mathbb{R}$ lies in the neighborhood $V_{1/2}(2) = \left(\frac{3}{2}, \frac{5}{2}\right)$, so that $|x - 2| < \frac{1}{2}$. Prove that for any $\varepsilon > 0$, if we further restrict $x$ to satisfy $|x - 2| < \min\left\{\frac{1}{2}, \frac{5\varepsilon}{2}\right\}$, then
> $$
> \left|\frac{1}{x^2 + 1} - \frac{1}{5}\right| < \varepsilon
> $$

**Solution:**
Let's first simplify the distance between $\frac{1}{x^2 + 1}$ and $\frac{1}{5}$ by putting the fractions over a common denominator and using the multiplicativity of absolute value:
$$
\begin{aligned}
\left|\frac{1}{x^2 + 1} - \frac{1}{5}\right| &= \left|\frac{5 - (x^2 + 1)}{5(x^2 + 1)}\right| \\
&= \frac{|4 - x^2|}{5(x^2 + 1)} \\
&= \frac{|x + 2| \cdot |x - 2|}{5(x^2 + 1)}
\end{aligned}
$$
We want to make this quantity smaller than $\varepsilon$ when $|x - 2|$ is small. Notice that $|x - 2|$ is already in the numerator, capability-ready to be made small, provided we can bound the remaining factor $\frac{|x + 2|}{5(x^2 + 1)}$ from above by a fixed constant!
Because $x$ is restricted to satisfy $|x - 2| < \frac{1}{2}$, we can bound $|x + 2|$ from above using the **Triangle Inequality** by writing $x + 2 = (x - 2) + 4$:
$$
|x + 2| = |(x - 2) + 4| \le |x - 2| + 4 < \frac{1}{2} + 4 = \frac{9}{2}
$$
Meanwhile, to bound the fraction $\frac{1}{x^2 + 1}$ from above, we need a *lower* bound on the denominator $x^2 + 1$, which means we need a lower bound on $|x|$. Using the **Reverse Triangle Inequality** on $x = 2 + (x - 2)$:
$$
|x| \ge |2| - |x - 2| > 2 - \frac{1}{2} = \frac{3}{2}
$$
Squaring both sides gives $x^2 > \frac{9}{4}$, so $x^2 + 1 > \frac{9}{4} + 1 = \frac{13}{4} > \frac{9}{4}$. Taking reciprocals reverses the inequality:
$$
\frac{1}{x^2 + 1} < \frac{4}{9}
$$
Combining our upper bound on $|x + 2|$ and our upper bound on $\frac{1}{x^2 + 1}$ yields:
$$
\frac{|x + 2| \cdot |x - 2|}{5(x^2 + 1)} < \frac{(9/2) \cdot |x - 2|}{5 \cdot (9/4)} = \frac{2}{5} |x - 2|
$$
Therefore, whenever $|x - 2| < \min\left\{\frac{1}{2}, \frac{5\varepsilon}{2}\right\}$, both $|x - 2| < \frac{1}{2}$ and $|x - 2| < \frac{5\varepsilon}{2}$ hold simultaneously, so
$$
\left|\frac{1}{x^2 + 1} - \frac{1}{5}\right| < \frac{2}{5} |x - 2| < \frac{2}{5} \left(\frac{5\varepsilon}{2}\right) = \varepsilon
$$
as required. $\blacksquare$

## The $\varepsilon$-Principle and Supremum Characterization

In high-school algebra, you prove that two numbers are equal ($a = b$) by manipulating formulas until both sides look identical. In Real Analysis, numbers are often defined indirectly as limits, supremums, or integrals, making direct algebraic manipulation impossible. Instead, analysts prove $a \le b$ (or $a = b$) by giving away an arbitrary positive margin of error $\varepsilon > 0$: if $a$ cannot exceed $b$ by *any* positive tolerance $\varepsilon > 0$—no matter how microscopic—then $a$ cannot exceed $b$ at all!

> [!theorem] The $\varepsilon$-Principle for Inequalities and Equality
> Let $a, b \in \mathbb{R}$ be fixed real numbers.
> 1. **Inequality Form:** If $a \le b + \varepsilon$ (or $a < b + \varepsilon$) for **every** real number $\varepsilon > 0$, then $a \le b$.
>    Similarly, if $a \ge b - \varepsilon$ for **every** $\varepsilon > 0$, then $a \ge b$.
> 2. **Equality Form:** If $|a - b| \le \varepsilon$ (or $|a - b| < \varepsilon$) for **every** real number $\varepsilon > 0$, then $a = b$.
> 3. **Neighborhood Intersection Form:** For any $a \in \mathbb{R}$, the intersection of all $\varepsilon$-neighborhoods centered at $a$ contains only the point $a$ itself:
>    $$
>    \bigcap_{\varepsilon > 0} V_\varepsilon(a) = \{a\}
>    $$

**Proof:**
- **Proof of (1):** Suppose that $a \le b + \varepsilon$ holds for every $\varepsilon > 0$. We want to prove $a \le b$. By trichotomy, suppose for the sake of contradiction that $a > b$. Then the gap between $a$ and $b$ is a strictly positive real number, $a - b > 0$. Consequently, half of that gap,
  $$
  \varepsilon_0 := \frac{a - b}{2} > 0
  $$
  is also a valid, strictly positive real number! Because our hypothesis says that $a \le b + \varepsilon$ holds for *every* $\varepsilon > 0$, we are completely free to specialize it to the particular choice $\varepsilon = \varepsilon_0$:
  $$
  a \le b + \varepsilon_0 = b + \frac{a - b}{2} = \frac{a + b}{2}
  $$
  However, since $b < a$, adding $a$ to both sides gives $a + b < 2a$, so $\frac{a + b}{2} < a$. Chaining these inequalities together yields
  $$
  a \le \frac{a + b}{2} < a \implies a < a
  $$
  which contradicts the irreflexivity of strict order! Thus our assumption $a > b$ is impossible, and we must have $a \le b$. (The second statement $a \ge b - \varepsilon \implies a \ge b$ follows immediately by rearranging $a \ge b - \varepsilon$ into $b \le a + \varepsilon$ and applying the first statement with $a$ and $b$ swapped.)

- **Proof of (2):** Suppose $|a - b| < \varepsilon$ (which implies $|a - b| \le 0 + \varepsilon$) for every $\varepsilon > 0$. Applying Part (1) with the real numbers $|a - b|$ and $0$ yields $|a - b| \le 0$. On the other hand, non-negativity of absolute value gives $|a - b| \ge 0$. By antisymmetry of $\le$, we have $|a - b| = 0$, which by definiteness forces $a = b$.

- **Proof of (3):** Clearly $a \in V_\varepsilon(a)$ for every $\varepsilon > 0$ since $|a - a| = 0 < \varepsilon$, so $\{a\} \subseteq \bigcap_{\varepsilon > 0} V_\varepsilon(a)$. Conversely, if $x \in \bigcap_{\varepsilon > 0} V_\varepsilon(a)$, then by definition of set intersection, $x \in V_\varepsilon(a)$ for every $\varepsilon > 0$, meaning $|x - a| < \varepsilon$ for all $\varepsilon > 0$. By Part (2), $x = a$, so $\bigcap_{\varepsilon > 0} V_\varepsilon(a) \subseteq \{a\}$. $\blacksquare$

> [!warning] Crucial Pitfall: Why Quantifier Order Matters in the $\varepsilon$-Principle!
> Students seeing the $\varepsilon$-Principle for the first time are often puzzled: *"Wait—if $|a - b| < \varepsilon$, doesn't that just mean $a$ and $b$ are close, not necessarily equal?"*
> Everything hinges on **which variables are fixed first** (the order of logical quantifiers):
> - **Fixed $a, b$, Arbitrary $\varepsilon$:** In the $\varepsilon$-Principle, $a$ and $b$ are chosen and **locked in place first**, and then the inequality $|a - b| < \varepsilon$ must hold for **all** $\varepsilon > 0$:
>   $$
>   \forall a, b \in \mathbb{R}, \quad \big(\forall \varepsilon > 0,\ |a - b| < \varepsilon\big) \implies a = b
>   $$
>   Because $a$ and $b$ cannot move while $\varepsilon$ shrinks closer and closer to $0$, the fixed nonnegative number $|a - b|$ is crushed down to $0$.
> - **Arbitrary $\varepsilon$ First, Dependent $x$ Second:** By contrast, the statement
>   $$
>   \forall \varepsilon > 0, \ \exists x \in \mathbb{R} \setminus \{a\} \text{ such that } |x - a| < \varepsilon
>   $$
>   is **completely true** (for example, $x = a + \frac{\varepsilon}{2}$ satisfies $x \neq a$ and $|x - a| = \frac{\varepsilon}{2} < \varepsilon$). Here $x$ is allowed to **change depending on $\varepsilon$**, moving closer to $a$ as $\varepsilon$ shrinks. The $\varepsilon$-Principle only applies when the two numbers being compared are **independent of $\varepsilon$**!

### The $\varepsilon$-Characterization of the Supremum

Recall from Section 0.5 that for a non-empty bounded-above set $E \subseteq \mathbb{R}$, the supremum $\alpha = \sup E$ is characterized by two properties:
1. $\alpha$ is an upper bound of $E$ ($x \le \alpha$ for all $x \in E$).
2. No number smaller than $\alpha$ is an upper bound of $E$ (if $\gamma < \alpha$, then $\gamma$ is not an upper bound of $E$).

How do we translate Condition (2) into a concrete tool for equations and inequalities? Any number $\gamma$ strictly smaller than $\alpha$ can be written as $\gamma = \alpha - \varepsilon$ for some $\varepsilon > 0$. Saying that $\alpha - \varepsilon$ fails to be an upper bound of $E$ is the logical negation of "$\forall x \in E, \ x \le \alpha - \varepsilon$"—which is **"$\exists x \in E$ such that $x > \alpha - \varepsilon$."** This gives us the most practical characterization of $\sup E$ in analysis.

> [!theorem] The $\varepsilon$-Characterization of the Supremum
> Let $E \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ be a non-empty subset that is bounded above, and let $\alpha \in \mathbb{R}$ be an upper bound of $E$ (meaning $x \le \alpha$ for all $x \in E$). Then $\alpha = \sup E$ **if and only if** for every $\varepsilon > 0$, there exists at least one element $x \in E$ such that
> $$
> \alpha - \varepsilon < x \le \alpha
> $$

**Proof:**
- **($\implies$):** Suppose $\alpha = \sup E$, and let $\varepsilon > 0$ be arbitrary. Because $\varepsilon > 0$, we have $\alpha - \varepsilon < \alpha$. Since $\alpha$ is the *least* upper bound of $E$, the strictly smaller number $\alpha - \varepsilon$ cannot be an upper bound of $E$. By the definition of an upper bound, the failure of $\alpha - \varepsilon$ to bound $E$ from above means there must exist at least one element $x \in E$ that strictly exceeds $\alpha - \varepsilon$. Since $\alpha$ itself is an upper bound of $E$, this element $x$ satisfies $\alpha - \varepsilon < x \le \alpha$.
- **($\Longleftarrow$):** Conversely, suppose $\alpha$ is an upper bound of $E$ with the property that for every $\varepsilon > 0$, there exists $x \in E$ such that $\alpha - \varepsilon < x \le \alpha$. To show that $\alpha$ is the *least* upper bound of $E$, let $M \in \mathbb{R}$ be any upper bound of $E$; we must prove $\alpha \le M$.
  Let $\varepsilon > 0$ be arbitrary. By our hypothesis, there exists an element $x \in E$ such that $\alpha - \varepsilon < x$. Since $M$ is an upper bound of $E$, we also have $x \le M$. Chaining these two inequalities gives
  $$
  \alpha - \varepsilon < x \le M \implies \alpha < M + \varepsilon
  $$
  Because $\alpha < M + \varepsilon$ holds for **every** $\varepsilon > 0$ (and both $\alpha$ and $M$ are fixed numbers independent of $\varepsilon$), the **$\varepsilon$-Principle** immediately implies $\alpha \le M$! Thus $\alpha = \sup E$. $\blacksquare$

Geometrically, the $\varepsilon$-characterization says that an upper bound $\alpha$ is the supremum of $E$ if and only if you cannot slip a gap of *any* positive width $\varepsilon > 0$ between $E$ and $\alpha$: no matter how small $\varepsilon > 0$ is, the window $(\alpha - \varepsilon, \alpha]$ always captures at least one point $x \in E$. Notice that $x$ does not need to equal $\alpha$ (as $\alpha$ might not belong to $E$), and $x$ will generally depend on $\varepsilon$ (as $\varepsilon$ gets smaller, we may have to pick a different element $x \in E$ closer to $\alpha$).

> [!example] Example: Computing a Supremum Using the $\varepsilon$-Characterization and the Archimedean Property
> Consider the set
> $$
> E := \left\{ 3 - \frac{2}{n} : n \in \mathbb{N}^+ \right\} = \left\{ 1, \, 2, \, \frac{7}{3}, \, \frac{5}{2}, \, \frac{13}{5}, \, \dots \right\}
> $$
> Prove rigorously that $\sup E = 3$. Does $\max E$ exist?

**Solution:**
We verify the two requirements of the $\varepsilon$-Characterization of the Supremum for $\alpha = 3$:
1. **$3$ is an upper bound of $E$:** For every $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$, since $n \ge 1 > 0$, we have $\frac{2}{n} > 0$, which implies
   $$
   3 - \frac{2}{n} < 3
   $$
   Thus every element $x \in E$ satisfies $x < 3 \le 3$, so $3$ is an upper bound of $E$. (Notice also that since $3 - \frac{2}{n} < 3$ strictly for all $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$, the number $3$ is **not** an element of $E$.)
2. **The $\varepsilon$-approximation condition:** Let $\varepsilon > 0$ be arbitrary. We must find at least one element $x = 3 - \frac{2}{n} \in E$ such that $x > 3 - \varepsilon$, which is equivalent to
   $$
   3 - \frac{2}{n} > 3 - \varepsilon \iff \frac{2}{n} < \varepsilon \iff n > \frac{2}{\varepsilon}
   $$
   By the **Archimedean Property of $\mathbb{R}$** (proven in Section 0.5), the set of natural numbers $\mathbb{N}^+$ is not bounded above in $\mathbb{R}$, so for the real number $\frac{2}{\varepsilon}$ there exists an integer $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$ such that $n > \frac{2}{\varepsilon}$. Since both $n$ and $\varepsilon$ are strictly positive, multiplying $n > \frac{2}{\varepsilon}$ by $\frac{\varepsilon}{n} > 0$ gives $\frac{2}{n} < \varepsilon$, and therefore the element $x = 3 - \frac{2}{n} \in E$ satisfies
   $$
   3 - \varepsilon < 3 - \frac{2}{n} \le 3
   $$
By the $\varepsilon$-Characterization of the Supremum, $\sup E = 3$. Finally, because $\sup E = 3 \notin E$, the set $E$ does **not** have a maximum ($\max E$ does not exist). $\blacksquare$

Let's also see how the $\varepsilon$-Characterization and the $\varepsilon$-Principle work together as a two-step machine to prove identities involving supremums of sets!

> [!example] Example: Additivity of the Supremum ($\sup(A + B) = \sup A + \sup B$)
> Let $A, B \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ be non-empty subsets that are bounded above, and define their pairwise sumset (or Minkowski sum) by
> $$
> A + B := \{a + b : a \in A \text{ and } b \in B\}
> $$
> Prove that $A + B$ is bounded above and that $\sup(A + B) = \sup A + \sup B$.

**Solution:**
Let $\alpha := \sup A$ and $\beta := \sup B$. To prove that $\sup(A + B) = \alpha + \beta$, we show two inequalities—first that $\alpha + \beta$ is an upper bound of $A + B$ (which gives $\sup(A + B) \le \alpha + \beta$), and second that $\alpha + \beta \le \sup(A + B)$ using the $\varepsilon$-Characterization and the $\varepsilon$-Principle:

1. **Upper Bound ($\sup(A + B) \le \sup A + \sup B$):** For any element $x \in A + B$, by definition of $A + B$ there exist $a \in A$ and $b \in B$ such that $x = a + b$. Since $\alpha = \sup A$ is an upper bound of $A$ and $\beta = \sup B$ is an upper bound of $B$, we have $a \le \alpha$ and $b \le \beta$. Adding these two inequalities gives
   $$
   a + b \le \alpha + \beta
   $$
   Because this holds for every $a + b \in A + B$, the set $A + B$ is non-empty and bounded above by $\alpha + \beta$. By the Least Upper Bound Property of $\mathbb{R}$, $\sup(A + B)$ exists in $\mathbb{R}$, and since $\sup(A + B)$ is the *least* upper bound of $A + B$, we immediately have
   $$
   \sup(A + B) \le \alpha + \beta
   $$

2. **Reverse Inequality via the $\varepsilon$-Principle ($\alpha + \beta \le \sup(A + B)$):** Let $\varepsilon > 0$ be arbitrary. Because we are adding two quantities ($a$ and $b$), each of which will contribute an error, we use the classic **$\frac{\varepsilon}{2}$-splitting trick**: apply the $\varepsilon$-Characterization of the Supremum to $A$ and $B$ using tolerance $\frac{\varepsilon}{2} > 0$ on each!
   - Since $\alpha = \sup A$ and $\frac{\varepsilon}{2} > 0$, there exists an element $a \in A$ such that $\alpha - \frac{\varepsilon}{2} < a$.
   - Since $\beta = \sup B$ and $\frac{\varepsilon}{2} > 0$, there exists an element $b \in B$ such that $\beta - \frac{\varepsilon}{2} < b$.
   Adding these two inequalities together and noting that $a + b \in A + B$ gives:
   $$
   (\alpha + \beta) - \varepsilon = \left(\alpha - \frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right) + \left(\beta - \frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right) < a + b \le \sup(A + B)
   $$
   Rearranging $(\alpha + \beta) - \varepsilon < \sup(A + B)$ yields
   $$
   \alpha + \beta < \sup(A + B) + \varepsilon
   $$
   Because this inequality holds for **every** $\varepsilon > 0$, the **$\varepsilon$-Principle** (Part 1) implies $\alpha + \beta \le \sup(A + B)$.

Combining $\sup(A + B) \le \alpha + \beta$ and $\alpha + \beta \le \sup(A + B)$ gives $\sup(A + B) = \sup A + \sup B$. $\blacksquare$

## Infimums and the Extended Real Number System

When we established the completeness of $\mathbb{R}$ in Section 0.5, we proved the **Least Upper Bound Property**: every non-empty subset $E \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ that is bounded *above* has a supremum $\sup E \in \mathbb{R}$. What about the mirror-image question: if a non-empty subset $E \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ is bounded *below*, does it automatically have a **greatest lower bound** (or **infimum**) $\inf E \in \mathbb{R}$, or do we need to postulate a separate axiom?

We get the existence of $\inf E$ completely for free! Geometrically, multiplying every number on the real line by $-1$ reflects the real line across the origin $0$, reversing the order of all points ($x \le y \iff -y \le -x$). Under this reflection, every lower bound of a set $E$ turns into an upper bound of the reflected set $-E$, and the *greatest* lower bound of $E$ is simply the negative of the *least* upper bound of $-E$.

> [!theorem] The Reflection Principle and Greatest Lower Bound Property of $\mathbb{R}$
> Every non-empty subset $E \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ that is bounded below in $\mathbb{R}$ has a unique greatest lower bound (infimum) $\inf E \in \mathbb{R}$. Specifically, if we define the reflected set
> $$
> -E := \{-x : x \in E\}
> $$
> then $E$ is bounded below in $\mathbb{R}$ if and only if $-E$ is bounded above in $\mathbb{R}$, in which case
> $$
> \inf E = -\sup(-E) \qquad \text{and equivalently} \qquad \sup(-E) = -\inf E
> $$
> Furthermore, a lower bound $\beta \in \mathbb{R}$ of $E$ equals $\inf E$ **if and only if** for every $\varepsilon > 0$, there exists at least one element $x \in E$ such that
> $$
> \beta \le x < \beta + \varepsilon
> $$

**Proof:**
- **Reflection and Existence of $\inf E$:** Since $E$ is non-empty, $-E$ is also non-empty. Suppose $m \in \mathbb{R}$ is a lower bound of $E$, meaning $m \le x$ for all $x \in E$. Multiplying by $-1$ reverses the inequality to $-x \le -m$ for all $x \in E$. Since every element of $-E$ has the form $-x$ for some $x \in E$, this says that $-m$ is an upper bound of $-E$.
  By the Least Upper Bound Property of $\mathbb{R}$, the non-empty, bounded-above set $-E$ has a supremum $\alpha := \sup(-E) \in \mathbb{R}$. Let $\beta := -\alpha = -\sup(-E)$. We verify that $\beta$ satisfies the two defining conditions of $\inf E$:
  1. **$\beta$ is a lower bound of $E$:** For every $x \in E$, we have $-x \in -E$, so $-x \le \alpha$. Multiplying by $-1$ gives $x \ge -\alpha = \beta$. Thus $\beta$ is a lower bound of $E$.
  2. **$\beta$ is the *greatest* lower bound of $E$:** Let $m \in \mathbb{R}$ be any lower bound of $E$. As shown above, $-m$ is an upper bound of $-E$. Because $\alpha = \sup(-E)$ is the *least* upper bound of $-E$, we have $\alpha \le -m$. Multiplying by $-1$ yields $m \le -\alpha = \beta$. Thus $\beta = \inf E = -\sup(-E)$.

- **The $\varepsilon$-Characterization of $\inf E$:** Let $\beta \in \mathbb{R}$ be a lower bound of $E$, so $-\beta$ is an upper bound of $-E$. By the Reflection Principle and the $\varepsilon$-Characterization of the Supremum applied to $-E$:
  $$
  \begin{aligned}
  \beta = \inf E &\iff -\beta = \sup(-E) \\
  &\iff \forall \varepsilon > 0, \ \exists (-x) \in -E \text{ such that } -\beta - \varepsilon < -x \le -\beta \\
  &\iff \forall \varepsilon > 0, \ \exists x \in E \text{ such that } \beta \le x < \beta + \varepsilon
  \end{aligned}
  $$
  where the last equivalence follows by multiplying the chain of inequalities by $-1$. $\blacksquare$

> [!note] How the Reflection Principle Saves Half Your Work
> Because $\inf E = -\sup(-E)$, you almost **never** need to write a separate proof from scratch for a theorem about infimums once you have proven the corresponding theorem for supremums! For example, since we already proved $\sup(A + B) = \sup A + \sup B$, we can deduce $\inf(A + B) = \inf A + \inf B$ in a single line of algebra by noting that $-(A + B) = (-A) + (-B)$:
> $$
> \inf(A + B) = -\sup(-(A + B)) = -\sup((-A) + (-B)) = -\big(\sup(-A) + \sup(-B)\big) = \inf A + \inf B
> $$

### The Extended Real Number System $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$

In our theorems so far, we always had to attach two annoying hypotheses before writing $\sup E$ or $\inf E$: the set $E \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ must be **non-empty** and **bounded above** (or **bounded below**). What if $E = \mathbb{N}$ (which is not bounded above), or $E = \mathbb{Z}$ (which is bounded neither above nor below), or even $E = \emptyset$ (the empty set)?

To make $\sup E$ and $\inf E$ unconditionally well-defined for **every subset $E \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ without exception**, analysts adjoin two formal boundary symbols, $-\infty$ ("minus infinity") and $+\infty$ ("plus infinity"), to the real line $\mathbb{R}$, forming the **extended real number system** $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$.

> [!abstract] Definition: The Extended Real Number System $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$ and Unbounded Supremums/Infimums
> The **extended real number system** is the set
> $$
> \overline{\mathbb{R}} := \mathbb{R} \cup \{-\infty, +\infty\}
> $$
> where $-\infty$ and $+\infty$ are two distinct symbols not belonging to $\mathbb{R}$, equipped with the extended total order relation extending $<$ on $\mathbb{R}$ by decreeing that
> $$
> -\infty < x < +\infty \quad \text{for all } x \in \mathbb{R}
> $$
> Within $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$, every subset $E \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ has a supremum $\sup E \in \overline{\mathbb{R}}$ and an infimum $\inf E \in \overline{\mathbb{R}}$ defined as follows:
> 1. **Bounded Non-Empty Sets:** If $E \neq \emptyset$ is bounded above in $\mathbb{R}$, $\sup E \in \mathbb{R}$ is its usual least upper bound; if $E \neq \emptyset$ is bounded below in $\mathbb{R}$, $\inf E \in \mathbb{R}$ is its usual greatest lower bound.
> 2. **Unbounded Non-Empty Sets:**
>    - If $E \neq \emptyset$ is **not bounded above** in $\mathbb{R}$ (meaning for every $M \in \mathbb{R}$, there exists $x \in E$ with $x > M$), we define $\sup E := +\infty$.
>    - If $E \neq \emptyset$ is **not bounded below** in $\mathbb{R}$ (meaning for every $m \in \mathbb{R}$, there exists $x \in E$ with $x < m$), we define $\inf E := -\infty$.
> 3. **The Empty Set:** For the empty set $E = \emptyset$, we define
>    $$
>    \sup \emptyset := -\infty \qquad \text{and} \qquad \inf \emptyset := +\infty
>    $$

Wait a second—look closely at Rule (3): why on earth is $\sup \emptyset = -\infty$ and $\inf \emptyset = +\infty$? At first glance, that looks completely backwards! Shouldn't a supremum be larger than an infimum?

Let's unpack why $\sup \emptyset = -\infty$ and $\inf \emptyset = +\infty$ is not an arbitrary convention, but the **only** logically consistent consequence of the definition of an upper bound and **vacuous truth** (from Section 0.1):
- **Which extended real numbers $M \in \overline{\mathbb{R}}$ are upper bounds of $\emptyset$?** By definition, $M$ is an upper bound of a set $E$ if the implication
  $$
  \forall x \in \overline{\mathbb{R}}, \quad (x \in E \implies x \le M)
  $$
  is true. When $E = \emptyset$, the premise "$x \in \emptyset$" is **always false**! As we learned in Section 0.1, an implication with a false premise is **vacuously true**. Therefore, *every* extended real number $M \in \overline{\mathbb{R}} = [-\infty, +\infty]$ is an upper bound of the empty set $\emptyset$!
- **What is the *least* upper bound of $\emptyset$?** Since the set of all upper bounds of $\emptyset$ in $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$ is the entire extended real line $[-\infty, +\infty]$, the **smallest** element in that set of upper bounds is the bottom endpoint of $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$, namely $-\infty$! Thus, the least upper bound of $\emptyset$ is literally $\sup \emptyset = -\infty$.
- **What is the *greatest* lower bound of $\emptyset$?** By the exact same vacuous-truth logic, every $m \in [-\infty, +\infty]$ satisfies $\forall x \in \emptyset, \ m \le x$, so the set of all lower bounds of $\emptyset$ is all of $[-\infty, +\infty]$. The **largest** element in the set of lower bounds is the top endpoint $+\infty$, so $\inf \emptyset = +\infty$.

Even better, this definition preserves the fundamental **monotonicity law of supremums and infimums** for *all* subsets $A \subseteq B \subseteq \mathbb{R}$: whenever $A \subseteq B$, shrinking a set gives fewer elements to bound, so $\sup A \le \sup B$ and $\inf A \ge \inf B$. Because $\emptyset \subseteq B$ for every set $B$, we *must* have $\sup \emptyset \le \sup B$ and $\inf \emptyset \ge \inf B$ for every subset $B \subseteq \mathbb{R}$—which forces $\sup \emptyset = -\infty$ and $\inf \emptyset = +\infty$!

> [!warning] Caution: Arithmetic Conventions and Indeterminate Forms in $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$
> Always remember that $+\infty$ and $-\infty$ are **not real numbers** ($+\infty, -\infty \notin \mathbb{R}$), and $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$ is **not a field**! Whenever a theorem in Real Analysis states "Let $x \in \mathbb{R}$" or "Suppose $\sup E$ is finite," it explicitly excludes $\pm\infty$.
> For convenience when studying limits and measure theory, standard arithmetic is partially extended to $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$ for $x \in \mathbb{R}$ by setting:
> - **Addition:** $x + (+\infty) = (+\infty) + x = +\infty$, $\quad x + (-\infty) = (-\infty) + x = -\infty$, $\quad (+\infty) + (+\infty) = +\infty$, $\quad (-\infty) + (-\infty) = -\infty$.
> - **Multiplication:**
>   $$
>   x \cdot (\pm\infty) = \begin{cases}
>   \pm\infty & \text{if } x \in (0, +\infty] \\
>   \mp\infty & \text{if } x \in [-\infty, 0)
>   \end{cases}
>   $$
> - **Division by $\pm\infty$:** $\frac{x}{+\infty} = \frac{x}{-\infty} = 0$ for all $x \in \mathbb{R}$.
>
> However, expressions such as
> $$
> (+\infty) - (+\infty), \qquad (-\infty) + (+\infty), \qquad 0 \cdot (\pm\infty), \qquad \frac{\pm\infty}{\pm\infty}, \qquad \frac{0}{0}
> $$
> are **strictly undefined indeterminate forms** in Real Analysis. Why? Because if $(+\infty) - (+\infty)$ were defined to be $0$, then from $1 + (+\infty) = +\infty$ we could subtract $+\infty$ from both sides and falsely deduce $1 = 0$! Because $+\infty$ absorbs finite numbers under addition, the additive cancellation law fails at $\pm\infty$, so $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$ can never form a group or a field.

## 0.6 Exercises

### Problem 0.6.1: Multiplicative and Reciprocal Algebra of Supremums and Infimums

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $A, B \subseteq (0, \infty)$ be non-empty bounded subsets of strictly positive real numbers, and define their pairwise product set and reciprocal set by
> $$
> A \cdot B := \{a \cdot b : a \in A \text{ and } b \in B\}, \qquad A^{-1} := \left\{\frac{1}{a} : a \in A\right\}
> $$
> 1. Using the $\varepsilon$-characterization of the supremum and infimum, prove that $A \cdot B$ is bounded and satisfies
>    $$
>    \sup(A \cdot B) = (\sup A) \cdot (\sup B) \qquad \text{and} \qquad \inf(A \cdot B) = (\inf A) \cdot (\inf B)
>    $$
> 2. Prove that $A^{-1}$ is bounded above in $\mathbb{R}$ **if and only if** $\inf A > 0$, in which case
>    $$
>    \sup(A^{-1}) = \frac{1}{\inf A} \qquad \text{and} \qquad \inf(A^{-1}) = \frac{1}{\sup A}
>    $$
> 3. Give an explicit counterexample showing that $\sup(A \cdot B) = (\sup A)(\sup B)$ can fail when $A$ and $B$ are allowed to contain negative numbers, and write down a general formula for $\sup(A \cdot B)$ in terms of $\sup A, \inf A, \sup B, \inf B$ valid for all non-empty bounded sets $A, B \subset \mathbb{R}$.

**Solution:**
**Part 1: Supremum and Infimum of $A \cdot B$ for Positive Sets.**
Let $\alpha := \sup A$ and $\beta := \sup B$. Since $A, B \subseteq (0, \infty)$ are non-empty, picking any $a_0 \in A$ and $b_0 \in B$ shows $\alpha \ge a_0 > 0$ and $\beta \ge b_0 > 0$.
- **Upper bound for $\sup(A \cdot B)$:** For any $a \in A$ and $b \in B$, we have $0 < a \le \alpha$ and $0 < b \le \beta$. Because multiplication by positive numbers preserves inequalities, multiplying these gives
  $$
  0 < a \cdot b \le \alpha \cdot b \le \alpha \cdot \beta
  $$
  Thus $A \cdot B$ is bounded below by $0$ and bounded above by $\alpha \beta$, which means $\sup(A \cdot B) \le \alpha \beta$.
- **Reverse inequality for $\sup(A \cdot B)$:** Let $\varepsilon > 0$ be arbitrary; we want to find $a \in A$ and $b \in B$ such that $a b > \alpha \beta - \varepsilon$. To see how close $a$ must be to $\alpha$ and $b$ must be to $\beta$, notice the algebraic identity for the error $\alpha \beta - a b$:
  $$
  \alpha \beta - a b = \alpha(\beta - b) + b(\alpha - a) \le \alpha(\beta - b) + \beta(\alpha - a)
  $$
  where we used $b \le \beta > 0$ and $\alpha - a \ge 0$. To make each of the two terms on the right strictly less than $\frac{\varepsilon}{2}$, we apply the $\varepsilon$-characterization of the supremum to $A$ with tolerance $\varepsilon_A := \frac{\varepsilon}{2\beta} > 0$ and to $B$ with tolerance $\varepsilon_B := \frac{\varepsilon}{2\alpha} > 0$:
  - There exists $a \in A$ such that $\alpha - \frac{\varepsilon}{2\beta} < a \le \alpha$, i.e., $\alpha - a < \frac{\varepsilon}{2\beta}$.
  - There exists $b \in B$ such that $\beta - \frac{\varepsilon}{2\alpha} < b \le \beta$, i.e., $\beta - b < \frac{\varepsilon}{2\alpha}$.
  Substituting these two bounds into our identity gives
  $$
  \alpha \beta - a b = \alpha(\beta - b) + b(\alpha - a) < \alpha \left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2\alpha}\right) + \beta \left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2\beta}\right) = \frac{\varepsilon}{2} + \frac{\varepsilon}{2} = \varepsilon
  $$
  Thus $\alpha \beta - \varepsilon < a b \le \sup(A \cdot B)$. By the $\varepsilon$-Principle (or the $\varepsilon$-Characterization of the Supremum), $\alpha \beta \le \sup(A \cdot B)$, and hence $\sup(A \cdot B) = \alpha \beta = (\sup A)(\sup B)$.

- **Formula for $\inf(A \cdot B)$:** Let $\alpha_* := \inf A \ge 0$ and $\beta_* := \inf B \ge 0$. For all $a \in A$ and $b \in B$, we have $a \ge \alpha_* \ge 0$ and $b \ge \beta_* \ge 0$, so $a b \ge \alpha_* \beta_*$, making $\alpha_* \beta_*$ a lower bound of $A \cdot B$ and giving $\inf(A \cdot B) \ge \alpha_* \beta_*$.
  For the reverse inequality, let $\varepsilon > 0$ be arbitrary. By the $\varepsilon$-characterization of the infimum, we can choose $a \in A$ and $b \in B$ satisfying $a < \alpha_* + \delta$ and $b < \beta_* + \delta$ for any $\delta > 0$ we wish. Then
  $$
  a b < (\alpha_* + \delta)(\beta_* + \delta) = \alpha_* \beta_* + \delta(\alpha_* + \beta_* + \delta)
  $$
  Choosing $\delta := \min\left\{1, \frac{\varepsilon}{\alpha_* + \beta_* + 1}\right\} > 0$ guarantees $\delta(\alpha_* + \beta_* + \delta) \le \delta(\alpha_* + \beta_* + 1) \le \varepsilon$, so
  $$
  \inf(A \cdot B) \le a b < \alpha_* \beta_* + \varepsilon
  $$
  By the $\varepsilon$-Principle, $\inf(A \cdot B) \le \alpha_* \beta_*$, and thus $\inf(A \cdot B) = (\inf A)(\inf B)$.

**Part 2: Supremum and Infimum of the Reciprocal Set $A^{-1}$.**
Let $\alpha_* := \inf A \ge 0$ and $\alpha := \sup A > 0$.
- **($\implies$):** Suppose $A^{-1}$ is bounded above in $\mathbb{R}$ by some $M > 0$. Then for every $a \in A$, we have $\frac{1}{a} \le M$, which (since $a > 0$ and $M > 0$) rearranges to $a \ge \frac{1}{M} > 0$. Thus $\frac{1}{M}$ is a positive lower bound of $A$, which forces $\inf A \ge \frac{1}{M} > 0$.
- **($\Longleftarrow$ and Formula for $\sup(A^{-1})$):** Suppose $\alpha_* = \inf A > 0$. Then for every $a \in A$, $a \ge \alpha_* > 0$, so $\frac{1}{a} \le \frac{1}{\alpha_*}$. Thus $A^{-1}$ is bounded above by $\frac{1}{\alpha_*}$. To show $\frac{1}{\alpha_*} = \sup(A^{-1})$, let $M > 0$ be any upper bound of $A^{-1}$. Then for all $a \in A$, $\frac{1}{a} \le M \implies a \ge \frac{1}{M}$, so $\frac{1}{M}$ is a lower bound of $A$. Since $\alpha_* = \inf A$ is the *greatest* lower bound of $A$, we have $\frac{1}{M} \le \alpha_*$, which rearranges to $\frac{1}{\alpha_*} \le M$. Thus $\sup(A^{-1}) = \frac{1}{\inf A}$.
- **Formula for $\inf(A^{-1})$:** Notice that $A^{-1}$ is always bounded below by $0$ (and hence $\inf(A^{-1})$ always exists in $\mathbb{R}$, even if $\inf A = 0$). Since $0 < a \le \alpha = \sup A$ for all $a \in A$, we have $\frac{1}{a} \ge \frac{1}{\alpha} > 0$, so $\frac{1}{\alpha}$ is a lower bound of $A^{-1}$. Conversely, if $m > 0$ is any lower bound of $A^{-1}$, then $\frac{1}{a} \ge m \implies a \le \frac{1}{m}$ for all $a \in A$, so $\alpha = \sup A \le \frac{1}{m}$, which gives $m \le \frac{1}{\alpha}$. Hence $\inf(A^{-1}) = \frac{1}{\sup A}$.

**Part 3: Counterexample for Signed Sets and General Formula.**
- **Counterexample:** Let $A = B = [-2, -1]$. Then $\sup A = -1$ and $\sup B = -1$, so $(\sup A)(\sup B) = (-1)(-1) = 1$. However, multiplying two numbers in $[-2, -1]$ produces positive products ranging from $(-1)(-1) = 1$ up to $(-2)(-2) = 4$, so $A \cdot B = [1, 4]$ and $\sup(A \cdot B) = 4 \neq 1$!
- **General Formula for Bounded Sets $A, B \subset \mathbb{R}$:** Let $\alpha_* = \inf A$, $\alpha = \sup A$, $\beta_* = \inf B$, and $\beta = \sup B$. For any $a \in A$ and $b \in B$, we have $\alpha_* \le a \le \alpha$ and $\beta_* \le b \le \beta$. For fixed $a \in [\alpha_*, \alpha]$, the linear function $b \mapsto a b$ is either non-decreasing (if $a \ge 0$) or non-increasing (if $a < 0$), so its maximum on $[\beta_*, \beta]$ is always achieved at one of the endpoints: $a b \le \max\{a \beta_*, a \beta\}$. Repeating the same logic for $a \in [\alpha_*, \alpha]$ shows that for all $a \in A, b \in B$,
  $$
  a b \le \max\{\alpha_* \beta_*, \, \alpha_* \beta, \, \alpha \beta_*, \, \alpha \beta\}
  $$
  Conversely, by choosing sequences of elements in $A$ approaching $\alpha_*$ or $\alpha$ and in $B$ approaching $\beta_*$ or $\beta$, the product $a b$ can be made arbitrarily close to each of the four corner products $\alpha_* \beta_*, \alpha_* \beta, \alpha \beta_*, \alpha \beta$. Therefore, for all non-empty bounded sets $A, B \subset \mathbb{R}$,
  $$
  \sup(A \cdot B) = \max\big\{ (\inf A)(\inf B), \, (\inf A)(\sup B), \, (\sup A)(\inf B), \, (\sup A)(\sup B) \big\}
  $$
  (and similarly $\inf(A \cdot B)$ is the $\min$ of those same four products). $\blacksquare$

### Problem 0.6.2: Supremum of Pairwise Differences and Set Diameter

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $A, B \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ be non-empty bounded subsets of $\mathbb{R}$, and define $A - B := \{a - b : a \in A \text{ and } b \in B\}$.
> 1. Prove that
>    $$
>    \sup(A - B) = \sup A - \inf B \qquad \text{and} \qquad \inf(A - B) = \inf A - \sup B
>    $$
> 2. Define the **diameter** of a non-empty bounded set $A \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ by $\operatorname{diam}(A) := \sup \{|x - y| : x, y \in A\}$. Prove that
>    $$
>    \operatorname{diam}(A) = \sup A - \inf A
>    $$

**Solution:**
**Part 1: Supremum and Infimum of $A - B$.**
Rather than repeating an $\varepsilon/2$ argument from scratch, we can combine the **Additivity of the Supremum** ($\sup(X + Y) = \sup X + \sup Y$, proven in the text) with the **Reflection Principle** ($\sup(-B) = -\inf B$)!
Notice that subtracting an element $b \in B$ is identical to adding the element $-b \in -B$:
$$
A - B = \{a + (-b) : a \in A \text{ and } b \in B\} = A + (-B)
$$
Because $B$ is non-empty and bounded (both above and below), the reflected set $-B$ is also non-empty and bounded. Applying the additivity of the supremum to $A$ and $-B$, and then invoking the Reflection Principle $\sup(-B) = -\inf B$, we immediately obtain:
$$
\sup(A - B) = \sup(A + (-B)) = \sup A + \sup(-B) = \sup A - \inf B
$$
Similarly, applying the Reflection Principle to $A - B$ by noting that $-(A - B) = B - A$:
$$
\inf(A - B) = -\sup(-(A - B)) = -\sup(B - A) = -(\sup B - \inf A) = \inf A - \sup B
$$
Intuitively, to make a difference $a - b$ as *large* as possible, you make $a$ as large as possible ($\sup A$) and subtract the *smallest* possible $b$ ($\inf B$); to make $a - b$ as *small* as possible, you start with the smallest $a$ ($\inf A$) and subtract the largest $b$ ($\sup B$).

**Part 2: Diameter of a Bounded Set $A$.**
Let $D := \{|x - y| : x, y \in A\}$, so that $\operatorname{diam}(A) = \sup D$. We want to prove $\sup D = \sup A - \inf A$.
- **$\operatorname{diam}(A) \le \sup A - \inf A$:** Let $x, y \in A$ be arbitrary. Since $\inf A$ is a lower bound of $A$ and $\sup A$ is an upper bound of $A$, we have
  $$
  \inf A \le x \le \sup A \qquad \text{and} \qquad -\sup A \le -y \le -\inf A
  $$
  Adding these two inequalities gives
  $$
  -(\sup A - \inf A) \le x - y \le \sup A - \inf A
  $$
  By the Sandwich Equivalence (Property 3 of absolute value), this two-sided inequality is equivalent to
  $$
  |x - y| \le \sup A - \inf A
  $$
  Since this holds for all $x, y \in A$, the number $\sup A - \inf A$ is an upper bound of $D$, so $\operatorname{diam}(A) = \sup D \le \sup A - \inf A$.
- **$\sup A - \inf A \le \operatorname{diam}(A)$:** For any $x, y \in A$, we always have $x - y \le |x - y| \le \sup D = \operatorname{diam}(A)$. Therefore, $\operatorname{diam}(A)$ is an upper bound of the difference set $A - A = \{x - y : x, y \in A\}$. Taking the supremum over $A - A$ and using Part 1 with $B = A$ gives
  $$
  \sup A - \inf A = \sup(A - A) \le \operatorname{diam}(A)
  $$
Combining both inequalities yields $\operatorname{diam}(A) = \sup A - \inf A$. $\blacksquare$

### Problem 0.6.3: Fekete's Subadditive Infimum Property

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $a_1, a_2, a_3, \dots$ be a sequence of positive real numbers satisfying the **subadditivity** inequality
> $$
> a_{m+n} \le a_m + a_n \quad \text{for all } m, n \in \mathbb{N}^+
> $$
> Consider the set of averages $S := \left\{\frac{a_n}{n} : n \in \mathbb{N}^+\right\}$, which is bounded below by $0$, and let $\alpha := \inf S \in \mathbb{R}$. Using the Euclidean Division Algorithm on $\mathbb{N}$ ($n = qm + r$ with $0 \le r < m$) and the $\varepsilon$-characterization of $\inf S$, prove that for every $\varepsilon > 0$, there exists an integer $N \in \mathbb{N}^+$ such that for all $n \ge N$,
> $$
> \alpha \le \frac{a_n}{n} < \alpha + \varepsilon
> $$

**Solution:**
First, let's observe by induction on $q \in \mathbb{N}^+$ that the subadditivity hypothesis $a_{m+n} \le a_m + a_n$ implies $a_{q m} \le q a_m$ for any $q, m \in \mathbb{N}^+$. Indeed, for $q = 1$ we have $a_{1 \cdot m} = 1 \cdot a_m$, and if $a_{q m} \le q a_m$, then
$$
a_{(q+1)m} = a_{qm + m} \le a_{qm} + a_m \le q a_m + a_m = (q + 1)a_m
$$
Now let $\varepsilon > 0$ be given. Since $\alpha = \inf S$ is a lower bound of $S$, the left-hand inequality $\alpha \le \frac{a_n}{n}$ already holds automatically for every $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$. To establish the right-hand inequality $\frac{a_n}{n} < \alpha + \varepsilon$ for all sufficiently large $n$, we split our error budget $\varepsilon$ into two halves of size $\frac{\varepsilon}{2}$:

1. **Fixing the Base Step $m$ via the $\varepsilon$-Characterization of $\inf S$:**
   Applying the $\varepsilon$-characterization of the infimum to $S$ with tolerance $\frac{\varepsilon}{2} > 0$, there exists a fixed positive integer $m \in \mathbb{N}^+$ such that
   $$
   \frac{a_m}{m} < \alpha + \frac{\varepsilon}{2}
   $$
   Once this $m$ is chosen, keep it **completely fixed** for the rest of the proof!

2. **Dividing an Arbitrary $n \ge m$ by $m$:**
   For any integer $n \ge m$, the Euclidean Division Algorithm allows us to write
   $$
   n = q m + r \quad \text{where } q \in \mathbb{N}^+ \text{ and } r \in \{0, 1, \dots, m - 1\}
   $$
   To handle $r = 0$ and $1 \le r < m$ uniformly, define $a_0 := 0$ and let
   $$
   M := \max\{a_0, a_1, a_2, \dots, a_{m-1}\} \ge 0
   $$
   Notice that since $m$ is fixed, $\{a_0, a_1, \dots, a_{m-1}\}$ is a fixed finite set of numbers, so $M$ is a fixed constant independent of $n$! Applying subadditivity to $n = qm + r$ gives
   $$
   a_n = a_{qm + r} \le a_{qm} + a_r \le q a_m + M
   $$
   Dividing both sides by $n > 0$ and noticing that $n = qm + r \ge qm \implies \frac{qm}{n} \le 1$ (and since $a_m > 0$), we obtain:
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   \frac{a_n}{n} &\le \frac{q a_m}{n} + \frac{M}{n} \\
   &= \left(\frac{q m}{n}\right) \frac{a_m}{m} + \frac{M}{n} \\
   &\le 1 \cdot \frac{a_m}{m} + \frac{M}{n} \\
   &< \left(\alpha + \frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right) + \frac{M}{n}
   \end{aligned}
   $$

3. **Choosing the Threshold $N$ via the Archimedean Property:**
   We only need the remaining tail term $\frac{M}{n}$ to be strictly less than $\frac{\varepsilon}{2}$. By the Archimedean Property, there exists a positive integer $N_0 \in \mathbb{N}^+$ such that $N_0 > \frac{2M}{\varepsilon}$. Setting $N := \max\{m, N_0\}$, we see that for every integer $n \ge N$, we have $\frac{M}{n} \le \frac{M}{N_0} < \frac{\varepsilon}{2}$, and therefore
   $$
   \alpha \le \frac{a_n}{n} < \left(\alpha + \frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right) + \frac{\varepsilon}{2} = \alpha + \varepsilon
   $$
   as desired. (In the language of Chapter 1, this proves **Fekete's Subadditive Lemma**: for any subadditive sequence of positive numbers, the sequence of averages $\frac{a_n}{n}$ converges to its infimum $\inf_{n \ge 1} \frac{a_n}{n}$ as $n \to \infty$!) $\blacksquare$

### Problem 0.6.4: Supremum and Infimum of Functions and the Minimax (Saddle-Point) Inequality

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> In analysis and optimization, one frequently takes supremums and infimums of functions over their domains. Given a non-empty set $D$ and a bounded function $f : D \to \mathbb{R}$ (meaning its range $f(D) := \{f(x) : x \in D\}$ is a bounded subset of $\mathbb{R}$), we define
> $$
> \sup_{x \in D} f(x) := \sup f(D) \qquad \text{and} \qquad \inf_{x \in D} f(x) := \inf f(D)
> $$
> 1. Let $f, g : D \to \mathbb{R}$ be bounded functions on a non-empty set $D$. Prove that
>    $$
>    \sup_{x \in D} (f(x) + g(x)) \le \sup_{x \in D} f(x) + \sup_{x \in D} g(x) \qquad \text{and} \qquad \inf_{x \in D} (f(x) + g(x)) \ge \inf_{x \in D} f(x) + \inf_{x \in D} g(x)
>    $$
>    Give an explicit example of a set $D$ and bounded functions $f, g : D \to \mathbb{R}$ where both inequalities are **strict**, and explain why this does not contradict the identity $\sup(A + B) = \sup A + \sup B$ for sets!
> 2. Prove that if $f(x) \le g(x)$ for all $x \in D$, then
>    $$
>    \sup_{x \in D} f(x) \le \sup_{x \in D} g(x) \qquad \text{and} \qquad \inf_{x \in D} f(x) \le \inf_{x \in D} g(x)
>    $$
>    By contrast, what condition on $f$ and $g$ is required to conclude that $\sup_{x \in D} f(x) \le \inf_{x \in D} g(x)$?
> 3. **(The Minimax / Saddle-Point Inequality):** Let $X$ and $Y$ be non-empty sets, and let $h : X \times Y \to \mathbb{R}$ be a bounded function of two variables. Prove that
>    $$
>    \sup_{x \in X} \left( \inf_{y \in Y} h(x, y) \right) \le \inf_{y \in Y} \left( \sup_{x \in X} h(x, y) \right)
>    $$
>    and give an explicit example on $X = Y = \{0, 1\}$ (or $X = Y = [0, 1]$) where the inequality is strict.

**Solution:**
**Part 1: Subadditivity of $\sup_{x \in D}$ and Superadditivity of $\inf_{x \in D}$.**
- **Proof of the inequalities:** For every $x \in D$, the value $f(x)$ belongs to $f(D)$ and $g(x)$ belongs to $g(D)$, so by definition of supremum and infimum:
  $$
  \inf_{t \in D} f(t) \le f(x) \le \sup_{t \in D} f(t) \qquad \text{and} \qquad \inf_{t \in D} g(t) \le g(x) \le \sup_{t \in D} g(t)
  $$
  Adding these two chains of inequalities gives, for every $x \in D$:
  $$
  \inf_{t \in D} f(t) + \inf_{t \in D} g(t) \le f(x) + g(x) \le \sup_{t \in D} f(t) + \sup_{t \in D} g(t)
  $$
  Look closely at the right-hand inequality: the constant $M := \sup_{t \in D} f(t) + \sup_{t \in D} g(t)$ does not depend on $x$, and it bounds $f(x) + g(x)$ from above for *every* $x \in D$. Therefore, $M$ is an upper bound for the set $\{f(x) + g(x) : x \in D\}$. Since the supremum is the *least* upper bound, we immediately conclude
  $$
  \sup_{x \in D} (f(x) + g(x)) \le \sup_{x \in D} f(x) + \sup_{x \in D} g(x)
  $$
  Similarly, the constant $m := \inf_{t \in D} f(t) + \inf_{t \in D} g(t)$ is a lower bound for $\{f(x) + g(x) : x \in D\}$, so the greatest lower bound satisfies
  $$
  \inf_{x \in D} (f(x) + g(x)) \ge \inf_{x \in D} f(x) + \inf_{x \in D} g(x)
  $$
- **Example of strict inequality and explanation:** Let $D = [0, 1]$, and define $f(x) = x$ and $g(x) = 1 - x$. Then
  $$
  \sup_{x \in [0, 1]} f(x) = 1 \quad (\text{attained at } x = 1) \qquad \text{and} \qquad \sup_{x \in [0, 1]} g(x) = 1 \quad (\text{attained at } x = 0)
  $$
  so $\sup_{x \in D} f(x) + \sup_{x \in D} g(x) = 1 + 1 = 2$ (and similarly $\inf_{x \in D} f(x) + \inf_{x \in D} g(x) = 0 + 0 = 0$). However, for every $x \in [0, 1]$, the sum is the constant function $f(x) + g(x) = x + (1 - x) = 1$, so
  $$
  \sup_{x \in [0, 1]} (f(x) + g(x)) = 1 < 2 \qquad \text{and} \qquad \inf_{x \in [0, 1]} (f(x) + g(x)) = 1 > 0
  $$
  Why doesn't this contradict $\sup(A + B) = \sup A + \sup B$? Because in the set sum $f(D) + g(D) = \{f(x) + g(y) : x, y \in D\}$, you are allowed to pick **two different inputs** $x$ and $y$ independently (e.g., $x = 1$ to maximize $f(x)$ and $y = 0$ to maximize $g(y)$). By contrast, in $\sup_{x \in D} (f(x) + g(x))$, both $f$ and $g$ are forced to be evaluated at the **exact same input $x$**—meaning the set $\{f(x) + g(x) : x \in D\}$ is only the *diagonal subset* of $f(D) + g(D)$, and $f$ and $g$ might peak at completely different points of $D$!

**Part 2: Pointwise Comparison vs. Uniform Comparison.**
- Suppose $f(x) \le g(x)$ for all $x \in D$. For any $x \in D$, we have $f(x) \le g(x) \le \sup_{t \in D} g(t)$. Thus $\sup_{t \in D} g(t)$ is an upper bound for $f(D)$, which implies $\sup_{x \in D} f(x) \le \sup_{x \in D} g(x)$. Similarly, for every $x \in D$, $\inf_{t \in D} f(t) \le f(x) \le g(x)$, so $\inf_{t \in D} f(t)$ is a lower bound for $g(D)$, which implies $\inf_{x \in D} f(x) \le \inf_{x \in D} g(x)$.
- By contrast, what does it take to get $\sup_{x \in D} f(x) \le \inf_{y \in D} g(y)$? Notice that $\sup_{x \in D} f(x) \le \inf_{y \in D} g(y)$ means that *every* value of $f$ is less than or equal to *every* value of $g$, i.e.,
  $$
  f(x) \le g(y) \quad \text{for all pairs } x, y \in D
  $$
  (not merely when $x = y$!). Indeed, if $f(x) \le g(y)$ for all $x, y \in D$, then for any fixed $y \in D$, $g(y)$ is an upper bound for $f(D)$, so $\sup_{x \in D} f(x) \le g(y)$; since this holds for all $y \in D$, $\sup_{x \in D} f(x)$ is a lower bound for $g(D)$, giving $\sup_{x \in D} f(x) \le \inf_{y \in D} g(y)$.

**Part 3: The Minimax (Saddle-Point) Inequality.**
- **Proof:** Let $x_0 \in X$ and $y_0 \in Y$ be arbitrary elements. By definition of the infimum over $y \in Y$ (with $x_0$ held fixed) and the supremum over $x \in X$ (with $y_0$ held fixed), we have:
  $$
  \inf_{y \in Y} h(x_0, y) \le h(x_0, y_0) \le \sup_{x \in X} h(x, y_0)
  $$
  Look at the two ends of this inequality: the left-hand side $F(x_0) := \inf_{y \in Y} h(x_0, y)$ depends *only* on $x_0 \in X$, while the right-hand side $G(y_0) := \sup_{x \in X} h(x, y_0)$ depends *only* on $y_0 \in Y$. Because
  $$
  F(x_0) \le G(y_0) \quad \text{for all } x_0 \in X \text{ and all } y_0 \in Y
  $$
  we are in the exact two-variable situation analyzed at the end of Part 2! Specifically:
  1. Holding $y_0 \in Y$ temporarily fixed, $G(y_0)$ is an upper bound for the set $\{F(x_0) : x_0 \in X\}$, so taking the least upper bound over $x_0 \in X$ gives
     $$
     \sup_{x \in X} F(x) \le G(y_0)
     $$
  2. Now, since the constant $\sup_{x \in X} F(x)$ is less than or equal to $G(y_0)$ for *every* $y_0 \in Y$, it is a lower bound for the set $\{G(y_0) : y_0 \in Y\}$. Taking the greatest lower bound over $y_0 \in Y$ gives
     $$
     \sup_{x \in X} F(x) \le \inf_{y \in Y} G(y) \quad \text{i.e.,} \quad \sup_{x \in X} \left( \inf_{y \in Y} h(x, y) \right) \le \inf_{y \in Y} \left( \sup_{x \in X} h(x, y) \right)
     $$
- **Strict Inequality Example (Game Theory Interpretation):** Let $X = Y = \{0, 1\}$ and define $h(x, y)$ by the "Matching Pennies" payoff table $h(0, 0) = 1$, $h(1, 1) = 1$, and $h(0, 1) = h(1, 0) = -1$ (or on $[0, 1] \times [0, 1]$, take $h(x, y) = (x - y)^2$ or $\cos(\pi(x - y))$). Then:
  - For each fixed $x \in \{0, 1\}$, $y$ can be chosen opposite to $x$ to make $h(x, y) = -1$, so $\inf_{y \in Y} h(x, y) = -1$ for both $x = 0$ and $x = 1$. Hence $\sup_{x \in X} \inf_{y \in Y} h(x, y) = -1$.
  - Conversely, for each fixed $y \in \{0, 1\}$, $x$ can be chosen equal to $y$ to make $h(x, y) = 1$, so $\sup_{x \in X} h(x, y) = 1$ for both $y = 0$ and $y = 1$. Hence $\inf_{y \in Y} \sup_{x \in X} h(x, y) = 1$.
  Thus $-1 < 1$ strictly! Intuitively, in a two-player zero-sum game where Player $X$ tries to maximize $h(x, y)$ and Player $Y$ tries to minimize $h(x, y)$, $\sup_x \inf_y h(x, y)$ is Player $X$'s guaranteed payoff when Player $X$ must move **first** (allowing $Y$ to respond after seeing $x$), whereas $\inf_y \sup_x h(x, y)$ is Player $X$'s payoff when Player $Y$ must move **first** (allowing $X$ to respond after seeing $y$). Moving second is always an advantage! $\blacksquare$

### Problem 0.6.5: Distance from a Point to a Set and Lipschitz Continuity

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $A \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ be a non-empty subset of $\mathbb{R}$. For any point $x \in \mathbb{R}$, we define the **distance from $x$ to the set $A$** by
> $$
> d(x, A) := \inf \{ |x - a| : a \in A \}
> $$
> 1. Explain why $d(x, A)$ is a well-defined non-negative real number for every $x \in \mathbb{R}$ (even if $A$ itself is an unbounded set!).
> 2. Prove that $d(x, A) = 0$ **if and only if** every $\varepsilon$-neighborhood $V_\varepsilon(x) = (x - \varepsilon, x + \varepsilon)$ around $x$ intersects $A$ (that is, $V_\varepsilon(x) \cap A \neq \emptyset$ for all $\varepsilon > 0$). Give an example of a set $A \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ and a point $x \notin A$ such that $d(x, A) = 0$.
> 3. Prove that for all $x, y \in \mathbb{R}$, the function $x \mapsto d(x, A)$ satisfies the **Lipschitz / Non-Expansive Inequality**:
>    $$
>    \big| d(x, A) - d(y, A) \big| \le |x - y|
>    $$

**Solution:**
**Part 1: Well-Definedness and Non-Negativity of $d(x, A)$.**
Fix any $x \in \mathbb{R}$, and consider the set of distances $D_x := \{|x - a| : a \in A\} \subseteq \mathbb{R}$.
- Because $A$ is non-empty, there exists at least one element $a_0 \in A$, so $|x - a_0| \in D_x$ and $D_x$ is non-empty.
- By the non-negativity of absolute value, $|x - a| \ge 0$ for every $a \in A$. Therefore, $0$ is a lower bound for $D_x$ (regardless of whether $A$ is bounded or unbounded above/below!).
By the **Greatest Lower Bound Property of $\mathbb{R}$**, the non-empty, bounded-below set $D_x$ has a unique infimum $d(x, A) = \inf D_x \in \mathbb{R}$, and since $0$ is a lower bound of $D_x$ while $\inf D_x$ is the *greatest* lower bound, we have $d(x, A) \ge 0$.

**Part 2: Characterization of $d(x, A) = 0$ via $\varepsilon$-Neighborhoods.**
Since we already know $0$ is a lower bound of $D_x = \{|x - a| : a \in A\}$, we can apply the **$\varepsilon$-Characterization of the Infimum** directly to the lower bound $\beta = 0$:
$$
\begin{aligned}
d(x, A) = 0 &\iff 0 = \inf \{|x - a| : a \in A\} \\
&\iff \forall \varepsilon > 0, \ \exists a \in A \text{ such that } 0 \le |x - a| < 0 + \varepsilon \\
&\iff \forall \varepsilon > 0, \ \exists a \in A \text{ such that } a \in V_\varepsilon(x) \\
&\iff \forall \varepsilon > 0, \ V_\varepsilon(x) \cap A \neq \emptyset
\end{aligned}
$$
Notice that if $x \in A$, then taking $a = x \in A$ gives $|x - x| = 0$, so $d(x, A) = 0$. However, the converse ($d(x, A) = 0 \implies x \in A$) is **false** in general! For example, let $A = (0, 1)$ be the open unit interval and take the endpoint $x = 0 \notin A$. For every $\varepsilon > 0$, the element $a := \min\left\{\frac{1}{2}, \frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right\} \in (0, 1) = A$ satisfies $|0 - a| = a \le \frac{\varepsilon}{2} < \varepsilon$, so $V_\varepsilon(0) \cap (0, 1) \neq \emptyset$ for all $\varepsilon > 0$, which means $d(0, (0, 1)) = 0$ even though $0 \notin (0, 1)$. (In topology, the set of all points $x \in \mathbb{R}$ satisfying $d(x, A) = 0$ is called the **closure** of $A$, denoted $\overline{A}$.)

**Part 3: The Lipschitz / Non-Expansive Inequality $\big| d(x, A) - d(y, A) \big| \le |x - y|$.**
Let $x, y \in \mathbb{R}$ be arbitrary. To compare the infimum $d(x, A) = \inf_{a \in A} |x - a|$ with the infimum $d(y, A) = \inf_{a \in A} |y - a|$, we start with a single arbitrary element $a \in A$ and apply the **Metric Triangle Inequality** with intermediate point $y$:
$$
|x - a| \le |x - y| + |y - a|
$$
Now observe that since $d(x, A) = \inf_{a' \in A} |x - a'|$ is a lower bound for all distances from $x$ to points of $A$, we can bound the left-hand side from below by $d(x, A)$:
$$
d(x, A) \le |x - a| \le |x - y| + |y - a|
$$
Subtracting $|x - y|$ from both sides isolates $|y - a|$ on the right:
$$
d(x, A) - |x - y| \le |y - a|
$$
Look carefully at what this inequality says: the number $d(x, A) - |x - y|$ (which is completely independent of $a$!) is less than or equal to $|y - a|$ for **every** $a \in A$. In other words, $d(x, A) - |x - y|$ is a **lower bound** for the set $D_y = \{|y - a| : a \in A\}$. Because $d(y, A) = \inf D_y$ is the *greatest* lower bound of $D_y$, we must have
$$
d(x, A) - |x - y| \le d(y, A) \implies d(x, A) - d(y, A) \le |x - y|
$$
By complete symmetry, swapping the roles of $x$ and $y$ gives
$$
d(y, A) - d(x, A) \le |y - x| = |x - y| \implies -|x - y| \le d(x, A) - d(y, A)
$$
Combining both inequalities gives $-|x - y| \le d(x, A) - d(y, A) \le |x - y|$, which by the Sandwich Equivalence is equivalent to
$$
\big| d(x, A) - d(y, A) \big| \le |x - y|
$$
(Notice that when $A = \{0\}$ is a single point, $d(x, \{0\}) = |x|$ and $d(y, \{0\}) = |y|$, so this inequality reduces directly to the classical Reverse Triangle Inequality $\big| |x| - |y| \big| \le |x - y|$!) $\blacksquare$

---

# 0.7 Cardinality: Countable and Uncountable Sets

We conclude our preliminary foundation with one of the most breathtaking discoveries of 19th-century mathematics, due to Georg Cantor: **not all infinities are the same size**.

Think back to the journey we took across the previous sections as we expanded our number systems step by step:
$$
\mathbb{N} \subsetneq \mathbb{Z} \subsetneq \mathbb{Q} \subsetneq \mathbb{R}
$$
At each stage, we threw in seemingly vast new collections of numbers. Going from $\mathbb{N}$ to $\mathbb{Z}$, we mirrored the positive integers across zero. Going from $\mathbb{Z}$ to $\mathbb{Q}$, we packed infinitely many fractions between every pair of integers, making $\mathbb{Q}$ *dense* along the entire number line. Did our sets actually grow in "infinite size" at each step?

Remarkably, the answer for the first two expansions is **no**: $\mathbb{N}$, $\mathbb{Z}$, and $\mathbb{Q}$ all have the **exact same cardinality**. As we will prove shortly, they are all *countably infinite*—you can arrange every single rational number into a single discrete list $q_1, q_2, q_3, \dots$ indexed by the positive integers $\mathbb{N}^+$ without missing a single fraction! However, when we pass from $\mathbb{Q}$ to $\mathbb{R}$ via Dedekind cuts to fill in the irrational gaps, the size of infinity explodes. The set of real numbers $\mathbb{R}$ is **uncountable**: no sequential list, no matter how cleverly ordered, can ever capture all real numbers.

 If you have taken Probability and Statistics, you have already witnessed the shadow of this distinction in the contrast between **discrete** and **continuous** random variables. A discrete random variable takes values in a set that can be listed as $x_1, x_2, x_3, \dots$, allowing us to compute probabilities by summing a series $\sum_{n=1}^\infty \mathbb{P}(X = x_n) = 1$. A continuous random variable on an interval $[a, b] \subseteq \mathbb{R}$, by contrast, takes values in an uncountable continuum where every individual point has probability $\mathbb{P}(X = x) = 0$, forcing us to replace summation with integration. In this section, we make the mathematics behind these different levels of infinity completely rigorous.

## Equinumerosity and Finite vs. Infinite Sets

How can we compare the sizes of two sets $A$ and $B$—especially when both are infinite—without "counting them to the end"?

Imagine walking into a massive auditorium filled with people and chairs. If someone asks you whether there are the exact same number of people as chairs, you do not need to count all the people, count all the chairs, and compare the two totals. Instead, you simply ask every person to sit down in a distinct chair! If every person gets a seat (so the assignment is **injective**) and no chair is left empty (so the assignment is **surjective**), then you know with absolute certainty that there are just as many people as chairs.

This simple pairing idea—existence of a **bijection** (a one-to-one correspondence)—requires no counting at all, which means it works effortlessly for infinite sets just as well as for finite ones.

> [!abstract] Definition: Equinumerosity, Finite Sets, and Countable Sets
> Let $A$ and $B$ be sets. We say that $A$ and $B$ are **equinumerous** (or are **equipollent**, or have the **same cardinality**), written $A \sim B$, if there exists a **bijection** $f: A \to B$.
>
> For each positive integer $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$, define the standard initial segment of positive integers:
> $$
> \mathbb{N}_n := \{1, 2, \dots, n\}
> $$
> Using equinumerosity with these standard yardsticks, we classify all sets into the following hierarchy:
> 1. **Finite Set:** A set $A$ is **finite** if either $A = \emptyset$ (in which case we say $A$ has cardinality $0$, written $|A| = 0$) or $A \sim \mathbb{N}_n$ for some $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$ (in which case we say $A$ has cardinality $n$, written $|A| = n$).
> 2. **Infinite Set:** A set $A$ is **infinite** if it is not finite.
> 3. **Countably Infinite (Denumerable) Set:** A set $A$ is **countably infinite** (or **denumerable**) if $A \sim \mathbb{N}^+$.
> 4. **Countable (At Most Countable) Set:** A set $A$ is **countable** (or **at most countable**) if it is either finite or countably infinite.
> 5. **Uncountable Set:** A set $A$ is **uncountable** if it is not countable—that is, if $A$ is infinite and $A \not\sim \mathbb{N}^+$.

Let's immediately verify that "having the same cardinality" behaves the way any sensible notion of equality of size should: it is an equivalence relation on collections of sets.

1. **Reflexivity ($A \sim A$):** For any set $A$, the identity map $\mathrm{id}_A: A \to A$ given by $\mathrm{id}_A(x) = x$ is a bijection.
2. **Symmetry ($A \sim B \implies B \sim A$):** If $f: A \to B$ is a bijection, then its inverse function $f^{-1}: B \to A$ is also a bijection.
3. **Transitivity ($A \sim B \text{ and } B \sim C \implies A \sim C$):** If $f: A \to B$ and $g: B \to C$ are bijections, then their composition $g \circ f: A \to C$ is a bijection.

What does it mean in concrete, everyday terms for a set $A$ to be **countably infinite** ($A \sim \mathbb{N}^+$)? A bijection $f: \mathbb{N}^+ \to A$ assigns a unique element $a_1 := f(1)$ to $1$, a second distinct element $a_2 := f(2)$ to $2$, a third distinct element $a_3 := f(3)$ to $3$, and so on. Because $f$ is injective, no element of $A$ is ever listed twice; because $f$ is surjective, every element of $A$ appears at some finite stage $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$. In other words, a set $A$ is countably infinite if and only if its elements can be arranged in a duplicate-free infinite **sequence**
$$
a_1, \; a_2, \; a_3, \; a_4, \dots
$$
that eventually hits every element of $A$.

> [!warning] Terminology Convention: "Countable" vs. "Countably Infinite"
> Be careful when comparing different Real Analysis textbooks! Following Walter Rudin's *Principles of Mathematical Analysis* and most modern measure-theory texts, we define **countable** to mean *finite or countably infinite* (so every finite set is automatically countable). Some other authors use "countable" to mean strictly *countably infinite* ($A \sim \mathbb{N}^+$), and write "at most countable" when they wish to include finite sets. Whenever you want to emphasize that a countable set is strictly infinite, saying **countably infinite** (or **denumerable**) removes all ambiguity.

### Hilbert's Grand Hotel and Bijections with Proper Subsets

When we deal with finite sets, the **Pigeonhole Principle** guarantees that a set can never be placed in bijection with a *proper subset* of itself: if $B \subsetneq A$ and $A$ is finite, then $|B| < |A|$, so there are not enough elements in $B$ to pair one-to-one with all of $A$.

Once we cross the threshold into infinite sets, our finite intuition breaks down completely: **an infinite set can have the exact same cardinality as a proper subset of itself!** The mathematician David Hilbert illustrated this phenomenon with a famous thought experiment known as **Hilbert's Grand Hotel**.

Imagine a hotel with a countably infinite number of rooms, numbered $1, 2, 3, 4, \dots$, and suppose every single room is currently occupied (so the set of guests is in bijection with $\mathbb{N}^+$). In a finite hotel, a "No Vacancy" sign means a new guest must be turned away. In Hilbert's Grand Hotel, however, what happens if one new guest arrives at the front desk? The manager simply announces over the intercom: *"Everyone currently in Room $n$, please move to Room $n + 1$!"*
$$
1 \mapsto 2, \quad 2 \mapsto 3, \quad 3 \mapsto 4, \quad \dots, \quad n \mapsto n + 1
$$
Every existing guest gets a new room, and Room $1$ is now completely free for the newcomer! Algebraically, this says that even though $\mathbb{N}^+ = \{1, 2, 3, \dots\}$ is a proper subset of $\mathbb{N} = \{0, 1, 2, 3, \dots\}$, the shift map $f: \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{N}^+$ defined by $f(n) = n + 1$ is a bijection, so $\mathbb{N} \sim \mathbb{N}^+$.

What if an infinite bus containing a countably infinite sequence of new guests $g_1, g_2, g_3, \dots$ pulls up to the full hotel? The manager announces: *"Everyone currently in Room $n$, please move to Room $2n$!"* Every existing guest moves into an even-numbered room, leaving all infinitely many odd-numbered rooms $1, 3, 5, 7, \dots$ vacant to accommodate the entire infinite bus! Let's formalize this in a concrete worked example.

> [!example] Example: Proper Subsets and Interval Bijections
> 1. Prove that the set of even positive integers $E := \{2, 4, 6, 8, \dots\}$ is countably infinite, even though $E \subsetneq \mathbb{N}^+$ omits half of the positive integers.
> 2. Prove that any open interval $(a, b) \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ with $a < b$ is equinumerous with the unit open interval $(0, 1)$, and that $(0, 1)$ is equinumerous with the entire real line $\mathbb{R}$.

**Solution:**
**Part 1:** Define the function $f: \mathbb{N}^+ \to E$ by
$$
f(n) := 2n \quad \text{for all } n \in \mathbb{N}^+
$$
To see that $f$ is injective, suppose $f(m) = f(n)$ for some $m, n \in \mathbb{N}^+$. Then $2m = 2n$, and multiplying both sides by $\frac{1}{2}$ yields $m = n$. To see that $f$ is surjective onto $E$, note that by definition every element $y \in E$ has the form $y = 2k$ for some $k \in \mathbb{N}^+$, so $y = f(k)$. Because $f: \mathbb{N}^+ \to E$ is a bijection, we have $E \sim \mathbb{N}^+$, proving that $E$ is countably infinite.

**Part 2:** First, let $a < b$ be real numbers. We can stretch and shift the unit interval $(0, 1)$ onto $(a, b)$ using the linear function $L: (0, 1) \to (a, b)$ defined by
$$
L(t) := a + (b - a)t
$$
Since $b - a > 0$, if $0 < t < 1$, then $0 < (b - a)t < b - a$, so $a < L(t) < b$, meaning $L$ maps $(0, 1)$ into $(a, b)$. Furthermore, $L$ has the explicit inverse $L^{-1}: (a, b) \to (0, 1)$ given by $L^{-1}(x) = \frac{x - a}{b - a}$. Since $L$ has a two-sided inverse, $L$ is a bijection, so $(0, 1) \sim (a, b)$. In particular, choosing $a = -1$ and $b = 1$ gives $(0, 1) \sim (-1, 1)$.

Next, to show that $(-1, 1) \sim \mathbb{R}$ without relying on trigonometric functions like $\tan\!\left(\frac{\pi x}{2}\right)$ (which we will construct rigorously later in the course), consider the purely algebraic function $g: (-1, 1) \to \mathbb{R}$ defined by
$$
g(x) := \frac{x}{1 - |x|}
$$
Notice that for $x \in (-1, 1)$, we have $1 - |x| > 0$, so $g(x)$ is well-defined and has the exact same sign as $x$. Can we solve $y = \frac{x}{1 - |x|}$ uniquely for $x \in (-1, 1)$ in terms of any given $y \in \mathbb{R}$? Taking absolute values on both sides gives
$$
|y| = \frac{|x|}{1 - |x|} \iff |y|(1 - |x|) = |x| \iff |y| = |x|(1 + |y|) \iff |x| = \frac{|y|}{1 + |y|}
$$
Since $x$ and $y$ have the same sign (and both are $0$ simultaneously), substituting $1 - |x| = 1 - \frac{|y|}{1 + |y|} = \frac{1}{1 + |y|}$ back into $y = \frac{x}{1 - |x|}$ yields the explicit inverse function $g^{-1}: \mathbb{R} \to (-1, 1)$:
$$
g^{-1}(y) = \frac{y}{1 + |y|}
$$
For every $y \in \mathbb{R}$, we have $|g^{-1}(y)| = \frac{|y|}{1 + |y|} < 1$, so $g^{-1}(y) \in (-1, 1)$, and direct substitution confirms $g(g^{-1}(y)) = y$ and $g^{-1}(g(x)) = x$. Thus $(-1, 1) \sim \mathbb{R}$, and by transitivity of equinumerosity, $(a, b) \sim (0, 1) \sim (-1, 1) \sim \mathbb{R}$. No matter how tiny a microscopic open interval $(a, b)$ you pick, it contains the exact same cardinality of points as the entire infinite real line $\mathbb{R}$! $\blacksquare$

## Countability of $\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$, $\mathbb{Z}$, and $\mathbb{Q}$

Before we test the countability of $\mathbb{Z}$ and $\mathbb{Q}$, let's establish a foundational structural principle: **$\mathbb{N}^+$ is the smallest possible infinity**. What does this mean? If you take any subset $E \subseteq \mathbb{N}^+$, there is no "intermediate" infinite size between finite sets and $\mathbb{N}^+$—every infinite subset of $\mathbb{N}^+$ is itself countably infinite!

Intuitively, why is every subset $E \subseteq \mathbb{N}^+$ countable? Imagine scanning through the positive integers $1, 2, 3, 4, \dots$ in increasing order and highlighting only the numbers that belong to $E$. Because $\mathbb{N}^+$ is well-ordered, there is a first highlighted number $f(1)$, a second highlighted number $f(2)$, a third highlighted number $f(3)$, and so on. If $E$ never runs out of elements, this ordered scanning process naturally pairs $1, 2, 3, \dots$ bijectively with the elements of $E$.

> [!theorem] Every Subset of a Countable Set Is Countable
> Every subset $E \subseteq \mathbb{N}^+$ is countable (either finite or countably infinite). Consequently, for any non-empty set $A$, the following three statements are equivalent:
> 1. $A$ is **countable** (finite or countably infinite).
> 2. There exists an **injective** function $g: A \hookrightarrow \mathbb{N}^+$.
> 3. There exists a **surjective** function $h: \mathbb{N}^+ \twoheadrightarrow A$.

**Proof:**
First, let $E \subseteq \mathbb{N}^+$. If $E$ is finite, then $E$ is countable by definition. Suppose instead that $E$ is an infinite subset of $\mathbb{N}^+$. We construct a bijection $f: \mathbb{N}^+ \to E$ recursively using the **Well-Ordering Principle** of $\mathbb{N}^+$ (which guarantees that every non-empty subset of $\mathbb{N}^+$ has a unique minimum element):
- **Base Step ($n = 1$):** Since $E$ is infinite, $E$ is non-empty, so we define
  $$
  f(1) := \min E
  $$
- **Recursive Step ($n \to n + 1$):** Suppose $f(1), f(2), \dots, f(n) \in E$ have been defined. Consider the remaining subset
  $$
  E_n := E \setminus \{f(1), f(2), \dots, f(n)\}
  $$
  Because $E$ is infinite and $\{f(1), \dots, f(n)\}$ is finite, $E_n$ cannot be empty! By the Well-Ordering Principle, $E_n$ has a least element, so we define
  $$
  f(n + 1) := \min E_n
  $$

Let's verify that $f: \mathbb{N}^+ \to E$ is a bijection:
1. **Strict Monotonicity and Injectivity:** For every $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$, $f(n + 1) = \min E_n$ is chosen from $E_n = E \setminus \{f(1), \dots, f(n)\}$, so $f(n + 1) \neq f(k)$ for all $1 \le k \le n$. Moreover, since $E_n \subsetneq E_{n-1}$, taking the minimum over a smaller set cannot decrease the minimum, so $f(1) < f(2) < f(3) < \dots$ is strictly increasing. In particular, if $i < j$, then $f(i) < f(j)$, so $f$ is injective.
2. **Lower Bound $f(k) \ge k$:** We prove by induction on $k \in \mathbb{N}^+$ that $f(k) \ge k$. For $k = 1$, $f(1) \in \mathbb{N}^+$ immediately satisfies $f(1) \ge 1$. If $f(k) \ge k$, then because $f(k + 1) > f(k)$ is an integer, we have $f(k + 1) \ge f(k) + 1 \ge k + 1$.
3. **Surjectivity onto $E$:** Take any element $m \in E$. By the lower bound property, $f(m + 1) \ge m + 1 > m$. Consider the set $\{f(1), f(2), \dots, f(m)\}$. If $m$ did not belong to $\{f(1), \dots, f(m)\}$, then $m$ would still lie in the remaining set $E_m = E \setminus \{f(1), \dots, f(m)\}$, which would force $f(m + 1) = \min E_m \le m$, contradicting $f(m + 1) \ge m + 1 > m$! Therefore $m \in \{f(1), \dots, f(m)\}$, proving that $f$ is surjective onto $E$. Thus $E \sim \mathbb{N}^+$.

Now let $A$ be any non-empty set, and let's prove the equivalence of (1), (2), and (3):
- **(1) $\implies$ (2):** If $A$ is finite with $|A| = n \ge 1$, there is a bijection $g: A \to \mathbb{N}_n \subseteq \mathbb{N}^+$, which is an injection into $\mathbb{N}^+$. If $A$ is countably infinite, there is a bijection $g: A \to \mathbb{N}^+$, which is also an injection into $\mathbb{N}^+$.
- **(2) $\implies$ (1):** Suppose $g: A \hookrightarrow \mathbb{N}^+$ is injective. Then $g$ restricts to a bijection between $A$ and its image $E := g(A) \subseteq \mathbb{N}^+$, so $A \sim E$. Since we just proved every subset $E \subseteq \mathbb{N}^+$ is countable, $A$ is also countable!
- **(1) $\implies$ (3):** If $A$ is countably infinite, any bijection $h: \mathbb{N}^+ \to A$ is surjective. If $A$ is finite and non-empty, there is a bijection $\phi: \mathbb{N}_n \to A$ for some $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$; we extend $\phi$ to a surjection $h: \mathbb{N}^+ \twoheadrightarrow A$ by repeating the last element: $h(k) := \phi(k)$ for $1 \le k \le n$, and $h(k) := \phi(n)$ for all $k > n$.
- **(3) $\implies$ (2):** Suppose $h: \mathbb{N}^+ \twoheadrightarrow A$ is surjective. For each element $a \in A$, the preimage $h^{-1}(\{a\}) = \{k \in \mathbb{N}^+ : h(k) = a\}$ is a non-empty subset of $\mathbb{N}^+$ because $h$ is surjective. By the Well-Ordering Principle, we can define $g: A \to \mathbb{N}^+$ by assigning to each $a \in A$ the *first* index where $a$ appears in the list:
  $$
  g(a) := \min \{k \in \mathbb{N}^+ : h(k) = a\}
  $$
  Since $h(g(a)) = a$ for all $a \in A$, if $g(a_1) = g(a_2)$, applying $h$ to both sides yields $a_1 = h(g(a_1)) = h(g(a_2)) = a_2$. Thus $g: A \hookrightarrow \mathbb{N}^+$ is injective! $\blacksquare$

> [!note] Why Injections and Surjections Make Proving Countability Easy
> Look closely at what the equivalence of (1), (2), and (3) buys us in practice. To prove that a set $A$ is countable, you **do not** have to construct a delicate, duplicate-free bijection $f: \mathbb{N}^+ \to A$ by hand!
> - Using **(2)**, it suffices to find an **injection** $g: A \hookrightarrow \mathbb{N}^+$ (unique integer ID codes for elements of $A$, even if many integers are skipped).
> - Using **(3)**, it suffices to list all elements of $A$ in a sequence $h(1), h(2), h(3), \dots$ **even if elements are repeated infinitely many times**, because we can always delete duplicates Afterward by keeping only the first occurrence $g(a) = \min h^{-1}(\{a\})$.

### Cantor's Diagonal Zig-Zag and Countable Unions

With this powerful toolkit in hand, let's tackle $\mathbb{Z}$, the two-dimensional grid $\mathbb{N}^+ \times \mathbb{N}^+$, countable unions of countable sets, and $\mathbb{Q}$.

How can we count the two-dimensional infinite grid $\mathbb{N}^+ \times \mathbb{N}^+$? Imagine arranging all ordered pairs $(m, n) \in \mathbb{N}^+ \times \mathbb{N}^+$ into an infinite matrix with rows indexed by $m = 1, 2, 3, \dots$ and columns indexed by $n = 1, 2, 3, \dots$:
$$
\begin{matrix}
(1, 1) & (1, 2) & (1, 3) & (1, 4) & \cdots \\
(2, 1) & (2, 2) & (2, 3) & (2, 4) & \cdots \\
(3, 1) & (3, 2) & (3, 3) & (3, 4) & \cdots \\
(4, 1) & (4, 2) & (4, 3) & (4, 4) & \cdots \\
\vdots & \vdots & \vdots & \vdots & \ddots
\end{matrix}
$$
If you tried to count this grid by reading across the first row $(1, 1), (1, 2), (1, 3), \dots$, you would spend all of eternity in Row $1$ and never reach $(2, 1)$!

Cantor realized that instead of sweeping along infinite rows or columns, we should sweep along the **finite anti-diagonals** where the sum of the coordinates $s = m + n$ is constant:
- **Diagonal $s = 2$ ($1$ pair):** $(1, 1)$
- **Diagonal $s = 3$ ($2$ pairs):** $(1, 2), \; (2, 1)$
- **Diagonal $s = 4$ ($3$ pairs):** $(1, 3), \; (2, 2), \; (3, 1)$
- **Diagonal $s = 5$ ($4$ pairs):** $(1, 4), \; (2, 3), \; (3, 2), \; (4, 1)$

Because each diagonal $m + n = s$ contains only $s - 1$ pairs, we finish each diagonal in finitely many steps and move on to the next, eventually hitting every single pair $(m, n)$ in the entire two-dimensional grid!

> [!theorem] Countability of $\mathbb{Z}$, $\mathbb{N}^+ \times \mathbb{N}^+$, Countable Unions, and $\mathbb{Q}$
> 1. The set of integers $\mathbb{Z}$ is countably infinite.
> 2. The Cartesian product $\mathbb{N}^+ \times \mathbb{N}^+$ is countably infinite. More generally, if $A$ and $B$ are countable sets, then $A \times B$ is countable (and by induction, any finite Cartesian product $A_1 \times A_2 \times \dots \times A_k$ of countable sets is countable).
> 3. If $\{A_n\}_{n=1}^\infty$ is a sequence of countable sets, then their **countable union**
>    $$
>    A := \bigcup_{n=1}^\infty A_n
>    $$
>    is countable.
> 4. The set of rational numbers $\mathbb{Q}$ is countably infinite.

**Proof:**
**1. Countability of $\mathbb{Z}$:**
We list the integers by starting at $0$ and alternating between positive and negative integers of increasing magnitude:
$$
0, \; 1, \; -1, \; 2, \; -2, \; 3, \; -3, \dots
$$
Formally, define $f: \mathbb{N}^+ \to \mathbb{Z}$ by
$$
f(n) := \begin{cases}
\dfrac{n}{2} & \text{if } n = 2k \text{ is even} \\[6pt]
-\dfrac{n - 1}{2} & \text{if } n = 2k - 1 \text{ is odd}
\end{cases}
$$
