Every even positive integer $2k$ maps bijectively onto the positive integers $k \in \{1, 2, 3, \dots\}$, and every odd positive integer $2k - 1$ maps bijectively onto the non-positive integers $-(k - 1) \in \{0, -1, -2, \dots\}$. Because $\{1, 2, 3, \dots\}$ and $\{0, -1, -2, \dots\}$ partition $\mathbb{Z}$ into two disjoint sets, $f: \mathbb{N}^+ \to \mathbb{Z}$ is a bijection, so $\mathbb{Z} \sim \mathbb{N}^+$.

**2. Countability of $\mathbb{N}^+ \times \mathbb{N}^+$ and Finite Products:**
We can establish $\mathbb{N}^+ \times \mathbb{N}^+ \sim \mathbb{N}^+$ in two illuminating ways:
- *Method A (Explicit Cantor Pairing Bijection):* Following the diagonal sweep where $m + n = s$ ($s \ge 2$), how many pairs precede the diagonal containing $(m, n)$? The preceding diagonals have coordinate sums $2, 3, \dots, m + n - 1$, containing $1, 2, \dots, m + n - 2$ elements respectively, which totals to the triangular number
  $$
  1 + 2 + \dots + (m + n - 2) = \frac{(m + n - 2)(m + n - 1)}{2}
  $$
  Within its own diagonal $s = m + n$, the pair $(m, n)$ is the $m$-th entry (since the first coordinate increases from $1$ to $m$). Thus the **Cantor pairing function** $J: \mathbb{N}^+ \times \mathbb{N}^+ \to \mathbb{N}^+$ defined by
  $$
  J(m, n) := \frac{(m + n - 2)(m + n - 1)}{2} + m
  $$
  gives the exact position of $(m, n)$ in the diagonal list and is an explicit bijection!
- *Method B (Prime Factorization Injection):* Using our previous theorem, we do not even need to verify that $J$ is a bijection. Instead, define $g: \mathbb{N}^+ \times \mathbb{N}^+ \hookrightarrow \mathbb{N}^+$ by
  $$
  g(m, n) := 2^m 3^n
  $$
  Suppose $g(m, n) = g(m', n')$, so $2^m 3^n = 2^{m'} 3^{n'}$. Without loss of generality, suppose $m \le m'$; dividing both sides by $2^m$ gives $3^n = 2^{m' - m} 3^{n'}$. The left-hand side is an odd integer, whereas the right-hand side is even unless $m' - m = 0$. Thus $m = m'$, which leaves $3^n = 3^{n'}$ and forces $n = n'$. Therefore $g$ is an injection from the infinite set $\mathbb{N}^+ \times \mathbb{N}^+$ into $\mathbb{N}^+$, which immediately implies $\mathbb{N}^+ \times \mathbb{N}^+ \sim \mathbb{N}^+$!

More generally, if $A$ and $B$ are countable sets, there exist injections $g_A: A \hookrightarrow \mathbb{N}^+$ and $g_B: B \hookrightarrow \mathbb{N}^+$. Then $(a, b) \mapsto (g_A(a), g_B(b))$ injects $A \times B$ into $\mathbb{N}^+ \times \mathbb{N}^+ \sim \mathbb{N}^+$, so $A \times B$ is countable. By mathematical induction on $k$, since $A_1 \times \dots \times A_{k+1} \sim (A_1 \times \dots \times A_k) \times A_{k+1}$, any finite Cartesian product of countable sets is countable.

**3. Countable Union of Countable Sets:**
Let $\{A_n\}_{n=1}^\infty$ be a sequence of countable sets, and let $A = \bigcup_{n=1}^\infty A_n$. If $A = \emptyset$, then $A$ is finite and hence countable. Otherwise, by removing any empty sets $A_n = \emptyset$ from the union (which does not change $A$), we may assume each $A_n$ is non-empty and countable.

By our characterization of non-empty countable sets, for each $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$ there exists a surjection $h_n: \mathbb{N}^+ \twoheadrightarrow A_n$, allowing us to list the elements of $A_n$ (possibly with repetitions) as a row:
$$
A_n = \{a_{n, 1}, \; a_{n, 2}, \; a_{n, 3}, \; \dots\} \quad \text{where } a_{n, k} := h_n(k)
$$
Now define the two-index map $H: \mathbb{N}^+ \times \mathbb{N}^+ \to A$ by
$$
H(n, k) := a_{n, k}
$$
Because every element $x \in \bigcup_{n=1}^\infty A_n$ belongs to $A_n$ for at least one $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$, it appears as $a_{n, k}$ for some $k \in \mathbb{N}^+$, meaning $H: \mathbb{N}^+ \times \mathbb{N}^+ \twoheadrightarrow A$ is surjective! Composing the bijection $\phi: \mathbb{N}^+ \to \mathbb{N}^+ \times \mathbb{N}^+$ from Part (2) with $H$ gives a surjection $H \circ \phi: \mathbb{N}^+ \twoheadrightarrow A$. Therefore, $A = \bigcup_{n=1}^\infty A_n$ is countable.

**4. Countability of $\mathbb{Q}$:**
For each positive denominator $q \in \mathbb{N}^+$, let $A_q$ denote the set of all rational numbers that can be written with denominator $q$:
$$
A_q := \left\{\frac{p}{q} : p \in \mathbb{Z}\right\}
$$
For each fixed $q \in \mathbb{N}^+$, the map $p \mapsto \frac{p}{q}$ is a bijection from $\mathbb{Z}$ onto $A_q$. Since $\mathbb{Z}$ is countably infinite by Part (1), each set $A_q$ is countably infinite. Because every rational number has a positive denominator $q \in \mathbb{N}^+$, the set of all rational numbers is the countable union
$$
\mathbb{Q} = \bigcup_{q=1}^\infty A_q
$$
By Part (3), as a countable union of countable sets, $\mathbb{Q}$ is countable! Since $\mathbb{Q}$ contains the infinite set $\mathbb{N}^+$, $\mathbb{Q}$ is countably infinite ($\mathbb{Q} \sim \mathbb{N}^+$). $\blacksquare$

> [!note] Connection to Probability & Statistics: Countable Additivity
> Why is the theorem that *a countable union of countable sets is countable* one of the pillars of modern probability theory?
>
> Recall from Probability and Statistics that a probability measure $\mathbb{P}$ satisfies the axiom of **countable additivity**: for any sequence of pairwise disjoint events $E_1, E_2, E_3, \dots$,
> $$
> \mathbb{P}\!\left(\bigcup_{n=1}^\infty E_n\right) = \sum_{n=1}^\infty \mathbb{P}(E_n)
> $$
> Suppose a random experiment selects a real number $X$ uniformly at random from $[0, 1]$, so that the probability of landing on any single point $x \in [0, 1]$ is $\mathbb{P}(X = x) = 0$. What is the probability that $X$ lands on a **rational number** in $[0, 1]$?
>
> Because $\mathbb{Q} \cap [0, 1]$ is countably infinite, we can list all rational numbers in $[0, 1]$ as a sequence $q_1, q_2, q_3, \dots$ and write the event $\{X \in \mathbb{Q} \cap [0, 1]\}$ as the countable disjoint union $\bigcup_{n=1}^\infty \{X = q_n\}$. By countable additivity,
> $$
> \mathbb{P}\!\left(X \in \mathbb{Q} \cap [0, 1]\right) = \sum_{n=1}^\infty \mathbb{P}(X = q_n) = \sum_{n=1}^\infty 0 = 0
> $$
> Even though $\mathbb{Q}$ is dense in $[0, 1]$ (every tiny subinterval contains infinitely many rationals!), a dart thrown uniformly at $[0, 1]$ hits a rational number with probability **$0$** and hits an irrational number with probability **$1$**! As we are about to prove, this is only possible because $[0, 1]$ is **uncountable**, so $[0, 1]$ cannot be written as a countable union of singletons $\{x_n\}$.

## Uncountability of $\mathbb{R}$ and Existence of Irrationals

Since $\mathbb{Q}$ is densely packed along the entire number line and yet remains countable, mathematicians in the 19th century naturally wondered whether *every* infinite set is countably infinite. Could $\mathbb{R}$ also be arranged into a clever sequence $x_1, x_2, x_3, \dots$?

In 1874, Georg Cantor shocked the mathematical world by proving that **$\mathbb{R}$ is uncountable**. We will present two distinct, deeply illuminating proofs of this milestone:
1. **Cantor's Original 1874 Proof (via Nested Closed Intervals and the Least Upper Bound Property):** This proof uses **only** the ordered field axioms and the Least Upper Bound Property of $\mathbb{R}$ from Section 0.5. It requires zero machinery about decimal or binary expansions!
2. **Cantor's 1891 Diagonalization Proof (via Decimal Expansions):** This famous visual proof constructs an explicit real number $x^*$ digit-by-digit along the main diagonal of an infinite table of decimals so that $x^*$ differs from the $n$-th listed number in the $n$-th decimal place.

How does the Nested Intervals proof work conceptually? Suppose someone hands you a sequence $x_1, x_2, x_3, \dots$ and claims it lists every real number. We play an "evasion game" against their list using a shrinking sequence of closed intervals $I_1 \supseteq I_2 \supseteq I_3 \supseteq \dots$:
- First, we pick a closed interval $I_1 = [a_1, b_1]$ that misses their first number $x_1$.
- Next, inside $I_1$, we pick a smaller closed subinterval $I_2 = [a_2, b_2] \subseteq I_1$ that misses their second number $x_2$. Because $I_2 \subseteq I_1$, every point in $I_2$ now misses *both* $x_1$ and $x_2$!
- At step $n$, we trisect $I_n$ into three equal closed pieces and choose $I_{n+1} = [a_{n+1}, b_{n+1}] \subseteq I_n$ to be a piece that does not contain $x_{n+1}$.
- Finally, the **Least Upper Bound Property** guarantees that as these closed intervals nest inside one another forever, their left endpoints $a_1 \le a_2 \le a_3 \le \dots$ converge to a real number $x^* = \sup \{a_n\}$ trapped inside **every** $I_n$ simultaneously. Because $x^* \in I_n$ and $x_n \notin I_n$, this fugitive number $x^*$ cannot equal $x_n$ for *any* $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$!

> [!theorem] Uncountability of $\mathbb{R}$ and the Irrational Numbers $\mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$
> 1. The set of real numbers $\mathbb{R}$ (and consequently any open interval $(a, b)$ or closed interval $[a, b]$ with $a < b$) is **uncountable**.
> 2. The set of **irrational numbers** $\mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$ is **uncountable**. In particular, irrational numbers exist in abundance!

**Proof:**
**Part 1 (Nested Intervals Proof via Completeness):**
Suppose for the sake of contradiction that $\mathbb{R}$ is countable. Since $\mathbb{R}$ is infinite, it would have to be countably infinite, meaning we could list all real numbers in a sequence:
$$
\mathbb{R} = \{x_1, \; x_2, \; x_3, \; x_4, \; \dots\}
$$
We construct by recursion a nested sequence of non-degenerate closed intervals $I_1 \supseteq I_2 \supseteq I_3 \supseteq \dots$, where $I_n = [a_n, b_n]$ with $a_n < b_n$, such that for every $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$,
$$
x_n \notin I_n
$$
- **Base Step ($n = 1$):** Choose $a_1 := x_1 + 1$ and $b_1 := x_1 + 2$. Then $I_1 = [a_1, b_1]$ satisfies $a_1 < b_1$, and since $x_1 < x_1 + 1 = a_1$, we have $x_1 \notin I_1$.
- **Recursive Step ($n \to n + 1$):** Suppose $I_n = [a_n, b_n]$ has been constructed with $a_n < b_n$ and $x_n \notin I_n$. Let $L_n := b_n - a_n > 0$ be the length of $I_n$, and trisect $I_n$ into three closed subintervals of equal length $\frac{L_n}{3}$:
  $$
  J_1 := \left[a_n, \; a_n + \frac{L_n}{3}\right], \qquad J_2 := \left[a_n + \frac{L_n}{3}, \; a_n + \frac{2L_n}{3}\right], \qquad J_3 := \left[a_n + \frac{2L_n}{3}, \; b_n\right]
  $$
  Notice that $J_1$ and $J_3$ are completely disjoint (since $a_n + \frac{L_n}{3} < a_n + \frac{2L_n}{3}$), while $J_2$ shares only the single endpoint $a_n + \frac{L_n}{3}$ with $J_1$ and the single endpoint $a_n + \frac{2L_n}{3}$ with $J_3$. Consequently, the single real number $x_{n+1}$ can belong to at most two of the three intervals $J_1, J_2, J_3$ (and only belongs to two if it lands directly on one of the two interior division points). Thus **at least one** of the three closed subintervals $J_1, J_2, J_3$ does not contain $x_{n+1}$! Define $I_{n+1} = [a_{n+1}, b_{n+1}]$ to be the leftmost of $J_1, J_2, J_3$ such that $x_{n+1} \notin I_{n+1}$. By construction, $I_{n+1} \subseteq I_n$, $a_{n+1} < b_{n+1}$, and $x_{n+1} \notin I_{n+1}$.

Now consider the non-empty set of all left endpoints:
$$
A := \{a_1, \; a_2, \; a_3, \; \dots\} \subset \mathbb{R}
$$
Because the intervals are nested ($I_1 \supseteq I_2 \supseteq I_3 \supseteq \dots$), their endpoints satisfy the chain of inequalities
$$
a_1 \le a_2 \le a_3 \le \dots \le a_n < b_n \le \dots \le b_3 \le b_2 \le b_1
$$
In particular, for any two indices $n, m \in \mathbb{N}^+$, letting $k := \max(n, m)$ gives $I_k \subseteq I_n$ and $I_k \subseteq I_m$, so
$$
a_n \le a_k < b_k \le b_m
$$
This shows that **every right endpoint $b_m$ is an upper bound for the entire set $A$**! Since $A$ is non-empty and bounded above in $\mathbb{R}$, the **Least Upper Bound Property** guarantees that the supremum
$$
x^* := \sup A \in \mathbb{R}
$$
exists. Let's check where $x^*$ lives relative to each interval $I_n = [a_n, b_n]$:
1. Because $x^*$ is an upper bound for $A$ and $a_n \in A$, we have $a_n \le x^*$ for every $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$.
2. Because each right endpoint $b_n$ is an upper bound for $A$ and $x^*$ is the *least* upper bound of $A$, we have $x^* \le b_n$ for every $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$.

Combining these two inequalities gives $a_n \le x^* \le b_n$, which means
$$
x^* \in I_n \quad \text{for every } n \in \mathbb{N}^+
$$
However, our construction guaranteed that $x_n \notin I_n$ for every $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$! Therefore, $x^* \neq x_n$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$, which means the real number $x^* \in \mathbb{R}$ is completely missing from the list $\{x_1, x_2, x_3, \dots\}$—contradicting the assumption that the sequence listed all of $\mathbb{R}$! Hence $\mathbb{R}$ is uncountable. Since $(a, b) \sim \mathbb{R}$ by our earlier example and $(a, b) \subseteq [a, b]$, every non-degenerate interval $(a, b)$ and $[a, b]$ is also uncountable.

**Part 2 (Uncountability of the Irrational Numbers):**
Observe that $\mathbb{R}$ is the union of the rational numbers and the irrational numbers:
$$
\mathbb{R} = \mathbb{Q} \cup (\mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q})
$$
We already proved in the previous subsection that $\mathbb{Q}$ is countable. If the set of irrational numbers $\mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$ were also countable, then $\mathbb{R}$ would be the union of two countable sets and therefore countable itself—contradicting Part (1)! Thus $\mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$ must be **uncountable**. $\blacksquare$

### Cantor's Diagonal Argument and the Hierarchy of Infinities

Let's now look at Cantor's famous 1891 **Diagonal Argument** using decimal expansions on the open unit interval $(0, 1)$. Why did we give the Nested Intervals proof first? Because writing real numbers as infinite decimals $0.d_1 d_2 d_3 \dots$ technically relies on infinite series $\sum_{k=1}^\infty d_k 10^{-k}$ (which we will develop formally in the chapter on sequences and series), and has one subtle pitfall: **certain rational numbers have two different decimal expansions**! Specifically, any terminating decimal has an equivalent expansion ending in an infinite string of $9$s, such as
$$
0.50000\dots = 0.49999\dots \qquad \text{or} \qquad 0.20000\dots = 0.19999\dots
$$
Once we standardize each real number $x \in (0, 1)$ to have a unique decimal expansion (for instance, by forbidding expansions that end in an infinite string of $9$s) and avoid the digits $0$ and $9$ when constructing our new number, the diagonal argument becomes completely airtight and unforgettable.

> [!note] Cantor's Diagonal Argument (Decimal Picture on $(0, 1)$)
> Suppose for contradiction that the open interval $(0, 1)$ is countable, so we can list every real number $x \in (0, 1)$ as a sequence $x_1, x_2, x_3, x_4, \dots$ and write out their decimal expansions row by row in an infinite grid:
> $$
> \begin{aligned}
> x_1 &= 0.\mathbf{d_{11}}\, d_{12}\, d_{13}\, d_{14} \dots \\
> x_2 &= 0.d_{21}\, \mathbf{d_{22}}\, d_{23}\, d_{24} \dots \\
> x_3 &= 0.d_{31}\, d_{32}\, \mathbf{d_{33}}\, d_{34} \dots \\
> x_4 &= 0.d_{41}\, d_{42}\, d_{43}\, \mathbf{d_{44}} \dots \\
> &\;\;\vdots
> \end{aligned}
> $$
> Here $d_{nk} \in \{0, 1, 2, \dots, 9\}$ denotes the $k$-th decimal digit of the $n$-th listed number $x_n$.
>
> We now construct a brand-new real number $x^* = 0.c_1 c_2 c_3 c_4 \dots \in (0, 1)$ by walking down the **main diagonal** $\mathbf{d_{11}}, \mathbf{d_{22}}, \mathbf{d_{33}}, \dots$ and choosing the $n$-th digit $c_n$ of $x^*$ according to the rule:
> $$
> c_n := \begin{cases}
> 1 & \text{if } d_{nn} \neq 1 \\
> 2 & \text{if } d_{nn} = 1
> \end{cases}
> $$
> Why is $x^*$ guaranteed to be in $(0, 1)$ and missing from the entire list?
> 1. Because every digit $c_n$ is either $1$ or $2$, the decimal $x^* = 0.c_1 c_2 c_3 \dots$ never ends in all $0$s or all $9$s! Thus $x^*$ has a completely unambiguous decimal representation and satisfies $\frac{1}{9} = 0.111\dots \le x^* \le 0.222\dots = \frac{2}{9}$, so $x^* \in (0, 1)$.
> 2. For **every** index $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$, the $n$-th decimal digit of $x^*$ is $c_n \neq d_{nn}$, whereas the $n$-th decimal digit of $x_n$ is $d_{nn}$. Because $x^*$ has no dual-representation ambiguity, differing in the $n$-th digit forces $x^* \neq x_n$ for every $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$!
>
> Thus $x^* \in (0, 1)$ cannot appear anywhere in the sequence $x_1, x_2, x_3, \dots$, proving that $(0, 1)$—and hence $\mathbb{R}$—is uncountable!

Does the hierarchy of infinities stop at $\mathbb{R}$? Not even close! In the exercises below (specifically Problem 0.7.2), you will use a breathtaking abstraction of Cantor's diagonal argument to prove **Cantor's Power Set Theorem**: for *any* set $X$ whatsoever, its **power set**
$$
\mathcal{P}(X) := \{S : S \subseteq X\}
$$
(the collection of all subsets of $X$) has a strictly larger cardinality than $X$ itself ($X \not\sim \mathcal{P}(X)$, while $X$ injects into $\mathcal{P}(X)$). Starting with $\mathbb{N}^+$, taking power sets repeatedly produces an unending tower of strictly larger and larger infinities:
$$
\mathbb{N}^+ \quad \longrightarrow \quad \mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}^+) \sim \mathbb{R} \quad \longrightarrow \quad \mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}) \quad \longrightarrow \quad \mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R})) \quad \longrightarrow \quad \cdots
$$
There is no "largest" infinite set—above every infinity lies an even vaster infinity!

> [!summary] Summary: Countable vs. Uncountable Number Systems
> | Set | Notation | Cardinality Class | Key Mechanism / Proof Idea |
> | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
> | Positive Integers / Naturals | $\mathbb{N}^+, \; \mathbb{N}$ | Countably Infinite | Baseline of countable infinity ($n \mapsto n + 1$ gives $\mathbb{N} \sim \mathbb{N}^+$) |
> | Subsets of $\mathbb{N}^+$ | $E \subseteq \mathbb{N}^+$ | Countable | Well-Ordering Principle enumerates elements in strictly increasing order |
> | Integers | $\mathbb{Z}$ | Countably Infinite | Alternating enumeration $0, 1, -1, 2, -2, 3, -3, \dots$ |
> | Integer Grid | $\mathbb{N}^+ \times \mathbb{N}^+$ | Countably Infinite | Cantor's diagonal zig-zag ($m + n = s$) or prime encoding $2^m 3^n$ |
> | Countable Unions | $\bigcup_{n=1}^\infty A_n$ | Countable | Arrange each $A_n$ in row $n$ of a 2D grid and sweep via $\mathbb{N}^+ \times \mathbb{N}^+$ |
> | Rational Numbers | $\mathbb{Q}$ | Countably Infinite | Countable union $\bigcup_{q=1}^\infty \left\{\frac{p}{q} : p \in \mathbb{Z}\right\}$ |
> | Algebraic Numbers | $\overline{\mathbb{Q}} \cap \mathbb{R}$ | Countably Infinite | Countable union of finite root sets of integer polynomials (Problem 0.7.4) |
> | Real Numbers & Intervals | $\mathbb{R}, \; (a, b)$ | Uncountable | Nested closed intervals ($\sup A \in \bigcap I_n$) or Cantor's Diagonal Argument |
> | Irrational Numbers | $\mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$ | Uncountable | Complement of countable $\mathbb{Q}$ inside uncountable $\mathbb{R}$ |
> | Power Set of any Set $X$ | $\mathcal{P}(X)$ | Strictly larger than $X$ | Diagonal set $D = \{x \in X : x \notin f(x)\}$ avoids every $f(x)$ (Problem 0.7.2) |

## 0.7 Exercises

### Problem 0.7.1: The Cantor–Schröder–Bernstein Theorem

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $A$ and $B$ be sets, and suppose there exist an injective function $f: A \hookrightarrow B$ and an injective function $g: B \hookrightarrow A$. We want to prove—without invoking the Axiom of Choice—that $A$ and $B$ are equinumerous ($A \sim B$).
> 1. Let $\mathcal{P}(A)$ denote the power set of all subsets of $A$, and define the operator $\Phi: \mathcal{P}(A) \to \mathcal{P}(A)$ by
>    $$
>    \Phi(E) := A \setminus g\big(B \setminus f(E)\big)
>    $$
>    Prove that $\Phi$ is **monotone** with respect to set inclusion: if $E_1 \subseteq E_2 \subseteq A$, then $\Phi(E_1) \subseteq \Phi(E_2)$.
> 2. Define the subset $E^* \subseteq A$ by taking the union of all subsets expanded by $\Phi$:
>    $$
>    E^* := \bigcup \{E \subseteq A : E \subseteq \Phi(E)\}
>    $$
>    Prove that $E^*$ is a **fixed point** of $\Phi$, i.e., $\Phi(E^*) = E^*$.
> 3. Using $\Phi(E^*) = E^*$, prove that the piecewise function $h: A \to B$ defined by
>    $$
>    h(x) := \begin{cases}
>    f(x) & \text{if } x \in E^* \\
>    g^{-1}(x) & \text{if } x \in A \setminus E^*
>    \end{cases}
>    $$
>    is a well-defined **bijection** from $A$ onto $B$.

**Solution:**
Why is the Cantor–Schröder–Bernstein Theorem one of the most useful tools in set theory and analysis? Constructing an explicit single formula for a bijection $h: A \to B$ between two complicated sets (like $[0, 1]$ and $(0, 1)$, or $(0, 1)^2$ and $(0, 1)$) is often awkward and tedious. However, finding an injection $f: A \hookrightarrow B$ and a separate injection $g: B \hookrightarrow A$ is usually effortless! This theorem guarantees that whenever $A$ injects into $B$ and $B$ injects into $A$, a bijection $h: A \to B$ automatically exists. Let's prove all three parts step by step.

**Part 1 (Monotonicity of $\Phi$):**
Suppose $E_1 \subseteq E_2 \subseteq A$. Let's track how each layer of the definition $\Phi(E) = A \setminus g(B \setminus f(E))$ affects the inclusion:
1. Applying the direct image under $f$ preserves inclusion:
   $$
   E_1 \subseteq E_2 \implies f(E_1) \subseteq f(E_2)
   $$
2. Taking the complement inside $B$ reverses the inclusion:
   $$
   f(E_1) \subseteq f(E_2) \implies B \setminus f(E_2) \subseteq B \setminus f(E_1)
   $$
3. Applying the direct image under $g$ preserves this reversed inclusion:
   $$
   B \setminus f(E_2) \subseteq B \setminus f(E_1) \implies g\big(B \setminus f(E_2)\big) \subseteq g\big(B \setminus f(E_1)\big)
   $$
4. Finally, taking the complement inside $A$ reverses the inclusion a second time, restoring the original direction:
   $$
   A \setminus g\big(B \setminus f(E_1)\big) \subseteq A \setminus g\big(B \setminus f(E_2)\big) \iff \Phi(E_1) \subseteq \Phi(E_2)
   $$
Thus $\Phi$ is monotone with respect to set inclusion.

**Part 2 ($E^*$ Is a Fixed Point of $\Phi$):**
Let $\mathcal{F} := \{E \subseteq A : E \subseteq \Phi(E)\}$ be the collection of all subsets $E \subseteq A$ that are expanded by $\Phi$ (note that $\mathcal{F}$ is non-empty since $\emptyset \subseteq \Phi(\emptyset)$), so that $E^* = \bigcup_{E \in \mathcal{F}} E$. We prove $\Phi(E^*) = E^*$ by establishing mutual inclusion:
- **Showing $E^* \subseteq \Phi(E^*)$:** For every set $E \in \mathcal{F}$, we have $E \subseteq E^*$ by definition of the union. Applying the monotonicity of $\Phi$ from Part (1) to $E \subseteq E^*$ gives $\Phi(E) \subseteq \Phi(E^*)$. Since $E \in \mathcal{F}$, we also know $E \subseteq \Phi(E)$. Combining these gives
  $$
  E \subseteq \Phi(E) \subseteq \Phi(E^*) \quad \text{for every } E \in \mathcal{F}
  $$
  Because $\Phi(E^*)$ contains every $E \in \mathcal{F}$, it contains their union:
  $$
  E^* = \bigcup_{E \in \mathcal{F}} E \subseteq \Phi(E^*)
  $$
- **Showing $\Phi(E^*) \subseteq E^*$:** Now apply the monotone operator $\Phi$ to both sides of the inclusion $E^* \subseteq \Phi(E^*)$ that we just proved! By Part (1), this yields
  $$
  \Phi(E^*) \subseteq \Phi\big(\Phi(E^*)\big)
  $$
  Look closely at what this inequality says: the set $S := \Phi(E^*)$ satisfies $S \subseteq \Phi(S)$, which means $S = \Phi(E^*)$ is itself a member of the family $\mathcal{F}$! Since $E^*$ is the union of *all* sets in $\mathcal{F}$ and $\Phi(E^*) \in \mathcal{F}$, we immediately have
  $$
  \Phi(E^*) \subseteq \bigcup_{E \in \mathcal{F}} E = E^*
  $$

Combining $E^* \subseteq \Phi(E^*)$ and $\Phi(E^*) \subseteq E^*$ proves that $\Phi(E^*) = E^*$. (This general principle—that any monotone operator on a power set has a fixed point—is known as the **Knaster–Tarski Fixed Point Theorem**.)

**Part 3 ($h: A \to B$ Is a Well-Defined Bijection):**
What does the fixed-point equation $E^* = \Phi(E^*) = A \setminus g(B \setminus f(E^*))$ tell us when we take complements inside $A$? Taking $A \setminus (\cdot)$ of both sides gives
$$
A \setminus E^* = g\big(B \setminus f(E^*)\big)
$$
Let's unpack this remarkable identity! It partitions $A$ into two disjoint pieces, $E^*$ and $A \setminus E^*$, and simultaneously partitions $B$ into two corresponding disjoint pieces, $f(E^*)$ and $B \setminus f(E^*)$, such that:
1. Because $f: A \hookrightarrow B$ is injective, $f$ maps $E^*$ bijectively onto $f(E^*)$.
2. Because $g: B \hookrightarrow A$ is injective and $A \setminus E^* = g(B \setminus f(E^*))$, every element $x \in A \setminus E^*$ lies in the image of $g$ (specifically, $x = g(y)$ for a unique $y \in B \setminus f(E^*)$). Thus $g^{-1}(x)$ is **well-defined** for every $x \in A \setminus E^*$, and $g^{-1}$ maps $A \setminus E^*$ bijectively onto $B \setminus f(E^*)$!

Now let's verify formally that $h: A \to B$ is both injective and surjective:
- **Injectivity of $h$:** Suppose $h(x_1) = h(x_2)$ for some $x_1, x_2 \in A$.
  - If $x_1, x_2 \in E^*$, then $f(x_1) = h(x_1) = h(x_2) = f(x_2)$, and injectivity of $f$ implies $x_1 = x_2$.
  - If $x_1, x_2 \in A \setminus E^*$, then $g^{-1}(x_1) = h(x_1) = h(x_2) = g^{-1}(x_2)$, and applying $g$ to both sides yields $x_1 = x_2$.
  - Could one element lie in $E^*$ (say $x_1 \in E^*$) and the other lie in $A \setminus E^*$ (say $x_2 \in A \setminus E^*$)? No! For $x_1 \in E^*$, we have $h(x_1) = f(x_1) \in f(E^*)$, whereas for $x_2 \in A \setminus E^* = g(B \setminus f(E^*))$, we have $h(x_2) = g^{-1}(x_2) \in B \setminus f(E^*)$. Because $f(E^*)$ and $B \setminus f(E^*)$ are disjoint, $h(x_1) \neq h(x_2)$. Thus $h$ is injective.
- **Surjectivity of $h$:** Let $y \in B$ be arbitrary.
  - **Case 1 ($y \in f(E^*)$):** Then there exists $x \in E^*$ such that $f(x) = y$, and by definition of $h$ on $E^*$, we have $h(x) = f(x) = y$.
  - **Case 2 ($y \in B \setminus f(E^*)$):** Let $x := g(y)$. Since $y \in B \setminus f(E^*)$, we have $x \in g(B \setminus f(E^*)) = A \setminus E^*$. By definition of $h$ on $A \setminus E^*$, we have $h(x) = g^{-1}(x) = g^{-1}(g(y)) = y$.

Thus $h: A \to B$ is a bijection, proving that $A \sim B$. $\blacksquare$

### Problem 0.7.2: Cantor's Power Set Theorem and the Infinite Hierarchy of Infinities

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> For any set $X$, let $\mathcal{P}(X) := \{S : S \subseteq X\}$ denote the **power set** of $X$.
> 1. Prove that the map $x \mapsto \{x\}$ is an injection from $X$ into $\mathcal{P}(X)$.
> 2. Prove that **no function $f: X \to \mathcal{P}(X)$ can be surjective** by analyzing the diagonal subset
>    $$
>    D := \{x \in X : x \notin f(x)\} \in \mathcal{P}(X)
>    $$
>    and deduce that $X$ and $\mathcal{P}(X)$ are never equinumerous ($X \not\sim \mathcal{P}(X)$), even when $X = \emptyset$ or $X$ is infinite.
> 3. Prove that $\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}^+)$ is equinumerous with the set $\{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+}$ of all infinite binary sequences $(b_1, b_2, b_3, \dots)$ with $b_n \in \{0, 1\}$.

**Solution:**
**Part 1 ($X$ Injects into $\mathcal{P}(X)$):**
Define the singleton map $\iota: X \to \mathcal{P}(X)$ by $\iota(x) := \{x\}$ for each $x \in X$. Since $\{x\} \subseteq X$, we have $\iota(x) \in \mathcal{P}(X)$. Suppose $\iota(x_1) = \iota(x_2)$ for some $x_1, x_2 \in X$, meaning $\{x_1\} = \{x_2\}$ as sets. By the Axiom of Extensionality, two sets are equal if and only if they have the same elements; since $x_1 \in \{x_1\}$, we must have $x_1 \in \{x_2\}$, which forces $x_1 = x_2$. Thus $\iota: X \hookrightarrow \mathcal{P}(X)$ is injective (showing that the cardinality of $\mathcal{P}(X)$ is at least as large as the cardinality of $X$).

**Part 2 (Cantor's Theorem—No Function $f: X \to \mathcal{P}(X)$ Is Surjective):**
Let $f: X \to \mathcal{P}(X)$ be *any* function. Notice that for each element $x \in X$, its image $f(x)$ is a **subset** of $X$. Because $x$ is an element of $X$ and $f(x)$ is a subset of $X$, we can meaningfully ask whether $x$ belongs to its own assigned subset $f(x)$ or not: either $x \in f(x)$ or $x \notin f(x)$.

Following Cantor's diagonal idea, we collect all elements $x \in X$ that do **not** belong to their own assigned subset $f(x)$:
$$
D := \{x \in X : x \notin f(x)\}
$$
Because $D$ is a subset of $X$, $D$ is a bona fide element of the power set: $D \in \mathcal{P}(X)$. We claim that $D$ is missing from the image of $f$!

Suppose for the sake of contradiction that $D$ lies in the image of $f$, so there exists some element $x_0 \in X$ such that
$$
f(x_0) = D
$$
Now ask the decisive question: **Does $x_0$ belong to $D$ or not?** There are only two possibilities, and both lead to an immediate contradiction:
- **Case 1 ($x_0 \in D$):** By the defining membership rule of $D = \{x \in X : x \notin f(x)\}$, belonging to $D$ means that $x_0 \notin f(x_0)$. Since $f(x_0) = D$, this says $x_0 \notin D$—contradicting $x_0 \in D$!
- **Case 2 ($x_0 \notin D$):** Since $f(x_0) = D$, the statement $x_0 \notin D$ is identical to $x_0 \notin f(x_0)$. However, by the definition of $D$, any element $x \in X$ satisfying $x \notin f(x)$ is automatically placed inside $D$, so $x_0 \in D$—contradicting $x_0 \notin D$!

In summary, $x_0 \in D \iff x_0 \notin f(x_0) \iff x_0 \notin D$, which is impossible! Thus no such $x_0 \in X$ can exist, proving that $D \notin f(X)$ and therefore $f: X \to \mathcal{P}(X)$ cannot be surjective. Since every bijection is surjective, no bijection between $X$ and $\mathcal{P}(X)$ can exist, so $X \not\sim \mathcal{P}(X)$. (Notice that this works even when $X = \emptyset$, where $|X| = 0$ and $\mathcal{P}(\emptyset) = \{\emptyset\}$ has $|\mathcal{P}(\emptyset)| = 1 = 2^0$.)

**Part 3 ($\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}^+) \sim \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+}$ via Characteristic/Indicator Functions):**
Recall that $\{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+}$ denotes the set of all functions $\mathbf{b}: \mathbb{N}^+ \to \{0, 1\}$, which we write as infinite binary sequences $\mathbf{b} = (b_1, b_2, b_3, \dots)$ with $b_n := \mathbf{b}(n) \in \{0, 1\}$.

How do we encode a subset $S \subseteq \mathbb{N}^+$ as an infinite binary sequence? For each $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$, we simply record a $1$ in the $n$-th position if $n \in S$, and a $0$ in the $n$-th position if $n \notin S$! In Probability and Statistics (and measure theory), this is called the **indicator function** (or **characteristic function**) $\chi_S: \mathbb{N}^+ \to \{0, 1\}$ of the subset $S$, defined by
$$
\chi_S(n) := \begin{cases}
1 & \text{if } n \in S \\
0 & \text{if } n \notin S
\end{cases}
$$
Let $\Psi: \mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}^+) \to \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+}$ be the map that sends each subset $S \subseteq \mathbb{N}^+$ to its binary indicator sequence:
$$
\Psi(S) := (\chi_S(1), \; \chi_S(2), \; \chi_S(3), \; \dots)
$$
Let's verify that $\Psi$ is a bijection by writing down its explicit inverse $\Psi^{-1}: \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+} \to \mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}^+)$. Given any binary sequence $\mathbf{b} = (b_1, b_2, b_3, \dots) \in \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+}$, define the subset of indices where $\mathbf{b}$ has a $1$:
$$
\Psi^{-1}(\mathbf{b}) := \{n \in \mathbb{N}^+ : b_n = 1\} \in \mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}^+)
$$
- For any subset $S \subseteq \mathbb{N}^+$, we have $\Psi^{-1}(\Psi(S)) = \{n \in \mathbb{N}^+ : \chi_S(n) = 1\} = \{n \in \mathbb{N}^+ : n \in S\} = S$.
- For any binary sequence $\mathbf{b} = (b_1, b_2, \dots) \in \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+}$, letting $S := \Psi^{-1}(\mathbf{b}) = \{n \in \mathbb{N}^+ : b_n = 1\}$, we have $\chi_S(n) = 1 \iff n \in S \iff b_n = 1$, and since $b_n \in \{0, 1\}$, this means $\chi_S(n) = b_n$ for every $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$, so $\Psi(\Psi^{-1}(\mathbf{b})) = \mathbf{b}$.

Because $\Psi$ has a two-sided inverse, $\Psi: \mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}^+) \to \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+}$ is a bijection, so $\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}^+) \sim \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+}$. Combining this with Part (2) gives an immediate alternative proof that the set $\{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+}$ of all infinite binary sequences is **uncountable**: since $\mathbb{N}^+ \not\sim \mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}^+)$ and $\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}^+) \sim \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+}$, we have $\{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+} \not\sim \mathbb{N}^+$! $\blacksquare$

### Problem 0.7.3: Countability of Disjoint Open Intervals and Uncountable Positive Families

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> 1. Let $\{I_\alpha\}_{\alpha \in \Lambda}$ be a family of pairwise disjoint non-empty open intervals $I_\alpha = (a_\alpha, b_\alpha)$ in $\mathbb{R}$ (so $I_\alpha \cap I_\beta = \emptyset$ whenever $\alpha \neq \beta$). Using the Density of $\mathbb{Q}$ in $\mathbb{R}$ and the countability of $\mathbb{Q}$, prove that the index set $\Lambda$ must be **countable**.
> 2. Let $\{x_\alpha\}_{\alpha \in \Lambda}$ be a family of strictly positive real numbers ($x_\alpha > 0$ for all $\alpha \in \Lambda$) indexed by an **uncountable** set $\Lambda$. Prove that there exists some positive integer $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$ such that the set $\Lambda_n := \left\{\alpha \in \Lambda : x_\alpha \ge \frac{1}{n}\right\}$ is infinite, and deduce that for every real bound $M > 0$, there exists a finite subset $F \subset \Lambda$ such that $\sum_{\alpha \in F} x_\alpha > M$.

**Solution:**
Both parts of this problem reveal profound topological and analytical consequences of countability on the real line $\mathbb{R}$: Part (1) shows that you can **never** pack uncountably many disjoint open intervals into $\mathbb{R}$, and Part (2) shows that an uncountable sum of strictly positive numbers $\sum_{\alpha \in \Lambda} x_\alpha$ must always diverge to $+\infty$!

**Part 1 (Any Family of Pairwise Disjoint Open Intervals in $\mathbb{R}$ Is Countable):**
If $\Lambda = \emptyset$, then $\Lambda$ is finite and hence countable. Suppose $\Lambda \neq \emptyset$. Since $\mathbb{Q}$ is countably infinite, there is a bijection $\phi: \mathbb{Q} \to \mathbb{N}^+$.

For each index $\alpha \in \Lambda$, the open interval $I_\alpha = (a_\alpha, b_\alpha)$ is non-empty, which means $a_\alpha < b_\alpha$. By the **Density of $\mathbb{Q}$ in $\mathbb{R}$** (proven in Section 0.5), there exists at least one rational number $q \in \mathbb{Q}$ such that $a_\alpha < q < b_\alpha$, so the intersection $I_\alpha \cap \mathbb{Q}$ is non-empty! Using the bijection $\phi: \mathbb{Q} \to \mathbb{N}^+$, the subset of positive integers $\phi(I_\alpha \cap \mathbb{Q}) \subseteq \mathbb{N}^+$ is non-empty, so by the Well-Ordering Principle we can canonically pick the rational number $q_\alpha \in I_\alpha \cap \mathbb{Q}$ with the smallest index $\phi(q_\alpha) \in \mathbb{N}^+$.

This defines a function $g: \Lambda \to \mathbb{Q}$ given by $g(\alpha) := q_\alpha \in I_\alpha \cap \mathbb{Q}$. Let's check that $g$ is **injective**:
- Suppose $\alpha, \beta \in \Lambda$ with $\alpha \neq \beta$.
- Because the intervals in the family are pairwise disjoint, $I_\alpha \cap I_\beta = \emptyset$.
- Since $g(\alpha) = q_\alpha \in I_\alpha$ and $g(\beta) = q_\beta \in I_\beta$, the disjointness $I_\alpha \cap I_\beta = \emptyset$ makes it impossible for $q_\alpha$ and $q_\beta$ to be equal! Thus $g(\alpha) \neq g(\beta)$.

Because $g: \Lambda \hookrightarrow \mathbb{Q}$ is an injection and $\phi: \mathbb{Q} \to \mathbb{N}^+$ is a bijection, the composition $\phi \circ g: \Lambda \hookrightarrow \mathbb{N}^+$ is an injection of $\Lambda$ into $\mathbb{N}^+$. By our characterization theorem for countable sets, $\Lambda$ must be **countable**!

**Part 2 (Uncountable Families of Strictly Positive Reals Diverge):**
For each positive integer $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$, define the level set
$$
\Lambda_n := \left\{\alpha \in \Lambda : x_\alpha \ge \frac{1}{n}\right\}
$$
We claim that $\Lambda$ is the countable union of these sets $\Lambda_n$:
$$
\Lambda = \bigcup_{n=1}^\infty \Lambda_n
$$
Why does every $\alpha \in \Lambda$ belong to $\Lambda_n$ for at least one $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$? By hypothesis, $x_\alpha > 0$ for every $\alpha \in \Lambda$. By the **Archimedean Property** of $\mathbb{R}$ (Section 0.5), for the positive real number $x_\alpha > 0$, there exists a positive integer $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$ such that $\frac{1}{n} < x_\alpha$ (i.e., $n > \frac{1}{x_\alpha}$). For that integer $n$, we have $x_\alpha \ge \frac{1}{n}$, which means $\alpha \in \Lambda_n$! Thus $\Lambda = \bigcup_{n=1}^\infty \Lambda_n$.

Now suppose for the sake of contradiction that **every** $\Lambda_n$ were finite (or even countable!). Since a countable union of countable sets is countable, $\Lambda = \bigcup_{n=1}^\infty \Lambda_n$ would have to be countable—contradicting the hypothesis that $\Lambda$ is **uncountable**! Therefore, there must exist at least one positive integer $n_0 \in \mathbb{N}^+$ such that $\Lambda_{n_0}$ is **uncountable**, and in particular **infinite**.

Finally, let $M > 0$ be any real bound. Using the Archimedean Property again, choose a positive integer $K \in \mathbb{N}^+$ large enough that
$$
K > n_0 M \iff \frac{K}{n_0} > M
$$
Because $\Lambda_{n_0}$ is an infinite set, we can select $K$ distinct indices $\alpha_1, \alpha_2, \dots, \alpha_K \in \Lambda_{n_0}$ and form the finite subset $F := \{\alpha_1, \alpha_2, \dots, \alpha_K\} \subset \Lambda_{n_0} \subseteq \Lambda$ of cardinality $|F| = K$. Since every index $\alpha_j \in \Lambda_{n_0}$ satisfies $x_{\alpha_j} \ge \frac{1}{n_0}$, summing over $F$ gives
$$
\sum_{\alpha \in F} x_\alpha = \sum_{j=1}^K x_{\alpha_j} \ge \sum_{j=1}^K \frac{1}{n_0} = \frac{K}{n_0} > M
$$
Thus the finite partial sums over $F \subset \Lambda$ exceed every real bound $M > 0$! (In Probability and Statistics, this explains why a discrete probability distribution can assign strictly positive probability $\mathbb{P}(X = x_\alpha) > 0$ to **at most countably many** outcomes: if uncountably many outcomes had strictly positive probability, a finite collection of disjoint outcomes would already have total probability $> 1$, violating $\mathbb{P}(\Omega) = 1$!) $\blacksquare$

### Problem 0.7.4: Countability of Algebraic Numbers and Existence of Transcendental Numbers

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> A real number $x \in \mathbb{R}$ is called **algebraic** if it is a root of a non-zero polynomial with integer coefficients—that is, if there exist a positive integer $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$ and integer coefficients $c_0, c_1, \dots, c_n \in \mathbb{Z}$ with $c_n \neq 0$ such that
> $$
> c_n x^n + c_{n-1} x^{n-1} + \dots + c_1 x + c_0 = 0
> $$
> A real number $x \in \mathbb{R}$ that is *not* algebraic is called **transcendental** (famous examples include $\pi$ and $e$, though proving that a specific number like $\pi$ or $e$ is transcendental requires substantial work). Let $\mathbb{A} \subset \mathbb{R}$ denote the set of all real algebraic numbers, and let $\mathbb{T} := \mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{A}$ denote the set of all real transcendental numbers.
> 1. Show that $\mathbb{Q} \subset \mathbb{A}$ and that $\sqrt{2} \in \mathbb{A} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$, so $\mathbb{A}$ is strictly larger than $\mathbb{Q}$ and contains infinitely many irrational numbers.
> 2. For each degree $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$, let $\mathcal{P}_n$ denote the set of all non-zero polynomials $P(x) = c_n x^n + \dots + c_1 x + c_0$ of degree $n$ with integer coefficients $c_0, \dots, c_n \in \mathbb{Z}$ ($c_n \neq 0$). Prove that $\mathcal{P}_n$ is countably infinite.
> 3. Using the algebra fact that a non-zero polynomial $P \in \mathcal{P}_n$ of degree $n$ has at most $n$ real roots, prove that the set $\mathbb{A}$ of all real algebraic numbers is **countably infinite**, and deduce—without having to test any single number like $\pi$ or $e$—that **transcendental numbers exist** and that the set $\mathbb{T} = \mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{A}$ is **uncountable**!

**Solution:**
Before Cantor's work in 1874, proving that even a *single* transcendental number exists was considered a major triumph (Joseph Liouville constructed the first explicit transcendental number $\sum_{k=1}^\infty 10^{-k!}$ in 1844, Charles Hermite proved $e$ is transcendental in 1873, and Ferdinand von Lindemann proved $\pi$ is transcendental in 1882). Cantor stunned his contemporaries by showing that we can prove transcendental numbers exist—and in fact make up almost *all* of the real line—simply by counting!

**Part 1 ($\mathbb{Q} \subsetneq \mathbb{A}$):**
Let $r \in \mathbb{Q}$ be any rational number, so $r = \frac{p}{q}$ for some $p \in \mathbb{Z}$ and $q \in \mathbb{N}^+$. Then $r$ satisfies the degree-$1$ polynomial equation
$$
q x - p = 0
$$
with integer coefficients $c_1 = q \neq 0$ and $c_0 = -p \in \mathbb{Z}$. Thus every rational number is algebraic, so $\mathbb{Q} \subseteq \mathbb{A}$. Moreover, the irrational number $x = \sqrt{2} \in \mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$ satisfies the degree-$2$ polynomial equation
$$
x^2 - 2 = 0
$$
with integer coefficients $c_2 = 1 \neq 0$, $c_1 = 0$, and $c_0 = -2$. Hence $\sqrt{2} \in \mathbb{A} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$, proving $\mathbb{Q} \subsetneq \mathbb{A}$.

**Part 2 (Countability of the Polynomial Set $\mathcal{P}_n$):**
Fix a degree $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$. Every polynomial $P(x) = c_n x^n + c_{n-1} x^{n-1} + \dots + c_1 x + c_0 \in \mathcal{P}_n$ is uniquely determined by its ordered $(n + 1)$-tuple of integer coefficients:
$$
(c_0, c_1, \dots, c_{n-1}, c_n) \in \mathbb{Z}^n \times (\mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0\}) \subset \underbrace{\mathbb{Z} \times \mathbb{Z} \times \dots \times \mathbb{Z}}_{n + 1 \text{ factors}} = \mathbb{Z}^{n+1}
$$
Specifically, the map $\Psi_n: \mathcal{P}_n \to \mathbb{Z}^{n+1}$ defined by $\Psi_n(P) := (c_0, c_1, \dots, c_n)$ is a bijection from $\mathcal{P}_n$ onto the infinite subset $\mathbb{Z}^n \times (\mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0\}) \subset \mathbb{Z}^{n+1}$.

We proved in Theorem 0.7.2 that $\mathbb{Z}$ is countably infinite, and that any finite Cartesian product of countable sets is countable. Therefore, the $(n + 1)$-fold Cartesian product $\mathbb{Z}^{n+1}$ is countably infinite! Since $\Psi_n$ injects the infinite set $\mathcal{P}_n$ into the countable set $\mathbb{Z}^{n+1}$, our subset theorem guarantees that $\mathcal{P}_n$ is **countably infinite** for every $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$.

**Part 3 (Countability of $\mathbb{A}$ and Uncountability of Transcendental Numbers $\mathbb{T}$):**
First, let $\mathcal{P} := \bigcup_{n=1}^\infty \mathcal{P}_n$ denote the set of **all** non-zero polynomials of any degree $n \ge 1$ with integer coefficients. Because each $\mathcal{P}_n$ is countable by Part (2), their countable union $\mathcal{P} = \bigcup_{n=1}^\infty \mathcal{P}_n$ is also **countably infinite**! Thus we can enumerate all non-zero integer polynomials in a single infinite sequence:
$$
\mathcal{P} = \{P_1, \; P_2, \; P_3, \; P_4, \; \dots\}
$$
For each polynomial $P_k \in \mathcal{P}$, let $R(P_k)$ denote the set of all real roots of $P_k$:
$$
R(P_k) := \{x \in \mathbb{R} : P_k(x) = 0\}
$$
If $P_k$ has degree $n \ge 1$, basic algebra tells us that $P_k(x) = 0$ has at most $n$ distinct real solutions (since each real root $r$ factors out a linear term $(x - r)$ by polynomial division). Therefore, every root set $R(P_k)$ is **finite** (with $|R(P_k)| \le \deg(P_k)$), and in particular $R(P_k)$ is **countable**!

By definition, a real number $x \in \mathbb{R}$ is algebraic ($x \in \mathbb{A}$) if and only if $x \in R(P_k)$ for at least one polynomial $P_k \in \mathcal{P}$. Thus the set of all real algebraic numbers is the countable union
$$
\mathbb{A} = \bigcup_{k=1}^\infty R(P_k)
$$
Because a countable union of countable (here, finite!) sets is countable, $\mathbb{A}$ is countable! Since $\mathbb{A}$ contains the infinite set $\mathbb{Q}$ from Part (1), **$\mathbb{A}$ is countably infinite** ($\mathbb{A} \sim \mathbb{N}^+$).

Finally, consider the partition of $\mathbb{R}$ into algebraic and transcendental numbers:
$$
\mathbb{R} = \mathbb{A} \cup (\mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{A}) = \mathbb{A} \cup \mathbb{T}
$$
If $\mathbb{T}$ were countable (or empty!), then $\mathbb{R} = \mathbb{A} \cup \mathbb{T}$ would be the union of two countable sets and therefore countable itself—contradicting the uncountability of $\mathbb{R}$! Consequently, **transcendental numbers exist**, and the set $\mathbb{T} = \mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{A}$ of all real transcendental numbers is **uncountable**! $\blacksquare$

### Problem 0.7.5: Finite Subsets of $\mathbb{N}^+$ vs. $\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}^+) \sim \mathbb{R} \sim \mathbb{R}^2$

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> In Problem 0.7.2, we proved that the full power set $\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}^+)$ of all subsets of $\mathbb{N}^+$ is uncountable. What happens if we restrict our attention to the collection of all **finite** subsets of $\mathbb{N}^+$, and how does the cardinality of the full power set $\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}^+)$ compare to the cardinality of the real line $\mathbb{R}$ and the two-dimensional plane $\mathbb{R}^2 = \mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{R}$?
> 1. Let $\mathcal{P}_{\text{fin}}(\mathbb{N}^+) := \{F \subseteq \mathbb{N}^+ : F \text{ is finite}\}$ denote the collection of all finite subsets of $\mathbb{N}^+$. Prove that $\mathcal{P}_{\text{fin}}(\mathbb{N}^+)$ is **countably infinite** (for instance, by stratifying finite subsets by their maximum element or cardinality, or by constructing an explicit injection into $\mathbb{N}^+$ via binary expansion $\sum_{k \in F} 2^k$).
> 2. Using the **Cantor–Schröder–Bernstein Theorem** (Problem 0.7.1) and the identification $\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}^+) \sim \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+}$ (Problem 0.7.2), prove that the open unit interval $(0, 1)$ is equinumerous with $\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}^+)$ (and hence $\mathbb{R} \sim \mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}^+)$). *(Hint: Map $\mathbf{b} = (b_1, b_2, \dots) \in \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+}$ into $(0, 1)$ via the decimal expansion $0.d_1 d_2 d_3 \dots$ with digits $d_n = b_n + 1 \in \{1, 2\}$, and map $x \in (0, 1)$ into $\{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+}$ via its unique non-terminating binary expansion).*
> 3. Prove that $\{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+} \times \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+} \sim \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+}$ by interleaving pairs of binary sequences into a single sequence, and deduce that the two-dimensional Euclidean plane $\mathbb{R}^2 = \mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{R}$ has the **exact same cardinality** as the one-dimensional real line $\mathbb{R}$ ($\mathbb{R}^2 \sim \mathbb{R}$)!

**Solution:**
This problem ties together every major idea in Section 0.7, showing the razor-sharp boundary between countable and uncountable collections and establishing one of Cantor's most mind-bending discoveries: a two-dimensional square $(0, 1)^2$ (or the entire plane $\mathbb{R}^2$) has the exact same number of points as a one-dimensional line segment $(0, 1)$!

**Part 1 (Countability of $\mathcal{P}_{\text{fin}}(\mathbb{N}^+)$):**
We give two clean proofs that $\mathcal{P}_{\text{fin}}(\mathbb{N}^+)$ is countably infinite:
- *Method A (Countable Union Stratified by Cardinality or Bounding Box):* For each $m \in \mathbb{N}^+$, let $\mathcal{F}_m := \mathcal{P}(\{1, 2, \dots, m\})$ be the collection of all subsets of $\{1, 2, \dots, m\}$. Since $\{1, 2, \dots, m\}$ has $m$ elements, $\mathcal{F}_m$ is a **finite** set of size $|\mathcal{F}_m| = 2^m$. Now let $F \in \mathcal{P}_{\text{fin}}(\mathbb{N}^+)$ be any finite subset of $\mathbb{N}^+$. If $F = \emptyset$, then $F \in \mathcal{F}_1$; if $F \neq \emptyset$, then because $F$ is finite it has a maximum integer $m := \max F \in \mathbb{N}^+$, which implies $F \subseteq \{1, 2, \dots, m\}$, so $F \in \mathcal{F}_m$! Therefore,
  $$
  \mathcal{P}_{\text{fin}}(\mathbb{N}^+) = \bigcup_{m=1}^\infty \mathcal{F}_m
  $$
  Because each $\mathcal{F}_m$ is finite (hence countable), their countable union $\mathcal{P}_{\text{fin}}(\mathbb{N}^+)$ is countable! Since $\mathcal{P}_{\text{fin}}(\mathbb{N}^+)$ contains the infinitely many singletons $\{1\}, \{2\}, \{3\}, \dots$, it is **countably infinite**.
- *Method B (Explicit Base-$2$ Injection):* Define $g: \mathcal{P}_{\text{fin}}(\mathbb{N}^+) \to \mathbb{N}^+$ by setting $g(\emptyset) := 1$ and, for every non-empty finite subset $F \subseteq \mathbb{N}^+$,
  $$
  g(F) := 1 + \sum_{k \in F} 2^k
  $$
  Notice that $\sum_{k \in F} 2^k$ is simply the integer whose base-$2$ (binary) representation has a $1$ in the $2^k$-th place for each $k \in F$ and $0$ everywhere else (because $F$ is finite, this is a finite sum!). By uniqueness of binary expansions of positive integers (or by induction on $\max F$, noting that $2^m > \sum_{k=1}^{m-1} 2^k = 2^m - 2$), if $g(F_1) = g(F_2)$ then $F_1$ and $F_2$ must have the exact same maximum element, and peeling off the largest powers of $2$ one by one forces $F_1 = F_2$. Thus $g: \mathcal{P}_{\text{fin}}(\mathbb{N}^+) \hookrightarrow \mathbb{N}^+$ is injective, proving $\mathcal{P}_{\text{fin}}(\mathbb{N}^+) \sim \mathbb{N}^+$!

**Part 2 ($\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}^+) \sim \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+} \sim (0, 1) \sim \mathbb{R}$):**
By Problem 0.7.2(3), we already know $\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}^+) \sim \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+}$, and by our earlier worked example we know $(0, 1) \sim \mathbb{R}$. By transitivity of $\sim$, it suffices to prove that $\{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+} \sim (0, 1)$.

If we simply tried to map each binary sequence $\mathbf{b} = (b_1, b_2, b_3, \dots) \in \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+}$ to the real number with binary expansion $0.b_1 b_2 b_3 \dots_2 = \sum_{n=1}^\infty b_n 2^{-n}$, this map would be **almost** a bijection onto $[0, 1]$, except that dyadic rationals have two binary expansions (such as $0.1000\dots_2 = 0.0111\dots_2 = \frac{1}{2}$). Instead of repairing those countably many collisions by hand, the **Cantor–Schröder–Bernstein Theorem** lets us bypass the headache completely by constructing two separate, collision-free injections!

1. **Injection $f: \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+} \hookrightarrow (0, 1)$:** Given any binary sequence $\mathbf{b} = (b_1, b_2, b_3, \dots) \in \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+}$, define $d_n := b_n + 1 \in \{1, 2\}$ for each $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$, and let $f(\mathbf{b}) \in (0, 1)$ be the real number whose **base-$10$ (decimal)** expansion is
   $$
   f(\mathbf{b}) := 0.d_1 d_2 d_3 d_4 \dots = \sum_{n=1}^\infty \frac{b_n + 1}{10^n}
   $$
   Because every digit $d_n \in \{1, 2\}$ avoids both $0$ and $9$, we have $\frac{1}{9} = 0.111\dots \le f(\mathbf{b}) \le 0.222\dots = \frac{2}{9}$, so $f(\mathbf{b}) \in (0, 1)$. More importantly, because no decimal expansion using only digits $1$ and $2$ can ever end in an infinite string of $0$s or $9$s, the decimal representation of $f(\mathbf{b})$ is **completely unique**! Thus if $\mathbf{b} \neq \mathbf{b}'$ (say they first differ at index $k$), then $f(\mathbf{b})$ and $f(\mathbf{b}')$ have different $k$-th decimal digits and cannot be equal. Hence $f: \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+} \hookrightarrow (0, 1)$ is injective.

2. **Injection $g: (0, 1) \hookrightarrow \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+}$:** Every real number $x \in (0, 1)$ has a binary expansion $x = \sum_{n=1}^\infty a_n 2^{-n}$ with digits $a_n \in \{0, 1\}$. For numbers that have two binary expansions (one ending in $1000\dots$ and one ending in $0111\dots$), we make a **canonical choice**: always choose the unique binary expansion $(a_1, a_2, a_3, \dots) \in \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+}$ that does **not** end in an infinite string of $0$s (i.e., contains infinitely many $1$s). Define $g: (0, 1) \to \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+}$ by mapping $x$ to this canonical binary sequence $g(x) := (a_1, a_2, a_3, \dots)$. Since we can recover $x$ uniquely from $g(x)$ via $x = \sum_{n=1}^\infty a_n 2^{-n}$, if $g(x) = g(y)$ then $x = y$, so $g: (0, 1) \hookrightarrow \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+}$ is injective!

By the **Cantor–Schröder–Bernstein Theorem**, the existence of injections $f: \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+} \hookrightarrow (0, 1)$ and $g: (0, 1) \hookrightarrow \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+}$ guarantees that $\{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+} \sim (0, 1)$, and therefore
$$
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}^+) \sim \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+} \sim (0, 1) \sim \mathbb{R}
$$

**Part 3 ($\{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+} \times \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+} \sim \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+}$ and $\mathbb{R}^2 \sim \mathbb{R}$):**
How can we combine **two** infinite binary sequences $\mathbf{a} = (a_1, a_2, a_3, \dots) \in \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+}$ and $\mathbf{b} = (b_1, b_2, b_3, \dots) \in \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+}$ into a **single** infinite binary sequence without losing any information? We simply **interleave** (or "zip") their digits into odd and even positions!

Define the interleaving map $\Omega: \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+} \times \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+} \to \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+}$ by
$$
\Omega(\mathbf{a}, \mathbf{b}) := (a_1, \; b_1, \; a_2, \; b_2, \; a_3, \; b_3, \; \dots)
$$
Specifically, the $(2k - 1)$-th entry of $\Omega(\mathbf{a}, \mathbf{b})$ is $a_k$, and the $2k$-th entry of $\Omega(\mathbf{a}, \mathbf{b})$ is $b_k$. Notice that $\Omega$ has an immediate, explicit inverse $\Omega^{-1}: \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+} \to \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+} \times \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+}$ that "unzips" any single binary sequence $\mathbf{c} = (c_1, c_2, c_3, c_4, \dots)$ into its odd-indexed subsequence and its even-indexed subsequence:
$$
\Omega^{-1}(c_1, c_2, c_3, c_4, \dots) := \Big((c_1, c_3, c_5, \dots), \; (c_2, c_4, c_6, \dots)\Big)
$$
Because zipping and unzipping are exact two-sided inverses of each other, $\Omega$ is a **bijection**, proving that
$$
\{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+} \times \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+} \sim \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+}
$$
Finally, let's translate this bijection from $\{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+}$ to $\mathbb{R}$! From Part (2), we have a bijection $\psi: \mathbb{R} \to \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+}$. Therefore, the coordinate-wise map $\psi \times \psi: \mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{R} \to \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+} \times \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+}$ defined by $(\psi \times \psi)(x, y) := (\psi(x), \psi(y))$ is also a bijection. Chaining these bijections together gives
$$
\mathbb{R}^2 = \mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{R} \;\sim\; \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+} \times \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+} \;\stackrel{\Omega}{\sim}\; \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+} \;\sim\; \mathbb{R}
$$
Thus $\mathbb{R}^2 \sim \mathbb{R}$ (and by mathematical induction on the dimension $d \in \mathbb{N}^+$, $\mathbb{R}^d \sim \mathbb{R}$ for every finite dimension $d$!). When Cantor first discovered this result in 1877—that two real coordinates $(x, y) \in \mathbb{R}^2$ carry no more set-theoretic cardinality than a single real coordinate $z \in \mathbb{R}$—he famously wrote to his friend Richard Dedekind: *"I see it, but I don't believe it!"* $\blacksquare$
