# Skeleton Index

## 0.1 Foundations: Sets, Relations, and Equivalence Classes

### Naive Set Theory Essentials

**[Subset, Set Equality, and Proper Subset]**
- Define the subset relation $A \subseteq B$, state the Axiom of Extensionality for set equality $A = B$, describe the mutual inclusion proof strategy, and define proper subsets $A \subsetneq B$.
- Define the empty set $\emptyset$ and prove using vacuous truth and the Axiom of Extensionality that $\emptyset$ is unique and is a subset of every set $A$.

**[Russell's Paradox, Restricted Comprehension, and Power Sets]**
- Formulate Russell's Paradox under unrestricted comprehension and explain how ZFC resolves it via the Axiom Schema of Specification (Restricted Comprehension).
- Define the power set $\mathcal{P}(X)$ of a set $X$ and distinguish between elements of $X$ and elements of $\mathcal{P}(X)$.

**[Ordered Pair, Cartesian Product, Relations, and Functions]**
- State Kuratowski's set-theoretic definition of the ordered pair $(a, b)$ and explain why it encodes order using only unordered sets.
- Define the Cartesian product $A \times B$ of two sets $A$ and $B$.
- Define a binary relation $R$ between $A$ and $B$ (and on a set $X$) and define a function $f: A \to B$ purely as a subset of $A \times B$.

### Set Operations, Indexed Families, and De Morgan's Laws

**[Set Operations and Arbitrary Indexed Unions/Intersections]**
- Define binary union $A \cup B$, intersection $A \cap B$, set difference $A \setminus B$, complement $A^c$, and disjoint sets.
- Define an arbitrary indexed family of sets $\{A_i\}_{i \in I}$ and state the formal quantifier definitions of the arbitrary indexed union $\bigcup_{i \in I} A_i$ and intersection $\bigcap_{i \in I} A_i$.
- Evaluate the infinite union and intersection of the nested families $A_n = \left(-\frac{1}{n}, \frac{1}{n}\right)$ and $B_n = \left(0, \frac{1}{n}\right]$ over $n \in \mathbb{N}$ and justify each result via mutual inclusion.

**[De Morgan's Laws for Arbitrary Indexed Families]**
- State and prove De Morgan's Laws for the complement of arbitrary indexed unions and intersections of a family $\{A_i\}_{i \in I}$ of subsets of $X$.

### Functions, Bijections, and Direct/Inverse Images

**[Injective, Surjective, and Bijective Functions]**
- Define the domain, codomain, and range (image) of a function $f: A \to B$.
- Define injective (one-to-one), surjective (onto), and bijective functions $f: A \to B$, and state and justify the necessary and sufficient condition for $f$ to possess a two-sided inverse function $f^{-1}: B \to A$.

**[Direct Image and Inverse Image (Preimage)]**
- Define the direct image $f(E)$ of a subset $E \subseteq A$ and the inverse image (preimage) $f^{-1}(H)$ of a subset $H \subseteq B$ for an arbitrary function $f: A \to B$.
- Explain the distinction between the point-to-point inverse function $f^{-1}: B \to A$ and the set-to-set preimage operation $f^{-1}: \mathcal{P}(B) \to \mathcal{P}(A)$.

**[Behavior of Direct and Inverse Images under Set Operations]**
- State and prove how inverse images $f^{-1}(\cdot)$ and direct images $f(\cdot)$ interact with arbitrary indexed unions, arbitrary indexed intersections, and complements.
- Determine the necessary and sufficient condition on $f: A \to B$ for direct images to preserve intersections across all families of subsets of $A$, prove it, and construct a concrete counterexample using $f(x) = x^2$ when this condition fails.

### Equivalence Relations and Quotients

**[Equivalence Relation, Equivalence Class, and Quotient Set]**
- State the three defining axioms of an equivalence relation $\sim$ on a set $X$.
- Define the equivalence class $[x]$ of an element $x \in X$, a representative of $[x]$, the quotient set $X / {\sim}$, and the canonical projection $\pi: X \to X / {\sim}$.

**[Fundamental Theorem of Equivalence Relations and Partitions]**
- State and prove the three fundamental properties of equivalence classes under an equivalence relation $\sim$ on $X$.
- Define a partition of a set $X$ and explain the exact bijective correspondence between equivalence relations on $X$ and partitions of $X$.
- Describe the equivalence classes and quotient sets for congruence modulo $3$ on $\mathbb{Z}$ and for the relation $u \sim v \iff T(u) = T(v)$ induced by a linear transformation $T: \mathbb{R}^3 \to \mathbb{R}^2$.

**[Well-Definedness on Quotient Sets]**
- Explain what it means for a function or binary operation defined on equivalence classes in $X / {\sim}$ via representatives to be well-defined, and state the exact logical conditions that must be verified.

### 0.1 Exercises

**[Problem 0.1.1: Kuratowski's Ordered Pair Uniqueness Theorem]**
- Using only the Axiom of Extensionality and the definition of the Kuratowski ordered pair $(a, b) := \{\{a\}, \{a, b\}\}$, prove that for any objects $a, b, c, d$, $\{\{a\}, \{a, b\}\} = \{\{c\}, \{c, d\}\} \iff a = c \text{ and } b = d$. Be sure to explicitly account for the degenerate case in which $a = b$ (so that $\{\{a\}, \{a, b\}\}$ collapses to the singleton $\{\{a\}\}$).

**[Problem 0.1.2: Characterizing Injectivity and Surjectivity via Image–Preimage Compositions]**
- Let $f: A \to B$ be any function. (1) Prove that for every subset $E \subseteq A$, $E \subseteq f^{-1}(f(E))$. Then prove that $f$ is injective if and only if $E = f^{-1}(f(E))$ holds for all subsets $E \subseteq A$. (2) Prove that for every subset $H \subseteq B$, $f(f^{-1}(H)) \subseteq H$. Then prove that $f$ is surjective if and only if $f(f^{-1}(H)) = H$ holds for all subsets $H \subseteq B$.

**[Problem 0.1.3: Universal Factorization of Functions Through Quotient Sets]**
- Let $\sim$ be an equivalence relation on a set $X$, and let $\pi: X \to X / {\sim}$ be the canonical projection map defined by $\pi(x) := [x]$. Suppose $f: X \to Y$ is a function into another set $Y$. (1) Prove that there exists a well-defined function $\tilde{f}: X / {\sim} \to Y$ satisfying $\tilde{f} \circ \pi = f$ (meaning $\tilde{f}([x]) = f(x)$ for all $x \in X$) if and only if $f$ is constant on each equivalence class of $\sim$ (i.e., $x \sim x' \implies f(x) = f(x')$), and show that $\tilde{f}$ is unique when it exists. (2) Assuming $\tilde{f}$ exists, state and prove the necessary and sufficient conditions on $f$ and $\sim$ for the induced quotient map $\tilde{f}: X / {\sim} \to Y$ to be injective, and to be surjective.

**[Problem 0.1.4: Saturation of Subsets and Exactness of Direct Images on Intersections]**
- Let $f: A \to B$ be a function, and define the kernel equivalence relation $\sim_f$ on $A$ by declaring $x \sim_f x' \iff f(x) = f(x')$. A subset $E \subseteq A$ is called $f$-saturated if whenever $x \in E$ and $x' \in A$ satisfies $x' \sim_f x$, we also have $x' \in E$. (1) Prove that for any subset $E \subseteq A$, $f^{-1}(f(E)) = \{x \in A : \exists e \in E \text{ such that } x \sim_f e\} = \bigcup_{e \in E} [e]_{\sim_f}$. (2) Using Part 1, prove that the following three conditions on a subset $E \subseteq A$ are logically equivalent: (a) $E$ is $f$-saturated; (b) $E = f^{-1}(f(E))$; (c) $E = f^{-1}(H)$ for some subset $H \subseteq B$. (3) Suppose $E_1, E_2 \subseteq A$ are two subsets of $A$, and suppose that at least one of $E_1$ or $E_2$ is $f$-saturated. Prove that $f(E_1 \cap E_2) = f(E_1) \cap f(E_2)$, and conclude in particular that for any $E \subseteq A$ and any $H \subseteq B$, the projection formula $f(E \cap f^{-1}(H)) = f(E) \cap H$ holds.

**[Problem 0.1.5: Monotone Maps on Power Sets and Tarski's Fixed-Point Lemma]**
- Let $X$ be a set, and let $\Phi: \mathcal{P}(X) \to \mathcal{P}(X)$ be a function from the power set of $X$ to itself that is inclusion-preserving ($A \subseteq B \implies \Phi(A) \subseteq \Phi(B)$ for all $A, B \subseteq X$). Define $\mathcal{F} := \{A \subseteq X : \Phi(A) \subseteq A\}$ and $E^* := \bigcap_{A \in \mathcal{F}} A$. (1) Explain why the index family $\mathcal{F}$ is guaranteed to be non-empty, and prove that for every $A \in \mathcal{F}$, we have $\Phi(E^*) \subseteq A$. (2) Deduce from Part 1 that $\Phi(E^*) \subseteq E^*$, and then apply $\Phi$ to both sides of this inclusion to prove that $\Phi(E^*) = E^*$ (so $E^*$ is the smallest fixed point of $\Phi$ under set inclusion). (3) Suppose $f: X \to Y$ and $g: Y \to X$ are two functions. Verify that the map $\Phi: \mathcal{P}(X) \to \mathcal{P}(X)$ defined by $\Phi(A) := X \setminus g(Y \setminus f(A))$ is inclusion-preserving, and conclude by Parts 1–2 that there exists a subset $E^* \subseteq X$ satisfying $X \setminus E^* = g(Y \setminus f(E^*))$.

## 0.2 The Natural Numbers

**[Convention on Zero and Foundational Approaches to $\mathbb{N}$]**
- State the modern analytical convention regarding whether $0$ belongs to $\mathbb{N}$, and explain the structural advantages of this convention for set theory and recursive definitions.
- Compare the set-theoretic construction perspective and the axiomatic interface perspective for establishing the natural numbers.

### Set-Theoretic Realization: Von Neumann Ordinals

**[Von Neumann Construction of the Naturals]**
- Define $0$ and the successor operation $S(n)$ set-theoretically using Von Neumann ordinals, and write out the explicit set representations of $0, 1, 2, 3$, and general $n$.
- Explain the distinction between the set $n$ and the singleton set $\{n\}$ in the Von Neumann successor operation.

**[Example: Exploring Set Operations on Von Neumann Ordinals]**
- Using the Von Neumann definition of $0, 1, 2, 3$: (1) prove from set membership that $S(n) \neq 0$ for every set $n$; (2) verify how set membership ($\in$) and strict subset inclusion ($\subsetneq$) behave on $0, 1, 2, 3$ whenever $m$ precedes $n$.

**[The Axiom of Infinity and Minimal Inductive Set]**
- Define an inductive set in Zermelo–Fraenkel set theory (ZFC) and explain how $\mathbb{N}$ is obtained from the Axiom of Infinity.

### The Peano Axioms

**[The Peano Axioms]**
- State the three primitive ingredients and all five Peano Axioms for the natural numbers $(\mathbb{N}, 0, S)$.
- Explain what pathological behavior each of Peano Axioms 3, 4, and 5 prevents in the structure of the number line.

**[Existence and Uniqueness of Predecessors]**
- State and prove by mathematical induction that every non-zero natural number $n \in \mathbb{N}$ has a unique predecessor $m \in \mathbb{N}$ such that $S(m) = n$.

### Recursive Definition of Addition and Its Algebraic Laws

**[The Recursion Theorem on $\mathbb{N}$]**
- Explain why a step-by-step recursive recipe can fail to define a valid function on a structure that violates Peano Axioms 3 or 4.
- State and prove Dedekind's Recursion Theorem on $\mathbb{N}$ (establishing both uniqueness and existence of recursively defined functions $f: \mathbb{N} \to X$), and identify the exact step in the existence proof where each of Peano Axioms 3, 4, and 5 is used.

**[Defining Addition and Proving Its Laws]**
- State the recursive definition of addition $n + m$ on $\mathbb{N}$, specifying which variable the recursion acts upon.

**[Example: Proving $1 + 1 = 2$ and $2 + 2 = 4$ from Peano Arithmetic]**
- Using the abbreviations $1 := S(0)$, $2 := S(1)$, $3 := S(2)$, and $4 := S(3)$, prove from the recursive definition of addition that $n + 1 = S(n)$ for every $n \in \mathbb{N}$, and deduce that $1 + 1 = 2$ and $2 + 2 = 4$.

**[Left-Addition of Zero and Successors]**
- Explain why commutativity of addition cannot be assumed without proof, and state and prove by induction the two helper lemmas required to handle $0$ and $S$ in the left-hand slot of addition.

**[Algebraic Laws of Addition on $\mathbb{N}$]**
- State and prove by induction the associativity, commutativity, and cancellation laws of addition on $\mathbb{N}$.
- Explain how additive cancellation is established in $\mathbb{N}$ without appealing to negative numbers or subtraction.

**[Sum Equal to Zero in $\mathbb{N}$]**
- State and prove the necessary and sufficient condition for a sum $a + b$ of two natural numbers to equal $0$.

### Recursive Definition of Multiplication and Its Algebraic Laws

**[Multiplication of Natural Numbers]**
- State the recursive definition of multiplication $n \cdot m$ on $\mathbb{N}$.

**[Left-Multiplication by Zero and Successors]**
- State and prove by induction the two helper lemmas governing left-multiplication by $0$ and by a successor $S(n)$.

**[Algebraic Laws of Multiplication on $\mathbb{N}$]**
- State and prove the six fundamental algebraic laws of multiplication on $\mathbb{N}$: multiplicative identity, distributivity over addition, commutativity, associativity, absence of zero divisors, and multiplicative cancellation.

### Order on the Natural Numbers

**[Order on $\mathbb{N}$]**
- Define the order relations $a \le b$ and $a < b$ on $\mathbb{N}$ purely in terms of addition, and derive the equivalent characterization of strict inequality $a < b$ in terms of successors.

**[Example: Discreteness of the Natural Numbers (No Number Between $n$ and $S(n)$)]**
- Prove that for every $n \in \mathbb{N}$: (1) $0 \le n$; (2) $n < S(n)$; and (3) there is no natural number $x \in \mathbb{N}$ strictly between $n$ and $S(n)$ (i.e., if $n < x$, then $S(n) \le x$).

**[Fundamental Order Properties of $\mathbb{N}$]**
- State and prove the partial order axioms (reflexivity, antisymmetry, transitivity), the Trichotomy Law, additive and multiplicative compatibility of order, and the Well-Ordering Principle on $\mathbb{N}$.
- Explain the conceptual mechanisms of Strong Induction and Fermat's Method of Infinite Descent.

### 0.2 Exercises

**[Problem 0.2.1: Logical Independence of the Peano Axioms via Pathological Models]**
- Construct an explicit set $X$, a distinguished element $0_X \in X$, and a function $S_X: X \to X$ for each of the following scenarios, and prove that your construction satisfies the required axioms while violating the target axiom: (1) **Failure of Axiom 3 Alone:** Construct a model $(X, 0_X, S_X)$ that satisfies Peano Axioms 1, 2, 4, and 5, but violates Axiom 3 (i.e., $S_X(n) = 0_X$ for some $n \in X$). (2) **Failure of Axiom 4 Alone:** Construct a model $(X, 0_X, S_X)$ that satisfies Peano Axioms 1, 2, 3, and 5, but violates Axiom 4 (i.e., $S_X$ is not injective). (3) **Failure of Axiom 5 (Induction) Alone:** Construct a model $(X, 0_X, S_X)$ that satisfies Peano Axioms 1, 2, 3, and 4, but violates the Principle of Mathematical Induction.

**[Problem 0.2.2: Logical Equivalence of Induction, Strong Induction, and Well-Ordering]**
- Let $\mathbb{N}$ be equipped with $0$, $S$, addition, and the order $\le$. Complete the logical equivalence cycle among the three major induction principles by proving: (1) **Induction $\implies$ Strong Induction:** Prove that if $Q(n)$ is a statement about natural numbers such that for every $n \in \mathbb{N}$, $(\forall k \in \mathbb{N},\; k < n \implies Q(k)) \implies Q(n)$, then $Q(n)$ holds for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$, and explain why no separate base case $Q(0)$ needs to be stated in this formulation. (2) **Strong Induction $\implies$ Well-Ordering Principle:** Using Strong Induction directly, give a concise proof that every non-empty subset $E \subseteq \mathbb{N}$ has a least element. (3) **Well-Ordering Principle $\implies$ Induction:** Assuming Peano Axioms 1–4, the Existence of Predecessors ($n \neq 0 \implies \exists m \in \mathbb{N},\; S(m) = n$), the order properties of $\le$, and the Well-Ordering Principle on $\mathbb{N}$, prove that Peano Axiom 5 (Mathematical Induction) holds.

**[Problem 0.2.3: Euclidean Division Algorithm from Peano Arithmetic]**
- Using only addition, multiplication, order, and the Well-Ordering Principle on $\mathbb{N}$ (without appealing to negative numbers, subtraction, or fractions), prove that for any $a, b \in \mathbb{N}$ with $b \neq 0$, there exist unique natural numbers $q, r \in \mathbb{N}$ such that $a = b \cdot q + r$ and $0 \le r < b$.

**[Problem 0.2.4: Categoricity of the Peano Axioms (Uniqueness of $\mathbb{N}$ Up to Canonical Isomorphism)]**
- Call any triple $(N', 0', S')$ consisting of a set $N'$, an element $0' \in N'$, and a function $S': N' \to N'$ a **Peano system** if it satisfies all five Peano Axioms. Let $(\mathbb{N}, 0, S)$ and $(N', 0', S')$ be any two Peano systems, with addition ($+$, $+'$), multiplication ($\cdot$, $\cdot'$), and order ($\le$, $\le'$) defined recursively on each system. Prove Dedekind's Categoricity Theorem: (1) **Existence and Uniqueness of the Successor-Preserving Map:** Using the Recursion Theorem, prove that there exists a unique function $\Phi: \mathbb{N} \to N'$ such that $\Phi(0) = 0'$ and $\Phi(S(n)) = S'(\Phi(n))$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$. (2) **Bijectivity (Canonical Isomorphism):** By applying the Recursion Theorem in the reverse direction from $(N', 0', S')$ to $(\mathbb{N}, 0, S)$ (or by direct induction on $\mathbb{N}$ and $N'$), prove that $\Phi: \mathbb{N} \to N'$ is a bijection. (3) **Automatic Preservation of Arithmetic and Order:** Prove that for all $n, m \in \mathbb{N}$, the map $\Phi$ automatically preserves addition ($\Phi(n + m) = \Phi(n) +' \Phi(m)$), multiplication ($\Phi(n \cdot m) = \Phi(n) \cdot' \Phi(m)$), and order ($n \le m \iff \Phi(n) \le' \Phi(m)$).

**[Problem 0.2.5: Recursive Definition of Exponentiation, Its Algebraic Laws, and Fermat's Method of Infinite Descent]**
- For any fixed base $a \in \mathbb{N}$, define $a^b$ recursively on the exponent $b \in \mathbb{N}$ by setting $1 := S(0)$, $a^0 := 1$, and $a^{S(b)} := a^b \cdot a$ for all $b \in \mathbb{N}$. Using this recursive definition and the properties of Peano arithmetic established in Section 0.2, prove: (1) **The Three Laws of Exponents:** Prove by induction that for all $a, b, c \in \mathbb{N}$, $a^{b + c} = a^b \cdot a^c$, $(a^b)^c = a^{b \cdot c}$, and $(a \cdot b)^c = a^c \cdot b^c$. (2) **Parity of Squares in $\mathbb{N}$:** Call $n \in \mathbb{N}$ even if $n = 2q$ for some $q \in \mathbb{N}$, and odd if $n = 2q + 1$ for some $q \in \mathbb{N}$ (where $2 := S(1)$). Using the Euclidean Division Algorithm (Problem 0.2.3) with $b = 2$, prove that every natural number $n \in \mathbb{N}$ is either even or odd (never both), and prove that $n^2$ is even if and only if $n$ is even. (3) **Fermat's Method of Infinite Descent ($m^2 = 2n^2$ Has No Non-Zero Solutions):** Using the Well-Ordering Principle on $\mathbb{N}$, prove that there do not exist any non-zero natural numbers $m, n \in \mathbb{N}$ ($m \neq 0, n \neq 0$) such that $m^2 = 2n^2$.

## 0.3 Constructing the Integers from the Naturals

### Why Extend the Natural Numbers?

**[Motivation for Constructing $\mathbb{Z}$]**
- Identify the linear equation in $\mathbb{N}$ that fails to have solutions when $a < b$, compare this with the existence of additive inverses in a vector space, and explain how formal differences motivate the construction of $\mathbb{Z}$.

### Equivalence Classes of Formal Differences

**[The Equivalence Relation for Integers]**
- Explain why defining an integer directly as $a - b$ would be circular, and describe how ordered pairs $(a, b) \in \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$ represent formal differences via financial and displacement-vector analogies.
- Define the equivalence relation $\sim$ on $\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$ using only addition in $\mathbb{N}$, and define the set of integers $\mathbb{Z}$ and its equivalence classes $[a, b]$.

**[Equivalence of Formal Differences]**
- State and prove the theorem that the formal-difference relation $\sim$ on $\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$ is an equivalence relation, identifying the exact step where the additive cancellation law of $\mathbb{N}$ is required.

**[Example: Visualizing Equivalence Classes in $\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$]**
- List three explicit ordered-pair representatives belonging to $[5, 2]$, $[2, 5]$, and $[0, 0]$, and describe the geometric appearance of equivalence classes when $\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$ is plotted as a lattice in the first quadrant.

### Integer Arithmetic and Well-Definedness

**[Addition and Multiplication on $\mathbb{Z}$]**
- Derive from scratchpad intuition and formally state the definitions of addition $[a, b] + [c, d]$ and multiplication $[a, b] \cdot [c, d]$ of equivalence classes in $\mathbb{Z}$.

**[What Does "Well-Defined" Mean?]**
- Explain what it means for an algebraic operation on a quotient set of equivalence classes to be well-defined, and state and prove the theorem that addition and multiplication on $\mathbb{Z}$ are well-defined with respect to the choice of representatives.

**[Algebraic Structure: Commutative Rings and Integral Domains]**
- Define the abstract algebraic structures of a commutative monoid, an abelian group, a commutative ring with identity, zero divisors (contrasting with $2 \times 2$ matrices), and an integral domain.
- Identify the additive identity $0_{\mathbb{Z}}$, multiplicative identity $1_{\mathbb{Z}}$, and negation $-x$ in $\mathbb{Z}$, and state and prove the theorem that $(\mathbb{Z}, +, \cdot)$ is a commutative ring and an integral domain.
- Define subtraction $x - y$ in $\mathbb{Z}$ in terms of additive inverses and prove that every linear equation $b + x = a$ in $\mathbb{Z}$ has a unique solution.

### Embedding the Naturals and Ordering the Integers

**[Canonical Embedding of $\mathbb{N}$ into $\mathbb{Z}$ and Trichotomy Classification]**
- Explain why $\mathbb{N}$ is not literally a subset of $(\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N})/{\sim}$, define the canonical embedding $\iota: \mathbb{N} \hookrightarrow \mathbb{Z}$, and prove that $\iota$ is injective and preserves addition, multiplication, and identities.
- State and prove the canonical three-way classification of every integer $x \in \mathbb{Z}$ into positive integers, zero, or negative integers via the Trichotomy Law on $\mathbb{N}$, and use this classification to prove that $\mathbb{Z}$ has no zero divisors.

**[Ordering the Integers]**
- Define the order relations $x \le y$ and $x < y$ on $\mathbb{Z}$ both in terms of coordinates in $\mathbb{N}$ and in terms of differences $y - x$, and prove that these two characterizations are equivalent and well-defined.
- State and prove the four fundamental properties of the strict order $<$ on $\mathbb{Z}$: Trichotomy, Transitivity, Additive Compatibility, and Multiplicative Compatibility with positive and negative integers.

### 0.3 Exercises

**[Problem 0.3.1: Units of $\mathbb{Z}$ and the Grothendieck Cancellation Criterion]**
- 1. Let $(M, +)$ be any commutative monoid (a set $M$ equipped with an associative, commutative binary operation $+$ and an identity element $0 \in M$ satisfying $m + 0 = m$ for all $m \in M$). Suppose we attempt to mimic our construction of $\mathbb{Z}$ by defining a binary relation $\sim$ on $M \times M$ via $(a, b) \sim (c, d) \iff a + d = b + c$. Prove that $\sim$ is an equivalence relation on $M \times M$ if and only if $(M, +)$ satisfies the cancellation law ($x + z = y + z \implies x = y$ for all $x, y, z \in M$). Then show that for an arbitrary commutative monoid $(M, +)$ where cancellation might fail, the modified relation $\approx$ on $M \times M$ defined by $(a, b) \approx (c, d) \iff \exists k \in M \text{ such that } a + d + k = b + c + k$ is always an equivalence relation (whose quotient $(M \times M)/{\approx}$ is an abelian group called the Grothendieck group of $M$), and prove that the canonical map $\iota: M \to (M \times M)/{\approx}$ given by $\iota(a) = [a, 0]$ is injective if and only if cancellation holds in $M$.
- 2. In a commutative ring $R$ with multiplicative identity $1_R$, an element $u \in R$ is called a unit (or invertible element) if it possesses a multiplicative inverse in $R$, meaning there exists $v \in R$ such that $u \cdot v = 1_R$. Using the order and canonical form of elements in $\mathbb{Z}$, prove that the only units of the ring $\mathbb{Z}$ are $u = 1_{\mathbb{Z}} = [1, 0]$ and $u = -1_{\mathbb{Z}} = [0, 1]$.

**[Problem 0.3.2: Universal Property of the Integers]**
- Let $\iota: \mathbb{N} \hookrightarrow \mathbb{Z}$ be the canonical embedding $\iota(n) = [n, 0]$. Suppose $(R, +_R, \cdot_R, 0_R, 1_R)$ is any commutative ring, and let $\varphi: \mathbb{N} \to R$ be a function that preserves the monoid structures of $\mathbb{N}$, meaning that for all $n, m \in \mathbb{N}$: $\varphi(0) = 0_R$, $\varphi(1) = 1_R$, $\varphi(n + m) = \varphi(n) +_R \varphi(m)$, and $\varphi(n \cdot m) = \varphi(n) \cdot_R \varphi(m)$. Prove that there exists a unique ring homomorphism $\Phi: \mathbb{Z} \to R$—that is, a function satisfying $\Phi(x + y) = \Phi(x) +_R \Phi(y)$, $\Phi(x \cdot y) = \Phi(x) \cdot_R \Phi(y)$, $\Phi(0_{\mathbb{Z}}) = 0_R$, and $\Phi(1_{\mathbb{Z}}) = 1_R$ for all $x, y \in \mathbb{Z}$—such that $\Phi \circ \iota = \varphi$ (meaning $\Phi([n, 0]) = \varphi(n)$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$).

**[Problem 0.3.3: The Law of Signs and Direct Coordinate Proof of No Zero Divisors]**
- 1. (The Law of Signs from Equivalence Classes) Using only the equivalence-class definitions of negation $-[a, b] := [b, a]$ and multiplication $[a, b] \cdot [c, d] := [ac + bd, ad + bc]$ on $\mathbb{Z}$ (together with the arithmetic laws of $\mathbb{N}$), prove directly that for all $x, y \in \mathbb{Z}$: $(-x) \cdot y = -(x \cdot y) = x \cdot (-y)$ and $(-x) \cdot (-y) = x \cdot y$. In particular, verify that $(-1_{\mathbb{Z}}) \cdot x = -x$ for all $x \in \mathbb{Z}$.
- 2. (Direct Coordinate Proof that $\mathbb{Z}$ is an Integral Domain) Give an alternative, direct coordinate proof that if $[a, b] \cdot [c, d] = [0, 0]$ and $[a, b] \neq [0, 0]$, then $[c, d] = [0, 0]$, by assuming without loss of generality that $a > b$ (so $a = b + k$ for some non-zero $k \in \mathbb{N}$), substituting into the equivalence $(ac + bd, ad + bc) \sim (0, 0)$, and using the additive and multiplicative cancellation laws of $\mathbb{N}$.

**[Problem 0.3.4: Discreteness of $\mathbb{Z}$ and Well-Ordering of Bounded-Below Subsets]**
- 1. (Discreteness of $\mathbb{Z}$) Prove that for every integer $x \in \mathbb{Z}$, there is no integer $z \in \mathbb{Z}$ satisfying $x < z < x + 1_{\mathbb{Z}}$.
- 2. (Well-Ordering of Bounded-Below Subsets of $\mathbb{Z}$) A subset $S \subseteq \mathbb{Z}$ is said to be bounded below in $\mathbb{Z}$ if there exists an integer $m \in \mathbb{Z}$ (called a lower bound of $S$) such that $m \le s$ for all $s \in S$. Prove that every non-empty subset $S \subseteq \mathbb{Z}$ that is bounded below has a unique least element (or minimum) $s_0 \in S$ satisfying $s_0 \le s$ for all $s \in S$.
- 3. (Existence of Maximums for Bounded-Above Subsets) State the analogous definition of a subset $T \subseteq \mathbb{Z}$ being bounded above in $\mathbb{Z}$, and prove (by considering the set of negations $-T := \{-t : t \in T\}$) that every non-empty subset $T \subseteq \mathbb{Z}$ that is bounded above has a unique greatest element (or maximum) $t_0 \in T$ satisfying $t \le t_0$ for all $t \in T$.

## 0.4 Constructing the Rational Numbers from the Integers

### Why Extend the Integers?

**[Algebraic Limitation of $\mathbb{Z}$ and Motivation for $\mathbb{Q}$]**
- Identify the algebraic limitation of the commutative ring $\mathbb{Z}$ regarding linear equations $q \cdot x = p$ ($q \neq 0$) and multiplicative inverses.
- Explain why division by arbitrary non-zero scalars is indispensable in Linear Algebra and Calculus, and outline the ordered-pair blueprint for constructing $\mathbb{Q}$ from $\mathbb{Z}$.

### Equivalence Classes of Formal Fractions

**[The Equivalence Relation for Rationals]**
- Define the set of non-zero integers $\mathbb{Z}^*$, define the equivalence relation $\sim$ on $\mathbb{Z} \times \mathbb{Z}^*$ using only integer multiplication, and define the set of rational numbers $\mathbb{Q}$ as a quotient set.

**[Equivalence of Formal Fractions]**
- State and prove the theorem that $\sim$ on $\mathbb{Z} \times \mathbb{Z}^*$ is an equivalence relation.
- Explain why excluding $0$ from the second coordinate and using the absence of zero divisors in $\mathbb{Z}$ are essential for the proof of transitivity, and describe the collapse that would occur if $(0, 0)$ were permitted.

### Rational Arithmetic and Field Structure

**[Addition and Multiplication on $\mathbb{Q}$]**
- Define addition $[p, q] + [r, s]$ and multiplication $[p, q] \cdot [r, s]$ on $\mathbb{Q}$, and justify why the resulting denominators belong to $\mathbb{Z}^*$.

**[Well-Definedness of Rational Arithmetic]**
- State and prove that addition and multiplication on $\mathbb{Q}$ are well-defined (independent of the choice of representatives).
- Prove the cancellation law $[a \cdot k, b \cdot k] = [a, b]$ for $a \in \mathbb{Z}$ and $b, k \in \mathbb{Z}^*$, and use it to derive the common-denominator addition rule $[p, q] + [r, q] = [p + r, q]$.

**[Embedding the Integers and Solving Division]**
- Define the canonical embedding $\iota: \mathbb{Z} \hookrightarrow \mathbb{Q}$, and prove that $\iota$ is injective and preserves addition and multiplication.
- Identify the additive identity $0_{\mathbb{Q}}$, the multiplicative identity $1_{\mathbb{Q}}$, the additive inverse $-[p, q]$, and the multiplicative inverse $[p, q]^{-1}$ of any non-zero rational $[p, q] \in \mathbb{Q}$, and verify that $[p, q]$ solves $\iota(q) \cdot x = \iota(p)$.

### Ordered Fields and Positivity

**[Field Axioms]**
- State all nine axioms of a field $(F, +, \cdot)$ (Additive Axioms A1–A4, Multiplicative Axioms M1–M4, and Distributivity D), and explain their connection to the scalar fields used in Linear Algebra.
- Explain why Axiom (M3) requires $1 \neq 0$ and what happens to a ring when $1 = 0$.

**[Ordered Field Axioms]**
- State the four order axioms (O1 Trichotomy, O2 Transitivity, O3 Additive Compatibility, O4 Multiplicative Compatibility) that make a field $F$ into an ordered field $(F, +, \cdot, <)$.

**[Ordering the Rational Numbers $\mathbb{Q}$ and Fundamental Laws of Any Ordered Field]**
- Define positivity ($x > 0$) and strict order ($x < y$) on $\mathbb{Q}$, and prove that positivity on $\mathbb{Q}$ is well-defined and makes $(\mathbb{Q}, +, \cdot, <)$ an ordered field.
- State and prove the four fundamental inequality laws that hold in *any* ordered field $F$: sign reversal under negation, multiplication of inequalities by positive and negative elements, strict positivity of non-zero squares ($x^2 > 0$ for $x \neq 0$, and hence $1 > 0$), and positivity and inequality reversal of reciprocals ($0 < x < y \implies 0 < y^{-1} < x^{-1}$).
- Prove using the ordered field axioms why the field of complex numbers $\mathbb{C}$ cannot be given an order relation $<$ that makes $(\mathbb{C}, +, \cdot, <)$ into an ordered field.

**[Density of the Rationals]**
- Prove using the arithmetic midpoint of two elements that between any two distinct rational numbers $r < s$, there exist infinitely many rational numbers.

### The Incompleteness of the Rationals

**[Irrationality of $\sqrt{2}$]**
- State and prove that there is no rational number $x \in \mathbb{Q}$ satisfying $x^2 = 2$, justifying why a positive rational can be written in lowest terms via the Well-Ordering Principle on $\mathbb{N}$.

**[Upper Bounds, Supremums, Infimums, and the Least Upper Bound Property]**
- Define upper bound, bounded above, least upper bound (supremum, $\sup E$), lower bound, bounded below, greatest lower bound (infimum, $\inf E$), and the Least Upper Bound Property (Dedekind completeness) for an ordered set $(S, <)$.
- Prove that if $\sup E$ exists, it is unique.
- Determine $\sup E_1$ and $\sup E_2$ in $\mathbb{Q}$ for $E_1 = \{x \in \mathbb{Q} : 0 \le x \le 1\}$ and $E_2 = \{x \in \mathbb{Q} : 0 \le x < 1\}$, and determine whether each supremum belongs to the respective set.

**[Proving That $\mathbb{Q}$ Fails the Least Upper Bound Property]**
- For the subsets $A = \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p > 0 \text{ and } p^2 < 2\}$ and $B = \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p > 0 \text{ and } p^2 > 2\}$, state and prove analytically (without assuming the existence of $\mathbb{R}$) that $A$ has no largest element, $B$ has no smallest element, and $A$ has no supremum in $\mathbb{Q}$.
- Compute the first two rational approximations to $\sqrt{2}$ from below (starting at $p_0 = 1 \in A$) and from above (starting at $q_0 = 2 \in B$) using the rational iteration map $p_{n+1} = \frac{2p_n + 2}{p_n + 2}$.

### 0.4 Exercises

**[Problem 0.4.1: Every Ordered Field Contains a Unique Canonical Copy of $\mathbb{Q}$]**
- Let $(F, +, \cdot, <)$ be any ordered field with additive identity $0_F$ and multiplicative identity $1_F$. Recall that an injective map $\phi: \mathbb{Q} \to F$ is called an **order-preserving field homomorphism** (or **ordered field embedding**) if for all $x, y \in \mathbb{Q}$, $\phi(x + y) = \phi(x) + \phi(y)$, $\phi(x \cdot y) = \phi(x) \cdot \phi(y)$, $\phi(0_{\mathbb{Q}}) = 0_F$, $\phi(1_{\mathbb{Q}}) = 1_F$, and $x < y \implies \phi(x) < \phi(y)$ in $F$. Define a map $\chi: \mathbb{N} \to F$ recursively using the Peano successor operation $S(n) = n + 1$ by setting $\chi(0) := 0_F$ and $\chi(S(n)) := \chi(n) + 1_F$ for all $n \in \mathbb{N}$.
  1. Prove by mathematical induction that for all $n, m \in \mathbb{N}$, $\chi(n + m) = \chi(n) + \chi(m)$, $\chi(n \cdot m) = \chi(n) \cdot \chi(m)$, and if $n < m$ in $\mathbb{N}$, then $\chi(n) < \chi(m)$ in $F$. Deduce that $\chi(n) > 0_F$ (and hence $\chi(n) \neq 0_F$) for all $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$, and explain why this proves that **no finite field can ever be made into an ordered field**.
  2. Prove that $\chi$ extends uniquely to an injective, strictly order-preserving field homomorphism $\tilde{\chi}: \mathbb{Q} \hookrightarrow F$ given by
     $$
     \tilde{\chi}([p, q]) := (\chi(a) - \chi(b)) \cdot (\chi(c) - \chi(d))^{-1}
     $$
     where $p = [a, b] \in \mathbb{Z}$ and $q = [c, d] \in \mathbb{Z}^*$ are represented as equivalence classes of natural numbers.

**[Problem 0.4.2: A Non-Archimedean Ordered Field with Infinitesimals]**
- An ordered field $F$ is said to satisfy the **Archimedean Property** if for every element $x \in F$, there exists a natural number $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$ (viewed inside $F$ via $n = 1_F + \dots + 1_F$) such that $n > x$. Let $\mathbb{Q}(t)$ denote the **field of rational functions** in a formal variable $t$ with rational coefficients: elements of $\mathbb{Q}(t)$ are equivalence classes of formal quotients of polynomials $f(t) = \frac{P(t)}{Q(t)} = \frac{a_n t^n + \dots + a_0}{b_m t^m + \dots + b_0}$ where $a_i, b_j \in \mathbb{Q}$ and $b_m \neq 0$, under the equivalence relation $\frac{P(t)}{Q(t)} = \frac{R(t)}{S(t)} \iff P(t) S(t) = Q(t) R(t)$.
  1. Define a non-zero rational function $f(t) = \frac{P(t)}{Q(t)} \in \mathbb{Q}(t)$ (written with leading coefficients $a_n \neq 0$ and $b_m \neq 0$) to be **positive** ($f > 0$) if the product of the leading coefficients satisfies $a_n b_m > 0$ in $\mathbb{Q}$, and define $f < g \iff g - f > 0$. Prove that $(\mathbb{Q}(t), +, \cdot, <)$ satisfies all axioms of an **ordered field**.
  2. Consider the rational function $\varepsilon(t) := \frac{1}{t} \in \mathbb{Q}(t)$. Prove that for every positive rational number $r \in \mathbb{Q}^+$ (and in particular for $r = \frac{1}{n}$ for every $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$),
     $$
     0 < \varepsilon(t) < r
     $$
     Explain why this makes $\varepsilon(t) = \frac{1}{t}$ a positive **infinitesimal** (and $t$ an **infinitely large** element), and deduce that $\mathbb{Q}(t)$ violates both the Archimedean Property and the Least Upper Bound Property.

**[Problem 0.4.3: Diophantine Repulsion of Rationals Away from $\sqrt{2}$]**
- Working inside an ordered field containing $\mathbb{Q}$ and a positive element $\sqrt{2}$ satisfying $(\sqrt{2})^2 = 2$:
  1. Prove that for every rational number $\frac{p}{q} \in \mathbb{Q}$ (with $p \in \mathbb{Z}$ and $q \in \mathbb{N}^+$), the integer $p^2 - 2q^2$ is non-zero, and deduce that
     $$
     \left| \frac{p^2}{q^2} - 2 \right| \ge \frac{1}{q^2}
     $$
  2. Using this lower bound and the factorization $\frac{p^2}{q^2} - 2 = \left(\frac{p}{q} - \sqrt{2}\right)\left(\frac{p}{q} + \sqrt{2}\right)$, prove that for all rational numbers $\frac{p}{q} \in (1, 2)$,
     $$
     \left| \frac{p}{q} - \sqrt{2} \right| > \frac{1}{4q^2}
     $$

**[Problem 0.4.4: The Rational Root Theorem and Irrationality of $\sqrt{6}$ and $\sqrt{2} + \sqrt{3}$]**
- Recall that two integers $p, q \in \mathbb{Z}$ are **coprime** ($\gcd(p, q) = 1$) if no prime number divides both $p$ and $q$, and **Euclid's Lemma** implies that if $\gcd(p, q) = 1$ and $q$ divides $p \cdot k$, then $q$ divides $k$.
  1. **The Rational Root Theorem:** Let $P(x) = c_n x^n + c_{n-1} x^{n-1} + \dots + c_1 x + c_0$ be a polynomial with integer coefficients $c_0, c_1, \dots, c_n \in \mathbb{Z}$, where $c_n \neq 0$ and $c_0 \neq 0$. Prove that if $x = \frac{p}{q} \in \mathbb{Q}$ (written in lowest terms with $p \in \mathbb{Z} \setminus \{0\}$, $q \in \mathbb{N}^+$, and $\gcd(p, q) = 1$) is a rational root of $P(x) = 0$, then $p$ divides $c_0$ and $q$ divides $c_n$.
  2. **Monic Integer Polynomials:** Deduce that if a monic polynomial with integer coefficients ($c_n = 1$) has a rational root $x \in \mathbb{Q}$, then $x$ must be an integer ($x \in \mathbb{Z}$) that divides $c_0$. Use this to prove that there is no rational number $x \in \mathbb{Q}$ satisfying $x^2 = 6$ or $x^3 = 5$.
  3. **Irrationality of $\sqrt{2} + \sqrt{3}$:** In an ordered field extending $\mathbb{Q}$ that contains positive elements $\sqrt{2}$ and $\sqrt{3}$ satisfying $(\sqrt{2})^2 = 2$ and $(\sqrt{3})^2 = 3$, let $\alpha := \sqrt{2} + \sqrt{3}$. Find a monic degree-$4$ polynomial $P(x) = x^4 + c_3 x^3 + c_2 x^2 + c_1 x + c_0$ with integer coefficients such that $P(\alpha) = 0$, and use the Rational Root Theorem to prove that $\sqrt{2} + \sqrt{3} \notin \mathbb{Q}$.

**[Problem 0.4.5: Adjoining $\sqrt{2}$ to $\mathbb{Q}$ — The Quadratic Ordered Field $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})$ Still Fails Completeness]**
- Define the set $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2}) := \mathbb{Q} \times \mathbb{Q}$, where we write an ordered pair $(a, b) \in \mathbb{Q} \times \mathbb{Q}$ as $a + b\sqrt{2}$, and define addition and multiplication on $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})$ by
  $$
  \begin{aligned}
  (a + b\sqrt{2}) + (c + d\sqrt{2}) &:= (a + c) + (b + d)\sqrt{2} \\
  (a + b\sqrt{2}) \cdot (c + d\sqrt{2}) &:= (ac + 2bd) + (ad + bc)\sqrt{2}
  \end{aligned}
  $$
  1. Prove that for every non-zero element $x = a + b\sqrt{2} \in \mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2}) \setminus \{0 + 0\sqrt{2}\}$, the rational number $N(x) := a^2 - 2b^2 \in \mathbb{Q}$ is non-zero, and use this to prove that $x$ has a multiplicative inverse in $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})$ given by
     $$
     (a + b\sqrt{2})^{-1} = \frac{a}{a^2 - 2b^2} + \left(\frac{-b}{a^2 - 2b^2}\right)\sqrt{2}
     $$
     Verify that $\omega := 0 + 1\sqrt{2} \in \mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})$ satisfies $\omega^2 = 2 + 0\sqrt{2}$.
  2. Prove that there is no element $x = a + b\sqrt{2} \in \mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})$ such that $x^2 = 3 + 0\sqrt{2}$. Conclude that when $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})$ is ordered as an ordered field, the subset $E := \{x \in \mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2}) : x > 0 \text{ and } x^2 < 3\}$ is non-empty and bounded above in $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})$ but has **no supremum in $\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{2})$**.

## 0.5 Constructing the Real Numbers via Dedekind Cuts

### The Geometry and Definition of a Dedekind Cut

**[Dedekind Cut and the Real Numbers $\mathbb{R}$]**
- Explain the geometric motivation for representing a real number as an open left ray of rational numbers, and explain why cuts are required to have no greatest element.
- State the three defining axioms of a Dedekind cut $\alpha \subset \mathbb{Q}$ and define the set of real numbers $\mathbb{R}$.
- Prove that if $\alpha \in \mathbb{R}$ is a Dedekind cut, $p \in \alpha$, and $q \in \mathbb{Q} \setminus \alpha$, then $p < q$.
- Define the rational cut $r^*$ associated with a rational number $r \in \mathbb{Q}$ and prove that $r^*$ satisfies all three axioms of a Dedekind cut.
- Prove that the subset $\alpha = \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p \le 0 \text{ or } p^2 < 2\}$ is a valid Dedekind cut.

### Ordering the Real Numbers and the Least Upper Bound Property

**[Order on $\mathbb{R}$]**
- Define the order relations $\alpha \le \beta$ and $\alpha < \beta$ on Dedekind cuts $\alpha, \beta \in \mathbb{R}$ in terms of set inclusion.

**[Order Properties and Completeness (Least Upper Bound Property) of $\mathbb{R}$]**
- Prove that the order relation $<$ on $\mathbb{R}$ satisfies Transitivity and the Trichotomy Law.
- State and prove the Least Upper Bound Property (Dedekind Completeness) of $\mathbb{R}$ by constructing the candidate supremum $\gamma = \sup A$ for any non-empty bounded-above family of cuts $A \subseteq \mathbb{R}$, verifying that $\gamma$ is a Dedekind cut, and proving that $\gamma$ is the least upper bound of $A$.

### Addition of Dedekind Cuts

**[Addition, Zero, and Negation of Cuts]**
- Explain why defining the additive inverse of a cut $\alpha$ naively as $\{p \in \mathbb{Q} : -p \notin \alpha\}$ fails when $\alpha$ is a rational cut, and explain how introducing a positive rational margin resolves this issue.
- Define the sum $\alpha + \beta$, the additive identity $0^*$, and the additive inverse $-\alpha$ for Dedekind cuts $\alpha, \beta \in \mathbb{R}$.

**[Additive Group Structure of $\mathbb{R}$ and Order Compatibility]**
- Prove that $\alpha + \beta$ and $-\alpha$ are valid Dedekind cuts for all $\alpha, \beta \in \mathbb{R}$.
- Prove that $(\mathbb{R}, +, 0^*)$ satisfies Commutativity (A1), Associativity (A2), Additive Identity (A3: $\alpha + 0^* = \alpha$), and Additive Inverse (A4: $\alpha + (-\alpha) = 0^*$).
- Prove Order Preservation under Addition (O3): if $\alpha < \beta$, then $\alpha + \gamma < \beta + \gamma$ for any $\gamma \in \mathbb{R}$.

### Multiplication of Dedekind Cuts and the Complete Ordered Field

**[Multiplication, One, and Reciprocals of Dedekind Cuts]**
- Explain why naive pairwise multiplication of all rational elements in two Dedekind cuts fails to produce a Dedekind cut.
- Define the product $\alpha \cdot \beta$ for positive cuts $\alpha, \beta > 0^*$, extend multiplication to arbitrary cuts $\alpha, \beta \in \mathbb{R}$ by sign cases, and define the multiplicative identity $1^*$ and the multiplicative inverse $\alpha^{-1}$ for $\alpha \neq 0^*$.
- Prove directly from the definition of cut multiplication that the cut $\alpha = \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p \le 0 \text{ or } p^2 < 2\}$ satisfies $\alpha \cdot \alpha = 2^*$.

**[$\mathbb{R}$ Is a Complete Ordered Field Extending $\mathbb{Q}$]**
- State and prove that $(\mathbb{R}, +, \cdot, <)$ is a complete ordered field and that the map $\iota: \mathbb{Q} \hookrightarrow \mathbb{R}$ given by $r \mapsto r^*$ is an injective, order-preserving field embedding.

### Rewards of Completeness: The Archimedean Property, Density of $\mathbb{Q}$, and $n$-th Roots

**[The Archimedean Property of $\mathbb{R}$]**
- State and prove the Archimedean Property of $\mathbb{R}$ using the Least Upper Bound Property, and deduce both the unboundedness of $\mathbb{N}^+$ in $\mathbb{R}$ and the non-existence of positive infinitesimals ($\forall \varepsilon > 0, \exists n \in \mathbb{N}^+$ such that $0 < \frac{1}{n} < \varepsilon$).

**[Density of $\mathbb{Q}$ in $\mathbb{R}$]**
- State and prove the Density of $\mathbb{Q}$ in $\mathbb{R}$: for any two real numbers $x, y \in \mathbb{R}$ with $x < y$, prove that there exists a rational number $r \in \mathbb{Q}$ such that $x < r < y$.

**[Existence and Uniqueness of Positive $n$-th Roots in $\mathbb{R}$]**
- State and prove that for every real number $y > 0$ and every positive integer $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$, there exists a unique positive real number $x > 0$ such that $x^n = y$.

### 0.5 Exercises

**[Problem 0.5.1: Characterizing Rational vs. Irrational Cuts via the Upper Class]**
- For any Dedekind cut $\alpha \in \mathbb{R}$, let $\alpha^c := \mathbb{Q} \setminus \alpha$ denote the upper class of rational numbers not belonging to $\alpha$. Prove that $\alpha$ is a rational cut (meaning $\alpha = r^* = \{p \in \mathbb{Q} : p < r\}$ for some $r \in \mathbb{Q}$) if and only if the complement $\alpha^c$ possesses a smallest element in $\mathbb{Q}$ (meaning there exists $r \in \alpha^c$ such that $r \le q$ for all $q \in \alpha^c$).

**[Problem 0.5.2: The Straddling Lemma for Dedekind Cuts and Multiplicative Inverses]**
- (1) Let $\alpha \in \mathbb{R}$ be a Dedekind cut. Prove that for every positive rational number $\varepsilon \in \mathbb{Q}^+$, there exist rationals $r \in \alpha$ and $s \in \mathbb{Q} \setminus \alpha$ such that $s - r < \varepsilon$, and moreover $s$ can be chosen strictly greater than the boundary of $\alpha$ (meaning there exists $s' \notin \alpha$ with $s' < s$). (2) Using Part (1), prove that for any positive cut $\alpha > 0^*$ and any rational $M > 1$, there exist positive rationals $r \in \alpha$ ($r > 0$) and $s \notin \alpha$ (with some $s' \notin \alpha$ satisfying $s' < s$) such that $\frac{s}{r} < M$. Use this to prove directly from the definition of $\alpha^{-1}$ and cut multiplication that $\alpha \cdot \alpha^{-1} = 1^*$.

**[Problem 0.5.3: Dedekind's Closure Theorem (Taking Cuts of $\mathbb{R}$ Produces No New Numbers)]**
- Suppose we repeat Dedekind's construction on $\mathbb{R}$ itself: define a cut of $\mathbb{R}$ to be a subset $L \subset \mathbb{R}$ such that (i) $L \neq \emptyset$ and $L \neq \mathbb{R}$, (ii) if $x \in L$ and $y \in \mathbb{R}$ with $y < x$, then $y \in L$, and (iii) if $x \in L$, there exists $z \in L$ with $x < z$. Prove that because $\mathbb{R}$ satisfies the Least Upper Bound Property, every cut $L$ of $\mathbb{R}$ is already of the form $L = \{x \in \mathbb{R} : x < c\}$ for a unique real number $c \in \mathbb{R}$.

**[Problem 0.5.4: The Floor Function $\lfloor x \rfloor$ and Density of the Irrational Numbers $\mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$ in $\mathbb{R}$]**
- (1) Using the Archimedean Property of $\mathbb{R}$ and the Well-Ordering Principle of $\mathbb{N}$, prove that for every real number $x \in \mathbb{R}$, there exists a unique integer $m \in \mathbb{Z}$ such that $m \le x < m + 1$ (denoted $m = \lfloor x \rfloor$). (2) Using the Density of $\mathbb{Q}$ in $\mathbb{R}$ and the fact that $\sqrt{2} \in \mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$ is irrational, prove that between any two real numbers $x, y \in \mathbb{R}$ with $x < y$, there exists an irrational number $z \in \mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$ such that $x < z < y$.

**[Problem 0.5.5: Categorical Uniqueness of Complete Ordered Fields (Isomorphism of All Models of $\mathbb{R}$)]**
- Let $(\mathbb{F}_1, +_1, \cdot_1, <_1)$ and $(\mathbb{F}_2, +_2, \cdot_2, <_2)$ be any two complete ordered fields, and identify their canonical copies of $\mathbb{Q} \subset \mathbb{F}_1$ and $\mathbb{Q} \subset \mathbb{F}_2$. For each $x \in \mathbb{F}_1$, define $L_x := \{r \in \mathbb{Q} : r <_1 x\} \subset \mathbb{Q}$, and define $\Phi: \mathbb{F}_1 \to \mathbb{F}_2$ by $\Phi(x) := \sup_{\mathbb{F}_2} L_x$. Prove that: (1) For every $x \in \mathbb{F}_1$, $L_x$ is non-empty and bounded above in $\mathbb{F}_2$, $\Phi(q) = q$ for all $q \in \mathbb{Q}$, and $r <_1 x \iff r <_2 \Phi(x)$ for all $r \in \mathbb{Q}$ and $x \in \mathbb{F}_1$. (2) $\Phi: \mathbb{F}_1 \to \mathbb{F}_2$ is a strictly order-preserving bijection that preserves addition and multiplication. (3) $\Phi$ is the unique order-preserving field isomorphism from $\mathbb{F}_1$ to $\mathbb{F}_2$ (and in fact any field homomorphism $\Psi: \mathbb{F}_1 \to \mathbb{F}_2$ between complete ordered fields automatically preserves order and satisfies $\Psi = \Phi$).

## 0.6 Absolute Value, Distance, and the Extended Reals

### Absolute Value and the Triangle Inequality

**[Absolute Value and Distance on $\mathbb{R}$]**
- Define the absolute value $|x|$ of a real number $x \in \mathbb{R}$ both piecewise and as an extremum, and define the Euclidean distance $d(x, y)$ between two real numbers.

**[Fundamental Properties of Absolute Value and the Triangle Inequality]**
- State and prove the fundamental algebraic and order properties of the absolute value, including non-negativity and definiteness, symmetry and multiplicativity, the Sandwich Equivalence for absolute-value inequalities, the Triangle Inequality, and the Reverse Triangle Inequality.
- State the three defining axioms of a metric on $\mathbb{R}$ and prove that the Euclidean distance function satisfies them, explaining the geometric role of an intermediate point and how the Reverse Triangle Inequality is used to bound denominators away from zero.

**[$\varepsilon$-Neighborhoods and Punctured Neighborhoods]**
- Define the $\varepsilon$-neighborhood $V_\varepsilon(a)$ and the punctured $\varepsilon$-neighborhood $V_\varepsilon^*(a)$ centered at $a \in \mathbb{R}$ of radius $\varepsilon > 0$, and derive their explicit open-interval representations using the Sandwich Equivalence.
- Given $x \in V_{1/2}(2)$ and an arbitrary $\varepsilon > 0$, determine a radius $\delta > 0$ such that $|x - 2| < \delta$ guarantees $\left|\frac{1}{x^2 + 1} - \frac{1}{5}\right| < \varepsilon$, proving the bound via the Triangle and Reverse Triangle Inequalities.

### The $\varepsilon$-Principle and Supremum Characterization

**[The $\varepsilon$-Principle for Inequalities and Equality]**
- State and prove the $\varepsilon$-Principle for real inequalities, for equality of real numbers, and for the intersection of all $\varepsilon$-neighborhoods centered at a point $a \in \mathbb{R}$.
- Explain why the order of logical quantifiers is essential in the $\varepsilon$-Principle by contrasting fixed real numbers $a, b$ compared across all $\varepsilon > 0$ against a variable $x$ chosen after $\varepsilon > 0$ is specified.

**[The $\varepsilon$-Characterization of the Supremum]**
- State and prove the necessary and sufficient $\varepsilon$-approximation condition for an upper bound $\alpha$ of a non-empty bounded-above set $E \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ to equal $\sup E$.
- Prove rigorously using the $\varepsilon$-characterization of the supremum and the Archimedean Property that $\sup \left\{3 - \frac{2}{n} : n \in \mathbb{N}^+\right\} = 3$, and determine whether the maximum of this set exists.
- State the relationship between $\sup(A + B)$ and the supremums of two non-empty bounded-above subsets $A, B \subseteq \mathbb{R}$, and prove it using the $\varepsilon$-characterization of the supremum and the $\varepsilon$-Principle.

### Infimums and the Extended Real Number System

**[The Reflection Principle and Greatest Lower Bound Property of $\mathbb{R}$]**
- Define the reflected set $-E$ for a non-empty subset $E \subseteq \mathbb{R}$, state and prove the Reflection Principle relating $\inf E$ and $\sup(-E)$, and deduce the Greatest Lower Bound Property of $\mathbb{R}$ along with the $\varepsilon$-characterization of the infimum.
- Show how the Reflection Principle can be combined with the additivity of the supremum to deduce the formula for $\inf(A + B)$ without repeating an $\varepsilon$-argument.

**[The Extended Real Number System $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$]**
- Define the extended real number system $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$ and its extended order relation, and specify how $\sup E$ and $\inf E$ are defined in $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$ when $E \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ is unbounded above, unbounded below, or empty ($E = \emptyset$).
- Justify the values of $\sup \emptyset$ and $\inf \emptyset$ in $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$ using both the logic of vacuous truth and the subset monotonicity law of supremums and infimums.
- Summarize the valid arithmetic conventions on $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$, identify the undefined indeterminate forms, and explain why $\overline{\mathbb{R}}$ cannot form a group or a field.

### 0.6 Exercises

**[Problem 0.6.1: Multiplicative and Reciprocal Algebra of Supremums and Infimums]**
- Let $A, B \subseteq (0, \infty)$ be non-empty bounded subsets of strictly positive real numbers, and define their pairwise product set and reciprocal set by $A \cdot B := \{a \cdot b : a \in A \text{ and } b \in B\}$ and $A^{-1} := \left\{\frac{1}{a} : a \in A\right\}$. (1) Using the $\varepsilon$-characterization of the supremum and infimum, prove that $A \cdot B$ is bounded and satisfies $\sup(A \cdot B) = (\sup A) \cdot (\sup B)$ and $\inf(A \cdot B) = (\inf A) \cdot (\inf B)$. (2) Prove that $A^{-1}$ is bounded above in $\mathbb{R}$ if and only if $\inf A > 0$, in which case $\sup(A^{-1}) = \frac{1}{\inf A}$ and $\inf(A^{-1}) = \frac{1}{\sup A}$. (3) Give an explicit counterexample showing that $\sup(A \cdot B) = (\sup A)(\sup B)$ can fail when $A$ and $B$ are allowed to contain negative numbers, and write down a general formula for $\sup(A \cdot B)$ in terms of $\sup A, \inf A, \sup B, \inf B$ valid for all non-empty bounded sets $A, B \subset \mathbb{R}$.

**[Problem 0.6.2: Supremum of Pairwise Differences and Set Diameter]**
- Let $A, B \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ be non-empty bounded subsets of $\mathbb{R}$, and define $A - B := \{a - b : a \in A \text{ and } b \in B\}$. (1) Prove that $\sup(A - B) = \sup A - \inf B$ and $\inf(A - B) = \inf A - \sup B$. (2) Define the diameter of a non-empty bounded set $A \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ by $\operatorname{diam}(A) := \sup \{|x - y| : x, y \in A\}$ and prove that $\operatorname{diam}(A) = \sup A - \inf A$.

**[Problem 0.6.3: Fekete's Subadditive Infimum Property]**
- Let $a_1, a_2, a_3, \dots$ be a sequence of positive real numbers satisfying the subadditivity inequality $a_{m+n} \le a_m + a_n$ for all $m, n \in \mathbb{N}^+$. Consider the set of averages $S := \left\{\frac{a_n}{n} : n \in \mathbb{N}^+\right\}$, which is bounded below by $0$, and let $\alpha := \inf S \in \mathbb{R}$. Using the Euclidean Division Algorithm on $\mathbb{N}$ ($n = qm + r$ with $0 \le r < m$) and the $\varepsilon$-characterization of $\inf S$, prove that for every $\varepsilon > 0$, there exists an integer $N \in \mathbb{N}^+$ such that for all $n \ge N$, $\alpha \le \frac{a_n}{n} < \alpha + \varepsilon$.

**[Problem 0.6.4: Supremum and Infimum of Functions and the Minimax (Saddle-Point) Inequality]**
- Given a non-empty set $D$ and a bounded function $f : D \to \mathbb{R}$ (meaning its range $f(D) := \{f(x) : x \in D\}$ is a bounded subset of $\mathbb{R}$), define $\sup_{x \in D} f(x) := \sup f(D)$ and $\inf_{x \in D} f(x) := \inf f(D)$. (1) Let $f, g : D \to \mathbb{R}$ be bounded functions on a non-empty set $D$. Prove that $\sup_{x \in D} (f(x) + g(x)) \le \sup_{x \in D} f(x) + \sup_{x \in D} g(x)$ and $\inf_{x \in D} (f(x) + g(x)) \ge \inf_{x \in D} f(x) + \inf_{x \in D} g(x)$. Give an explicit example of a set $D$ and bounded functions $f, g : D \to \mathbb{R}$ where both inequalities are strict, and explain why this does not contradict the identity $\sup(A + B) = \sup A + \sup B$ for sets. (2) Prove that if $f(x) \le g(x)$ for all $x \in D$, then $\sup_{x \in D} f(x) \le \sup_{x \in D} g(x)$ and $\inf_{x \in D} f(x) \le \inf_{x \in D} g(x)$. By contrast, what condition on $f$ and $g$ is required to conclude that $\sup_{x \in D} f(x) \le \inf_{x \in D} g(x)$? (3) (The Minimax / Saddle-Point Inequality) Let $X$ and $Y$ be non-empty sets, and let $h : X \times Y \to \mathbb{R}$ be a bounded function of two variables. Prove that $\sup_{x \in X} \left( \inf_{y \in Y} h(x, y) \right) \le \inf_{y \in Y} \left( \sup_{x \in X} h(x, y) \right)$, and give an explicit example on $X = Y = \{0, 1\}$ (or $X = Y = [0, 1]$) where the inequality is strict.

**[Problem 0.6.5: Distance from a Point to a Set and Lipschitz Continuity]**
- Let $A \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ be a non-empty subset of $\mathbb{R}$, and for any point $x \in \mathbb{R}$, define the distance from $x$ to the set $A$ by $d(x, A) := \inf \{ |x - a| : a \in A \}$. (1) Explain why $d(x, A)$ is a well-defined non-negative real number for every $x \in \mathbb{R}$ (even if $A$ itself is an unbounded set). (2) Prove that $d(x, A) = 0$ if and only if every $\varepsilon$-neighborhood $V_\varepsilon(x) = (x - \varepsilon, x + \varepsilon)$ around $x$ intersects $A$ (that is, $V_\varepsilon(x) \cap A \neq \emptyset$ for all $\varepsilon > 0$), and give an example of a set $A \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ and a point $x \notin A$ such that $d(x, A) = 0$. (3) Prove that for all $x, y \in \mathbb{R}$, the function $x \mapsto d(x, A)$ satisfies the Lipschitz / Non-Expansive Inequality $\big| d(x, A) - d(y, A) \big| \le |x - y|$.

## 0.7 Cardinality: Countable and Uncountable Sets

### Equinumerosity and Finite vs. Infinite Sets

**[Equinumerosity, Finite Sets, and Countable Sets]**
- Define equinumerosity $A \sim B$ between two sets $A$ and $B$, and prove that equinumerosity is an equivalence relation.
- Using the standard initial segments $\mathbb{N}_n$, define finite sets, infinite sets, countably infinite (denumerable) sets, countable (at most countable) sets, and uncountable sets.
- Explain the sequential characterization of countably infinite sets and contrast the two common textbook conventions for the term "countable."

**[Hilbert's Grand Hotel and Bijections with Proper Subsets]**
- Explain why an infinite set can be equinumerous with a proper subset of itself, and illustrate this phenomenon using Hilbert's Grand Hotel.
- **[Example: Proper Subsets and Interval Bijections]** (1) Prove that the set of even positive integers $E := \{2, 4, 6, 8, \dots\} \subsetneq \mathbb{N}^+$ is countably infinite. (2) Prove that any open interval $(a, b) \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ with $a < b$ is equinumerous with $(0, 1)$, and construct an explicit algebraic bijection showing that $(-1, 1)$ (and hence $(0, 1)$) is equinumerous with $\mathbb{R}$.

### Countability of $\mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N}$, $\mathbb{Z}$, and $\mathbb{Q}$

**[Every Subset of a Countable Set Is Countable]**
- State and prove the theorem that every subset $E \subseteq \mathbb{N}^+$ is countable.
- State and prove the equivalence between a non-empty set $A$ being countable, the existence of an injection from $A$ into $\mathbb{N}^+$, and the existence of a surjection from $\mathbb{N}^+$ onto $A$.

**[Cantor's Diagonal Zig-Zag and Countable Unions]**
- State and prove the countability of the integers $\mathbb{Z}$, the Cartesian product $\mathbb{N}^+ \times \mathbb{N}^+$ (and finite Cartesian products of countable sets), any countable union of countable sets $\bigcup_{n=1}^\infty A_n$, and the rational numbers $\mathbb{Q}$.
- Explain how the countability of $\mathbb{Q}$ and countable additivity of probability measures determine the probability of selecting a rational number under a continuous uniform distribution on $[0, 1]$.

### Uncountability of $\mathbb{R}$ and Existence of Irrationals

**[Uncountability of $\mathbb{R}$ and the Irrational Numbers $\mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$]**
- State and prove the theorem that $\mathbb{R}$ (and any non-degenerate interval) is uncountable using a nested closed intervals construction, and deduce that the set of irrational numbers $\mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$ is uncountable.

**[Cantor's Diagonal Argument and the Hierarchy of Infinities]**
- Prove that the open unit interval $(0, 1)$ is uncountable using decimal expansions, and explain how to avoid dual-representation ambiguities in decimal expansions.
- Describe the infinite hierarchy of cardinalities generated by repeatedly taking power sets $\mathcal{P}(X)$.

### 0.7 Exercises

**[Problem 0.7.1: The Cantor–Schröder–Bernstein Theorem]**
- Let $A$ and $B$ be sets, and suppose there exist an injective function $f: A \hookrightarrow B$ and an injective function $g: B \hookrightarrow A$. Prove—without invoking the Axiom of Choice—that $A$ and $B$ are equinumerous ($A \sim B$):
  1. Let $\mathcal{P}(A)$ denote the power set of all subsets of $A$, and define the operator $\Phi: \mathcal{P}(A) \to \mathcal{P}(A)$ by
     $$
     \Phi(E) := A \setminus g\big(B \setminus f(E)\big)
     $$
     Prove that $\Phi$ is monotone with respect to set inclusion: if $E_1 \subseteq E_2 \subseteq A$, then $\Phi(E_1) \subseteq \Phi(E_2)$.
  2. Define the subset $E^* \subseteq A$ by taking the union of all subsets expanded by $\Phi$:
     $$
     E^* := \bigcup \{E \subseteq A : E \subseteq \Phi(E)\}
     $$
     Prove that $E^*$ is a fixed point of $\Phi$, i.e., $\Phi(E^*) = E^*$.
  3. Using $\Phi(E^*) = E^*$, prove that the piecewise function $h: A \to B$ defined by
     $$
     h(x) := \begin{cases}
     f(x) & \text{if } x \in E^* \\
     g^{-1}(x) & \text{if } x \in A \setminus E^*
     \end{cases}
     $$
     is a well-defined bijection from $A$ onto $B$.

**[Problem 0.7.2: Cantor's Power Set Theorem and the Infinite Hierarchy of Infinities]**
- For any set $X$, let $\mathcal{P}(X) := \{S : S \subseteq X\}$ denote the power set of $X$.
  1. Prove that the map $x \mapsto \{x\}$ is an injection from $X$ into $\mathcal{P}(X)$.
  2. Prove that no function $f: X \to \mathcal{P}(X)$ can be surjective by analyzing the diagonal subset
     $$
     D := \{x \in X : x \notin f(x)\} \in \mathcal{P}(X)
     $$
     and deduce that $X$ and $\mathcal{P}(X)$ are never equinumerous ($X \not\sim \mathcal{P}(X)$), even when $X = \emptyset$ or $X$ is infinite.
  3. Prove that $\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}^+)$ is equinumerous with the set $\{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+}$ of all infinite binary sequences $(b_1, b_2, b_3, \dots)$ with $b_n \in \{0, 1\}$.

**[Problem 0.7.3: Countability of Disjoint Open Intervals and Uncountable Positive Families]**
- 1. Let $\{I_\alpha\}_{\alpha \in \Lambda}$ be a family of pairwise disjoint non-empty open intervals $I_\alpha = (a_\alpha, b_\alpha)$ in $\mathbb{R}$ (so $I_\alpha \cap I_\beta = \emptyset$ whenever $\alpha \neq \beta$). Using the Density of $\mathbb{Q}$ in $\mathbb{R}$ and the countability of $\mathbb{Q}$, prove that the index set $\Lambda$ must be countable.
  2. Let $\{x_\alpha\}_{\alpha \in \Lambda}$ be a family of strictly positive real numbers ($x_\alpha > 0$ for all $\alpha \in \Lambda$) indexed by an uncountable set $\Lambda$. Prove that there exists some positive integer $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$ such that the set $\Lambda_n := \left\{\alpha \in \Lambda : x_\alpha \ge \frac{1}{n}\right\}$ is infinite, and deduce that for every real bound $M > 0$, there exists a finite subset $F \subset \Lambda$ such that $\sum_{\alpha \in F} x_\alpha > M$.

**[Problem 0.7.4: Countability of Algebraic Numbers and Existence of Transcendental Numbers]**
- A real number $x \in \mathbb{R}$ is called algebraic if it is a root of a non-zero polynomial with integer coefficients—that is, if there exist a positive integer $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$ and integer coefficients $c_0, c_1, \dots, c_n \in \mathbb{Z}$ with $c_n \neq 0$ such that
  $$
  c_n x^n + c_{n-1} x^{n-1} + \dots + c_1 x + c_0 = 0
  $$
  A real number $x \in \mathbb{R}$ that is not algebraic is called transcendental. Let $\mathbb{A} \subset \mathbb{R}$ denote the set of all real algebraic numbers, and let $\mathbb{T} := \mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{A}$ denote the set of all real transcendental numbers.
  1. Show that $\mathbb{Q} \subset \mathbb{A}$ and that $\sqrt{2} \in \mathbb{A} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$, so $\mathbb{A}$ is strictly larger than $\mathbb{Q}$ and contains infinitely many irrational numbers.
  2. For each degree $n \in \mathbb{N}^+$, let $\mathcal{P}_n$ denote the set of all non-zero polynomials $P(x) = c_n x^n + \dots + c_1 x + c_0$ of degree $n$ with integer coefficients $c_0, \dots, c_n \in \mathbb{Z}$ ($c_n \neq 0$). Prove that $\mathcal{P}_n$ is countably infinite.
  3. Using the algebra fact that a non-zero polynomial $P \in \mathcal{P}_n$ of degree $n$ has at most $n$ real roots, prove that the set $\mathbb{A}$ of all real algebraic numbers is countably infinite, and deduce that transcendental numbers exist and that the set $\mathbb{T} = \mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{A}$ is uncountable.

**[Problem 0.7.5: Finite Subsets of $\mathbb{N}^+$ vs. $\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}^+) \sim \mathbb{R} \sim \mathbb{R}^2$]**
- 1. Let $\mathcal{P}_{\text{fin}}(\mathbb{N}^+) := \{F \subseteq \mathbb{N}^+ : F \text{ is finite}\}$ denote the collection of all finite subsets of $\mathbb{N}^+$. Prove that $\mathcal{P}_{\text{fin}}(\mathbb{N}^+)$ is countably infinite.
  2. Using the Cantor–Schröder–Bernstein Theorem and the identification $\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}^+) \sim \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+}$, prove that the open unit interval $(0, 1)$ is equinumerous with $\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}^+)$ (and hence $\mathbb{R} \sim \mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}^+)$).
  3. Prove that $\{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+} \times \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+} \sim \{0, 1\}^{\mathbb{N}^+}$ by interleaving pairs of binary sequences into a single sequence, and deduce that the two-dimensional Euclidean plane $\mathbb{R}^2 = \mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{R}$ has the exact same cardinality as the one-dimensional real line $\mathbb{R}$ ($\mathbb{R}^2 \sim \mathbb{R}$).
